ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS
Th is study brings together the arts of biography, monograph and Masonic
history to fi x an eighteenth-century naval veteran and Masonic luminary in a
position he has never before occupied – his proper historical context. In the
process of research and writing I have incurred so many debts across disciplines
and continents that acknowledging them adequately is daunting. I must begin,
however, with my colleagues at Saint Vincent College, who have been unstint-
ing in their support and encouragement. Foremost amongst them is Fr. Rene
Kollar, OSB, the Dean of the School of Humanities and Fine Arts. Rene is him-
self a distinguished scholar, and he diligently fosters the scholarly endeavours
of his faculty. Over the years he has consistently found resources for my forays
into fraternal history, and without his stalwart support this project would not
have been possible. Likewise I deeply appreciate the support of the committee
entrusted with awarding Faculty Research Grants, which partially funded the
research for this study. Other colleagues at Saint Vincent and elsewhere who
read draft s and off ered invaluable suggestions include Eric Duff y, Br. Bruno Hei-
sey, OSB, Phyllis Riddle, Jim W. Daniel and James Smith Allen. John Bedell and
Bill Speck read the manuscript and asked sometimes painfully pointed questions
which I took to heart, and for which I am grateful. Dede Lingle Ittner’s enthu-
siastic reading of the entire draft  convinced me that non-historians could have
as much fun with Dunckerley’s story as I did. William D. Moore and Jan A. M.
Snoek generously shared their work in manuscript, and are duly noted in the
text. My most profound thanks are due to John M. Hamill, Aubrey Newman
and Andrew Prescott, all outstanding scholars and masters of Masonic history.
Th ey poured over my manuscript removing ‘Americanisms’, explained the more
arcane aspects of eighteenth-century Freemasonry and pointed me in the right
direction when I needed additional sources to get the story right. Th e fi nished
text is much richer and more accurate for their tremendous skill and generosity.
Any mistakes that crept in despite their attention are my own.
All contemporary historical studies depend on a network of archives and
libraries, as much for their professional staff  as for the collections they contain.

– xi –
BIOGRAPHICAL PROLEGOMENON
Th omas Dunckerley (1720–95) cut a swathe through late eighteenth-century
English Freemasonry. Over the last quarter of that century, Dunckerley set an
important example by building up the provincial organization of the Grand
Lodge in southern England, serving as Provincial Grand Master for eight
Masonic provinces by the early 1790s, and establishing a model of local gov-
ernance which has deeply infl uenced provincial Freemasonry in England to the
present day. Dunckerley also took a leading part in the integration of the Royal
Arch into the activities of the Modern Grand Lodge, presiding over Royal Arch
Masonry in eleven counties. Dunckerley enthusiastically and eff ectively pro-
moted other degrees, commanding both the English Knights Templar and the
recently formalized Royal Ark Masons. Th ere is even some evidence that he had
in mind the creation of a women’s order or English Adoptive Rite.1 His life and
accomplishments were celebrated by his Masonic colleagues, especially in the
provinces, and during his lifetime biographical sketches appeared in the contem-
porary Masonic press.2
In the nineteenth century, Dunckerley’s biography was made to serve new
purposes, as it was emphasized by Masonic admirers who portrayed him as an
example of just the sort of gentleman, Christian and Freemason the British
Empire had need of in the Victorian era. Th ey were particularly impressed by
his twenty-year naval career, the centrepiece of which was his participation in
the Siege of Quebec in 1759, cited as evidence of his manifest heroism and loyal
sacrifi ce.3 Still other Masonic writers, these with the goal of an explicit re-Chris-
tianization of the Order, held up Dunckerley as a Masonic sage, and credited
him with an intellectual as well as administrative reformation of Craft  Masonry.4
Despite his importance in the eighteenth century, and his celebrity in the
nineteenth, today Th omas Dunckerley is virtually unknown outside a select cir-
cle of Masonic historians. Th is is a pity for several reasons. First, his signifi cance
in the creation of the structure of modern English and, through that, American
Freemasonry in itself deserves recognition. Second, his life story is engaging,
and makes entertaining reading. Lastly, and most important, Dunckerley’s per-
sonal story is set against the broad transformations aff ecting Western societies

--- Sayfa 13 ---
xii
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
between 1750 and 1850. Historians frequently argue this was a time of uncom-
monly rapid and pervasive change or, as Dror Wahrman has termed it, ‘of radical
discontinuity’.5 It was an age when social and political remnants of the medieval
world were defi nitively left  behind by the dust of revolutions, and the founda-
tions of modernity rose everywhere on the horizons. Technological advances
transformed the business of life across society, while innovations in travel, scien-
tifi c equipment, medicine and the military meant that Westerners could travel
further, trade and conquer with greater impunity than ever before, and still stand
a reasonable chance of making it home to tell their version of the tale.
Against this backdrop, some daring individuals seized the chance to trans-
form themselves as well, taking advantage of poor communications, shoddy
record-keeping, shift ing social defi nitions and previously unheard-of oppor-
tunities to leave their homes, families, even social status behind, and defi ne
themselves anew, somewhere far away. Many literally ‘made a name for them-
selves’ by abandoning their former identity, vanishing into one of the great wars
of the eighteenth century, or the empire-building projects of the nineteenth.
Others, like Th omas Dunckerley, chose a more modest pathway to personal
aggrandizement by abandoning only the inconvenient parts of their family
history, and indulging in eighteenth-century style résumé enhancement. For
Dunckerley, personal rebranding was a conscious eff ort, with results he could
not have foreseen.

--- Sayfa 14 ---
– 1 –
PROLOGUE: IN THE AFTERMATH OF WAR
Th e Treaty of Paris was signed in February 1763, offi  cially ending the hostili-
ties between France and Great Britain that lay at the heart of the global confl ict
we call the Seven Years’ War. Hundreds of thousands of men were consequently
demobilized in Britain, France and across the Continent. Britain alone had
around 200,000 men in its army, militia and navy by the end of the war.1 Even
though Great Britain kept a strong contingent of regular troops stationed in
the American colonies, many more went home to the uncertainties of civilian
life. Among them, Th omas Dunckerley, master gunner and sometime teacher of
mathematics, had ample reason to be concerned. On 31 May 1763 he was paid
off , having served most recently as gunner on HMS Prince, a second-rate ship of
the line. Th is had been his most prestigious naval posting, and was the culmina-
tion of a career that dated back to at least 1744. Th at spring he was forty-two
years old, with a family to support, and no real prospects.
Dunckerley came from a modest but respectable family, though with no for-
mal education and no inheritance he looked rather grimly towards the peace. He
was not, however, entirely without assets he could leverage. According to con-
temporary accounts, Dunckerley was intelligent, articulate, ambitious and he
carried himself like the gentleman he longed to be. Rather, he comported him-
self as the gentleman he was convinced he had to be, for the sake of his readily
admitted pride, and because he had concluded that only a signifi cant rise in his
social status could bring the fi nancial stability he need to support his extended
family and meet past obligations.2 He also claimed to be £300 in debt, a ruinous
sum in the eighteenth century when individuals could not declare bankruptcy
unless they were in business. Faced with demanding creditors, ordinary people
like Dunckerley were sent to debtors’ prisons until debts were paid or settled,
which was of course diffi  cult to accomplish from prison.3
In May 1764, Dunckerley completed the paperwork that would provide him
with a pension of almost £45 per annum. Since warrant offi  cers were not auto-
matically eligible for any pension, this was more than most of his superannuated
(retired) colleagues could expect, but it was nowhere near enough.4 In this, as in
other diffi  cult situations, Dunckerley relied for assistance on his family’s long-

--- Sayfa 15 ---
2
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
time patron, Sir Edward Walpole, son of Sir Robert Walpole. Dunckerley’s late
grandmother, Elizabeth Bolnest, was a physician’s widow who had served as
infant nurse to Sir Edward for several years aft er his birth in 1706.5 By the 1760s,
Walpole’s childhood attachment had taken the form of benevolent interven-
tions on behalf of three generations of Bolnest’s family. Sir Edward secured the
grace-and-favour apartments at Somerset House occupied by Bolnest, as well
as those of her widowed daughter, Mary Dunckerley.6 Walpole successfully rec-
ommended Th omas Dunckerley’s mother and daughter for small government
pensions. Of greater interest for our story, Walpole assisted Th omas Dunckerley
as he sought advancement in the Royal Navy.7
But now Dunckerley apparently needed more than Sir Edward was able or
willing to provide. He sought a prestigious position and social advancement and,
aft er decades of faithful patronage, Walpole failed to deliver. Perhaps Dunck-
erley was too proud to ask – or maybe he thought his merit and need were so
apparent that he should not have to be specifi c. More likely, the social barrier he
endeavoured to overcome was simply too steep. However essential a gunner was
to the safety and eff ectiveness of a warship, he was nonetheless not a gentleman.8
Whatever the cause of the initial breakdown between erstwhile patron and cli-
ent, it certainly produced surprising results. Already thinking himself both an
offi  cer and gentleman, Dunckerley sought genteel ‘Employment in any Depart-
ment that is adequate to my poor abilities, and which would not depress me
beneath the character of a Gentleman’.9 If Sir Edward could not provide such an
employment, Dunckerley would see to it himself.
Distressingly, the way clear was not immediately apparent. Masonic histo-
rian William Moss suggests that in the year before his pension became payable,
Dunckerley’s fi nances were so perilous that he attempted to raise funds by pub-
lishing a book, Th e Complete Freemason: Multa Paucis, for Lovers of Secrets.10
Th is awkwardly produced and anonymous little book was aimed at Freemasons
– a burgeoning market in the 1760s. Dunckerley was initiated into Freemasonry
in 1754 and, aside from his own manifest enthusiasm for the Craft , he knew that
its popularity was generally on the rise. During the Seven Years’ War, Freema-
sonry spread rapidly outside London, proliferating in port towns and military
regiments. Moss argues that internal clues, such as otherwise unnecessary ref-
erences to William Byron, Lord Chesterfi eld, Somerset House, Quebec and
‘sea lodges’, point quite plausibly to Dunckerley as the author. He suggests that
Dunckerley put the work together in that period between his retirement in 1763
and the availability of his fi rst pension payment in late 1764 or early 1765. Cut-
ting it rather close, Moss posits Dunckerley published it as a fi nancial stopgap,
but when sales were sluggish and the printer’s bill came due, he was faced with
debtors’ prison or going back to sea.11 Dunckerley chose a trip to the Mediter-
ranean, signing on as an able seaman on HMS Guadaloupe under John Ruthven.

--- Sayfa 16 ---
Prologue
3
According to the ship’s register, Dunckerley signed on in January 1765, joining
his teenage son, who had come aboard in August 1764.12
It was probably about this time – between January and April 1765 – that
Dunckerley devised an audacious plan. At some point, he either recognized or
heard the suggestion that he bore a striking resemblance to members of the royal
family. Th ough the quality of Dunckerley’s extant portraits makes it diffi  cult
for us to see it now, there are enough contemporary reports that it seems quite
likely to have been the case.13 Using this as a starting point, Th omas Dunckerley
proceeded to provide an explanation for the purported resemblance, composing
and circulating a complicated and immensely engaging story, which he claimed
was his late mother’s deathbed confession of her seduction and adultery and his
own illegitimacy. Th e story was a sensation as it made the rounds of the Free-
masons and military men with whom Dunckerley shared it. At its heart was the
assertion that Th omas Dunckerley was not the son of Adam Dunckerley, porter
at Somerset House. Rather, he was the son of a king.14
Notes and Caveats
In January 1744⁄5, Th omas Dunckerley passed the Trinity House examination that
qualifi ed him to teach on shipboard.15 Onboard schoolmasters were introduced
in 1702, to make up for defi ciencies in the general education of young offi  cer can-
didates.16 Th ough schoolmasters never became part of the regular complement of
offi  cers and offi  cials on every ship in the Royal Navy, they were common enough
for a standard curriculum of mathematics, draft ing and calligraphy to be estab-
lished. While Dunckerley’s elegant handwriting is an eloquent testimony to his
profi ciency in that skill, for a teacher of mathematics he is surprisingly cavalier with
numbers. Th us, when Dunckerley claims to have been born in 1724, or to have
been made (initiated as) a Mason in 1747, or to have been a Grand Offi  cer in
Freemasonry for twenty-one years, we need to understand that these fi gures, rather
like the ages of the patriarchs in the Old Testament, serve a purpose other than
the plain truth. At least with regard to dates, Dunckerley used numbers as they
suited his need at the time, and it would be a mistake to dedicate much eff ort to
reconciling them with the dates or the passage of time indicated by contemporary
documentation. Such attempts have proved maddening for earlier Dunckerley
admirers, and obscured what the man was actually about.
Unlike an erratic use of dates, which was of Dunckerley’s own devising, early
modern spelling also seems daringly inventive to our eyes. In the eighteenth cen-
tury, standard modern spelling was only just coming into fashion amongst the
literate, and most people were not formally schooled. Faced with spelling choices
made idiosyncratic by variations in accent and education, researchers sometimes
resort to sounding out the words on the page to divine what they were meant to

--- Sayfa 17 ---
4
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
be. Along with other words, the spelling of names was in a state of fl ux during the
seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, and so the reader will encounter consider-
able variation, most notably in the spelling of both Dunckerley and Bolnest. In
this study, unfamiliar spellings typically arise from one of three sources. Th e fi rst
is modern variations between British and American English. Next comes com-
mon eighteenth-century abbreviations, such as ye for ‘ye’ or you. Th e fi nal source
is phonetic spellings that were aff ected by regional or class dialects. No note will
be made of the fi rst or the second though, when appropriate and convenient, the
third will be noted or, in extreme cases, translated.
One fi nal caveat is in order. Having hinted at what this study is about; it
is only fair to enter a little disclaimer about what it is not. Th omas Duncker-
ley’s name is mentioned in many explorations of Masonic history. He is credited
with creating or introducing Orders, degrees, rituals, symbols and catechisms.
Dunckerley is likewise hailed as the founding father of Freemasonry in various
English Masonic provinces and even places outside Britain. He is honoured as
a hero and crowned a Masonic genius. In many instances, Dunckerley’s reputa-
tion has made him the default progenitor for all things good in modern English
Freemasonry. Th e present study addresses few of these points directly, and some
not at all. In some cases, this biography will conclusively disprove one or another
of the fraternity’s most cherished beliefs about a man who undoubtedly played a
key role in its development during his lifetime. It is therefore important to recall
that this study is an attempt to lay out a more accurate and complete version of
Dunckerley’s biography than has heretofore been either attempted or accom-
plished. Th is means, of course, that the volume you hold is not a biography of
Freemasonry, but only of a Freemason.

--- Sayfa 18 ---
– 5 –
1 THE MAKING OF A MYTH
Th omas Dunckerley Fitz George
Th reat of debtors’ prison sharpened Dunckerley’s imagination tremendously.
Still, passing himself off  as a royal bastard seems an unlikely and outrageous
strategy, with very uncertain results. Th us the temptation for us to look into
Dunckerley’s past for clues about his mindset is irresistible. What could he have
been thinking? Fortunately, amongst his fairly slender literary remains, he does
leave a clue. In 1750, Th omas Dunckerley was a member of the Sober Society
of Portsmouth Common, in Hampshire. Th e society took as its motto ‘Virtus
tandem Viegbit’ or, as Dunckerley himself notes in a lecture, ‘Virtue at length
shall fl ourish’.1 Th e society was active from at least 1747 to 1754, though it left
only scattered traces in the historical literature.2 Th e earliest mention of the soci-
ety appears in a bound manuscript dated 3 April 1747, which was described in
the Journal of the Ex Libris Society in 1894.3 Unfortunately the location of this
manuscript is unknown and its survival uncertain. However, Dunckerley’s own
manuscript notebook of lectures survives at the Lady Lever Art Gallery at Port
Sunlight, outside Liverpool.
Th e structure and content of Dunckerley’s writing tells us a good deal
about the society. Th e notebook contains thirteen motivational lectures, dated
between December 1749 and December 1750. Th ese are apparently the clean
copies of his own compositions, dated at regular intervals, indicating that he was
responsible for at least monthly contributions to the presumed rotation of lec-
tures. Each appears to be suffi  cient for about half an hour of oration, though the
length varies slightly from topic to topic. Th e essays are sprinkled liberally with
quotes from the Bible, Milton, William of Wykeham (c. 1320–1404), Horace
and Scottish poet James Th omson (1700–48), author of the lyrics of ‘Rule, Bri-
tannia!’ whose poems Dunckerley found particularly moving. He prefaces the
notebook with the explanation, ‘Essays on various Subjects by Th os Dunckerley;
Member of a Society who met for mutual improvement in Morality & usefull
Knowledge at the Library on Portsmth Common 1750’.

--- Sayfa 19 ---
6
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
In his lecture for September 1750, ‘By what means may the conduct be
form’d to Decency and Virtue?’, Dunckerley advises fi rst, ‘know thyself ’, and
then describes how the society assists in this process. He explains:
What can be more Polite, more benefi cial, or more Sutable [sic] to a Rational Creature
than the Institution of this Society and the Erection of this Library? Th e man who
dress’d and went out to loiter and gather up something to entertain a Mind too vacant
may here fi nd Employment, he may here amuse himself with reading the Works of
our greatest Divines Philosophers & Historians and I make no doubt, but that we
shall be able (as our Subscription Increases) to furnish this Room with a Microscope
and Telescope, that we may see the Minute Wonders of Gods Works, and take a view
of our fellow Travelers around the Sun.4
In the same lecture, Dunckerley refers to ‘our worthy President’, an indication
that while he was very active in the society, he was not yet in the lead – a position
he quickly assumed aft er his initiation into Freemasonry.
A bit more information about the society comes to us from John Miller, a
surgeon’s mate on HMS Penzance, who published a book of poems in 1754.5 As
was typical in the eighteenth century, the book contains a list of subscribers –
individuals who were willing to pay for copies in advance to enable the author to
bring the book to publication.6 Subscribers had a variety of motives for under-
writing books, not the least of which was social aspiration. Subscriptions off ered
subscribers the opportunity to have their names seen in print alongside social or
professional superiors with whom they wanted to be associated. In this instance,
Dunckerley and his young son Th omas Bolnest Dunckerley both appear on the
list, together with an assortment of other civilian and naval supporters. One of
the poems included in the volume is titled, ‘An Ode, Composed for the Anni-
versary of the Foundation of the Sober Society’. In a footnote, Miller explains
the society was
A group of Gentlemen at Portsmouth who meet monthly for the Improvement of
each other, by Propounding Questions in Philosophy, Morality, Natural Knowledge,
&c, and have raised a Building for the Meeting of the Society, near the Rope Walk on
the Common.7
Collectively, this reveals rather a lot about a hither-to little known society. Mill-
er’s 1754 publication date and discussion of the society as an ongoing concern
suggests that this was a relatively stable association, lasting from at least 1745 to
1754, and with suffi  cient resources to furnish a library. In 1752 it was the only
organization to subscribe to Francis Lee’s Apoleipomena, or, Dissertations Th eo-
logical, Mathematical, and Physical, a posthumous collection which was printed
in two volumes by his daughter. While the subject matter is clearly of interest
to the Sober Society’s members, and Miss Lee a suitable object of charity, it is
tempting to read a bit more into the subscription and one wonders if Dunckerley

--- Sayfa 20 ---
Th e Making of a Myth
7
was still associated with the society at that time. Francis Lee was the son-in-law
and biographer of the mystic Jane Ward Lead, the founder of the Philadelphian
Society, an early Universalist group.8 Lee, an early member of the society, was
interested in pietism, especially the writings of Jakob Böehme (1575–1624), as
well as English interpretations of Cabalism, placing him on the relative fringe of
eighteenth-century esotericism. Lee was also an intimate of Sir Robert Walpole,
the father of Dunckerley’s patron, Sir Edward.9
Miller’s mention of monthly meetings suggests the essays in Dunckerley’s
notebook represent a very active participation, at least for thirteen months for
which they survive. Th e draft  of a fourteenth lecture, ‘On Patience’, is bound in
at the end of the notebook, hinting that perhaps only some of his compositions
for the society survive. Also bound in the notebook are two unrelated docu-
ments: a description of the sea battle between the British ship HMS Vanguard
under Captain Swanton and a small French squadron of M. Dubois de la Motte
off  Brest in November 1757, signed ‘By inserting this account in the Public
Papers you’ll oblige your & st. T.D’; and a four-page fragment of a St John’s Day
Masonic lecture based on 1 Corinthians 5–8.10
Dunckerley’s enthusiastic participation and the topics of the essays he pre-
pared for presentation are accurate predictors for his later sometimes ferocious
engagement with Freemasonry.11 In his April 1753 essay, ‘What is our Duty to
our Selves?’, Dunckerley explains, this time in verse, his prime motivation, one
that carried him through all his associations, as well as his biographical reinven-
tion of the 1760s:
What is our Duty to our Selves?
As I was walking by my self
I talked to my self,
And thus my self said to me,
‘Look to thy self
Take care of thy self
For that will be best for Th ee.’12
It is not clear who wrote the poem. An anonymous engraved version, dated
1776, is mentioned in the Transactions of the California State Agricultural Society
in 1862, so it is entirely possible that Dunckerley simply found it in the library
and decided to include it in his talk. Association, fi rst with the Sober Society and
later with the Freemasons and other societies, clearly suggested to Dunckerley
an avenue for self-improvement, social advancement and a means by which he
could exert control over at least part of his life in ways not possible through more
ordinary social or military channels, where barriers were very real and virtually
insurmountable.

--- Sayfa 21 ---
8
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Another of Dunckerley’s Sober Society lectures, ‘What are the Reciprocal
Duties between Parents and their Children?’, is particularly interesting, given
his subsequent rejection of his own family.13 Th e lecture itself is unexceptional,
like most of Dunckerley’s writings. Despite his later infl ated reputation as a sage,
in print at least, Dunckerley was always more interested in pleasing his audience
and in reaffi  rming what they already believed to be good and noble, than in pro-
voking either animated conversation or hard thinking. He begins this essay with
the question of who is obliged to provide ‘assiduous Care, to Raise, Support,
and Nourish us’. Th e answer is obvious. ‘It is our Parents that are bound by the
great Law of Nature to “discharge this fi rst necessary Duty, they being (by Gods
permission) the Authors of our Existance [sic]”.’ Th en, perhaps looking back at
his own youth, when he absconded from the master to whom he was legally
apprenticed, Dunckerley points out:
As the Children arrive to Maturer Years, and begin to think they are capable of Gov-
erning themselves, it is then that they stand most in need of their Parents advice.
Company has great Infl uence, and the Impetuosity of Youth too oft en hurries them
down the Rapid Stream of irregular Passions.
Dunckerley concludes with a very truncated discussion of children’s reciprocal
duties, writing, ‘in return it is the Duty of Children to behave with Submission,
Gratitude Respect and Piety to their Parents’. On both sides, the relationship he
describes is practical and immediate, not aimed at noble sentiments or family
reputation.
Perhaps it is unfair to read too much into this little lecture. It was written sev-
eral years before Dunckerley decided to disown his father and repeatedly rebuke
his mother for not revealing his ‘true’ origins early enough to make a substantial
diff erence in his life. Dunckerley never names his actual father in writing, and in
1766 he chastised his mother and grandmother:
When I refl ect on the late King being my Father, which I sincerely believe, when I think
on the hardships I have known; my present obscurity, and narrow Circumstances; I
cannot help condemning that false Modesty and Delicacy of my Grandmother, and
Mother, that concealed me from the knowledge of my Royal Father.14
However, by the time Dunckerley embarked on his personal reinvention in the
mid-1760s, both his parents were long dead, placing them beyond the very prac-
tical strictures he had laid out in his Sober Society talk. Nonetheless, it is most
intriguing that Dunckerley considered the question in public and ‘soberly’ toed
the accepted line, only to completely kick over the traces a few years later.
Many children fantasize at some point that they are adopted, that surely these
boring, misguided people surrounding them are not really blood kin. It is a tempo-
rarily satisfying daydream, but rarely amounts to more than that. In Dunckerley’s

--- Sayfa 22 ---
Th e Making of a Myth
9
case, the fantasy came late and became the very core of his identity for the last
thirty years of his life. Th e story of his transformation unfolds for us in a series
of documents, the most important of which were written in midsummer 1766,
and probably represent the earliest manifestation of the Dunckerley myth. Collec-
tively, the story these documents tell is Th omas Dunckerley’s ‘accepted narrative’,
so-called because it was accepted as truth by so many people, for so long.
Th e earliest elements of the accepted narrative were fi rst noted in print by
Arlene Shy of the Clements Library at the University of Michigan. In 1997 she
wrote a little-noticed feature titled ‘Th e Confession of Mary Bolnist’, which
appeared in the Quarto, a library publication designed to showcase library col-
lections, in this case, the Clinton Papers. General Sir Henry Clinton (1730–95),
son of Admiral George Clinton, was a Freemason, which may be why the two
documents featured in Shy’s article ended up in his papers. In his quest for
preferment and recognition of his royal parentage, Dunckerley appealed most
directly to his Masonic brethren, who were aft er all sworn to give him assistance
in time of need. Th e letter and companion document, labelled ‘Mary Duncker-
ley’s Declaration to Mrs Pinkney, January 2d: 1760’, are unmistakably in Th omas
Dunckerley’s clear, distinctive script. Furthermore, the text of both documents is
carefully composed and more closely resembles the clean copies of inspirational
lectures Dunckerley transcribed into a bound volume in 1750, than they do his
usual correspondence which is frequently ungrammatical.
Th e letter and its enclosure were originally intended for Major Edward Wal-
pole (1737–71). Dunckerley undoubtedly felt he had good reason to expect
a sympathetic hearing from the major. Both of them were military men who
served in North America during the Seven Years’ War. Both were attached to
Freemasonry. While it is not clear that he was a member, Major Walpole’s inter-
est is indicated by his appearance alongside Dunckerley on the list of subscribers
to Wellins Calcott’s 1769 work on the practices and history of the Craft .15
Finally, the major was, signifi cantly, the illegitimate (but acknowledged) son of
Sir Edward Walpole, who appears repeatedly in Dunckerley’s life history as his
family’s chief benefactor.
Th e story Dunckerley presents is full of pathos. In the letter he begins by
explaining how he had unexpectedly stumbled across his true identity as a royal
bastard, but remained powerless to benefi t from the information without an
infl uential friend who would be willing to lay his claims before the current king,
George III. In the letter addressed to Walpole, Dunckerley relates that he arrived
in England in January 1760, fresh from the Siege of Quebec. Quebec, it must
be noted, features in so many suspect eighteenth-century biographies that it has
to be considered the mother of all mid-century heroes. Dunckerley rushed to
London but arrived too late, fi nding his mother had already died, and he was
only able to attend her funeral. Had he been there sooner, he would have been

--- Sayfa 23 ---
10
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
able to receive her alarming deathbed confession himself. Instead, it came to him
through a faithful neighbour, Mrs Ann Pinkney. Th is lady curiously insisted that
Dunckerley come back alone the next day for her awful revelation and explicitly
said he should not bring Hannah, his wife of fi ft een years, to share the news.
When Dunckerley arrived for the interview, Pinckney announced that she was
the only person left  alive who had been present at Th omas’s birth, and that his
mother had confessed, both during the pangs of childbirth and in the solem-
nity of her deathbed, that Th omas Dunckerley’s father was not her late husband,
Adam Dunckerley, but King George II.
Th at sort of bombshell needs a great deal of support to be believable, and
Dunckerley is attentive to that imperative. He is a deft  storyteller, interleaving
plausible yet purloined details with elements that may well be partial truths, to
create a tremendously engaging narrative. Dunckerley began by asserting that his
mother ‘was a Physician’s daughter who lived with the honourable Mrs Walpole’,
implying that she was a lady’s companion at Houghton Hall, the Walpole family
home in Norfolk. Dunckerley invites us to share Mrs Walpole’s dismay in dis-
covering her young companion had been ‘debauched’ by the Prince of Wales, the
future George II, while he was a house guest. Th e horrifi ed lady quickly married
the girl off  to a passing bachelor in the entourage of the Duke of Devonshire,
Adam Dunckerley. Th e account includes the detail that the couple was married
by Dr Henry Bland (d. 1746), a childhood friend of Sir Robert Walpole, who
capped an illustrious career as provost at Eton.16
At this point in his letter, Dunckerley makes a plea he reiterated frequently
over the next three decades: ‘I must confess this Information gave me great sur-
prise and much uneasiness…’. In point of fact, his only uneasiness was how to get
this fabrication laid before George III, the person best able to bestow the perqui-
sites Dunckerley craved, but that gets us a bit ahead of the story. So, Dunckerley
relates to Major Walpole, there was nothing to do at the time but report back to
his ship, HMS Vanguard – there was a war going on aft er all. Dunckerley claims
to have told no one but his captain, Robert Swanton, who was promoted to Rear
Admiral of the Blue in 1762 and who allegedly promised to help him when they
again returned to port. Dunckerley reports his despair when, in the intervening
months, both George II and Mrs Pinkney died, leaving no one who could cor-
roborate the aff air between Mary Bolnest Dunckerley and the man who became
king. Conveniently, Swanton died in 1765, just before Dunckerley began to cir-
culate his narrative, so even he could not corroborate that Dunckerley returned
from the funeral with staggering news.
In this letter, Dunckerley presents only the bare bones of his story; the
remainder of the narrative being taken up in the accompanying document, the
‘Declaration’. However, the letter also gives us a clue as to how broadly Duncker-
ley distributed his story. He relates:

--- Sayfa 24 ---
Th e Making of a Myth
11
I have since that time mentioned it [the claim] to several to whom I have the hon-
our to be known, particularly Lord Chesterfi eld, Lord William Gordon, Sir Edward
Walpole, Th os Missing, Esq. of Stubbington in Hampshire, Edd Maxwell, and sev-
eral offi  cers at Gibraltar, and Minorca: and to Captain Digby, Captain Ruthven, and
Captain Meadows of the Navy: who are all of opinion I should endeavor to get it
made known to the Royal Family.17
Th ere are two other such lists in existence, with a total of over forty names. Th is
means Dunckerley only revealed his mother’s carefully guarded secret to several
dozen of his most highly-placed acquaintances. Later biographers took these
lists to be evidence of Dunckerley’s ability to attract the friendship of his social
superiors, but they are actually mailing lists.18 Th ese are not personal friends,
but rather men whom Dunckerley enlisted in his audacious scheme, hoping that
one or more would bring his story to George III. ‘Hoping’ is too oblique a term;
he openly and even aggressively requested their help. As Dunckerley put it in
the letter to Major Walpole, ‘I am well convinced of your Friendship, and sub-
mit the above to your Consideration: requesting your Opinion and assistance.’
For the convenience of his correspondents, Dunckerley enclosed copies of the
‘Declaration’ as a handy reference. We know there were multiple copies, as two
survived at least until Dunckerley’s death in 1795 – the one sent to Major Wal-
pole, and another Dunckerley’s executors found in his papers upon his death,
which they then forwarded to the Freemasons’ Magazine for publication in Feb-
ruary 1796. Dunckerley refers to a third copy in an October 1766 letter to Lord
George Lennox, confi ding ‘I have wrote to Lord William Gordon in Scotland,
and acquainted him that I had the honour of showing to your Lordship the
substance of my mother’s declaration.’19 Another copy probably came into Sir
Edward Walpole’s hands, as Bray’s account mentions, ‘Sr E had a long letter on
the subject in his possession written by D. to a gent who gave it [to] Sr Ed’.20 In
their core narrative, the accounts are virtually identical.
‘Mary Dunckerley’s “Declaration” to Mrs Pinkney, January 2d: 1760’ is a fas-
cinating document. Dunckerley was sensitive to the power invested in deathbed
confessions, and he sets the scene to make the most of it.
Mary Dunckerley being dangerously ill with the gout in her stomach, and believing it
will be her Death, is desirous (at the request of her friend Mrs Pinkney) that this her
deathbed Declaration, may be made known to her son, and to none but him.
We have already noted that in his surviving correspondence, Dunckerley rarely
misses the opportunity to chastise his mother for concealing the secret of his
royal birth. Here he lays the groundwork for those later swipes – she only even
revealed it on her deathbed thanks to the insistence of Mrs Pinkney! What a
kind and thoughtful neighbour – what a boon to Th omas Dunckerley. How
unfortunate that Pinkney herself promptly died and was thus unable to verify

--- Sayfa 25 ---
12
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Dunckerley’s version of the story when he returned from war.21 Th e inclusion
of Ann Pinkney is one of Dunckerley’s most eff ective conceits and her death
provides him with yet another example of his outrageously bad fortune. Her
historical existence is verifi able. She was a long-time resident of Somerset House
and was undoubtedly thoroughly versed in the actual details of the Dunckerley
family. Her husband, Henry Pinkney, had been Adam Dunckerley’s junior col-
league as porter, and aft er his death in 1723 the widowed Ann Pinkney lived the
rest of her life alongside Mary Dunckerley, in another grace-and-favour apart-
ment at Somerset House.22 For the remainder of the tale, Pinkney takes the role
of interlocutor, as she relates the surprising news.
Th e account which follows is populated with, on the one hand, the rela-
tively obscure members of Dunckerley’s family and Somerset House neighbours
and, on the other, prominent fi gures who would have been well known at least
by reputation, to everyone who received one of Dunckerley’s written pleas for
assistance. Th ey were all real people with whom Dunckerley might have been
casually acquainted, especially through his life-long association with the Wal-
pole family. Additionally, the characters are portrayed plausibly, doing things
they likely did, but Dunckerley arranges them in fi ctional situations and in a
purposely vague or falsifi ed timeframe. Oh, and they were all already dead. Th at
was very, very important.
Th e ‘Declaration’ is densely packed with details. Dunckerley clearly intended
to overwhelm any doubters with verisimilitude, and he was largely successful.
Th ere were some disbelievers during his lifetime, but the repetition of the iden-
tical story, most famously at the time of Dunckerley’s death in 1795 when it
was presented as his own deathbed confession, written in his own hand, soon
silenced any critics. A transcript of the full ‘Declaration’ appears in Appendix 1,
so we cover only the essential points here, though even just the essentials make a
compelling drama.
Th us the story begins in medias res in November 1723, with Mary and Adam
Dunckerley already married and living at Somerset House. Adam went off  to
Chatsworth House, on an errand for the Duke of Devonshire, then William
Cavendish, the 4th duke (1720–64). He stayed away for several months, leaving
ample time for his wife to pay extended visits to friends over the Christmas holi-
days. Unfortunately, she happened to encounter the Prince of Wales, the future
King George II, at the home of Lady Margaret Ranelagh (1672–1727). Mary
Dunckerley lamented to Ann Pinkney that she went into the parlour, ‘where I
found the Prince of Wales, who I had too well known before my unhappy mar-
riage. At his request, for I could deny him nothing, I staid [sic] several days,
during which time he made me fi ve visits.’ Oh the folly that is woman! Th is
might well tell us something about what Dunckerley really thought of the fair

--- Sayfa 26 ---
Th e Making of a Myth
13
sex, though in later life and in a Masonic context, he went out of his way to rec-
oncile women to their exclusion from the Craft .
Of course Mary became pregnant, or there would have been no story. But
perhaps she was not entirely to blame. Dunckerley has already told Major Wal-
pole that the poor girl lost her virginity some time before to the prince, who
was well known as a philanderer, and now Mary herself reveals that her mar-
riage to Adam Dunckerley was unhappy. Upon discovering the she was ‘Sick
and Breeding’, Mary resolved to do away with herself and her unborn child, but
was prevented by the kind intervention of a number of women: Lady Stanley,
Lady Ranelagh and Mrs Mekin [Meakins], a Somerset House neighbour. While
Pinkney asserts that they were all sworn to silence, and specifi cally enjoined not
to reveal the pregnancy to the Prince of Wales, they nonetheless brought the
distraught woman gift s and assistance that would seem to be forthcoming only
if the Prince realized he had made her pregnant: the off er of a house in Rich-
mond, £50 in bank notes and the attendance of Dr Richard Mead (1673–1754),
a member of the Royal Society and physician to the Prince of Wales and the
Walpole family. Finally, when the time came for Mary to be delivered, Mrs Sid-
ney Kennon appeared. She was the most famous midwife in London and was
commonly known as midwife to the royal family.23 Her attendance was very
prestigious and would ordinarily have been far too expensive for the Dunckerley
family to aff ord.
All that seems fairly outrageous and maybe a bit too clichéd, even for presum-
ably less jaded audiences. Th e conclusion, though, is what makes this portion of
the narrative so eff ective. Mary Dunckerley tells Pinkney that when her husband
returned from the Duke’s business in the country, he seemed pleased to fi nd
his wife had been unfaithful. In the ‘Declaration’ Mary relates, ‘I disdained to
deceive him, and told him what had happened; he commended my Conduct
with so much Joy that I could not help despising his Meanness.’ Why would
the man be overjoyed? Th e answer is simple – he was bribed: ‘Our Separation
follow’d aft er my Delivery, and he kept the Secret on his Account, for he had
two Places, and several considerable Advantages at the price of my unhappiness.’
Typical spoiled rich elite – seduce the wife and pay off  the cuckold. Poor Mary
Dunckerley ends the tale with the lament: ‘My Son might have been known to
his Royal Father; but my dear Mother reclaimed me from so criminal a passion;
and dread of public shame prevented my making it public.’
Th e Stuff  of Th eatre
Th is, then, was the story Th omas Dunckerley shared with his most infl uential
acquaintances. Th ere were no immediate results, but then such things take time.
Within a few months, however, someone brought Dunckerley’s claim to the

--- Sayfa 27 ---
14
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
king. It could have been any one of a number of people for, as we have seen,
his story was widely disseminated. Th ere are several distinct accounts, both
published and in private correspondence, which relate how George III was
approached with the former gunner’s story and how he responded. Any and all
are possible, though some are more entertaining than others. Th e most amusing
appears amidst the fl urry of notices surrounding Dunckerley’s death, in which
‘Φιλος E’ of the Gentleman’s Magazine suggests that Dunckerley himself thrust
the petition into the monarch’s hand as the king attended a naval review at Ports-
mouth.24
Most versions of the story involve gentlemen with close connections at
Court. For example, Dunckerley placed a letter for publication in the Freema-
sons’ Magazine in October 1793 from Sir James Adolphus Oughton, dated 13
November 1767, in which Oughton mentions Lord H and Mr W as worthy
channels of the king’s bounty.25 While some have interpreted this letter as an
indication that Oughton himself brought Dunckerley to the king’s attention,
if we credit the letter at all, it is most likely that Oughton was acknowledging
Simon Harcourt, 1st Earl Harcourt, and Th omas Worsley. Harcourt had been
George III’s governor when the young king was Prince of Wales, and went on to
initiate negotiations leading to George III’s marriage with Charlotte of Meck-
lenburg. Worsley was riding instructor to the king when he was a boy. Either
man would have been well placed to mention Dunckerley’s story discreetly,
and both appear on the ‘mailing list’ of gentlemen with whom Dunckerley had
shared his claim. Dunckerley’s autobiographical essay published in the February
1796 Freemasons’ Magazine specifi cally credits Oughton with taking the initia-
tive and then coordinating the plan with Harcourt and Worsley.26 Th e Royal
Archives give us no clue on this point, as no letters or other documents to the
king from any of these gentlemen, or anyone else, mention Dunckerley at all,
aside from the Secret Service payment ledgers.27
On the other hand, some public accounts of how the king was informed
are distinctly hostile. It is clear that not everyone accepted the assertion that
Dunckerley was the son of George II, and doubters were not shy about airing
their disbelief. Dunckerley’s relatively unfl attering 1795 Gentleman’s Magazine
obituary asserts:
About twenty years ago he [Dunckerley] availed himself of the remarkable likeness
he bore to the Royal Family, to get it represented to his Majesty that the late King was
in truth his father, and that he owed his existence to a visit which that King, when
Prince, had paid to Houghton, and he ventured to refer to Sir Edward Walpole for
his knowledge of the circumstances. His Majesty, ever attentive to charitable applica-
tions, directed inquiry to be made of Sir Edward.28

--- Sayfa 28 ---
Th e Making of a Myth
15
Another account, found in the British Mercury in 1789, bypasses the king and
his courtiers entirely and asserts that Dunckerley came to royal attention by
happenstance, asserting: ‘He was at St James’s about six years ago, when he was
particularly noticed by the Prince of Wales for his strong resemblance to the
Royal Family.’29 Whether Dunckerley took the matter into his own hands, or
one or more of the well-placed gentlemen to whom he appealed came through,
it is clear that George III eventually did take notice and, for Dunckerley at least,
the eff ects were memorable.
A Sailor Boy Gains Royal Recognition
When Th omas Dunckerley died in 1795, a fuller, more complex version of the
‘Declaration’ was found amongst his papers, and his executors Arthur Robinson
and James Rowley duly forwarded it to the Freemasons’ Magazine as the most
appropriate way to commemorate a life lived in service to the nation and Free-
masonry.30 It was also an eff ective answer to persistent doubters, who peppered
the newspapers with anonymous and frequently sarcastic notices in the weeks
aft er Dunckerley was buried. Th is last autobiographical version of Dunckerley’s
interesting story faithfully recounts all the original details of Mary Dunckerley’s
confession back in 1760 and his own thunderstruck reception of the revela-
tion. It also contains anecdotes and embellishments composed and refi ned by
Dunckerley over the intervening thirty years. Th is then is the story that, along
with a vignette of Dunckerley’s childhood contributed a few years earlier to
the Freemasons’ Magazine by Masonic brethren James Grierson and Th omas
White, becomes the ‘accepted narrative’ of Dunckerley’s life.31 Th e charming lit-
tle account by Grierson and White, contained in ‘Sketch of the Life of Th omas
Dunckerley, Esq., P.G.M’, asserts:
‘Honestas et Fortitudo’ was a motto he took at ten years of age, when a thirst for
glory, and a desire to engage in the bustle of the world, induced him to leave his
school abruptly and enter the Royal Navy …32
Other additions to the Dunckerley myth that appear prominently in the 1796
narrative are the high points of Dunckerley’s naval service, the vicissitudes of his
family life, and an account of the hardships he faced in pursuit of acknowledge-
ment of his royal birth. It also pointedly features Dunckerley’s association with
and gratitude towards various Masonic brethren and noblemen he encountered
in his rather picaresque adventures. Th e culmination of this fi nal narrative is
royal recognition – the gratifying result of Dunckerley’s own unblemished char-
acter and determination. In Dunckerley’s words:

--- Sayfa 29 ---
16
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
In April 1767, General O. (who had known me for several years) acquainted Lord H.
with my situation: and that nobleman, with the assistance of Mr W. laid my mother’s
declaration before the king.
His majesty read it, seemed much concerned, and commanded that an inquiry should
be made of my character from Lord C. and Sir E. W. who had known me since my
infancy. Th e account they gave of me was so satisfactory to the king, that he was
graciously pleased to order me a pension of 100l. a year, from his privy purse, May
7, 1767.33
Th us, for nearly everyone who has bothered to think about Dunckerley for the
past 200 years, this has been the complete, truthful account of the most remark-
able facts of his extraordinary life. Except, of course, almost none of it is ‘true’ in
any conventional sense of the word.
Th e Devil Is in the Details
Th omas Dunckerley’s refashioning of his family history between 1712 and 1767
is remarkable not just for its audacity, but also for the obvious care he took in
its composition. Th e accepted narrative is replete with telling details that make
it entirely believable at fi rst glance, and which latterly invite the researcher
in for some serious demolition. Th e present study is at least the third to have
undertaken an examination of the truth of the accepted narrative and its myriad
details. Th e fi rst was written by Henry Sadler (1840–1911) in 1891. Sadler was
himself a distinguished Freemason, becoming the fi rst Librarian and Curator of
the United Grand Lodge of England in 1910. In 1910 he was also elected Mas-
ter of Quatuor Coronati Lodge No. 2076. However, Sadler was an autodidact
when it came to actually doing research, and his interest in history was decid-
edly antiquarian. He had two primary concerns. Th e fi rst was to determine what
Masonic sources could tell Freemasons about Freemasonry. Th e second was to
demonstrate that Dunckerley, whom he clearly idolized, was a role model not
only for his own time, but most importantly for late Victorian Britain mascu-
linity writ large. Sadler’s interest in Masonic history came at a transformative
time in the development of modern historical methodology, and he made some
heartfelt nods in the direction of historical research as a science, but they were
little more than that.
Sadler’s work thus stands at the intersection between antiquarianism and the
new methods of historical scholarship based on the critical scrutiny of original
documents.34 Attempting to be scientifi c, he carefully collected and published
as many letters, lodge minutes and newspaper accounts pertaining to Dunck-
erley as he could readily fi nd, sometimes editing them for the proper eff ect.35
In addition to being a genuine attempt at good scholarly form, this technique

--- Sayfa 30 ---
Th e Making of a Myth
17
was commonly utilized in the numerous biographies of signifi cant naval offi  cers
produced in the nineteenth century which were Sadler’s most immediate literary
models. Many, such as August Phillimore’s life of Admiral Parker, were similarly
fi lled with all manner of random documents in the belief that this was the best
way to enable readers’ immediate access to the authentic voice of a great sub-
ject.36 Despite Sadler’s insistence on the importance of allowing Dunckerley to
speak for himself through his own letters and writings, his commentary purpose-
fully presents Dunckerley as an upright character model. However, Sadler’s focus
on what Masonic sources could tell Freemasons about their own unique experi-
ence meant that he did not look beyond the archival holdings of the United
Grand Lodge in his search for material, nor did he consider the relevance of
non-Masonic sources or meta-narratives. Th e one probable exception seems to
be that Sadler did consult Admiralty records at the Public Record Offi  ce, but
used them sparingly and ignored them when they suggested uncomfortable con-
tradictions to the accepted narrative. Sadler would undoubtedly have nodded at
Tertullian’s question, ‘What has Athens to do with Jerusalem?’
Sadler thoughtfully reviewed a cluster of accounts of Dunckerley’s paternity
and early life that appeared in the press between 1793 and 1796, all of which
were intended to either build or demolish the undoubted prestige that accom-
panied his claim to be an illegitimate son of George II. He was inclined to accept
the veracity of the Freemasons’ Magazine articles because he considered other,
unattributed notices which appeared in the press aft er Dunckerley’s death but
which were critical of the accepted narrative as being inherently untrustworthy
on the basis of their anonymity.37 As we shall see, another force guiding Sadler’s
acceptance of the 1796 accepted narrative is that by 1891 the reality of Dunck-
erley’s royal birth had become so interwoven with the general mythology of the
triumph of English Freemasonry that, despite the persistence of very soft  subter-
ranean grumbles to the contrary, it was really not open to much scrutiny.38
Henry Sadler’s endorsement of the accepted narrative set the agenda for
all subsequent studies of Dunckerley, none of which disagrees with his basic
conclusions. Of the many briefer biographical essays and articles written about
Dunckerley in the intervening century, all focus primarily on his many Masonic
activities and none questions his claim to be the son of George II. In 1982, Ron
Chudley published a book-length study, Th omas Dunckerley: A Remarkable
Freemason, intended as an accessible biography for a popular Masonic audi-
ence.39 Chudley updates the information available to Sadler, providing some
useful additions, especially with regard to facsimiles of correspondence and cer-
tifi cates. However, the methodology presented is consistent with Sadler’s earlier
analysis. If anything, Chudley is less inclined to a critical examination of early
claims made with regard to Dunckerley’s lineage and career. From a scholar’s
vantage, a striking drawback of the work is the complete lack of notation.

--- Sayfa 31 ---
18
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Th us Th omas Dunckerley’s audacious scheme seems to have paid off , at least
in terms of longevity, and was even more eff ective than he might have dreamed.
His grand deception, the truly big lie he lived for thirty years, stood unchal-
lenged for the next 200. Th is need not have been the case. General acceptance of
‘Mary Dunckerley’s Declaration to Mrs Pinkney’ hinged on no one knowing it
was not true. And, since all of the people named in the Declaration were dead,
this seemed to be a pretty safe bet.
However, there were indeed some who knew better. Th omas Dunckerley was
one of six children born to Adam and Mary Dunckerley, and his grandmother
Elizabeth Bolnest’s 1747 will leaves bequests for Th omas and an unnamed grand-
daughter. Th is could have been either an adult sister or cousin – his mother had
a sister Elizabeth Bolnest Lucas who married and who might well have had
children. In addition, the brother of Dunckerley’s father had children living in
London until early in the nineteenth century. One cousin, James Dunckerley
(1728–1802), became a prominent goldsmith and enameller in Southampton
Buildings in London later in the eighteenth century.40 Th is means that in addi-
tion to his immediate household, Th omas Dunckerley had close family living
in London, and they might presumably have had something to say about his
purported royal ancestry, though they might not have even become aware of the
claim before his death brought the story to the press. Th eir reactions were not
recorded, and he appears to have been unchallenged from that quarter.
Th en there was Sir Edward Walpole, a man Dunckerley had known all his
life, a faithful family patron, and upon whose sympathy Dunckerley felt sure he
could rely. According to Dunckerley, Walpole was the fi rst to whom Dunckerley
confi ded, early in the construction of his new identity. Awkwardly, Walpole was
emphatically not inclined to entertain Dunckerley’s fantasy. Whether we choose
to believe Dunckerley or not, he includes the scenario in the fi nal version of
the accepted narrative, relating that he fi rst mentioned his extraordinary physi-
cal likeness to George II to Walpole in 1761. Dunckerley reports that, knowing
Sir Edward’s past benevolence, and his close Court connections, he went to Sir
Edward Walpole in the hope that he would put the case of Dunckerley’s royal
paternity before George III. Th is detail is repeated in the Gentleman’s Maga-
zine version of the story. In both accounts, Dunckerley claims he was put off
by Sir Edward, who denied seeing any physical resemblance between the late
king and the former gunner. Depending on which periodical one reads, Wal-
pole either did not understand what Dunckerley was trying to suggest, or he
did and abruptly dismissed the notion.41 Sadler fi nds it diffi  cult to reconcile Sir
Edward Walpole’s very real solicitude for Dunckerley and his family with his
refusal to support Dunckerley’s attempt to be recognized as George II’s son.42 In
another century, Walpole’s ambivalence on this point seems entirely plausible,
and his disparate actions entirely amenable to reconciliation. He truly believed

--- Sayfa 32 ---
Th e Making of a Myth
19
that Dunckerley was a worthy man and had been a fi ne gunner in service to the
Royal Navy. However, Walpole was also certain that Dunckerley was not George
II’s bastard, and would not discredit Dunckerley’s late parents just to please an
impecunious dependant.
Diff erent versions of this same story appear repeatedly in the various mid-
1790s accounts of Dunckerley’s quest for recognition, but the central point is
consistent: Walpole supported Dunckerley’s request for a pension on the basis of
merit, but denied his royal parentage. Walpole is mentioned in a variety of news-
paper accounts in the months surrounding Dunckerley’s death, but he comes to
us most directly through a brief biographical sketch of Dunckerley composed
by the prominent Surrey antiquarian William Bray (1736–1832). Buried in a
signifi cant collection of unpublished ‘Candid Biographies’, the sketch has long
been mis-catalogued and was only recently correctly identifi ed.43 Th e account
draws heavily on a letter to Bray from Sir Edward Walpole, dated April 1777.
Walpole’s voice is the primary one in this source, though Bray also includes
anecdotes from Bishop John Butler (1717–1802) and Walpole’s cook, Benjamin
Brown. (See Appendix 2 for the complete sketch.)
Th e Bray manuscript is of interest for a number of reasons. First, Sir Edward
Walpole was Dunckerley’s most consistent patron and played a central role in
securing the grace-and-favour apartments at Somerset House occupied by Dunck-
erley’s mother and grandmother, and subsequently by Dunckerley beginning in
1765.44 Dunckerley acknowledged Walpole’s patronage when it came to his mari-
time career as well, writing in 1753 that ‘all my preferments in the Royal Navy
proceeded from your Recommendations’.45 As we have read, when Dunckerley
applied to George III for recognition and a pension, the king naturally turned to
Sir Edward for a character reference. Walpole affi  rmed that Dunckerley was a good
man of sterling character (except perhaps for a propensity to lie about his family),
had been a loyal offi  cer, and was deserving of a pension, but most accounts agree
that Walpole vehemently rejected the claim that Dunckerley was a royal bastard.46
Th us fi nding Walpole’s own voice on this point is essential.
Bray’s sketch is additionally useful because it draws on fresh witnesses who
do not appear in other sources – Bishop Butler and Benjamin Brown – as well
as clarifying inconsistencies or omissions in other accounts. It reveals, for exam-
ple, Dunckerley’s touchingly close relationship with his grandmother Bolnest.
Most importantly, unlike the other contemporary accounts that dealt with
Dunckerley’s life, this sketch was written nearly two decades before Duncker-
ley’s death, and was unconnected with the dual crises of the French Revolution
and resulting offi  cial distrust of Masonic loyalty which surrounded Dunckerley’s
death in 1795, and which heightened the potential impact of his story. Bray col-
lected it because, as with the other stories in his collection, he was intrigued by
the human foibles it illustrated – Dunckerley’s story is in there with accounts

--- Sayfa 33 ---
20
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
of incest, adultery and clerical greed. Speaking through Bray, Walpole directly
refutes Dunckerley’s claims about his parentage. He argues that Dunckerley
opportunistically traded on his resemblance to the royal family, pretending to
be George II’s bastard in order to get a royal pension. Walpole was so angered by
the presumptuousness of the claim and by Dunckerley’s audacious involvement
of him in the scheme, that he banished Dunckerley from his home. Dunckerley
reportedly was not re-admitted until years later, when he tricked a new servant
into opening the door to him. If by some chance Dunckerley was made aware of
Walpole’s correspondence with Bray, he was undoubtedly relieved that as time
passed Bray’s manuscript did not make it into print. A fi nal striking observation
is the fact that Bray’s account of Dunckerley takes absolutely no notice of Free-
masonry nor of Dunckerley’s role in it, which suggests the relative insularity of
Dunckerley’s Masonic celebrity even twenty years aft er his initiation.

– 21 –
2 THOSE HE LEFT BEHIND
At least for a little while aft er May 1767, Th omas Dunckerley must have felt
like Jack Horner in the nursery rhyme. Eventually one tires of plums, but in the
beginning a Christmas plum is a very fi ne thing. Before we see what Dunckerley
did with his little share of the royal bounty, let us consider those he left  behind.
Th e actual story of his progenitors is as respectable as any Th omas Dunckerley
could have created for himself, but decidedly less aristocratic. Because Duncker-
ley chose a fairly straightforward chronological plot for his fi ction, the accepted
narrative will serve as our touchstone for an exploration of Bolnest and Dunck-
erley families, back into the London of the seventeenth century.
Somerset House, 1715–60
Both the accepted narrative and other primary sources refer to Mary Dunck-
erley’s husband, Adam, who was a porter at Somerset House, the building that
served as the venue for both Th omas Dunckerley’s real and imagined childhoods.
Th e main narrator in Dunckerley’s fi ctional biography, Ann Pinkney, was Mary
Dunckerley’s long-time neighbour at Somerset House. A cameo appearance was
also given to another actual neighbour, Mrs Meakins, who was allegedly the con-
duit of a very suspect £50 payment from the prince to Mary Dunckerley. Both
women were married to porters who were Adam Dunckerley’s junior colleagues
at Somerset House, Henry Pinkney (1680–1723) and John Meakins.1
In the eighteenth century porters were primarily gate- or door-keepers,
important arbiters of who passed through or was turned away, rather than
menial labourers who carried trunks or baggage. Th e porters were crown servants
appointed by warrant of the Lord Steward and were required to live at Somer-
set House. Th ey were thus provided with lodgings on site for their families.2
According to Bucholz, a great number of similar servants to the royal household
were entitled to lodgings, livery, plate and an allowance for food and beer, fuel,
candles and incidentals.3 So in addition to Adam Dunckerley’s salary of £40 per
annum with £10 more for fi ring and candles, he likely accrued other benefi ts
which allowed his family some real measure of comfort. Adam Dunckerley was

--- Sayfa 35 ---
22
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
not a gentleman, but so long as he was alive and employed, his family lived a
respectable, even comfortable life.
One point that comes up repeatedly in analyses of Dunckerley’s claim to be
a royal bastard is the fact that his mother occupied so-called grace-and-favour
apartments at Somerset House, and that Dunckerley later was able to enjoy
the same sort of arrangement both there and at Hampton Court Palace. Th is is
taken as an indication that the royal family knew about Mary Dunckerley and
her special son, decades before Th omas’s offi  cial ‘recognition’. In reality, this is
another instance in which context is critical to understanding the actual signifi -
cance of the situation. Grace-and-favour lodgings were not uncommon in early
modern London, where the crown had many properties and where there were
small crowds of people who asserted their right to occupy them. Th e space and
money devoted to this sort of largesse was staggering, though the individual
lodgings themselves were not necessarily luxurious. Records for the eighteenth
century are sketchy, but in the mid-nineteenth century ‘Palaces, &c not in the
personal Occupation of HER MAJESTY’ included large sections of St James’s
Palace, Marlborough House, Kensington Palace with its stables and outbuild-
ings, Hampton Court Palace with its stables and outbuildings, sections of Kew
Palace, Windsor Castle and the Military Knights’ Houses, Frogmore House,
Richmond Park and the buildings at Bushy Park. In 1854 the total expenditure
for upkeep and repairs to palaces not occupied by the royal family, but ‘occupied
under grace and favour’ was £15,151.4 Many would-be occupants were former
offi  ce-holders or dignitaries of one sort of another, but others had only the most
tenuous claim to either royal favour or free housing. When George III came to
the throne in 1760, little control was exercised over who occupied grace-and-
favour apartments. In his study of Hampton Court Palace, Ernest Law notes
that the ‘most important qualifi cation in favour of an applicant, in the days when
George III was king, was the friendship of someone at court, especially the Lord
Chamberlain’.5 Th e Lord Chamberlain between 1766 and 1782 was Francis Sey-
mour-Conway, later the 1st Marquess of Hertford. He was a close relative of Sir
Edward Walpole by marriage and, as we shall see at Hampton Court Palace, the
Walpoles took full advantage of that connection, fi lling apartments in both the
palace and associated buildings with their extended family.6
Even if one did not have friends at Court, it was frequently suffi  cient to merely
lay permanent claim to apartments one might have once occupied legitimately.
Th e problem of ‘squatters’ in Hampton Court Palace, Somerset House and other
royal palaces was a persistent one under George II, and confusion persisted under
George III. Law reports that the situation grew particularly embarrassing in the
1770s, when it was discovered that residents were structurally altering rooms
without permission, bartering between themselves to exchange apartments and
even leasing apartments to strangers. As a response the administration instituted

--- Sayfa 36 ---
Th ose He Left  Behind
23
the use of warrants to confi rm occupants in their grants, in the hope of regain-
ing control over the properties.7 In 1780, Lord Chamberlain Hertford wrote to
the Under Housekeeper at Hampton Court Palace announcing the new policy:
Th e reason[s] for the Order which I am now to signify, by His Majesty’s commands,
are too obvious to require Illustration. Th is mode of transfer prevents His Majesty
from favouring such persons as he may think most proper to indulge with Lodgings.
His Majesty so far from being consulted or His own pleasure being necessary to give
Lodging to any person who may apply for them, will not even know, except by acci-
dent, the persons residing in his Palaces; they will become a kind of freehold to any
persons who can once obtain possession therein, and the abuse of such a custom may
grow in time, and in other hands, than those who now possess the Lodgings, to be
still more unpleasant and off ensive to His Majesty, and produce a transfer for motives
far less pure than those of Friendship or Acquaintance, as was notoriously the case at
Somerset House.8
It thus seems probable that the widows Dunckerley, Meakins and Pinkney who
fi gured so prominently in both the accepted narrative and Dunckerley’s actual
childhood belonged to that group of people who, once ensconced, were diffi  cult
to remove from royal properties. Law recounts that in 1781 the Lord Chamber-
lain authorized warrants for many of the remaining squatters rather than going
through the eff ort and scandal of formal eviction.9 In addition, Dunckerley’s
grandmother, Elizabeth Burgis Bolnest, who was undoubtedly a fi ne woman but
no grand offi  ce-holder, occupied an apartment at Somerset House secured for
her by Sir Edward Walpole and Lord Plymouth (1707–32).10 When Dunckerley
returned from his working cruise around the Mediterranean in late 1765, still
broke and probably rather desperate, Sir Edward Walpole again intervened on
his behalf, this time securing for him the apartments left  empty a few years earlier
by his mother’s death. Dunckerley moved his family, apparently from Plymouth,
and installed them in his childhood home, probably in early November. Th e fact
that the apartments were still available speaks to the dilapidated and relatively
undesirable condition of the property, which was demolished in the early 1770s
when Dunckerley and other occupants moved to Hampton Court Palace, which
was largely unoccupied.11
Th us, aft er 1765, Dunckerley never again had to pay for housing. One would
have thought that this, combined with his naval pension, might have brightened
his outlook a bit. Notwithstanding the alleged cachet of the Somerset House
address and the undoubted benefi t of free housing, Dunckerley complained bit-
terly in a 1766 letter to Viscount Townshend, ‘I barely Exist, in this Apartment,
where my Mother seceded from the World near thirty years …’.12 Such an appar-
ent lack of gratitude goes against the accepted narrative and various pendant
accounts, which universally portray Dunckerley as touchingly grateful for every
favour ever done him.13 Th e real Dunckerley was much more complex and his

--- Sayfa 37 ---
24
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
personality exhibits a strong sense of expectation, if not outright entitlement. By
the end of his life, it appears likely he came to believe that his common birth was
a sort of cosmic accident, and that in some sense, he really was the son of a king,
because he should have been.
It is perhaps not surprising, then, that Sir Edward Walpole suff ers rather
unkindly in Dunckerley’s invented family history. Despite decades of faithful
and remarkably eff ective if not glamorous patronage, Dunckerley rejected him,
substituting the Duke of Devonshire in his place as the accepted narrative’s
totemic aristocrat. It is revealing that this Devonshire connection, which plays
such a prominent role in the accepted narrative, appears in no other versions of
the story. Th is leitmotif of the accepted narrative can be seen as Dunckerley’s
deliberate attempt to defl ect attention away from his actual patron and create an
ideal in his stead. It should be remembered that Walpole and Dunckerley had a
serious falling-out over Dunckerley’s attempt to have Walpole endorse Dunck-
erley’s fi ctional biography. As a result, Bray tells us, Dunckerley was barred from
Walpole’s home for several years, and Walpole’s name is widely cited as a pri-
mary source of information contained in many of the uncanonical obituaries,
all of which feature Walpole’s denial of Dunckerley’s claim of royal parentage.
By excluding Walpole from the accepted narrative, then, Dunckerley is not only
denying his patronage, but removing him as an authority and attempting to nul-
lify his opposition to Dunckerley’s scheme for royal recognition.
Each new episode in Dunckerley’s accepted narrative reveals afresh that
accurate dating within the story is very problematic if one takes dates or situa-
tions to be literally true. However, since we have already been disabused of that
delusion, it seems most likely that when Dunckerley fi rst refers to the Duke of
Devonshire, he means William Cavendish, the 2nd duke. Amongst the other
high offi  ces he held, Devonshire was Lord Steward in 1707–10 and again in
1714–16. Th e Duke’s name might well have insinuated itself into Dunckerley’s
imagination because Adam Dunckerley began to work at Somerset House in
1715, and the warrant for his position was under the jurisdiction of Devon-
shire as the current Lord Steward. However tenuous this connection might be,
Th omas Dunckerley systematically usurps Walpole’s benevolence towards his
family, substituting in his place two fond dukes of Devonshire. Just one would
not do, as over the course of the accepted narrative they rather inconveniently
kept dying.
One or another of the dukes of Devonshire thus appears at three strategic
junctures of Dunckerley’s accepted narrative. As we have already noted, Adam
Dunckerley was alleged to have come to Houghton Hall in the entourage of the
2nd Duke of Devonshire just aft er Mary Bolnest’s seduction by the prince. Th is
made Adam a convenient husband for the ruined girl, and they were duly mar-
ried. Some time then passes, though just how long is not specifi ed. At the next

--- Sayfa 38 ---
Th ose He Left  Behind
25
critical point Th omas Dunckerley asserts that Adam was personally employed by
the Duke, this time in the early 1720s. In this episode, Mary Dunckerley attests
that, ‘At the latter end of November 1723, Mr Dunckerley went to Chatsworth,
in Derbyshire, on some business for the Duke of Devonshire, and did not return
till the May following.’14 Even Henry Sadler, who generally believed Dunckerley’s
claims, notes that this is a diffi  culty – it being unlikely that a porter at Somerset
House would be sent into the country on the Duke’s private business for six
months, especially as Devonshire was no longer the Lord Steward.15 Finally, in
the 1796 version of the accepted narrative, Dunckerley gratefully acknowledges
that it was the 3rd Duke of Devonshire who secured the Somerset House apart-
ment for him in 1765. Th is was quite a nift y trick, as the Duke had died the
year before, but fi ngering the 4th Duke of Devonshire would have required the
involvement of a living person, something Dunckerley was at pains to avoid.
Th is connection to the dukes of Devonshire is particularly problematic
because the basis for it is apparently so fl imsy. Adam and Mary Dunckerley were
indeed married at Houghton in 1712, and while we know she was employed
there as a servant, his presence is less easily explained. Th at said, there is no
surviving record that identifi es anyone named Dunckerley as an employee or
dependant of the Cavendish family in the seventeenth or eighteenth centuries.16
Additionally, we can readily and continuously account for Adam Dunckerley’s
employment in London as a crown servant between 1715 and his death in 1729.
Finally, Sir Edward Walpole is on record as having been the benefactor who
secured grace-and-favour apartments for Elizabeth Bolnest and her family.17
Th ese complications are our fi rst clue that this key part of Dunckerley’s narrative
cannot possibly be accurate, which begs further investigation.
On the Distaff  Side: the Bolnest Family
Although Dunckerley claimed to have bitterly resented his mother’s false mod-
esty, he does not repudiate her entirely, so we begin a careful look at his actual
family with her relatives, the Bolnests. Dunckerley quotes Ann Pinkney in
claiming that his mother was a doctor’s daughter, and the assertion also appears
in the Weekly Entertainer’s account of Dunckerley’s ancestry under the title,
‘Authentic Particulars of the late Th omas Dunckerley, Esq’. Th e anonymous
author writes: ‘It farther appears from Mr Dunckerley’s account that his mother
was the daughter of a physician (Dr Bolnest), and lived as a companion with a
lady of fashion.’18 As with so many other aspects of Dunckerley’s idealized life,
only some of that is true.
Prior to her ‘unhappy’ marriage to Adam Dunckerley in October 1712, Mary
Dunckerley was Mary Bolnest, daughter of Elizabeth and Edward Bolnest (d.
1703). Th ough many of the Bolnest family seem to have lived in Cambridgesh-

--- Sayfa 39 ---
26
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
ire, Mary’s closest relatives were Londoners. Beginning in the 1640s, the Bolnests
were prosperous London merchants. Edward Bolnest and his wife, Mary, lived
in the parish of St Botolph Bishopsgate, in the City proper. Edward (d. January
1640⁄1) was a member of the Merchant Taylors’ Company, and his wife, Mary,
died 14 January1645⁄6, mentioning sons Edward (1627–c. 1680)19 and Philip (d.
1648) in her will.20 Th ere also seems to have been a son named Francis, born in
1630. Mary was confusingly married fi rst to Edward Bolnest and then, upon his
death, to William Bolnest, who appears in records as a citizen and member of
the Weavers’ Company.21
Mary’s and Edward’s son, Edward, who sometimes appears in records as
‘Bolnesse’, graduated from Oxford University and became chemical physician
in ordinary to Charles II.22 Th is unfortunately means that though Bolnest could
claim the prestige of serving the king, he did not actually draw a salary for it.
Bolnest was one of the organizers in 1665 of the Society of Chemical Physicians,
intended as competition to the Royal College of Physicians.23 His somewhat
bizarre medical treatises are ample testimony of an agile and rather perverse mind.
His writings include Medicina instaurata, or, A brief account of the true grounds
and principles of the art of physick (1665) and Aurora chymica, or, A rational way
of preparing animals, vegetables, and minerals for a physical use (1672), both dedi-
cated to the Duke of Buckingham. Allen Debus asserts that Medicina instaurata
was largely an advertisement for one of Bolnest’s concoctions, the ‘solar pill’.24
A third work, Fontina salutis, was advertised but not published. Bolnest may
have become acquainted with Th omas Vaughan (pseudo. Eugenius Philalethes)
through their Oxford connections. During the 1650s, Vaughan was active in a
wide circle of alchemists and others interested in hermetical philosophy. At the
time, Vaughan claimed that Bolnest had engaged him as a tutor in ‘natural phi-
losophy and Chimicall physicke’, but failed to pay the promised fee of £300.25
Bolnest, on the other hand, claimed to have forwarded money to Vaughan, but
not to have received the key to alchemy he believed he had been promised in
return. Bolnest threatened violence and they took the matter to court in 1661,
but the outcome is unclear. Lacking the secret to the philosopher’s stone, Bol-
nest was at least acknowledged in a 1669 work titled the Marrow of Chymical
