Physic, by ‘W.T’., who credits him with assisting in the discovery of the art of
making artifi cial rubies.26
Th e strangest of Bolnest’s works appears to have been Aurora chymica, pub-
lished in both Latin and English in 1672. In it, Bolnest laments the careless or
incorrect preparation of medicines, and proposes to properly ‘shew the Prepara-
tion of Animals, Vegetables and Minerals, for a Physical use’ so that each man
may be his own ‘carer’, or at least be competent to judge the fi tness of medicines
prescribed and prepared for his use.27 In the section in which Bolnest discusses
medicines derived from animals, he gives the recipe for a ‘Mummiall Quintes-

--- Sayfa 40 ---
Th ose He Left  Behind
27
sence’, which ‘produceth wonderful eff ects both in preserving and restoring
health’.28 Bolnest directs the careful apothecary to
take the fl esh of a sound young man dying a violent Death, about the middle of
August, three or four pounds, let the fl esh be taken from his Th ighs or other fl eshy
parts; put it into a fi t Glass, and pour upon it highly rectifi ed spirit of wine.29
Th e second recipe in the chapter, ‘Th e Quintessence of Mans Blood’, carries on in
a similar vein. In prescribing bits of distilled fl esh-and-blood cordial, Bolnest was
not an innovator, but was participating in one of the last waves of professional
enthusiasm for what Richard Sugg describes as a long fascination in England and
the rest of Europe for ‘corpse medicine’. While academically trained physicians
discarded corpse medicine with the coming of the Enlightenment, common folk
continued to place stock in blood therapies and the power of powdered skull to
cure epileptic fi ts and other ailments well into the nineteenth century.30
Dr Bolnest and his wife, Mildred, had at least seven children. Edward was
born in 1664 and died the following year. His grieving parents gave their next
son, born in 1666, the same name. Th is second Edward lived only a few months
and was buried in December 1666. A third Edward was born (c. 1667) and grew
to manhood to become Mary Bolnest Dunckerley’s father. A daughter Mildred
died in June 1665 and was buried at St Giles Cripplegate. A daughter Isabella
was born in 1660, but died of the plague in 1666.31 An unnamed son was bur-
ied in 1670, with the father identifi ed as ‘Bolnest, chemyst’. A third daughter,
also named Mildred, was born in 1676. Th e sons were baptized at St Giles Crip-
plegate, Isabella at Greenwich, and the second baby Mildred was baptized in
1676 at St Mary-le-Bow.32 In 1677 the family buried Better Coney, described
as ‘a negor and servant to C[hemyst]. Edward Bolnest’.33 It seems likely that the
third son named Edward became a physician of some sort as well. A ‘Dr Edward
Boldnest’ described as a ‘chymist’ is named in a warrant to appear at Guildhall
on 28 September 1687, as part of the Visitation of London.34 Th e Visitation of
London was one of a series of Heralds’ Visitations, which were surveys made
under royal commission to establish that arms were borne with proper authority
and to register the descendants of families entitled to bear arms. As the senior Dr
Bolnest is believed to have died around 1680, it is likely the survey identifi es the
younger Edward, Mary Bolnest Dunckerley’s father.35
Mary’s parents, Dr Edward Bolnest and Elizabeth Burgis, were married at
Knightsbridge Holy Trinity in Middlesex in 1688.36 A son, Edward, was born in
1694 and baptized at St Giles Cripplegate, as was a daughter, Elizabeth, born in
1696.37 In 1718, Elizabeth married John Lucas at the church of St Martin in the
Fields.38 Mary was probably born around 1690, though record of her baptism
has yet to be identifi ed. Dr Edward Bolnest was buried at St Giles Cripplegate in

--- Sayfa 41 ---
28
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
1703. Th us we have some idea of Mary Dunckerley’s Bolnest relatives, and her
grandfather, at least, was as colourful as they come.
So, Mary Bolnest was indeed a physician’s daughter, lending support to at
least that part of Dunckerley’s bid to build up his credentials as a gentleman.
However, Dunckerley strongly suggests that his mother was a lady’s companion
to Lady Walpole, and later biographers took up that notion with alacrity. Th e
accepted narrative tells us that Mary was living with the Walpoles at Houghton
Hall in Norfolk when the man who would become George II ‘debauched’ the
girl. Mrs Walpole, neé Catherine Shorter, supposedly married the girl off  quietly
to Adam Dunckerley. Th e diffi  culty with the Devonshire connection has already
been noted, but the local parish records at Houghton do indeed show that Mary
Bolnest was married to Adam Dunckerley on 29 October 1712.39 In the ‘Decla-
ration’, Dunckerley claims the ceremony was performed by Dr Henry Bland (d.
1746), a childhood friend of Sir Robert Walpole.40 Th e ceremony was actually
performed by Th omas Carter, the vicar at Houghton-Next-Harpley, a little-used
church located on the grounds of the Walpoles’ Norfolk estate, Houghton.41
Turning from the ‘Declaration’ to a less sympathetic source, the Weekly
Entertainer gives more titillating version of the seduction, which moves the
story along from the marriage, which we know happened in 1712, to events the
‘Declaration’ places in 1723, though again glossing over the passage of more
than a decade:
George the Second, (then Prince of Wales) became acquainted with and enamoured
of her; and that the lady [the then Mrs Walpole], discovering they were too intimate,
communicated the aff air to Mrs Bolnest, and contrived the unhappy marriage with
Mr Dunckerley, (who was a dependant on the Devonshire family) with whom they
thought she would be entirely removed from the Prince; but his Highness discover-
ing her, set his emissaries to persuade her husband to barter away his own and his
wife’s honour.42
Entertaining, but is it accurate?
Mary’s Bolnest’s fi rst royal seduction at Houghton Hall is a precursor to the
later account of Dunckerley’s conception, and so it deserves some careful consid-
eration. According to Pinkney, Mary Dunckerley told her that she had ‘too well
known’ the Prince of Wales before her marriage, and so, upon encountering him
later, ‘could deny him nothing’.43 Th e inclusion of this detail, though not strictly
necessary for the remainder of the story, must be signifi cant, perhaps because it
could be seen as exculpating the married woman from some of the stain of adul-
tery due to the lovers’ prior connection. Perhaps Dunckerley was not quite ready
to turn his mother into a complete wanton. Th en there is the fact that although
it is well-documented that the Prince of Wales had a roving eye, he tended to

--- Sayfa 42 ---
Th ose He Left  Behind
29
pursue Court ladies, and not chambermaids who were, as Bray adds perhaps gra-
tuitously, ‘uncommonly plain & homely’.44
Th is is where Dunckerley’s careful fi ction seriously unravels, threatening to
wreck his scheme had any of his correspondents been paying critical attention.
Since Mary and Adam Dunckerley were married in October 1712, her premari-
tal seduction would likely have happened earlier that year, as the alleged reason
for the hasty marriage was to provide cover for any accidental pregnancy. Th e
trick here is that Dunckerley does not put a date on the initial seduction, allow-
ing his audience to think that perhaps it happened in the early 1720s, not long
before he sends his mother off  on that fateful Christmas visit. But for his main
characters the world was a very diff erent and rather uncertain place in 1712.
A Brief History Lesson
We will probably never know whether Dunckerley cleverly fi nessed the problem
of chronology at the beginning of his deception, or whether it was his desire to
shave a few years off  his age that prompted him to hit upon the 1720s as a proper
decade for subjecting his mother to debauchery. Either way, his was a prudent
choice, as the year that actually led up to her marriage, 1712, was a fraught one
for Sir Robert Walpole, the House of Hanover, Queen Anne and generally most
of Continental Europe. Th e War of Spanish Succession (1710–14) was grinding
to a slow close and, in its waning months, George Lewis, Elector of Hanover and
soon to be George I of Great Britain, uncharacteristically chose to take a stand
putting him at odds with Queen Anne’s stated desire to end the war quickly.
In Hanover the Dowager Electress Sophia, heiress to the British throne, busily
conspired with her grandson the Electoral Prince George Augustus (the future
George II), to keep the family in Anne’s good graces. Sophia of the Palatinate
(1630–1714) was daughter of Frederick V, Elector Palatine, and Elizabeth of
Scotland, Queen of Bohemia. In 1658 she married Ernest Augustus, heir to the
Electorate of Hanover. Th ey had seven children who lived to adulthood. Th eir
eldest son became George I of Great Britain. By 1712 she feared her son’s intran-
sigence might cause them to be written out of the succession at the last moment,
even all those many years aft er the Protestant settlement established in the aft er-
math of the Glorious Revolution of 1688. Sophia’s anxiety was unnecessary as
the English Whig oligarchy was fi rmly behind the necessity of the Protestant
succession, and yet Jacobitism was rife in popular culture and from a distance
it looked more infl uential than 1715 proved it to be. For the interested reader,
there is a staggering popular literature on Jacobitism, much of which is to be
avoided. For a solid recent scholarly study, see Daniel Szechi, 1715: Th e Great
Jacobite Rebellion.45

--- Sayfa 43 ---
30
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Queen Anne was in a delicate frame of mind as well in the summer of 1712.
Her health was failing, and her conscience seems to have been troubled by the
role she continued to play in denying the throne to her stubbornly Catholic
half-brother, James Edward Stuart.46 She was anxious about the war, confl icted
over the succession and intolerant of the swirl of real and perceived diplomatic
intrigues that surrounded her. Her ambivalence about the Hanoverian succes-
sion resulted in bizarrely contradictory messages emanating from the Court of
St James, culminating in messages to Hanover that though Anne would, ‘enter
into any measure for securing the Hanoverian succession’, nonetheless, should
a prince of Hanoverian blood enter the British dominions while Anne was
still alive ‘nothing could be more dangerous to the tranquility’ of the islands.47
Indeed, one historian asserts: ‘the Queen was successful, now on one pretext,
now on another, in preventing the arrival of any member of the electoral family
in England’.48
Sir Robert Walpole was not having the best year either. In January 1712 he was
impeached for corruption as Secretary at War, expelled from Parliament and then
confi ned to the Tower for six months. He was released in early July and retreated
to the family estate, Houghton Hall, to repair his political career, plunging success-
fully into the general election as a candidate for King’s Lynn in Norfolk. Despite
Walpole’s enduring electoral popularity – he represented King’s Lynn in Parlia-
ment from 1702 to 1742 – this hardly seems a time when he might have risked
disaster and the queen’s wrath by playing host to a prince whose very presence
would have been an act of explicit rebellion on both their parts. Similarly, with the
very real anxiety the Hanoverians felt for the succession, Sophia hardly would have
acquiesced to her grandson gallivanting across the Norfolk countryside debauch-
ing chambermaids to the peril of family fortunes. Despite the romance of the story,
then, this part of the accepted narrative is irredeemable.
Cerridwen of Wales
Th e triple goddesses of maiden, mother and crone feature prominently in Brit-
ish Isles mythologies. In the medieval Welsh tale the Llyfr  Taliesin, Cerridwen
appears as a transformative goddess, oft en identifi ed most strongly with the
crone. Th e crone in Dunckerley’s life was his grandmother, Elizabeth Burgis
Bolnest, who plays a tremendously important role in his imagined history, his
actual history and, through the gift  of another tongue, his associational history.
Because of naming customs and the curious fact that until the second half of
the eighteenth century, mother’s names were oft en omitted entirely from birth
or baptismal records, women are frequently diffi  cult to trace over time. Th us
it is not surprising that we know tantalizingly little about Grandmother Bol-
nest. She is far less well documented than many of Th omas’s other relatives, yet

--- Sayfa 44 ---
Th ose He Left  Behind
31
aspects of her personality come through more strongly than they do for the rest.
In Dunckerley’s fantasy narrative, she is the moral force who dissuaded Th omas
Dunckerley’s mother from either committing suicide, or naming his royal father
and claiming an easy life. Elizabeth Bolnest also appears in outsiders’ accounts of
Dunckerley’s life, which are of varying degrees of accuracy – either as his guard-
ian, taking the place of an absent or deceased mother, or as a more comfortable
grandmotherly fi gure, as she appears in William Bray’s account. Bray took care
to secure documentation for his sketches, so here we have him writing on the
authority of Sir Edward Walpole’s cook, Benjamin Brown:
Th e old lady said she loved roast beef, but had no convenience to dress it, nor coud
[sic] she dispose of so large a joint, wished she might send on a Mond. Morn. to Sr Eds
house (then at Chelsea) – accordingly her grandson, Mr D., then a little boy, brought
a basket & clean napkins every Mond. Morng for a long time.49
Another indication of their close relationship is Mrs Bolnest’s will, by which
one of the largest bequests she left  was to her grandson Th omas. Th is intimacy
probably gave rise to the erroneous statement, found in the Gentleman’s Maga-
zine aft er Th omas Dunckerley’s own death, that his mother died when he was a
small child, and ‘his grandmother took care of him till he was put apprentice to
a barber’.50
Elizabeth Burgis Bolnest married Edward Bolnest in 1688. Despite the com-
mon misperception that ‘everyone’ married early in the old days, this assertion is
not borne out by the records. Unless the economic or political situation was dire
(as they commonly were in the seventeenth century), English women typically
married in their mid-twenties, men a few years older.51 If things looked risky,
they postponed marriage until even later, so they could better aff ord a family.
Besides using the rather haphazard technique of counting backwards for a likely
birth year, Elizabeth Bolnest gives us two other important clues in fi nding her
family – her will and her grandson. In her 1747 will, Elizabeth Bolnest mentions
her three sisters, to whom she leaves 20 shillings ‘apecs’. (Her spelling throughout
is remarkably creative.) Th e other clue is Th omas Dunckerley’s name. Naming
patterns in early modern England oft en followed a consistent pattern. Children
were frequently named aft er their paternal grandparents, maternal grandparents
and their parents. As more children were born into a family, the names of aunts
and uncles were pressed into service. Th is works better as a rule of thumb than as
a strict methodology; still, as a forename, ‘Th omas’ is entirely absent from either
the immediate Bolnest or Dunckerley families. Th ere was an Elizabeth Burgis,
born in 1668 in London, who had three sisters (Mary, Margaret and Ann) who
appear to have survived to adulthood, and a father named Th omas. Th e prize
may be illusory, however. As a name, ‘Th omas Burgis’ is readily traced through
seventeenth-century London in various records. However, it has been impos-

--- Sayfa 45 ---
32
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
sible thus far to be sure which, if any, of these men named Th omas Burgis was
Elizabeth Bolnest’s father.
Th e woman who seems likely to have been Elizabeth Burgis Bolnest’s mother
is even more of a will-o’-the-wisp. She appears variously as Marion, Maryon,
Marina and Merinah. To be fair, the baptismal entries of her ten children, born
between 1665 and 1676, give the family name as Burgis, Burges and Burgesse.
All the Burgis children were baptized at St Andrew, Holborn: Elizabeth, 29
August 1665; Ann, 17 March 1666⁄7; Mary, 21 March 1668⁄9; Th omas, 19 April
1668; Elizabeth, 31 December 1668; Augustine, 17 April 1670; Margaret, 18
June 1671; John, 9 June 1672; Mary, 25 January 167¾  ; Margaret, 8 October
1676. Th is was astonishing fertility, though it came at a price. Note the repeti-
tion of names as several children did not survive infancy. Th e girl who became
Dunckerley’s grandmother was the second Elizabeth born into the family.
What makes the mother’s name really interesting, though, is that she seems
to have been Welsh, and one is tempted to see in the various spellings London-
ers’ attempts to cope with a foreign-sounding name. We arrive at this conclusion
through a process of elimination, which is neither sound historical method nor
even good common sense. Still, there it is. Th omas Dunckerley was very close to
his grandmother Bolnest – closer than he was to any other relative aside from
his mother. His grandmother lived nearby in Somerset House through all of his
early life. As a child, Th omas even fetched roast beef for her from the Walpole
home in Chelsea every Monday (with a basket and clean napkin, no less), and
he is the only grandchild mentioned in her will by name, even though she spells
it incorrectly.52 Could ‘Tunikley’ be another clue to an accent of sorts? Given
their bond and proximity no one would be in a better position to teach him
Welsh, and it now appears certain that Th omas Dunckerley had a command of
suffi  cient Welsh to get up and give a toast, or recite a poem, and as it states in the
1778 list of members of the Honourable Society of Cymmrodorion, he was ‘of
Welsh descent’.53
Th e Dunckerleys
Th e Dunckerley family originated in and around Oldham, Lancashire. Given
the number of Dunckerley births, deaths and marriages commemorated at the
parish church of St Mary, it seems likely that most of them stayed close to home.
However, the pull of London was strong, even in the sixteenth century, and in
1545 an Edward Dunckerley was buried at the church of St Pancras, Soper Lane,
London. Despite Th omas Dunckerley’s claims about royal paternity, this was
more likely his actual ancestral line, and it was respectable.
Adam’s own father, Adam Dunckerley (1689–1729) was a Yeoman of the
Guard, a royal bodyguard, from 1694 until 1718. Th e Yeomen were to be ‘the

--- Sayfa 46 ---
Th ose He Left  Behind
33
best quality under Gentry, and of a larger Stature’.54 Specifi cally, they were sup-
posed to be at least six feet tall, so perhaps this is the genetic source of Th omas
Dunckerley’s regal bearing? In 1716, Adam Dunckerley, pater, became a Yeo-
man Usher, a post he occupied until 1718, when he seems likely to have been
the Adam Dunckerley who took up a position as Customs offi  cer.55 Dunckerley
vacated his government posts by 1723 and is presumed to have died around that
time.56 Th e salary he earned was comfortable, £30 per annum as a Yeoman of the
Guard and £40 as a Yeoman Usher.
Adam Dunckerley the elder married Mary Wilkinson at St James Duke’s
Place, London, in 1688.57 Curiously, this was a place known at precisely that
time for hasty marriages without licence or banns – which was a convenience
Th omas Dunckerley would have recourse to as well. Th e couple had at least two
children: Adam, born in 1689, and Lewis Charles Dunckerley, born in 1698.58
Both were baptized at St James, Westminster. Lewis grew to adulthood, mar-
rying Susanna Carr at the church of St Martin in the Fields in 1722. Th ey had
at least four children, Adam (b. 1723), Elizabeth (b. 1725), Lewis Charles (b.
1726) and James (b. 1728). All the children were baptized at St James, West-
minster. Lewis Charles Jr married in 1747, and both he and his brother James
became goldsmiths.59 Th is means that Th omas Dunckerley had an uncle and
cousins living in London throughout much of his time there. Cousin James even
named one of his sons Th omas Dunckerley (b. 1760); before the story came out
that our hero claimed they were not actually related.
To return for a moment to Th omas Dunckerley’s narrative, recall that Mary
Dunckerley asserted that her liaison with the Prince of Wales gained her husband
‘two places, and considerable advantages’. Remember too that Th omas Duncker-
ley is very coy about exactly when things happened prior to 1723. At the time he
evaluated the accepted narrative, Henry Sadler assumed that the places were as a
result of Mary’s later alleged indiscretion, but Pinkney’s statement is worded so
that Mary could actually have been referring to her fi rst seduction by the prince
and to her husband’s initial employment at Somerset House. On the other hand,
given the employment history of the elder Adam Dunckerley, we would be on
sounder footing to look to longstanding Dunckerley family connections for an
explanation rather than to illicit royal ones.
Th omas Dunckerley’s father followed in his own father’s footsteps profes-
sionally. Th ough Adam’s employment history was respectable, his positions were
not so grand as to excite suspicion – he seems not to have been quite as ambitious
or successful as his father. Adam Dunckerley fi ls was fi rst employed at Somerset
House as an Under-Porter and Lamplighter in February 1715, gaining promo-
tion to Second Porter at the Westgate of Somerset House in November, 1715,
and then First Porter in 1717.60 His name continues on the roster of employees
through his death in January 1729⁄30 and, as we have noted, his widow remained a

--- Sayfa 47 ---
34
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
resident until her own death in 1760. While these were all respectable positions,
they seem an unlikely royal gift . If a prince were to interfere to ensure a man’s
silence, it does not seem suffi  cient merely to bestow the offi  ce of Under-Porter at
a property on the decline.
Th e Busy Dunckerley Household
Upon reading any of the traditional versions of Dunckerley’s life, one could
be forgiven for assuming he was an only child. Indeed, given the ‘unhappi-
ness’ Mary Dunckerley voices in the accepted narrative, one might fear that
they never enjoyed conjugal relations at all. Nothing could be further from the
truth. Th omas Dunckerley was the fi ft h of six children born to Adam and Mary.
Th eir fi rst child was born in December 1713, and named Elizabeth aft er her
maternal grandmother.61 She was baptized at St James, Westminster. A brother
Adam soon followed (b. September 1715). He was followed by Mary, (b. June
1717) and Luke James (b. July 1719) – both of these children were baptized
at St Mary-le-Strand. Th en there is a gap of a number of years before Th omas’s
birth – though exactly how many is open to scrutiny, and is one of the most
persistently perplexing aspects of Dunckerley’s biography. Th omas’s birth was
followed in 1725⁄6 by Edward Bolnest Dunckerley, who was also baptized at St
James, Westminster. Interestingly, Th omas is the only one of the known children
of Mary and Adam Dunckerley for whom we as of yet have no baptismal record.
Before anyone jumps to any conclusions on that account, we should note there
are frequent gaps in eighteenth-century records. Mice, fi re, drunken curates and
that catchall, ‘the Blitz’, are blamed for many of the gaps, but defective records
are a persistent and pervasive annoyance for historians and genealogists, and it
would be a mistake to read too much into them. Does a missing baptismal entry
mean Dunckerley was indeed the son of George II? Not on your life. Does it
mean Dunckerley visited the church and surreptitiously removed his entry? Th at
is more plausible, but still a stretch.
Th e other obvious diffi  culty with the accepted narrative touches on the
purported misery of this marriage. If one gives any credence to the accepted nar-
rative, Adam and Mary Dunckerley did not make a love match. Still, a marriage
that produces six recorded births over a space of thirteen years was probably not
as miserable as all that. Via Mrs Pinkney, Mary claims Adam mistreated her in
the fi nal month of her pregnancy with Th omas,
... his barbarous behavior to me in the last month of my time was what I always
resented, when he threw a cat in my face, and swore he would mark the bastard. Our
separation soon followed aft er my delivery.62

--- Sayfa 48 ---
Th ose He Left  Behind
35
Not likely. Especially since if Adam and Mary did separate, then how does one
account for the birth of little of Edward Bolnest Dunckerley in February 1725⁄6?63
Nothing else is known of any of Dunckerley’s siblings, though the unnamed
granddaughter in Elizabeth Burgis Bolnest’s will was likely a sister. Dunckerley
had to leave his siblings out of the story for it to be eff ective. Th e existence of the
elder children would have betrayed the passage of time, and the birth of Edward
would have additionally revealed the likelihood of a reasonably happy marriage.
Besides, why would a man accustomed to bedding ladies of the Court come
chasing aft er a porter’s wife who had already borne four children? None of those
elements was conducive to Dunckerley’s drama.
Th omas’s father died in January 1729⁄30, leaving his widow to make the best of
an unpleasant situation. Mary Dunckerley was not entirely without resources.
Both she and her widowed mother had grace-and-favour apartments at Som-
erset House. Sir Edward Walpole had also arranged for Elizabeth Bolnest to
receive a small royal pension of £20 per annum, given as a Christmas bounty.
Records in the Royal Archives are incomplete, but indicate this amount was paid
beginning in 1735, and aft erwards her daughter continued to receive the pen-
sion at least until 1757.64 Th e crown probably disbursed payments aft er that date
as well, since Sir Edward contrived immediately aft er Mary Dunckerley’s death
in 1760 quietly to transfer the funds to Th omas’s daughter.65 Walpole wrote:
While the vacancy on the List is yet unknown it would be a great Charity to let the
young woman whose names are the same, Mary Dunckerley, pass upon the List in
place of her Grandmothers. And I should hope not a very diffi  cult matter.66
It is diffi  cult to say whether this bid was successful, but records in the Royal
Archives do indicate that, in 1776, Mary Ann Dunckerley received £45 from
the royal bounty.67
Like the widowed Bolnest, who supported her family as an infant nurse,
Mary Dunckerley put her skills to work, earning what must have been only a
small income as a mantua or dressmaker. Th is information comes to us from
Bray’s account, which cites Walpole: ‘Her dau: became a mantua maker &
was employed for Sr Edws children & Mrs Clements, as they have many rects
[receipts] to bills to testify.’68 Mrs Dorothy Clement (c. 1715–39) was Sir
Edward’s beloved mistress and the mother of his children. When the time came,
the resourceful widow duly put her son to learn a trade, and in 1735 Th omas
Dunckerley was apprenticed to William Simpson, a barber and peruke (wig)
maker. Dunckerley lasted for two years of a seven-year apprenticeship before he
could not take any more and ran away to sea. Not merely in search of glory, as
Th omas White’s 1793 sketch asserts, but to escape from barbering.

--- Sayfa 50 ---
– 37 –
3 DUNCKERLEY ALL AT SEA
Such were the people young Th omas Dunckerley left  behind when he ran away
in 1737.1 Th at much of the narrative is truthful – he really did run away, and he
eventually made his way into the Royal Navy. Th e rest of his maritime career,
not surprisingly, is less straightforward than one might hope. Some of this is due
to Dunckerley’s own obfuscation – aft er he manufactured the little fi b about
his birth, he was deliberately misleading about other dates as well. If they even
noticed this coyness, it may have amused his contemporaries, who might have
taken it for braggadocio, or absent-mindedness. For his nineteenth-century
biographers this trait was maddening – they wanted to chart Dunckerley’s naval
and Masonic careers precisely, and the arithmetic just did not work. Finally,
what appeared to be Dunckerley’s carelessness with dates was a hint to the cur-
rent author that entirely aside from his bogus royal pedigree, he was not being as
frank about other essential elements of his biography as one would have hoped.
Th e result is that we are presented with contradictory statements from Duncker-
ley and his contemporaries about just how long he was in the navy, with terms of
service ranging from sixteen to twenty-six years – and then there is an apparent
gap in the early 1740s that no one acknowledges, but which appears clearly in
the trail of documents.
Writing at the end of the nineteenth century, Henry Sadler praises Duncker-
ley for his ‘peculiar zeal in the Cause’ of Masonry, but reserves special enthusiasm
for his outstanding naval career, devoting most of a chapter to a description of
what Dunckerley might have seen or might have done during the siege of Quebec
– had he not been in the gunroom of HMS Vanguard, that is.2 In point of fact,
his captain, Robert Swanton, was noted as not having done anything remarkable
during the siege and battle, and it is terribly unlikely that Dunckerley did either.3
Nonetheless, Sadler echoes the almost universal observation that Dunckerley
‘behaved so well’ at the battle. One certainly hopes he behaved well. Gunners
were responsible for all the explosives and weaponry on the ship. If the gunner
were sloppy, the ship might well blow up, which was generally to be avoided.
Actually, the encomiums about his performance were likely drawn from Dunck-
erley’s few extant leave-taking certifi cates, which were a necessary part of the

--- Sayfa 51 ---
38
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
paperwork for an offi  cer seeking a warrant to be posted to another ship. As with
most others, Dunckerley’s are brief and formulaic, and do not actually tell us
much about his performance. Transcripts of three such certifi cates are in Wil-
liam Moss’s ‘Masonic Essays’, at the Library and Museum of Freemasonry.4 Th ey
correspond exactly to the form specifi ed in Th e Sea-gunner’s vade-mecum from
1812, which suggests that originality in such certifi cates would have been very
unusual.5 Th e originals were apparently sent by Dunckerley to George, Viscount
Townshend in 1766 to demonstrate the truth of his claims of good service, and
to bolster his plea for assistance in getting a position or pension. Th e two cer-
tifi cates from John Byron, captain of HMS Vanguard, noted that Dunckerley
‘Discharged his Trust faithfully, as a sober, Diligent Offi  cer’. Joseph Peyton, of
HMS Prince, reported Dunckerley served under him from March to Decem-
ber 1761, ‘During which time he behav’d with Sobriety and Diligence, and was
Obedient to Command’.6 While these are good reports, they are rather typical,
and not the stuff  of legend. Here the confusion seems to have come not from
Dunckerley being deliberately misleading, but from later Masonic biographers
mistaking the meaning of the certifi cates at hand.
Th e most infl uential interpreter with regard to Dunckerley’s reputation as a
naval hero has been Henry Sadler, who had himself done a stint in the merchant
marine, and who unsurprisingly saw the English sailor as a supreme masculine
role model. Sadler was keen to demonstrate that Dunckerley was an exemplary
offi  cer, and to off er him as a role model with contemporary, that is late-Victo-
rian, relevance. Sadler becomes eff usive whenever he mentions Dunckerley’s
connection with the Royal Navy. Baffl  ed as to why Dunckerley did not give
much information about his rather undistinguished naval career, Sadler argues
that this demonstrated his great modesty.7 For Sadler, Dunckerley’s naval back-
ground explains all and forgives much, including his repeated sharp confl icts
with Masonic lodges later in his career as Provincial Grand Master of much of
southern England between 1767 and 1795.
Whatever his temperament and management style owed to the Royal Navy,
Dunckerley’s innovative approach to Freemasonry was indeed fi rst manifested
aboard ship. Long before Dunckerley had Masonic provinces under his com-
mand, he exercised his creative spirit by becoming the father of English sea
lodges.8 Given the lack of pertinent records, the brief history of these lodges has
already been discussed in as much detail as is possible by enthusiastic researchers
of maritime Freemasonry, but their importance in the context of Dunckerley’s
naval career and the creation of his ambitious Masonic agenda has only recently
been recognized.9
It is virtually impossible to separate Th omas Dunckerley’s later naval career
from his early Masonic identity, which underscores the point made more gen-
erally by Jessica Harland-Jacobs in her recent work on Freemasonry and the

--- Sayfa 52 ---
Dunckerley all at Sea
39
British Empire. In her study, Builders of Empire, she makes the case for Brit-
ish, and particularly English, Freemasonry as a powerful tool in British hands
as the empire spread throughout the world in the eighteenth and nineteenth
centuries. Dunckerley’s experiences echo and prefi gure those of thousands of his
countrymen, and his energetic eff orts on behalf of Freemasonry undoubtedly
strengthened the effi  cacy of the Craft  as a contributing factor in building later
imperial cohesion.
Th e story of the precocious sailor-lad seems to originate in the 1793 bio-
graphical sketch of Dunckerley in the Freemasons’ Magazine written by Th omas
White, master of the Academy in Colchester, and then Provincial Junior Grand
Warden for Essex under Dunckerley.10 In the acknowledgements of that volume
of the magazine, Bro. J. Grierson, Deputy Provincial Grand Master of Hamp-
shire, is credited with having provided information for the sketch.11 Th e account
by White and Grierson asserts that Dunckerley ran away from school at age ten,
when ‘a thirst for glory, and a desire to engage in the bustle of the world, induced
him to leave his school abruptly and enter the Royal Navy’.12 Th ey mention no
source for this engaging anecdote, but an interesting version of the same story
appears in the Gentleman’s Magazine in December 1795. Here an anonymous
author asserts that Dunckerley’s impetuous decision to go to sea provided yet
another opportunity for Sir Edward Walpole to demonstrate his benevolence
towards the boy. Th is account maintains that Walpole took suffi  cient interest in
Dunckerley’s welfare to intervene with Sir John Norris, then Admiral of the Fleet
and commander-in-chief, ‘requesting that the boy might have such instruction
given him as the ship would aff ord’. Another illustrious fi gure, Admiral Sir John
‘Jack’ Norris, is widely cited as young Dunckerley’s fi rst commanding offi  cer,
though at this point the claim is without any supporting evidence. Wherever he
ended up, Dunckerley indeed gratefully acknowledged Walpole’s repeated kind-
nesses, writing in 1753, ‘all my preferments in the Royal Navy proceeded from
your Recommendations’.13 Walpole’s exertions on Dunckerley’s behalf are fur-
ther corroborated by Bray’s sketch, for which correspondence with Sir Edward
was a primary source.14
How much schooling did the young man need? Much hinges on the spurious
1724 birth date. Dunckerley goes out of his way to enshrine 1724 in the accepted
narrative, and that year is later held up by admirers as proof of his precociousness
– that though Dunckerley left  school at ten he still qualifi ed as a schoolmaster in
January 1744⁄5. In short, it is used as evidence of his general excellence and inher-
ent intellectual acumen. As romantically appealing as this story is, it is part of
the larger fi ction. Despite the lack of a birth or baptismal registration, it is still
possible to reconstruct a probable birth ‘window’ for Dunckerley. Th e strongest
evidence to surface so far is the 1 September 1735 registration of his apprentice-
ship for a period of seven years to William Simpson, a barber and peruke-maker

--- Sayfa 53 ---
40
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
of St Martin-in-the-Fields, for which the boy’s family paid a premium of £10.15
Th e customary age for apprenticeship at this time hovered closely around four-
teen, and certainly not much younger, making a birth year in 1720 or 1721 most
likely.16 Simpson paid a stamp duty on the apprenticeship on 23 June, 1737,
indicating that Dunckerley was still working with him at that point, which fi xes
his escape in the second half of 1737 or even later. Th is evidence is also compat-
ible with Sir Edward Walpole’s claim to have pacifi ed Simpson the abandoned
barber with a 10 guinea bribe, in order to stave off  legal action against the family
when Dunckerley absconded.17
A second piece of independent data is provided by Sir Philip Stevens (1728–
1809), later Lord Commissioner of the Admiralty, who processed the paperwork
for Dunckerley’s pension in 1764. Stevens recorded that, on 9 May 1764, Dunck-
erley swore that he was forty-three years old.18 Th is is revealing, coming as it does
several years aft er Dunckerley claimed to have learned the truth about his iden-
tity – which prominently features a birth in late 1724. It seems that when money
was on the line, he was willing to tell the truth. Th e information in Dunckerley’s
sworn statement places his birth at some time between April 1720 and April
1721. Finally, if we look at the intervals between the births of Dunckerley’s four
elder siblings, we come up with an average space of twenty-two months, which
would make a probable birth date for Th omas in May 1721. While this makes
sense in terms of the evidence, it is at odds with his alleged birthday of 23 Octo-
ber. We have already asserted that Dunckerley deliberately falsifi ed his birth year
to gloss over diffi  culties at the beginning of his accepted narrative, but surely he
could easily have left  the day itself undisturbed? Th is leaves us with 23 October
1720 as a likely birth date until his baptismal record surfaces.
Th us Dunckerley probably went to sea about the time he turned seventeen, a
much less remarkable age than ten. Th e apprenticeship record further militates
against the claim put forward by his admirers that Dunckerley had fi ne classical
training, instead suggesting that he actually had little or no formal education.
Dunckerley seems to corroborate this lack of schooling in his later observation
to George Townsend: ‘God hath been pleas’d to bless me with common Sense;
which I have for many years endeavor’d to improve by Reading & Study, not hav-
ing it in my power to get assistance from Teachers.’19
Dunckerley left  the navy in May 1763, and in June 1764 was awarded a pen-
sion of £45 12s 6d, reportedly upon the recommendation of Henry, Earl Digby,
then a Lord of the Admiralty. While Digby was in the commission, he was not,
as reported in the accepted narrative, First Lord, a position held in 1763 by a
succession of offi  cers: George Grenville, the Earl of Sandwich, and the Earl of
Egmont.20 Despite the complete inadequacy of a £45 pension, just securing one
was a substantial accomplishment as gunners were typically eligible for neither
half-pay nor pensions.21 Beginning with Robert Freke Gould in the nineteenth

--- Sayfa 54 ---
Dunckerley all at Sea
41
century, Dunckerley’s biographers have noted that the dates he provides for his
naval services are problematic and, while clearing up his age, his pension applica-
tion provides another striking example of years of service that seem awry.22 In
the statement taken by Stevens in 1764, Dunckerley swore that he had served for
sixteen years in the Royal Navy, which would mean he joined in 1748.
Th is is where we must take to heart the earlier warning about the unreli-
ability of Dunckerley’s dates – but here the warning applies equally to dates
surrounding Dunckerley, but not necessarily of his making. Biographers have
always assumed that once Dunckerley went to sea around 1737, he stayed there.
Th at does not appear to be the case. In Th omas Dunckerley: A Remarkable Free-
mason, Chudley reproduces a facsimile of a page from the muster book of HMS
Namur, which shows Th omas Dunckerley posted on the ship from 14 April to
4 August 1742 as an able seaman.23 Dunckerley’s document trail next leads us to
the Fleet Prison, where he married Ann Martin in December 1743. Dunckerley
told the parson he was employed as a groom, living in Hanover Square, London.
By the time Dunckerley married Hannah Somerset in June 1745, he was still
living in Hanover Square, but had worked his way up to being a gentleman’s serv-
ant. At that point the Admiralty records pick up Dunckerley again, and show he
was assigned to a ship in February 1744⁄5, which is just a month aft er he passed the
Trinity House examination and was certifi ed as a schoolmaster.24
Dunckerley’s apparent sojourn on land, gaps in Admiralty records, and
confl icting evidence about where he was during the fi rst half of 1745 all bring
the continuity of his naval service into question. Th e accepted narrative, Wil-
liam Bray, and everyone else who comments are in agreement that Dunckerley
ran away to sea. Th e assumption has always been that, once at sea, he found his
métier, and stayed there. But he did not. Th e information contained in his mar-
riage records necessarily sends us in another direction, and perhaps provides
another, more immediate source of Dunckerley’s ambition to be a gentleman.
Suppose, for the sake of it, that being an intelligent youth, he realized there was
no future in being a common sailor. He decided that his best option was to study
for the schoolmaster exams – he was, according to Bray and Walpole, the benefi -
ciary of on-board schooling, and if it had been a positive experience it might well
have inspired him. Being a sailor does not provide much time to study, but going
ashore he might fi nd a little employment that would give him some spare time;
perhaps as a servant. Bearing in mind this is all purely conjecture, such a situa-
tion would have given Sir Edward Walpole another opportunity to be of use, and
simultaneously, sown the seeds of Dunckerley’s ultimate rejection of Walpole.
When the 3rd Duke of Devonshire was named Lord Lieutenant of Ireland
in 1737, Sir Edward became his chief secretary. Shortly thereaft er, in 1740,
Devonshire completed his grand new London mansion, Devonshire House. He
needed a small army of servants to keep his household in order. Th ough Devon-

--- Sayfa 55 ---
42
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
shire House, demolished in 1924, was located in Piccadilly, its parish church
was St George’s, Hanover Square. Th e parish was a new creation, carved out
of the much older parish St Martin-in-the-Fields. Th e name of the church, St
George’s, Hanover Square, and the neighbourhood in which it stands, Hanover
Square, were so named as tribute to the Hanoverian accession. If Walpole had
recommended Dunckerley as a likely servant, this could explain Dunckerley’s
residence in what the Fleet parsons casually called Hanover. His time in Devon-
shire House might well have demonstrated to him that to be a gentleman is a fi ne
thing, indeed. Living in Devonshire House would have also introduced Dunck-
erley to a new set of fashionable people with whom to populate his accepted
narrative. Th at said, there is still no Dunckerley in the Chatsworth archives –
though we should recognize there is a signifi cant diff erence between a groom
casually employed in the city, and a ‘man of business’ who would travel in the
lord’s entourage and be trusted with lengthy errands into the country.
Th is must have been a very dark and challenging time in Th omas Duncker-
ley’s life. He lost two young wives in rapid succession, perhaps in childbirth. His
persistence in marrying yet a third time demonstrates he was marriage-minded,
which may have led him to conclude that while it got him out of barbering, per-
haps running off  to sea hadn’t been so clever aft er all. He needed to make plans
that would meet a family’s needs and his own social ambitions. Here he was, the
grandson of a physician and a Yeoman of the Guard, reduced to being a mere
servant. It is no wonder that in later accounts he glossed over several years in the
early 1740s, and apparently never referred to his fi rst two wives.
Steady as She Blows?
Years beneath the deck aside, what ultimately matters is rank, and Sadler and
subsequent researchers have made much of Dunckerley’s standing as a naval
offi  cer. Still, they have frankly wondered why a man of such outstanding charac-
teristics did not move into the commissioned ranks.25 Th e short answer is that it
would have been extraordinarily diffi  cult for him to pass that social barrier. He
would have needed great talent, an outstanding record of service, and a devoted
commanding offi  cer willing to see him through the vicissitudes of navy life. As
a matter of record, Dunckerley held at least nine, and maybe ten, diff erent naval
posts from 1744 to his offi  cial retirement in 1763, making it unlikely that he had
the opportunity to cultivate a close mentoring relationship with any of the offi  c-
ers under whom he served. Biographical accounts of his naval service diff er on
many points, but some facts may be established, and they reveal a rather incon-
gruous career. Although much is made of Dunckerley’s status as a schoolmaster,
he actually taught for only a brief period, and he most likely saw it as a fi rst
step on the way to a more lucrative, secure and prestigious position. Ship-board

--- Sayfa 56 ---
Dunckerley all at Sea
43
schoolmasters were an innovation implemented in 1702 as part of an ongoing
attempt to attract and groom a more genteel and highly educated offi  cer corps
and, as we have seen, Dunckerley likely received his own education on board
ship.26 Yet, throughout the eighteenth century, these schoolmasters were neither
regularly employed, well-regarded nor well paid.
Nonetheless, schoolmasters were warrant offi  cers, and candidates had to
provide evidence of their good character, and obtain a certifi cate from Trinity
House at Deptford proving they had been examined and found competent to
teach calligraphy, draft ing, mathematics and navigation. Dunckerley did just
that, though even here the evidence is enigmatic – the minutes of Trinity House
are dated 6 January 1744⁄5 and read:
Mr Th omas Dunckerley being Examin’d & found Qualify’d to be a School Master in
his Majesty’s Navy & having produc’d a certifi cate (as usual) of his Sobriety & good
Aff ection to his Majesty, he was certify’d accordingly.27
Th is entry is unique in two regards. Th e parenthetical comment ‘(as usual)’ is
not in itself usual – it appears nowhere in the adjoining register. Th en there is
an additional special annotation. Above Dunckerley’s name, a new hand has
sketched an imperial crown, and aft erward inserted ‘(abt. 30)’. Who annotated
the minutes, when and to what end? Th e comments, spare though they are, are in
a diff erent hand and in a lighter shade of ink. If Dunckerley were about thirty in
January 1745, that would put his birth even earlier, in 1715 or thereabouts – the
year his eldest brother, Adam, was born.28 While this seems unlikely, it further
muddies the water in the absence of Dunckerley’s actual baptismal registration.
Dunckerley abandoned teaching aft er a year, and is not noted again in that
capacity until he was serving aboard HMS Vanguard, between 1757 and 1761.29
His skill as a teacher at that point was allegedly such that aft er the Seven Years’
War he was promised a teaching post at the Portsmouth Naval Academy, though
in the event the position did not materialize.30 In any case, Dunckerley undoubt-
edly had grander, or at least more remunerative and secure, things in mind in
January 1744⁄5 when he was examined for certifi cation as a schoolmaster – the
position of gunner.31 Th is was, according to Th e Sea-gunner’s vade-mecum of
1812, a respectable post, and so the man who fi lled it must be respectable as
well.32 To qualify, a candidate again had to pass an examination, present cer-
tifi cates from captains under whom he had served testifying to his ‘diligence
and sobriety’, and also had to have served as a petty offi  cer for at least a year.33
Th us when Dunckerley laboured away as schoolmaster on HMS Edinburgh,
he was eff ectually biding time until he could qualify as a gunner and secure a
posting. His chance came in 1746, when he was assigned to HMS Fortune, a
fourteen-gun sloop, which had been recaptured from the French and refi tted.34
Dunckerley passed the examination for certifi cation as gunner on 10 April 1746,

--- Sayfa 57 ---
44
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
and received his ‘seniority’ or warrant on 22 April.35 As an interesting aside, the
author of Th e Sea-gunner’s vade-mecum noted that not every young gunner
would have had the opportunity for proper schooling, and that an aspiring man
could ‘acquire, by solitary application and little diffi  culty, suffi  cient information
to enable him to discharge the duty of master-gunner with credit to himself ’.36
Th ough his mature career seems to have begun normally enough, Dunck-
erley soon established a profoundly atypical pattern of service. Gunners were
responsible for all the cannon, ammunition, powder, small arms and other muni-
tions on the ship, including fi reworks.37 Because of their critical role in the safety
and success of the fl eet, eighteenth-century gunners were typically assigned to a
new ship when it was ordered, and then stayed with it as a permanent standing
offi  cer until either the ship or the gunner retired. Th us, Dunckerley might be
expected to spend his twenty-odd-year career on perhaps as many as two ships.
Instead, aft er becoming a gunner, he went from HMS Fortune to the Crown
(1747–53), the Nonsuch (1753), the Tyger (1753–4), the Vanguard (1754), the
Eagle (1754–5) and back to the Vanguard (1755–61), before ending his active
career on the Prince (1761–3).38
Th is is an idiosyncratic record. Henry Sadler requested a search of Royal
Navy records at the old Public Record Offi  ce, and thus had most of the details of
Dunckerley’s curiously peripatetic career before him as he wrote Th omas Dunck-
erley, His Life, Labours, and Letters in 1891.39 What the sources revealed about
Sadler’s hero was stridently at odds with the image Sadler wanted to present.40
Sadler therefore included only a few necessary dates and details, largely with-
out comment, and went on instead to lovingly describe Dunckerley’s entirely
hypothetical actions at the Siege of Quebec.41 Presented with Dunckerley’s his-
tory of appointments, naval historian Henry Dickinson confi rms that not only is
this pattern of service quite unusual, but that King’s Regulations mandated that
gunners could be moved only with their own specifi c consent; thus, he argues,
Dunckerley’s many moves were almost certainly made at his instigation. Dunck-
erley seems to have been a restless, perhaps diffi  cult, colleague – an observation
borne out by his later Masonic correspondence.42 Scrutiny of his assignments
reveals an underlying motivation – he seems not to have cared much for actu-
ally being at sea. Of the twenty or so years we can be certain Dunckerley was
in the navy, he spent around a quarter of that time in port, assigned to ships
‘in ordinary’ for refi tting. Furthermore, he stayed with two ships, HMS Crown
and HMS Eagle through extended stretches in ordinary, four years and one
year respectively, only to leave them as soon as they were returned to commis-
sion. Eight of his assignments to ships in commission were of less than twelve
months’ duration, and he only stayed with HMS Crown, Vanguard and Prince
for stretches of more than a year while they were at sea. Th us while the career of
a typical gunner might be measured in years spent with a given ship, in Dunck-

--- Sayfa 58 ---
Dunckerley all at Sea
45
erley’s case we must speak of months, while on two occasions his posting lasted
only a few days. He was, for example, with HMS Crown in April 1753 for only
twelve days aft er she was returned to commission, and he was with HMS Non-
such for less than a week in that same month.
How can we account for Dunckerley’s restlessness? If it were purely a matter
of not liking the water, he would surely have found a way to leave the navy. Since
he stayed, aft er his fashion, this is not a suffi  cient explanation. We know from
his later Masonic correspondence that he had a forceful personality, and was
fond of being in charge of any given situation. Given the limitations of his rank,
this character trait might not have worked well on board ship, where he had to
comply with the orders and tolerate the personality quirks of sometimes diffi  -
cult superior offi  cers. Additionally, there were strict social delineations between
warranted and commissioned offi  cers, and Dunckerley had no real prospect for
either fraternization or advancement beyond the position he occupied. Th omas
White, J. Grierson, Sadler and others surely overstate the case when they extol
Dunckerley’s ability to cultivate warm personal friendships with his superior
offi  cers – that opportunity simply did not arise.43 While the status of warrant
offi  cers in general rose signifi cantly during the eighteenth century, this was
not true for gunners, whom N. A. M. Rodger observes almost invariably came
up from the ranks of common seamen, and had no pretensions to gentility.44
Th ough being a gunner off ered neither high social status nor the opportunity to
rise into the commissioned ranks, it did promise tremendous job security as the
professional knowledge acquired by gunners made them quite valuable. Gunners
also had the opportunity to introduce their sons on shipboard as apprentices or
servants, to themselves or other offi  cers, including commissioned offi  cers, thus
giving the boys the chance to rise into the higher ranks from which they them-
selves were barred.45 Dunckerley seems to have done just that, as his son Th omas
Bolnest Dunckerley appears alongside him in the muster books of HMS Van-
guard and Guadaloupe.46
Th e social and intellectual fulfi lment Dunckerley found in his associational
life provides a clue to his restiveness within the strictures of naval service. Dur-
ing an extended period of time in ordinary at Portsmouth, between May 1749
and April 1753, Th omas Dunckerley became acquainted with Freemasonry, and
resolved to be initiated. He later claimed to have been initiated by Portsmouth
Lodge No. 21, later the Lodge of Antiquity. Th e date Dunckerley provides is 10
January 1754, and he claimed to have been exalted in the Royal Arch the same
year.47 Predictably, the January date does pose a problem because Dunckerley was
at sea aboard HMS Tyger until the end of March 1754. Furthermore, in 1793
he suggested that he was ‘passed to the Second Degree’ in 1747.48 Nonetheless,
sometime in 1754 is generally agreed upon by Masonic sources, and there is suf-

--- Sayfa 59 ---
46
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
fi cient documentation to place Dunckerley in and out of Portsmouth during
that year, so it will have to suffi  ce until better evidence presents itself.
Th is was a particularly tumultuous period in Dunckerley’s naval career. He
was attached to HMS Crown in June 1747, and followed the ship into ordinary
in May 1749. He and the ship remained in port until mid-April 1753, when the
ship was put back into commission. Faced with going to sea again, Dunckerley
immediately began searching for a more congenial post. His search took him
in fairly rapid succession from the Crown to the Nonsuch, Tygre, Vanguard and
Eagle. Th e last post brought him some comfort, as it placed him back on land for
a year, while the ship was refi tted. If we accept that he was initiated in 1754, then
he was made a Mason in the midst of this upheaval. Dunckerley’s initiation into
Freemasonry seems to have been a harbinger of greater stability. Ashore, he rap-
idly became a prominent Mason, and by 1756 he was the master of two lodges in
and around Plymouth, the Pope’s Head Tavern No. 134 (later the Prince George
Lodge) and the Masons’ Arms Tavern Lodge No. 67, erased in 1777.49
While this swift  progression to leadership pointed to his promise as a Free-
mason, it was Dunckerley’s subsequent founding of ‘sea lodges’, a novelty aboard
British ships of war, which marked him as a noteworthy Masonic innovator. Th e
next phase of Dunckerley’s naval career was characterized by what was for him
remarkable stability, notwithstanding its backdrop of the intense pressures of
the Seven Years’ War. Dunckerley returned to HMS Vanguard in September
1755. Th is was a ship he had fl ed the year before aft er only four months’ service,
when it was commanded by John Byron. Masonic historian William Moss sug-
gested Dunckerley disliked Byron, perhaps on account of his brother, William
Byron, whose grand mastership of the Modern Grand Lodge from 1747 to 1752
was tarnished with the probably exaggerated accusation that his neglect sub-
stantially contributed to the schism between the Ancients and Moderns.50 Now
commanded by Captain Robert Swanton, to whom Dunckerley referred as ‘that
good man’, the Vanguard proved Dunckerley’s favourite ship.51 He thrived under
Swanton’s leadership, and was rewarded by being appointed schoolmaster as well
as gunner, a move surely intended to give Dunckerley a bit more pay, though red
tape is reported to have delayed his payment by several years.52
Dunckerley stayed with HMS Vanguard, a third-rate ship of the line, for
three-and-a-half years, to January 1763, and during that time he made it the home
of the fi rst sea lodge, No. 254. On a brief return to England in 1760, Dunckerley
secured a most novel warrant from the Modern Grand Lodge, dated 16 January
1760 – the fi rst ever Grand Lodge dispensation for a lodge to be held on ship-
board. Th e constitution and fi rst annual return are duly recorded in the minute
book of the Modern Grand Lodge in May 1760, though what is missing is any
mention of Dunckerley’s alleged ‘power from the Grand [Lodge] of England to
inspect into the state of the Craft  wheresoever he might go’.53 Th is authority is

--- Sayfa 60 ---
Dunckerley all at Sea
47
widely referred to, without any substantiation in the record. While Dunck-
erley may indeed have carried such authority with him to Canada, there is
no record of it in the minute book, and no out-letter book survives for the
Grand Lodge from that time.54 Interestingly, a similarly broad dispensation
was granted to John Revis, a long-time Grand Offi  cer, in 1764, and this war-
rant is recorded in the appropriate place in the Grand Lodge minutes, on 23
April 1764. Whatever the offi  cial status of Dunckerley’s authority, he did act
as though he had been duly empowered, installing Simon Fraser as Provincial
Grand Master for (Upper) Canada in June 1760. Largely on the basis of this
ceremony, Dunckerley is oft en credited with securing Canada for the Mod-
ern Grand Lodge, as opposed to the Ancients, who had greater numerical
strength in the region. When the Vanguard returned from the relief of Que-
bec in 1761, Dunckerley made a prudent career move to the larger and more
prestigious HMS Prince. He also retained the Vanguard warrant for future
use. Dunckerley was apparently so pleased with the success of No. 254 that
he petitioned for another warrant, No. 279, dated 22 May 1762, to found a
lodge on the Prince.
Dunckerley ended his naval career on the Prince. Carrying ninety guns,
it was a second-rate ship of the line, and the largest he ever served as gunner.
As pensions were tied to the ranking of the last ship upon which an offi  cer
served, Dunckerley’s posting to the Prince qualifi ed him for a slightly larger
pension than would otherwise have been the case. He left  service in May 1763,
his pension commencing a year later, in June 1764. Dunckerley’s professional
expertise, service during war and his ability fi nally to achieve a good fi t with
his commanding offi  cers were rewarded with a pension of just over £45 per
annum – hardly a ‘princely’ sum, but better than nothing. Dunckerley was the
only gunner offi  cially retiring in 1764 to be granted a pension.55
Dunckerley reused the Prince warrant to establish a lodge on HMS Gua-
deloupe, when he sailed with Captain John Ruthven in 1765, allegedly to
escape arrest for debt. Again, there is a discrepancy in the dates for this voy-
age. Interestingly, the commonly repeated account of Dunckerley’s presence on
the Guadeloupe comes from the accepted narrative, which deviates fairly sub-
stantially from the details found in offi  cial Admiralty records. Th e accepted
narrative says that he left  ‘the kingdom’ in August 1764 aboard the Guadaloupe,
which was headed for the Mediterranean, and suggests he sailed as a guest. Th e
accepted narrative states, rather ambiguously, that along the way Dunckerley
‘had the happiness to be known to’ Lord William Gordon, who was travelling to
Minorca. Th e accepted narrative then skips to June 1765, when Dunckerley was
allegedly put ashore at Marseilles with a serious case of scurvy. Aft er convalesc-
ing, he was then supposed to have made his way to Minorca to wait on George,

--- Sayfa 61 ---
48
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Viscount Townshend, and thence back to Marseilles.56 However, according to
the Admiralty records, when Dunckerley signed on the Guadeloupe in January
1765 he was already at Mahon on Minorca, and on 13 April 1765, only three
months later, he was put ashore in Cadiz, Spain.57 Marseilles and Cadiz are not
close neighbours, and it is diffi  cult to reconcile the two accounts. If we acknowl-
edge that the accepted narrative is much less reliable than Admiralty records, we
are still left  to explain this bizarre little trip. How did he come to be in Mahon in
the fi rst place, and why did Ruthven put him off  at Cadiz?
When Dunckerley returned to England, probably in November 1765, he
retained the Prince warrant as well. Both warrants issued to Dunckerley for sea
lodges subsequently became part of the materiel of his rise to Masonic stardom
and like their master successfully made the transition to civilian life, becoming
London Lodge No. 108 and the Somerset House Lodge No. 279. A third sea
lodge, No. 224, established aboard HMS Canceaux sometime in 1762, cannot
be attributed to Dunckerley; however Sadler plausibly credits Dunckerley with
having been its inspiration.58 John Ross Robinson, the fi rst authoritative histo-
rian of Freemasonry in Canada, further argues that the lodge on the Canceaux
does not actually qualify as a sea lodge, because members met at convenient loca-
tions on land when the ship docked.59 Apparently no minute books survive for
any of the sea lodges.
Psychological history, or psychobiography, is badly out of fashion, and yet
it is tempting to fi nd a connection between Dunckerley’s commitment to Free-
masonry, fi rst with his ability to fi nally settle into sea service, and subsequently
to his ability to adapt to life on land with considerable dignity. For many years
his career was characterized by as much transience as service as a standing offi  cer
in the Royal Navy could accommodate. Dunckerley was not an enthusiastic
sailor – the Admiralty records show he contrived to stay in port as much as he
could. One could argue that he was a family man, and that he simply wanted
to stay close to home. But then why did he not leave the navy? And once out,
why did he pursue a Masonic path that required almost constant travel in order
to manage his provinces? It is also true that, with no particular connections or
education, his opportunity to become a gentleman was practically nonexistent.60
With Freemasonry as an anchor, a former seaman could, just possibly, manage
that transformation – especially if he had a very good yarn, and a couple of spare
lodge warrants in his pocket.

--- Sayfa 62 ---
– 49 –
4 DUNCKERLEY ASHORE
Given all the time Th omas Dunckerley actually spent ashore as an offi  cer in the
Royal Navy it seems a bit artifi cial to talk about his settling on land as something
novel. He had managed to have a relatively stable home and associational life
during much of his naval career, though some of the details are not entirely clear
to us. Dunckerley appears to have had a chequered marital history. Despite his
predilection for married life, he suff ered a spate of very bad luck. When Dunck-
erley married Ann Martin within the Rules of the Fleet Prison in December
1743, both bride and groom were listed as having been married before.1 Ann
herself presumably died shortly thereaft er, because Th omas married again in June
1745, this time at a tavern within the Rules of the Fleet Prison.2 Fleet marriages
were performed within the boundaries, or ‘Rules’, of the Fleet Prison.3 Unlike
modern prisons, the boundaries of the Fleet and many other such contempo-
rary institutions were permeable, with people coming and going, and claiming
their own peculiar habits of self-governance. Th e Fleet Prison was demolished
in 1846 and immediately became a fascination for nineteenth-century scholars,
who produced a series of books on the prison and its inmates. Th ere has been
no authoritative recent history of the prison; however, thorough studies of debt
and debtors like Finn’s Th e Character of Credit give a vivid picture of life within
the prison.4 Fortunately, Dunckerley was not in prison for debt, but just visiting
for a convenient wedding. Both of Dunckerley’s Fleet marriages were considered
clandestine under English law. Calling a marriage ‘clandestine’ could mean any
number of things in the eighteenth century. Generally, a marriage was defi ned as
clandestine if it was not held in the home parish of either the bride or groom, or
was not preceded by either reading of the banns or procuring a marriage licence.
According to the Report of the Royal Commission on the Laws of Marriage
(1868), up to a third of marriages performed in Great Britain before the Mar-
riage Act of 1753 were clandestine; many of these were conducted in the Fleet
Prison or other similarly liminal venues.5 Because of a quirk in enforcement, so-
called Fleet parsons could fl out marriage regulations with some impunity, and
they did.6 Th us within the Rules of the Fleet, most bets were off , and under cover
of relative anonymity, all sorts of marriages could be contracted for a fee.

--- Sayfa 63 ---
50
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Th e marriage that united Th omas Dunckerley and Hannah Somerset (b. c.
1712–1801) within the Rules of the Fleet Prison on 29 June 1745 was therefore
clandestine, but there is no reason to suspect any funny business.7 Both had been
married before and probably welcomed the convenience of a relatively anony-
mous ceremony. Th omas and Hannah, who later in life signed her name ‘Ann’,
were married by Walter Wyatt, one of the most notorious and prolifi c of the
marrying parsons in the Fleet. Th e ceremony was held in the Wheatsheaf Tavern
in Fleet Market, owned by Mr Boyce.8 In his notebook, Wyatt described the
bride and groom as ‘Th os Dunckerley Gent. Servt of Hanr wr & Hannah Somer-
set do widow at Boyces’. Being a gentleman’s servant was a step up from being a
groom, which is the occupation Dunckerley listed at the time of his 1743 mar-
riage to Ann Martin, but he aspired to be a gentleman. Whatever Dunckerley
told the parson, he must have thought of himself as something more respectable.
He had already been warranted a schoolmaster on shipboard for the Royal Navy,
making him a petty offi  cer, and he might thus with some justifi cation refer to
himself as a gentleman, and not merely the servant of one.9
However cryptic, the marriage register also tells us something about the
marital history of both parties. It was not unusual at that time for a woman in
her early thirties to have already been widowed and, in a 1788 letter, Dunckerley
refers to ‘my Wife’s Daughter of a former Husband’, indicating Hannah brought
at least one child with her to the marriage.10 What is somewhat surprising is
Wyatt’s observation that Dunckerley was himself a widower – and had already
been one two years prior when he married Ann Martin. Even with a presumed
birth date of 1720, he would be very young to have already been married three
times. Nothing else is known of his fi rst marriage, though it could not have
been much before 1740. Finally, one can never be too careful when verifying
Dunckerley’s dates, but a 1745 marriage accords nicely with his 1792 musings
about their upcoming fi ft ieth anniversary, recorded while he was presiding over
a Masonic cornerstone ceremony for All Saints Church in Southampton.11
Ann Siddall (1770–1842), a granddaughter of Hannah Dunckerley, relates
that besides her own mother, a daughter from Hannah’s fi rst marriage, Th omas
and Hannah Dunckerley had four children of their own, two dying young.12
No birth dates are recorded for any of the Dunckerley children, nor do we have
the names of the two who did not live. Son Th omas Bolnest Dunckerley was a
source of continual concern to his father, who complained he was a drain on the
perennially strained family fi nances.13 Dunckerley unsuccessfully attempted to
groom his son for a maritime career. We fi rst fi nd Th omas Bolnest in 1754 as
a boy of around seven, listed as a subscriber to a volume of poems published by
John Miller, a surgeon’s mate. Most of the other subscribers were similarly navy
men, and putting young Dunckerley’s name forward in that context was likely
intended to bring him to the attention of the offi  cers on the list.14 Th omas fi ls

--- Sayfa 64 ---
Dunckerly Ashore
51
appears along with his father on the muster of HMS Vanguard in 1757.15 Later
he appears on the muster of HMS Guadeloupe in 1764–5, at the same time the
elder Dunckerley was eluding arrest for debt. By 1764, the young man is listed as
being seventeen years of age, which would put his birth date right around 1747,
which fi ts nicely with his parents’ marriage in 1745.16
Dunckerley fi ls appears to have stayed with the navy aft er his father retired,
though the evidence is incomplete, and rather damning. Th e London Gazette
carried a notice in October 1781 concerning those imprisoned for debt who
might come under the provisions of ‘An Act for the Discharge of Certain Insol-
vent Debtors’. Amongst the other unfortunates we fi nd, ‘Th omas Dunckerley,
formerly of Plymouth in the County of Devon, late of the City of Bristol, Mari-
ner’.17 He was in trouble again in 1784, and was twice briefl y committed to the
workhouse in St Martin’s parish. Th e record is a bit confusing, but he does seem
to have run away, as the reason given for his second discharge was ‘absented’.18
He appeared to do no better down the road, taking advantage of his father’s
Masonic connections to beg charity from lodges. Bro. William Epps writes in
1786 from Canterbury:
About 14 days ago – a son of Brother Dunkerly was here just returned from Sea we
reliev’d him Liberally he said he had been shipreak’d and wanted to get home to his
father but to our suprize we saw him a few days aft er (as he said on his road to Deal –
believe him to be a loose one.)19
Th e senior Dunckerley himself says much more, with scarcely a personal letter
aft er 1780 omitting an unfl attering revelation about the son. Dunckerley com-
plained in 1783 that ‘I have an only Son whose folly & Extravagance have too
oft en aff ected my Heart, & drain’d my Purse’.20 In 1788 the exasperated father
wrote to George Rose asking for additional fi nancial assistance, noting: ‘My
Son, whose improvidence & dissipation for 25 years, has been very expensive to
me, and for whom I have suff er’d that grief & anxiety which a Parent can only
feel.’21 In addition to previous infractions, son Th omas had recently remarried, in
his father’s view, ‘very imprudently’.22 By then bride Anne Garrett and her groom
were mature in years, and both are described as having been widowed. In the
same letter Dunckerley also refers to his son’s employment at the Custom House,
but worried that this position was ‘precarious’, and that he was forced to sup-
ply a ‘weekly allowance from me for his Subsistance [sic]’.23 A state of perpetual
near-insolvency seems to have plagued both father and son, though the senior
Dunckerley consistently argued that his fi nancial straits were the fault of others,
while his son’s were due to vice and improvidence.
It is not clear what happened to Th omas Bolnest Dunckerley. In her notes,
Ann Siddall says only that he died impoverished, in St Giles, London. Th is is
elaborated on in an entry in the Freemasons’ Quarterly Review, which states:

--- Sayfa 65 ---
52
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Every means were tried, ineff ectually, to reclaim the wretched son. At his father’s
death, there being no provision left , he became a wanderer and an outcast. Being a
Mason, he was ever besieging Lodges and individuals. At last he became a bricklayer’s-
labourer, and was seen carrying a hod on his shoulder ascending a ladder! Th is poor
fellow’s misfortunes and misconduct at length terminated, and the grand-son of a king
died in a cellar in Saint Giles.24
Th is rather romantic lament is suspect, coming as it does from the Freemasons’
Quarterly Review. Th is journal was founded in 1834 by Robert Th omas Cruce-
fi x as a vehicle for his many Masonic projects.25 In the early 1840s, the revival
and refashioning of Th omas Dunckerley’s reputation was one such project. Th e
author of this sketch, ‘Th e Late Brother Th omas Dunckerley’, was the anony-
mous Fidus, a stalwart supporter of both Crucefi x and the man who became his
primary Masonic ideologue, George Oliver. All three men were prone to dra-
matic fl ourishes. In this case, surviving correspondence makes it clear that the
younger Dunckerley did not wait until his father’s death to rely on the charity of
unwary or sentimental Freemasons, and while he may have died in a cellar in St
Giles it seems unlikely that this was because his father left  no provision. Th omas
Dunckerley’s will does not mention his son – though as a fond parent, this is
almost without a doubt because the younger Th omas predeceased him. It seems
likely that he was the Th omas ‘Dunkley’ buried in the Nonconformist burying
ground, Bunhill Fields, on 23 August 1791.
Th e other known child of Th omas and Hannah was a daughter, Mary Ann,
who suff ered ill health in the 1760s, and whom Th omas Dunckerley describes as
requiring expensive medical treatment culminating in a leg amputation. In 1780,
Mary Ann married John Edgar, a prominent Freemason and apothecary from
Salisbury. Th eir marriage was celebrated at St Mary-le-Strand.26 Th e relation
between Dunckerley and his son-in-law was close, bound as they were by both
marriage and the Craft . Mary Ann died in February 1783, probably in childbed,
apparently without leaving issue.27 She was buried in the church of St Martin,
Salisbury.28 Th omas Dunckerley wrote in March 1783 that he had promised
his son-in-law that he would live for the rest of his life with him in Salisbury,
‘except a little time each summer, at Hampton Court’29 Edgar, however, also pre-
deceased him, dying in 1789.30 Widow Hannah Dunckerley died at Hampton
Court Place in February 1801, and was buried at the Chapel Royal, Savoy.31
Civilian Occupations
Th omas Dunckerley was deeply engaged in Freemasonry from at least the mid-
1750s until the end of his life. Because of his relative anonymity otherwise,
Dunckerley is barely noticed in broader historical studies, and features promi-
nently only in Masonic works. As an indication of the weight which he might

--- Sayfa 66 ---
Dunckerly Ashore
53
have given to Freemasonry relative to other aspects of his life, this is probably
misleading. Th e point Dunckerley’s Masonic biographers have consistently over-
looked is that, for him, life really was not all about being a ‘Masonic luminary’, or
even about being the son of a king. Th ese were either tactics or refuges, devised
as solutions to otherwise intractable problems. How do I feed my family? How
do I maintain some dignity? A careful reading of Dunckerley’s remaining corre-
spondence reveals both of these preoccupations with sometimes painful clarity.
Dunckerley’s fi ctionalized biography was an expression in equal parts of
aspiration and desperation. He was by his own admission a proud man, and
he aspired to accomplish in life what he believed his intelligence and merit
deserved. He wrote to George, Viscount Townshend in 1766:
I am far from desiring to be troublesome to the Royal Family; or by having a pension,
become a dead weight to my Country: even my present distress shall not compel me
to wish for that which (to my Conscience) doth not appear right. God hath been
pleased to bless me with common Sense; which I have for many years endeavor’d
to improve by Reading & Study, not having it in my power to get assistance from
Teachers. I should therefore be very happy if I could obtain any Employment in any
Department that is adequate to my poor abilities, and which would not depress me
beneath the character of a Gentleman.32
Dunckerley had in some respects a very fl exible conscience, so we should not
believe everything he says here. He was clearly hinting that a pension would be
welcome. However, this is another instance in which he openly states what his
later actions reveal to be true. He would willingly accept a pension, but what he
was really aft er was employment that allowed him to live like a gentleman.
Dunckerley felt very keenly the class boundaries and his lack of social con-
tacts that persistently came between him and his professional ambitions. A
concomitant worry was how to provide for his family. Given these twin realities,
we need to understand that the primary object of his fi ctional biography was to
secure a government position that would boost him socially. In 1763 this must
have truly seemed like an insurmountable obstacle, not just for Dunckerley, but
for all other social aspirants across the middling ranks. Consider how frequently
actual and fi ctional reinventions appear in eighteenth-century literature. Two
famously popular novels, Th e History of Tom Jones by Henry Fielding and Daniel
Defoe’s Moll Flanders, come to mind. Th ere are particularly interesting similari-
ties between the ‘hidden’ history Dunckerley invents for himself, and the plot
of Fielding’s Tom Jones, published in 1749. Not only is Tom revealed as being of
higher social standing than he previously suspected, but the virtuous characters
he encounters in the novel tend to be strongly Anglican and pro-Hanoverian,
like Dunckerley himself. Th e picaresque nature of Tom’s journey on the way to
his true position also resembles the story Dunckerley relates of his Masonic jour-
ney through the Mediterranean and France. Intended or accidental, personal

--- Sayfa 67 ---
54
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
reinvention was as genuine a form of social rebellion as one person could man-
age unaided, and the theme had broad appeal.
Two fascinating, if random, examples of actual eighteenth-century self-inven-
tion suggest how rich this genre must be. Th e fi rst is Tom Bell, who fl ourished
in America between 1738 and the early 1780s. Bell was a shape-shift er, who
wandered the colonies posing fi rst as this gentleman, and then that one. As he
