travelled, Bell perfectly mimicked the conversation, manners and dress of the gen-
tlemen he pretended to be, simultaneously reinforcing stereotypes of the gentry
while undermining their social and moral authority through his own bad behav-
iour. Once Bell imitated a noted preacher so convincingly that the poor man,
the Revd John Rowland, was prosecuted for theft s Bell perpetrated while using
his assumed identity.33 Th e second example is the Revd Samuel Peters, a Loyalist
from Connecticut.34 Peters was an active Freemason, and was probably person-
ally acquainted with Dunckerley from his involvement in the Knights Templar
while he was in exile in London. Peters was a prolifi c writer who published a
number of interesting works, the best-known and most infamous of which was
a fabricated genealogy, A History of the Rev. Hugh Peters: With an Appendix.35
Peters was apparently not suffi  ciently impressed by his actual ancestry, and so
wrote this purely fi ctitious version, tracing his decent from Anglo-Norman
lords, and connecting himself to the famous puritan divine, the Revd Hugh
Peters (1598–1660). Th is fantastic account gained credence, as demonstrated
by newspaper accounts from the 1780s.36 Peters further enhanced his personal
credentials by claiming, in addition to his genuine and genuinely impressive Yale
BA (1757) and MA (1760), and honorary MA from King’s College, Colum-
bia University (1761), to have earned an LLD from the fi ctitious University
of Cortona.37 He subsequently claimed that his grandson Samuel Peters Jarvis
(1792–1857) had so impressed the ‘emperor’ of the Mississaga tribe that he was
made a prince of the royal house. Peters said that Masonic brother Chief Joseph
Brant (1743–1807) had drawn up an offi  cial deed, giving the boy extensive lands
‘with a view to enable the young prince to live according to his exaltation’.38 In
1817, when he was well into his eighties, Peters headed west towards Minnesota
by canoe, hoping to take physical possession of the lands he wanted to unite
as ‘Petersylvania’.39 Willard Keyes, his much younger but nonetheless exhausted
travelling companion, left  an astonishing account of the voyage, which took
them as far as the confl uence of the Wisconsin and Mississippi rivers.40
Unlike Bell who was a thief, and Peters who was arguably at least a little
insane, Dunckerley’s goal was honest employment. Had he been content with
idleness combined with even modest affl  uence, he could have stayed at home
and lived very well on the £200 pension he did receive, or even enjoyed some
degree of security on the £100 to which he admitted.41 His was, however, a busy
personality and he needed respectable work. Th at is why Dunckerley took up

--- Sayfa 68 ---
Dunckerly Ashore
55
the study of law in 1770. It was not because he thought either law or study itself
were interesting, or a pleasant diversion. Dunckerley admitted that he was unac-
customed to schooling, and found it diffi  cult. On the contrary, his attraction
to law was purely practical. Someone, he does not divulge who, promised him
a government position if he acquired the necessary legal qualifi cations. Dunck-
erley’s legal studies and militia service are both saluted in White and Grierson’s
1793 biographical sketch, and are subsequently repeated in dozens of Masonic
biographies:
Possessing a strong active mind, with an easy fl uent delivery, he was advised in the
year 1770, to become a student in the law, and during fi ve years close application,
acquired such a fund of legal knowledge, that, in Michaelmas term 1774, he was
called to the bar by the honourable Society of the Inner Temple; but being fond of an
active life, and still animated by a thirst for glory, when the court of France became
hostile to this country in supporting American Independence, and an invasion was
threatened, he accepted a commission in the South Hampshire regiment of militia,
where he greatly distinguished himself during three years of service.42
Dunckerley himself tells a more pragmatic and, frankly, pathetic story. He seems
to have considered taking up a legal career as early as 1750, when he wrote an
essay for the Sober Society of Portsmouth on the happiness and virtue of a pub-
lic versus a private life. Dunckerley exclaimed:
What! Shall a Man who is by Natural Accomplishments, and a good Education
Qualifi ed to plead for Justice, and help those to Right that suff er wrong, shall he I say
desert that Glorious Cause unto which God has been pleas’d to call him and prefer
his own Philosophic Ease to that of the Oppres’d Many? No. Let him consider he has
a Talent committed to his care, which it is his Duty to Improve.43
Dunckerley had reason to believe he had the natural ability, and by the early
1770s he was encouraged to improve it by enrolling himself at the Inner Temple
to study law. In a lost letter, Dunckerley confi ded his aspirations to his friend
James Heseltine who responded, ‘I hope you are happily situated and most sin-
cerely wish your present plan of operations in the Law may answer your most
pleasing expectations.’44
According to Inner Temple records, Dunckerley was admitted between 18
November 1769 and 17 November 1770, paying £3 6s 8d as a matriculation
fee. He must have taken admission to the Inner Temple as a happy omen. Th e
Inner Temple is one of the Inns of Court most closely associated with the Tem-
ple Church, which was built by the Knights Templar in the twelft h century. Th e
church occupied a prominent place in Dunckerley’s psychological landscape –
he became the fi rst Grand Master of the Masonic Knights Templar in England,
and in his 1795 will he requested to be buried in the church, near the Knights
Templar tombs.

--- Sayfa 69 ---
56
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Dunckerley seems to have been a good student, and was nominated to be a
reader to give sermons at Lyons Inn, a position reserved for talented barristers
who were seen as likely future Masters of the Bench.45 One of the most amusing
anecdotes about Dunckerley’s appearance comes from this time. Th is vignette,
perhaps apocryphal, appeared in the Gentleman’s Magazine aft er Dunckerley’s
death:
Whoever has seen the late Mr Dunckerley will testify the exceeding great likeness
he bore to the diff erent branches of the royal family, insomuch that, when at dinner
with the students in the Inner Temple Hall, of which society he was a member, he was
always addressed by the title of Highness.46
However, like most young men who sought admission to the Inns of Court,
Dunckerley did not intend to practise law. In the eighteenth century, a stint at
legal study was seen as suitable preparation for all sorts of employment, and as
a good place to make friendships that could blossom into business or political
partnerships.47 As Dunckerley explained to William Hussey, he did not want to
merely rely on the royal pension, which was insuffi  cient to his needs in any case,
but rather that ‘I wish to serve my Country in any Department where I can be
useful’. Legal studies seemed a promising fi rst step.
Th us from 1770 to late 1774, Dunckerley devoted himself to mastering the
law, even though study at the Inns of Court was a young man’s game. Th ough he
complained about the expense, he persevered, and was called to the bar on 10
February 1775, having kept his obligatory twelve terms in the Inn’s commons.48
To his dismay, the job he was sure he had been promised failed to materialize.
He was betrayed again, just as when he did not get the promised position at the
Royal Naval Academy at Portsmouth.
Extant records off er no clue about who promised Dunckerley a position, or
what that position might have been, but he was bitterly disappointed. A few
years later Dunckerley explained to Hussey about his sojourns into the law and
the militia, detailing the fi nancial side of both decisions. Th e letter survives, and
provides a fascinating counterpoint to the panegyrics of Dunckerley’s biogra-
phers. In it, Dunckerley laments to Hussey that he became a barrister ‘having
been promised an employment in the Law Department; ... that promise was
never perform’d’.49 Dunckerley’s explanation for why he ultimately did not get
that job or any other paying employment is typical, in that he defl ects responsi-
bility. He writes, ‘I confess it was imprudent to lessen my little Income, in order
to gain professional knowledge, from which I have obtain’d no advantage; being
destitute of Patronage.’ It was always a problem of patronage.
To make matters worse, Dunckerley tells Hussey that in his next career move,
he chose to accept a commission in the South Hampshire Militia. Th is was ‘from
Principle’. Nonetheless, Dunckerley complains that his three years of service

--- Sayfa 70 ---
Dunckerly Ashore
57
left  him £200 in debt.50 If he could not aff ord to be in the militia, why did he
join? Dunckerley attests it was because he believed England was under threat of
French invasion aft er 1777.51 Th is was a widespread and legitimate concern, caus-
ing the government to call up, or ‘embody’, the militia for the fi rst time since the
end of the Seven Years’ War. Th ousands of gentleman offi  cers and their public-
minded neighbours took up the call and mustered at a series of encampments in
the summer and autumn of 1778, to receive military training. Th us volunteering
for the home defence was what a patriotic gentleman should do. Dunckerley’s
patriotism had never been called into question, but his status as a gentleman
might need some bolstering aft er the disappointing outcome of his legal studies.
One became a gentleman by acting like one, and by being befriended by gen-
tlemen. Rubbing uniformed shoulders with them surely could not hurt. At the
time, an anonymous 1778 writer from the camp at Coxheath, in Kent, pointedly
remarked:
Among other clubs, we have one of the Free-Masons, where the celebrated Lieut.
Dunkerly presides as Grand Master; you know Dunkerly, I presume; he has been long
distinguished for his merit and misfortunes; born of Royal blood, he has spent near
forty years in the service of his country, but from some unaccountable cause, is only
Lieutenant still!52
Th at threw Dunckerley’s own words back in his face – such merit, such bad luck, so
virtuous! Embarrassingly, Dunckerley seems to have actually worked his way up to
a lieutenancy, having joined as an ensign. He appears as ‘Ensign Dunckerley’ on the
subscription list for An Universal Military Dictionary, written by another military
man and colorful Masonic fi gure, Captain George Smith.53 Even Chudley com-
ments that although Dunckerley was proud of his militia commission, describing
himself as ‘Lieutenant’ on the dispensation he granted to Medina Lodge on the Isle
of Wight in 1779, it would be ‘rather fanciful to reiterate that “he greatly distin-
guished himself”’ as reported by White and Grierson.54
Extract of a Letter from the Camp at Coxheath
Occasionally a stray piece of ephemera allows the historian to see, however
briefl y, into the complicated back-story that was the real texture of our subject’s
everyday life. Th ere is more to the timing and content of Th omas Dunckerley’s
1783 letter to William Hussey than immediately meets the eye. True, Dunck-
erley was in debt and anxious about whether he could manage to retrieve the
royal pension he had temporarily lost due to Civil List reforms – but he was also
terribly concerned about his reputation. It seems odd that a man whose main
stock in trade was, let us be frank, one stupendous lie, should be so preoccupied
with virtue. Yet, his insistence on a very particular moral code was central to
Dunckerley’s conscientious pursuit of self-improvement. We can see this as early

--- Sayfa 71 ---
58
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
as 1750, when his essays for the Sober Society naturally emphasized sobriety;
but also loyalty to God and king, neighbourliness, politeness, public service and,
of most relevance here, ‘decency and virtue’.55 True to form, Dunckerley returns
to this theme in his letter to Hussey, ‘God is my witness … I have not known Vice
or Dissipation’.56 He uses this exact phrasing repeatedly in his correspondence,
and he seems to mean that he particularly did not drink, gamble or chase loose
women. But why should Dunckerley insist so emphatically, and at this precise
point in the letter, on his virtue? Th e context is important, so let’s look at a more
extended excerpt from the letter, putting the pieces back together in the right
order:
I was call’d to the Bar, eight years ago; having been promis’d an employment in the
Law Department: but that promise was never perform’d. I accepted a Commission
in the Militia, from Principle: believing at that time this Kingdom would have been
invaded.
     God is my witness … I have not known Vice or Dissipation.
Th is connection between the militia and virtue is not accidental. Dunckerley’s
commanding offi  cer in the South Hampshire Militia was Sir Richard Worsley. In
1782, Worsley accused his wife, Lady Seymour Dorothy Fleming, of infi delity,
and sued her lover, Captain Maurice George Bisset, for criminal conversation,
demanding £20,000 in damages. What made matters infi nitely worse was that
Bisset was Worsley’s neighbour and his subordinate in the militia. Th e court
found Bisset guilty, but because of Worsley’s alleged complicity in permitting
this aff air and others like it to go on under his very nose, he was awarded only
one shilling. Much of the naughtiness, it turned out, went on under Duncker-
ley’s nose as well, as he served in the militia from 1778 to 1780 at the same time
as the adulterous pair disported themselves across the militia encampment at
Coxheath. Th ey were not the only ones having a bit too much of a good time,
however. Both scholarly and popular studies of the Worsley aff air dwell on the
voluptuous decadence of the Coxheath encampment.57 McCreery’s study in
particular focuses on the dilemma posed by Sir Richard Worsley’s failure as a
husband and as a commanding offi  cer, to keep order and enforce conduct and
values appropriate to leaders of the kingdom’s social, political and military elites.
His failings and the humiliation he suff ered at the hands of both his wife and
the courts were seen as ominous symptoms of the moral decay of Britain’s mili-
tary preparedness and aristocratic governors. Th e militia, it became obvious, was
much more interested in glamorous uniforms, novel entertainments and sexual
adventure than in defending the country against very real French threats.
Dunckerley was in the thick of it, and it is no wonder he went into debt
again, though he was probably rarely solvent for long. He claims to have
accepted a commission – and he appears fi rst as an ensign when the militia was

--- Sayfa 72 ---
Dunckerly Ashore
59
embodied in 1778, then as a lieutenant. Even without the expense of having to
purchase a commission Dunckerley was expected to provide all his fi eld equip-
ment out of his own purse. Th en there was the matter of the uniform, which was
not inexpensive. It was a handsome aff air, as we can tell from the portraits Sir
Joshua Reynolds painted of Sir Richard and Lady Worsley in matching militia
costumes. Hers, dated around 1779, is a particularly fi ne example of what was
then current fashion amongst offi  cers’ wives – a very alluring feminine adap-
tation of her husband’s regimental uniform. Lady Worsley appears in a bright
scarlet uniform in the form of a riding habit, with an extravagantly plumed black
hat, an imperious expression on her face, gripping a riding whip. She must have
made quite an impression on the encampment.
Th e busy social life of the encampment was reported the newspapers and
caught the imagination of the reading public, who clamoured for more details.
Offi  cers and their wives set up lavish pavilions furnished with carpets and silver,
and hosted balls, card parties and dinners. Rubenhold comments that during
the height of the summer season, the encampment would have seemed more like
a pleasure garden than a military training ground.58 Newspapers ran columns
carrying news of the various encampments, including Coxheath, which was the
largest and which became an attraction for Londoners looking for a day-long
excursion. It is in such a feature, titled ‘Extract of a Letter from the Camp of
Coxheath’ that we fi nd Dunckerley, and though he could not have been pleased
with his treatment, at least he was in the same column as ‘the review of this camp
by his Majesty’.59 Dunckerley appears further down the column, in an entry that
we have seen before: ‘you know Dunckerley, I presume; he has been long distin-
guished for his merit and misfortunes’, going on to allude to his presumed royal
birth and surprising lack of rank. He was having a good time, however. True
to form, it appears Dunckerley has established a Masonic lodge: ‘Among other
clubs, we have one for the Free-Masons, where the celebrated Lieut. Dunkerly
presides as Grand Master.’ Th is lodge does not appear in John Lane’s Masonic
Records 1717–1886, and so was presumably some sort of occasional lodge. Th is
is fascinating – it shows Dunckerley in what must have seemed his most natural
milieu. He is in uniform – all of his favourite activities involved uniforms and
titles. Sadly, gunners did not have the opportunity to wear distinctive uniforms
until 1787, well aft er Dunckerley’s time.60 In lodge meetings, he would have
worn his Masonic regalia on top of his scarlet coat with its dark blue lapels and
silver-worked buttonholes. Very attractive, indeed. Th is episode shows Dunck-
erley’s genius – he has taken what must have been a rather foreign context, fi lled
with gentlemen amongst whom he was otherwise nothing special, and in it cre-
ated a space within which he was reassuringly both in command as Worshipful
Master, and in a brotherhood of equals.

--- Sayfa 73 ---
60
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Comeuppance
Th e glory of the militia encampment came at a steep price, hence Dunckerley’s
letter to Hussey a few months down the road. Th e irony of his situation clearly
weighed on Dunckerley – everything he did to improve his economic and social
standing ultimately made things worse. He was in debt, could not manage to
leverage any personal infl uence, and he was lampooned in the press. Part of his
misery was circumstantial – he wrote this letter to Hussey as new legislation was
being implemented which abolished the king’s secret funds and instituted the
Civil List. Dunckerley lost his pension from George III as a result, which made
him glum indeed. An examination of Dunckerley’s plea to Hussey demonstrates
just how perilous the situation was, as we follow Dunckerley into the Byzantine
world of Georgian fi nance. Th e account, convoluted as it is, deserves a full retell-
ing. Dunckerley prefaces the explanation with a confession that the expenses he
laid out for legal studies and maintaining a militia commission were more than
he could support:
To defray those Expenses, I was necessitated to sell my Annuity from the Privy Purse,
with forty Pounds per Annum of my Allowance at the Treasury; by a late Act of
Parliament that Allowance is now paid at the Exchequer, where they have deducted
7 ½ percent an; and I am now under the necessity of paying 5 percent an Interest on
having it advanced (for my Subsistence) by Anticipation: the payment being in the
third quarter of Arrear. Th us my income is reduced to £135 per ann m; and I am £200
in debt.
Despite the complex turns of Dunckerley’s accounts, this much seems clear: he
sold part of his pension for immediate cash, was insulted by bureaucratic fees on
the remainder, and paid interest to have sums advanced in anticipation of pay-
ment. Despite all that juggling he was in debt, and for a man without patronage
debt was perilous.
Curiously, for someone who claimed to be a natural son of a king, Dunck-
erley frequently complains that his professional aspirations led to fi nancial ruin
precisely because he was ‘destitute of Patronage’.61 He began his quest for rec-
ognition and preferment in 1766 with the lament that, ‘I am unacquainted at
Court, and know not how to gain access…’62 Even aft er what Dunckerley chose
to call royal ‘recognition’ in 1767, he was persistently frustrated by insuffi  cient
patronage. Dunckerley was clearly fl ummoxed. Th is was an outcome he had not
foreseen. How could the allegedly acknowledged son of a king, albeit a bastard,
be so destitute of infl uential friends willing to be of material assistance? And
yet, Dunckerley repeatedly and plausibly makes that out to be the case. On the
one hand, the situation in which Dunckerley persistently found himself recon-
fi rms the conclusion that George III declined to recognize Dunckerley’s claim to
royal blood. On the other hand, this also provides us with a much more nuanced

--- Sayfa 74 ---
Dunckerly Ashore
61
picture of Dunckerley’s naivety. He fundamentally misunderstood the social
dynamics that dictated that even the bastard sons of kings were supposed to be
the source of patronage and infl uence, not the benefi ciaries of them. Sir Edward
Walpole had told George III the truth about Dunckerley’s humble birth – and if
the unfl attering accounts that appeared in the papers at the time of Dunckerley’s
death are any indication, Sir Edward also shared his opinion with anyone who
asked. Th erefore the people who controlled the reins of patronage knew that
Dunckerley was an interloper. Perhaps had he not been awarded a royal pension
and latterly gained the protection of the princes, the sneers would have become
even more audible during his lifetime.

--- Sayfa 76 ---
– 63 –
5 THE TRAPPINGS OF ROYALTY
Th ough becoming a powerful force within Freemasonry was not Dunckerley’s
original life goal, he embraced it as a vocation. With no gainful employment,
ever, aft er 1764, Freemasonry presented itself as an engaging way to occupy him-
self. With no public rank, it enabled him to demonstrate his merit and achieve
status and authority. Having rejected his actual family, Freemasonry became his
new brotherhood. Eventually, Dunckerley even came to envision Freemasonry as
a creative outlet, a place he could make his own reality, one expressed in properly
arranged orders and degrees. Th us, even though the expenses associated with
Freemasonry contributed mightily to his constant fi nancial complaints, Dunck-
erley devoted the last thirty years of his life to the Craft  for which he developed
such heartfelt aff ection and respect. As much as Dunckerley embraced Freema-
sonry during his life, in the centuries since his death Freemasons have embraced
the heroic mannequin he invented.
If not for Freemasonry, Th omas Dunckerley would have been an obscure
historical fi gure indeed. It is unclear how early or how widely his spurious
birth story was disseminated beyond Dunckerley’s own mailing lists. Th e claim
appears in the press, casually, by the late 1770s, and the very fi rst public men-
tion may be the mocking notice about the occasional lodge Dunckerley held at
Coxheath. Th e eighteenth-century press was not known for its discretion, thus
its silence about Dunckerley’s alleged recognition is a persuasive argument that
Dunckerley was not entirely forthcoming about the reason George III awarded
him a pension. At this juncture, revisiting the 1796 version of the accepted nar-
rative, together with an examination of its importance to Masonic scholars,
would be helpful. Th e denouement of the fi nal instalment of the accepted nar-
rative is George III’s recognition of Dunckerley as an illegitimate son of George
II. All good things in Dunckerley’s later life might be seen to proceed from that.
Dunckerley acquired a modest title, ‘Esquire’, assumed a coat of arms based upon
those of his royal father, was awarded a very welcome pension, eventually was
assigned better apartments in Hampton Court Palace and, most important, he
fi nally commanded the respect he deserved. As with so many other Dunckerley
‘facts’, these are a good deal less factual than they might seem at fi rst blush. Still,

--- Sayfa 77 ---
64
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
George III’s recognition, sometimes termed ‘acknowledgement’, has a fi rm date
attached: 7 May 1767.
Th e Importance of a Patron
We are faced with a rather thin and contradictory trail of evidence to guide us
through the carefully, even evasively, worded statements Dunckerley provides
to demonstrate that his claim of royal paternity had George III’s endorsement.
Everything he says about being recognized is problematic because, lacking any
reliable independent verifi cation of Dunckerley’s acknowledgement; we have
no plausible evidence for this hoped-for event taking place. Given the emphasis
placed on royal recognition by both Dunckerley and his admirers, it is striking
that, aside from entries of his name in the Secret Service records from 1779 to
1782, the Royal Archives contain no notice of Dunckerley at all. Even the 7
May 1767 date he features in the accepted narrative cannot be verifi ed as hav-
ing any factual signifi cance, because Secret Service accounts remain only from
1779. Th us, from an archival point of view, Dunckerley was a nonentity, a mere
hiccup in royal fi nances. Surely this is the most damning lack of evidence yet – if
Dunckerley had been acknowledged as a royal off spring, he would undoubtedly
have appeared in the record as more than a small annual expense.1
However, each piece of questionable evidence Dunckerley presents elo-
quently demonstrates the vital importance of patronage. Every stage of his
quest for recognition and preferment involves the active intervention of actual
or constructed patrons. It is diffi  cult to emphasize just how critical all sorts of
patronage and infl uence were to people of virtually every social rank during the
early modern period. In the days before the widespread adoption of professional
standards, civil service exams or even of ‘merit’ as an organizational concept, the
everyday workings of social interactions, business, the government, the church
and even the military, all relied on patronage. Th is was not the straightforward
sort of patronage we associate with commissions granted to artists, musicians
and architects. Rather, who you knew, or who you could convince to be of ser-
vice to you, was vital to negotiating everyday situations.
We have already seen Dunckerley benefi t from and ultimately reject Sir
Edward Walpole’s eff ective, faithful and socially conservative patronage. He was
eff usive about Walpole in 1753:
When I refl ect on your continued kindness to me for this twelve years past, & that all
my preferments in the Royal Navy proceeded from your Recommendations; when I
consider how small a claim I have to your friendship, how little merit to plead … I say,
Sir, when I consider what small pretensions I have to yr favour, it is with the greatest
reluctance I sit down to write to you; lest by so many solicitations I should at last
off end so good a patron.2

--- Sayfa 78 ---
Th e Trappings of Royalty
65
But ask he did, and eventually Dunckerley mightily off ended his good patron.
Bray tells us that when Dunckerley turned to Walpole to substantiate his ‘rev-
elation’ of royal parentage, Walpole was incensed, and their relationship was
destroyed. Bray writes:
Sr. E was so off ended at his eff rontery in appealing to him for ye truth of a story, wch
Sr. E so well knew to be false, that he ordered his servt never to let him in, & did not
see him for 3 or 4 years.3
Th us by mid-1767, Dunckerley had severed his personal ties to Sir Edward,
though the lasting benefi ts of Walpole’s patronage continued to benefi t Dunck-
erley for the rest of his life. Th is rupture may possibly have been unintended,
but the eff ect was serious. At the same time, however, Dunckerley had already
constructed a new family patron, the Duke of Devonshire, and enshrined the
Cavendish family in the accepted narrative as the Dunckerley family’s estab-
lished patron. But perhaps Dunckerley only wanted to hedge his bets. Having
several patrons was surely better than only having one. Even fabricated relation-
ships could be useful. Th ose long lists of gentlemen Dunckerley had the pleasure
of ‘being made known to’ indicate his sensitivity to that reality. So, in the 1780s,
Dunckerley appears to have created yet another important patron, one with
whom he claimed to have been on intimate terms for decades.
Dunckerley’s earliest identifi ed reference to Philip Dormer Stanhope, the
4th Earl of Chesterfi eld, is in that very revealing 1783 letter to William Hussey.
Th e account is rather subdued, coming as it does at the same time as Duncker-
ley’s loss of the royal pension which had itself formerly been his best proof of
recognition. He writes:
His Majesty was most graciously pleas’d in consideration of my being a natural Son
of the late King (which Lord Chesterfi eld authenticated) to grant me a pension from
the Privy Purse; but by an unhappy mistake of Mr Worsley, it was no more than One
Hundred Pounds per Annum; nor could I obtain any addition for several years;
but when my Case was (by the friendship of Mr Robinson and Mr Brummel) laid
before Lord North, His Lordship was pleas’d to order money for my relief at diff erent
times; and obtain’d His Majesty’s permission to put me on a list of the Treasury for an
annual Bounty of two hundred Pounds.4
Here the acknowledgement is termed a ‘consideration’, which we might inter-
pret as a soft ening of the claim. Still, the critical information is there: Th e King
gave Dunckerley a pension because Chesterfi eld ‘authenticated’ that he was a
natural son of George II. Th is is the fi rst time Chesterfi eld appears in an active
role in the accepted narrative, and his presence is curious. As a young man, Lord
Chesterfi eld was closely associated with both Sir Robert Walpole and the future
George II. In 1733, Chesterfi eld married an illegitimate daughter of George
I, Petronilla Melusine von der Schulenburg, Countess of Walsingham (1693–

--- Sayfa 79 ---
66
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
1778). Presumably, if anyone other than Sir Edward Walpole might have had
an informed opinion about whether or not it would be possible for George II
to be Dunckerley’s father, it would have been Chesterfi eld. He possessed sev-
eral other characteristics that Dunckerley favoured in his dramatis personae. By
1783, Chesterfi eld was safely deceased, having died in 1773. He had impeccable
Masonic credentials. He was initiated into Freemasonry in 1721 at the King’s
Arms Tavern, took part in several noble ‘makings’, and was an associate of John
Th eophilus Desaguliers.5 Finally, Chesterfi eld had an adored illegitimate son,
Philip Stanhope, for whom he wrote Letters to his Son.6 In fi xing on Chester-
fi eld as an ally, one suspects Dunckerley was subconsciously holding him up
as a model father – just the sort an illegitimate son needed. We probably see
this same type of projection at work in Dunckerley’s previous attachments to
Sir Edward Walpole and his illegitimate son, Major Edward Walpole, both of
whom Dunckerley appealed to for support in seeking recognition of his illegiti-
macy. Chesterfi eld also appears on the list of well-placed offi  cers and gentlemen
to whom Dunckerley appealed in 1766. White and Grierson report that he
‘befriended’ Dunckerley in 1767.7 Curiously, they also list Sir Edward Walpole
as befriending Dunckerley in 1767, though that relationship, never properly a
friendship, turned sharply south just about then. Nonetheless, this later appeal
to Chesterfi eld’s ghost is surprising. He is mentioned briefl y in the fi nal 1796
accepted narrative, but in tandem with Sir Edward Walpole. Dunckerley writes
that when presented with Mary Dunckerley’s declaration, ‘His majesty read it,
seemed much concerned, and commanded that an inquiry should be made of
my character from Lord C. and Sir E. W. who had known me from my infancy.’8
It is not entirely clear whether Dunckerley meant that both men had known him
from infancy, or only Sir Edward. Perhaps the ambiguity is intentional.
Whichever the case, Dunckerley’s attempt to fashion a patron where one prob-
ably did not exist is curious. Moss, for one, is very dubious about the connection
and especially about the very odd ‘Chesterfi eld Letters’ produced by Dunckerley
at the end of his life. Dunckerley claimed these were copies of letters he sent to
Chesterfi eld at the nobleman’s specifi c request, while Dunckerley was on HMS
Crown in the late 1740s. Dunckerley’s letters discussing Gibraltar, Minorca and
Leghorn appeared in the Freemasons’ Magazine in June, August and October
1794, and he apparently had intended to print more.9 Th ese three places also show
up in Dunckerley’s 1765 sojourn in the Mediterranean, and one wonders whether
it was actually the later trip which actually provides the material for the sketches.
It was aft er all during 1765 and 1766 that Dunckerley composed the accepted
narrative, and he was at that time casting about for another, more suitable early
patron. In his handling of the letters, Sadler off ers helpful suggestions as to how
Chesterfi eld might have known the young gunner, but none of them is particularly
convincing. He muses, ‘was it possible that the noble lord, who was thoroughly

--- Sayfa 80 ---
Th e Trappings of Royalty
67
well versed in the Court intrigues and gossip of the period, could have been then
in possession of the secret of Dunckerley’s parentage’.10 Not likely, since there was
no actual secret. Moss is especially unconvinced, and argues that the story behind
these letters, appearing nearly fi ft y years aft er they were allegedly written, just does
not hold water.11 If the letters were composed in the 1740s and sent to the earl,
they either did not arrive, or subsequently vanished. Dunckerley appears nowhere
in the voluminous surviving Chesterfi eld correspondence.12
Becoming a Gentleman
Equally intriguing as Dunckerley’s adoption of yet another questionable patron
are the ancillary attributes of, if not royalty, then at least a signifi cant rise in
social status, which he assumes aft er 1767. First comes the very modest little
bit of a title. Upon surveying the sources then available, Sadler concludes that
Dunckerley began calling himself ‘Esq.’ in 1767, as a result of the fact that ‘King
George III, having on the 7th of May, formally acknowledged him as a natural
son of his predecessor on the Th rone of England’.13 What Sadler does not men-
tion is that esquires had become ubiquitous by the second half of the eighteenth
century, and one more or less would not have made much of a ripple. John
Chamberlayne, writing at the beginning of the eighteenth century, equated the
use of ‘Esquire’ with being a gentleman – and one was a gentleman if he could
pass for one.14 Along with the appropriate equipage and lifestyle, the middling
classes also took personal virtue as a qualifi cation for gentility – something that
would surely have resonated with Dunckerley’s perception of himself.15 Th is
intentional blurring of the boundary between commoners and the lowest ranks
of aristocracy was not merely an English phenomenon. Contemporaries on the
Continent, especially in France, believed that false claims to noble descent were
on the rise, especially with the surreptitious assumption of ‘de’ as part of one’s
surname.16 Michael Bush lays at least part of the blame on the permissiveness of
government at every level. With ever more pressing demands, offi  cials simply
turned a blind eye to the illegal assumption of noble titles.17
Still, ‘Esquire’ actually had a very specifi c meaning, a legal defi nition. One
was not supposed to simply put it on like a new suit of clothes. We fi nd a won-
derful fulmination against the informal and unwarranted assumption of the use
of Esq. in the Freemasons’ Magazine, of all places, in 1793. Here, just a few pages
away from White’s and Grierson’s panegyric of Th omas Dunckerley, Esq., the
anonymous author of ‘On the Title of Esquire’ complains that in the modern
usage, even ‘Tradesmen and mechanics, of all descriptions, confer this title on
each other’, while in reality, only the King can confer the title, and only does so
under certain circumstances.18 Tellingly, SIMPLEX argues, based on his read-
ings of jurists William Camden and Sir Edward Coke, that all these are public

--- Sayfa 81 ---
68
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
and legal conferrals, well-documented, and are accompanied by a formal grant
of arms. In other words, a well-mannered person did not simply assume the title.
Keen observers amongst Dunckerley’s circle of correspondents might indeed
have noted that some time aft er his alleged royal recognition, he began to use a new
seal. It was carved with George II’s coat of arms, with the bar sinister, indicating
illegitimacy. Underneath the arms, especially on his similarly engraved bookplates,
Dunckerley sometime inscribed ‘Th omas Dunckerley Fitz George’. Th is evidence
is widely cited by those who believe George III recognized Dunckerley’s royal par-
entage.19 Dunckerley would not just begin using royal arms without permission,
would he? Well, yes, he seems to have done just that. Not just out of vanity, of
course, but for consistency. In 1767 he hadn’t read Coke or Camden, but he knew
a thing or two about expectations. Dunckerley made up a very good story, and
it was having the desired eff ect, aft er a fashion. He did not have a job yet but he
had a pension, and that was a good beginning. Surely he should appear to believe
his own narrative? How better to demonstrate how thoroughly he believed his
mother’s deathbed confession than to use his purported father’s arms?
Masonic historian W. Harry Rylands wrote a careful discussion of the arms
and their use, mostly on Dunckerley’s bookplates, in his preface to Sadler’s biog-
raphy. Rylands notes with some surprise that Dunckerley, a man not given to
collecting books, would go to the trouble to create armorial bookplates, a few
of which have survived and are represented in the collection of the Library and
Museum of Freemasonry in London. Rylands adds that Dunckerley sometimes
used a matching seal on personal correspondence, and several examples of it have
also survived, the earliest on correspondence dated January 1783.20 George Oli-
ver, a writer of Masonic histories known for making extravagant claims, is in this
case uncharacteristically modest, noting that Dunckerley used the armorial seal
only for ‘confi dential correspondence’.21
Much has been made of the fact that Dunckerley was permitted to use these
arms, with the implication that his use constituted royal recognition of his right
to them. An examination of the admittedly incomplete Dunckerley correspond-
ence at the Library and Museum of Freemasonry demonstrates his use of two
seals and a Masonic stamp. Th e stamp, bearing a variety of Masonic symbols, was
frequently used on Masonic correspondence going out to the provinces. Dunck-
erley also had a seal with his initials, which he used regularly. Th e seal with a
derivation of the royal arms seems to have been used more discriminately, mostly
with Dunckerley’s intimates at Grand Lodge, though Aubrey Newman points
out that it appears occasionally on offi  cial provincial correspondence. Th e seal
appears to have been poorly or shallowly carved, as most of the extant imprints
are badly smudged, and do not reveal a clear impression of the royal arms.
Interestingly, Dunckerley commissioned the bookplate, and possibly the seal,
from Benjamin Levi, one of the early members of the small Jewish community in

--- Sayfa 82 ---
Th e Trappings of Royalty
69
Portsmouth.22 Dunckerley had probably known Levi for several years, as he had
engraved the bookplates for the Sober Society of Portsmouth in 1747. Levi, origi-
nally of Wiesbaden, is recorded in 1749 as one of the original four trustees of the
Jewish burying ground in Portsmouth, along with Mordecai Samuel, a jeweller,
Lazarus Moses and Mordecai Moses, both chapmen, or pedlars.23 Benjamin Levi
advertised that he engraved ‘seals, stamps, plates, copperplates and pewter’, so
he might well have executed all of Dunckerley’s commissions.24 J. Rodgers sug-
gests Levi had a diverse clientele, and may have been the fi rst engraver to create
a Jewish ex libris.25 It is revealing that Dunckerley chose to have his improperly
assumed arms engraved outside London, and that he gave the commission to an
immigrant, who would be less likely to question the design. If he were attempt-
ing to be both discreet and convincing, this was a successful strategy. Most
notably, generations of Masonic writers were convinced. Th is is not to belittle
scholars of the Craft , but as no one else was interested in Dunckerley, they were
the only ones thinking and writing about him. Dunckerley repeatedly insisted he
was a virtuous man, a characterization taken up and enthusiastically embellished
by his nineteenth-century biographers. Virtuous men do not pretend to be the
sons of kings if they really are not. Similarly, they do not use credentials, in this
case titles and arms, to which they are not entitled.
Dunckerley, however, did both. A careful investigation by the Windsor
Herald of the College of Arms (then Th eobald Mathew) found no evidence
that Th omas Dunckerley was ever granted arms, either by application or royal
licence.26 Additionally, a search of the records in the Royal Archives yields no
indication that George III ‘recognized’ or ‘acknowledged’ Th omas Dunckerley
as an illegitimate son of George II.27 Neither do either digital or manual searches
of contemporary newspapers and other publications, which would have been
sure to have gathered up that juicy morsel. Th ese more recent investigations,
coupled with Ryland’s and Oliver’s descriptions of the rather limited use of the
arms form a persuasive argument that Dunckerley was not ‘permitted’ to use the
arms – there was no offi  cial sanction, nor offi  cial notice, for that matter. Rather,
his use of the arms of George II with the bar sinister was a rather private personal
folly, an aff ectation tolerated by his closest colleagues, and unremarked upon by
the larger world. Despite his vaunted legal training, Dunckerley either did not
consult or chose to disregard both Camden and Coke on proper protocol.
More to the point, Dunckerley surely knew that he was perfectly secure in his
improper use of arms, for throughout most of the eighteenth century there was no
enforcement of the rules surrounding their use. Th e court with jurisdiction over
misuse of arms is the High Court of Chivalry, a civil court which sits in the Col-
lege of Arms under the Earl Marshal (and before 1521 the Lord High Constable).
Squibb’s defi nitive study reveals that although the court dated back to the Middle
Ages, it was only formally established in 1634 and suff ered an almost immediate

--- Sayfa 83 ---
70
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
diminution of its authority as a result of the disruptions caused by the Civil War
and Interregnum.28 Th e court was revived in 1687, but was never very eff ective and
attempts to give its authority a statutory basis were unsuccessful. Th e last recorded
sitting of the court in the eighteenth century was in 1737. P. E. Ashman tells us
that ‘According to Martin Leake (Norroy King of Arms, 17 December 1729) the
last sitting of the Court was held on 4 March 1737 but it was purely formal. It
was an inglorious end, but contemporary opinion was that the whole business was
imprudently begun, and unskilfully conducted’, and ‘the lawyers who were con-
sulted laughed at it’.29 By the time he published his Commentaries beginning in
1769, William Blackstone observed that the court, ‘is now grown almost entirely
out of use, on account of the feebleness of its jurisdiction, and want of power to
enforce its judgments’.30 Th e court did not sit again until its attempted revival in
1954 in a case brought by Manchester Corporation concerning the display of the
arms of the city corporation by a theatre, the Manchester Palace of Varieties.
In short, while the College of Arms derived income from granting and reg-
istration of arms, there was no judicial enforcement of the use of arms during
much of Dunckerley’s lifetime – something Dunckerley, who studied law in
the 1770s, and whose precious law books fi gure prominently in his will, surely
knew. Th is eff ective immunity from enforcement was noted by Lord Goddard,
sitting as surrogate for the Earl Marshal in the 1954 case, when he commented:
‘It is common knowledge that armorial bearings are widely used as a decora-
tion or embellishment without complaint. To take one instance, hundreds if
not thousands of inns and licensed premises throughout the land are known
as the so-and-so Arms, and the achievements of a nobleman or landowner are
displayed as their sign.’31
Th e next trapping of royal recognition that needs to be dealt with is Dunck-
erley’s almost life-long use of a royal apartment. Admirers have consistently cited
this as sure evidence of royal recognition. Dunckerley himself may have tried to
take advantage of the possible ambiguity of his living arrangements. Bray reports
that when Dunckerley laid his claim of royal birth before George III, he ‘brought
ye circumstances of ye apartment in Somerset House as a proof’. A fairly cheeky
angle, and one with currency among less well-connected observers, but probably
not gaining him much traction with George III.32 Indeed, Th omas Dunckerley
lived much of his life in grace-and-favour apartments, fi rst in Somerset House and,
aft er 1770, at Hampton Court Palace, when many of the remaining denizens of
Somerset House were moved in preparation for its demolition.33 As we have seen,
Somerset House was a sprawling and, by Dunckerley’s time, increasingly decrepit
complex used by the crown to house offi  ces and visiting dignitaries. Aft er 1692
spaces in Somerset House were assigned as grace-and-favor apartments. Large por-
tions of it were used for storage, and even occasionally as army barracks.34 It was

--- Sayfa 84 ---
Th e Trappings of Royalty
71
demolished in 1775 and replaced by the current elegant structure under the direc-
tion of Sir William Chambers and James Wyatt.
Th anks to Sir Edward Walpole’s intervention, Dunckerley was permitted to
assume his mother’s apartments at Somerset House in 1765, a few years aft er her
death but well before he laid his claim of kinship before George III. At Hamp-
ton Court Palace, Dunckerley and his wife occupied Suite 33, the ‘Clerk of the
Spicerie’s Lodging’, located on the ground fl oor of the west side of the Clock
Court.35 Th e ‘Mrs. Biddle’ noted immediately aft er Dunckerley in Law’s history
of Hampton Court Palace may actually be their granddaughter Ann Siddall. In
the accepted narrative Dunckerley claims to have obtained the Somerset House
apartments through the intervention of the 3rd Duke of Devonshire. Th is would
have been fantastic indeed, as the Duke had already died by time he was allegedly
securing the apartment. Reassuringly it turns out the boon was again on account
of Dunckerley’s actual patron Sir Edward Walpole.36 A little investigation proves
that the Walpole family enjoyed the use of many of the apartments in Hampton
Court Palace and other buildings on palace grounds. Sir Edward’s brother, the
writer Horace Walpole who lived at Strawberry Hill, was part of the social life of
nearby Hampton Court. Writing to Lady Ossory, he remarked:
I have dined again with Princess Amelia and with the Hertfords at Ditton, and see a
great deal of my family, who are cantoned around me like those of a patriarch, when
tribes begin to increase and remove to small distances. My brother [Sir Edward] is
at Isleworth, Lady Dysart at Ham, the Keppels at the Stud, the Waldegraves at the
Pavilions, and Lady Malpas in the Palace.37
Th e Hertfords were the Lord Chamberlain and his wife, whose son married Sir
Edward Walpole’s granddaughter. Lady Malpas was a niece by marriage, having
married Horace’s and Sir Edward’s nephew George, the eldest son of their sis-
ter Mary Walpole. Sir Edward’s daughters Laura, Maria and Charlotte married,
respectively, the Hon. Frederick Keppel, later Bishop of Exeter, the Earl of Wal-
degrave, subsequently Duke of Gloucester, and Lord Huntingtower, later Earl of
Dysart. Th e Pavilions and the Stud House were both residences on palace grounds.
Th is list of Walpole family, friends and close connections who lived at Hampton
Court Palace during Dunckerley’s occupancy, while remarkable, is only the begin-
ning of a long history of Walpoles resident there in grace-and-favour apartments,
stretching until at least the end of the nineteenth century.38 With this impressive
testimony to Walpole infl uence over nomination to Hampton Court Palace apart-
ments, it is obviously a mistake to see Dunckerley’s lodgings there as proof of royal
recognition. It is instead a manifestation of Walpole family infl uence at Court,
which continued to shelter Dunckerley long aft er the rupture between Dunckerley
and Sir Edward, and even aft er the latter’s death in 1784.

--- Sayfa 85 ---
72
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Th en there is the matter of a pension from the Secret Service, or private
account (oft en called the Privy Purse), widely reported at £100, but sometimes
cited as being as much as £800.39 Questions of Dunckerley’s parentage and
recognition fundamentally came down to money, so the pension is of critical
interest. In 1745, Th omas Dunckerley married a widow with at least one child,
and so assumed the fi nancial responsibilities of a family before his naval career
was at all remunerative, and he never earned enough through employment to
be considered the ‘gentleman’ he aspired to be. In one of the only non-Masonic
references to be found attached to Dunckerley’s name, naval historian Harry
Dickinson notes that Dunckerley is an excellent example of ‘how it was possible
to combine several jobs on [ship]board, although not necessarily to be paid for
them. On this point Dunckerley remained a bitter man’.40 Th is is borne out by
the fi nal version of his accepted narrative, in which Dunckerley lays the begin-
nings of his fi nancial ruin at the feet of his superiors:
At the siege of Louisburg, Admiral Boscawen granted me a warrant as teacher of the
mathematics on-board the Vanguard, in addition to my being gunner of the same
ship: and, though I discharged both duties for three years, to the satisfaction of my
captain, yet, when I expected to have received my pay, 130l. as teacher of the math-
ematics on-board the Vanguard, it could not be obtained, because Lord Anson had
not confi rmed the warrant which I received from Admiral Boscawen.41
A bit repetitive, but the point is clear. Th e narrative continues, ‘Fearful of being
arrested [for debt], I left  the kingdom in August 1764’.42 Th us begins a very pica-
resque (and possibly unreliable) story of travel, dire illness and at several critical
points Dunckerley’s rescue by the kindness of infl uential acquaintances and
Masonic brethren – ending, as it must, with his safe return home, and successful
plea for royal support.
William Moss suggests a further complication in the arrears of pay for teach-
ing, explaining that by the time Lord Anson received the application, he was
no longer in offi  ce, and had to pass on the paperwork to his successor, adding
to the delay.43 In his brief sketch of Dunckerley’s life, Derek Hall claims that
Vice-Admiral Lord William Gordon (1744–1823) eventually helped Dunck-
erley obtain the arrears.44 Moss also argues, in a 1948 letter to fellow Masonic
historian Heron Lepper, that Dunckerley was driven abroad more by new fol-
lies than old misfortunes. He proposes that although Dunckerley’s fi nances were
already perilous, the tipping point came with the publication of Multa Paucis, for
Lovers of Secrets, in 1763 or 1764, which Moss attributes to Dunckerley. If Moss
were correct, this would be another example of Dunckerley having an arguably
brilliant idea that ultimately made his life more diffi  cult, rather than less so.45
Records at the Royal Archives shed additional light on Dunckerley’s royal
pension.46 He claims a pension of £100 per annum in the accepted narrative,

--- Sayfa 86 ---
Th e Trappings of Royalty
73
and this fi gure is generally repeated thereaft er, though with some notable exag-
gerations. In the eighteenth century, £200 was widely considered the minimum
income necessary for a man to support himself as a gentleman. Th e early political
economist Joseph Massie, writing in 1759, calculated that £200 was the delinea-
tion between respectability and disappointment – listing gentlemen, tradesmen
and merchants as those who had £200 to £400 a year at their command, while
just below them were the aspirants: common clergy, lower-level military offi  cers,
tenant farmers and their ilk.47 Dunckerley’s pension, were it paid in full, would
have allowed him to live just about as well as a London bookseller or apothecary
or, by the end of the century, with all the comforts of a pawnbroker in York.48
Whichever comparison we choose, it seems strange that Dunckerley would
claim to have received only half as much as he actually did – which is only half
the honour, and hardly princely. We have to assume the pension begins in 1767,
as per Dunckerley’s claim. Th ere is circumstantial evidence to support this, but
the Secret Service ledgers do not survive before 1779. Aft er that, the ledgers
show that Dunckerley received £200 a year from the King until the establish-
ment of the public Civil List in 1782. Th is private account was not reported to
Parliament and, the Registrar at the Royal Archives notes, it ‘provided pensions
for such people as former royal servants, literary men, and impoverished rela-
tions of wealthy families’.49 A pension of this sort was doubly welcome, because
it could neither be taxed nor seized by creditors, because it was legally a royal
secret.50 Th e most fascinating fi nding is what comes next, however, and that is
with the disclosures forced by the Civil List and Secret Service Money Act 1782
(22 Geo. III, c. 82). George III stopped paying Dunckerley’s pension entirely.51
Whether this was caused by the very real reduction in funds available aft er the
reform or royal reluctance to reveal hush money for a putative bastard relative,
the cessation seems to have caused a fi nancial panic in Dunckerley’s household.
Th e remaining evidence suggests that Dunckerley launched another letter cam-
paign, much like the one he used in 1766 to successfully persuade someone to
lay his claims of royal birth before the King. Th is time, he was lobbying for a new
pension or, barring that, gainful employment. His targets this time were largely
politicians, and letters to some of them remain.52
We have already read most of one of these letters, addressed to William Hus-
sey MP. In it, Dunckerley provides insight into his complicated and unhappy
fi nances. He fi rst asserts that one of the ‘channels of the king’s bounty’, as
Oughton termed the courtiers who laid Dunckerley’s story before the King,
made a dreadful mistake.53 Th us it was Th omas Worsley’s fault that the King
initially authorized only £100 per annum, and then Dunckerley observes rather
implausibly that there was no way to correct this error for several years.54 Dunck-
erley relates that the oversight was eventually rectifi ed by the intervention of
Lord North, who ‘was pleas’d to order money for my relief at diff erent times;

--- Sayfa 87 ---
74
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
and obtain’d His Majesty’s permission to put me on a list at the Treasury for an
annual Bounty of two hundred Pounds’.55 While this contradicts the records in
the Royal Archives demonstrating that the King paid Dunckerley an annual pen-
sion of £200 we may allow the narrator his dramatic exaggeration – and should
probably take him at this word that Lord North provided additional sums from
time to time. At this point it becomes all too clear that Dunckerley did not have
a head for fi gures.
Although the year began in uncertainty, even consternation, by the end of
1783 it turns out that Dunckerley need not have worried overmuch about the
security of his pension, insuffi  cient as it was. George, Prince of Wales, and Fred-
erick, Duke of York, stepped into the breach, each pledging Dunckerley £100
per annum for the remainder of his life.56 By 1783, George, the Prince of Wales,
had his own establishment and an income. While he spent with abandon, he
paid Dunckerley’s pension faithfully. Prince Frederick, the Duke of York, spent
most of his time during the 1780s in Hanover, so it was unlikely that he either
knew Dunckerley well or cared much about his pension until aft er Frederick was
himself a Freemason. Still, from infancy Frederick was Prince-Bishop of Osna-
brück, a position that had a considerable income attached. He would not have
noticed an additional £100 per annum expense. Additionally, to put the Prince
of Wales’s priorities into perspective, one should recall he gave £500 a year to his
former mistress, Mrs Robinson, and more than £30 per annum to ‘Humphreys
the rat catcher’.57
Despite the reassurance of royal support, Dunckerley’s fi nancial woes con-
tinued unabated. As late as 1788 he was still fi nancially embarrassed, as his letter
to George Rose reveals. In it, Dunckerley describes his ongoing familial obliga-
tions, and hints that this why he seems always to have been short of funds:
I beg you will have the goodness to acquaint Mr Pitt, that my Wife’s Daughter by a
former husband is a Widow, with two Children; and has no other support than that
which she receives from me: Her Sister was also left  a Widow, in great distress, and is
solely dependent on me for a maintenance.58
We have also already seen that Dunckerley supported his wayward son with an
allowance, in an unsuccessful eff ort to keep him from destitution.59 Th e letter to
Rose was actually aimed at his employer, William Pitt, whom Dunckerley was at
pains not to alienate:
I humbly hope that these Circumstances will plead an excuse for the application I have
made: and that His Majesty by whose Royal Bounty I am maintained; and who has
been graciously pleas’d to send me relief at several times, will not be off ended.60
Being a Masonic luminary may have been personally gratifying, but it did not
pay well.

--- Sayfa 88 ---
– 75 –
6 MAKING A MASON
Nearly everything that has been written by or about Th omas Dunckerley over
the past 250 years has involved his role in promoting Freemasonry. As we have
seen, Th omas Dunckerley was a great promoter of himself, and as Freemasonry
became an increasingly important part of his public persona he appears to have
drawn little distinction between furthering it, and furthering himself as a leading
offi  cer and exemplar of the Craft .
Th omas Dunckerley was drawn to Freemasonry, but in eighteenth-century
England he had many other associational options. Th ere is a rich literature
documenting the origins and development of associational life in England – the
societies, clubs and academies that by Dunckerley’s time made up so much of the
fabric of civic life in England’s cities and towns. Inspired by the seminal work
by Jürgen Habermas, much of the recent historiography of association focuses
on the role played by these societies in creating and giving shape to the ‘public
sphere’ of eighteenth-century urban life.1 In his comprehensive study of Brit-
ish clubs and societies, Peter Clark discusses a vast array of associations, many
ephemeral, which fl ourished between 1580 and 1800.2 While many associa-
tions were quite casual, others began to take on a more formal structure early
in the eighteenth century, and soon characteristic patterns of institutionaliza-
tion emerged: regular meetings, designated offi  cers, by-laws and constitutions,
regalia and rudimentary record-keeping.3 Th ese associations appealed to a wide
variety of motivations and pursuits. Almost all of the membership was mascu-
line, though beyond that there was tremendous diversity. Some were organized
for men from specifi c localities or regions, or were dedicated to specifi c interests,
such as bell-ringing, antiquarianism or the promotion of science. Social reform-
ers had a number of associations to choose from, and so did men who dedicated
their free time to strictly convivial pastimes. Masonic and pseudo-Masonic socie-
ties were tremendously popular.4 Clark speculates that the visibility Freemasonry
gained by the formation of the Premier Grand Lodge in 1717 led to widespread
imitation, resulting in dozens of similarly-structured ‘quasi-secret, pseudo-mys-
tical organizations’.5 Along with Freemasonry itself, these other secret societies

--- Sayfa 89 ---
76
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
proliferated throughout the eighteenth century, spreading outside London to
towns across England, and into Scotland, Wales, Ireland and the colonies.
When Dunckerley was about to set out on his life aft er the navy, around
1760, Clark describes much of England as being truly an ‘associational world’.6
It was not a question of whether Dunckerley would join a society or two, but
which ones he would fi x on – membership in something was almost a given for
men with even modest means. Freemasonry’s allure for Dunckerley was probably
many-faceted. It was not unique in off ering ‘conviviality, entertainment, proces-
sions, fashionable patronage, employment, and help to migrants’.7 However,
Freemasonry additionally boasted an unusually upwardly mobile membership
and the most extensive network of associated lodges in the country, provid-
ing a natural entrée into advantageous social circles should a Mason move to a
new town.8 It was large, well-established, charitable, respectable and ultimately
off ered Dunckerley the second career he craved. Th e key to Dunckerley’s choice,
ultimately, may well have been the permanence and social status embodied in the
Grand Lodge itself. Th e Premier or Modern Grand Lodge acted as a dominant
central body, not entirely commanding, and yet coordinating the activities of
affi  liated lodges and Provincial Grand Lodges throughout the country and even
the empire. It was able to marshal far more in the way of fi nancial and social
resources than other associations, and in particular its ability to attract noble
and royal patronage gave the Grand Lodge public visibility and importance.9
It is a frustrating fact that record-keeping was still such a haphazard thing
in the eighteenth century. Even when records were kept, there was a general
sense that they were saved for immediately practical purposes, and that once that
necessity passed so too did their utility. Th e notion of institutional archives was
then only embryonic, and Masonic lodges in particular did not see themselves
as part of an institution. Freemasonry was a brotherhood, a Craft , a convivial
escape, a mystic bond – not an institution. Th is means that tracing the Masonic
career of any individual over the course of the eighteenth century is diffi  cult,
even if he promoted himself as enthusiastically as Dunckerley. Actually, the situ-
ation with Dunckerley is arguably more fraught than that of the average Grand
Lodge offi  cer. Th is is because although Dunckerley was a pioneer in insisting
on proper warrants, payments and returns of members, he was not forthcoming
about himself. It is only when record-keeping becomes thoroughly integrated
with the operation of at least the Grand Lodge itself that we begin to get a sense
of the chronology and interconnectedness of his Masonic activities.
We should begin with the beginning of Dunckerley’s Masonic life, but
we really do not know when that was. Th ere is no independent verifi cation of
Dunckerley’s early Masonic career, and he gives contradictory information about
the year he was initiated. In 1785, Dunckerley requested a warrant for the last of
the many Masonic lodges he helped establish, the Lodge of Harmony, No. 255,

--- Sayfa 90 ---
Making a Mason
77
meeting at Hampton Court Palace. Early minutes from the lodge are (predict-
ably) gone, but a list of members can be found in the Grand Lodge Register. Th e
fi rst name is Th omas Dunckerley. Th ough he never practised, he legitimately
lists his profession as ‘barrister’. Th e relatively modest membership of the lodge
is striking, the more so when the list is compared to other sources. For example,
Th omas Haverfi eld, another resident of Hampton Court Palace, listed in the
lodge roster as ‘Esq.’, appears in Law’s history as a gardener.10 Other members
of the lodge resident in Hampton Court Palace include Th omas Branson, an
operative mason, and James Christopher, an innkeeper (perhaps of the Toy Tav-
ern?) who turns up as a bankrupt in 1795.11 Dunckerley also records in the lodge
roster that he was thirty years old when he was initiated into Freemasonry on
10 January 1754.12 Of course he was thirty-four in 1754, but we may give him
credit for at least attempting to be consistent on this part of the accepted nar-
rative. Th ough, in fact, in January 1754 he should probably have claimed to be
twenty-nine. Dunckerley cited the same date in a 1773 letter to James Heseltine,
in which he complained about the conduct of his mother lodge, No. 21 (later
Antiquity), which met at the Th ree Tuns tavern in Portsmouth.13 With these
two prominent references, it is with some justifi cation that 10 January 1754 is
commonly cited as the date Th omas Dunckerley was made a Mason.
Except, Dunckerley is also the source for three other references that indi-
cate he was initiated into Freemasonry several years earlier than that. One can
be found in a January 1792 letter he wrote to John Knight, a close Masonic
colleague in Redruth, Cornwall. Th e letter is mostly dedicated to relatively
mundane business associated with the preparations for a Knights Templar con-
clave. In it, Dunckerley refers to ‘the Masonic knowledge which I have glean’d
in Europe, America and Africa for forty years past’.14 Th is statement pushes his
initiation into Freemasonry back to 1752, and one has to wonder about Africa.
Did he mean Gibraltar? Perhaps the monkeys fooled him. Th e next step back
into Dunckerley’s past takes us to two references to an initiation in 1747. Both
date from 1793, and both appear in the October edition of the Freemasons’ Mag-
azine. We have already looked in some detail at White’s and Grierson’s admiring
biographical sketch of Dunckerley. Doing the arithmetic quickly, if Dunckerley
had been, as they wrote, progressing in ‘moral, social, and scientifi c Masonry’ for
forty-six years, he had to have begun in 1747. All of the documents that contrib-
uted to the accepted narrative depended on Dunckerley for content. We can be
doubly confi dent in attributing this detail to him, since the wording is virtually
identical to that of the Knight letter which he wrote only a couple of years before,
down to the bit about Freemasonry in Africa. Th e other reference to 1747 is
found in the heading to a curious little poem. Th e brief prologue, addressed to
the printer of the Freemasons’ Magazine, reads, ‘Sir, It is Forty-Six Years since the
following Lines were written by a Gentleman the Morning aft er he was passed to

--- Sayfa 91 ---
78
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
the Second Degree in Masonry.’15 Th e poem is signed ‘Fitz George’. Again, doing
the calculations yields 1747 as the year Dunckerley was passed to the second
degree, and presumably initiated as well.
Th e poem is itself interesting. It appears in various forms in at least two pre-
vious Masonic publications, with no author attribution. Both went through
several editions, and were printed in Edinburgh between 1758 and 1776.
Th e fi rst, which debuted in 1758, was edited by James Callendar. Th e second
book was printed by William Auld and fi rst appeared in 1765. Th e diff erences
between the three versions are amusing, but do not clearly point to which came
fi rst or who wrote them. Th e fi rst two are virtually identical. Dunckerley’s ver-
sion is a bit shorter, and clearly edited for a more refi ned late-century audience.
With a nod to the ladies, he drops the lines, ‘Th e devil and woman gain’d the
wretch’s heart, and he forgot the Mason’s glorious art’. He also updated the
poem, eliminating the barbs aimed at Gormagons, an early rival fraternal society,
substituting ‘modern wits’, as the pests for whom he had no sympathy. Th e other
changes refl ect Dunckerley’s shift  in later life towards evangelical Christianity.
‘Almighty Mason’ becomes ‘Almighty Master’, for example, and man is trans-
formed from being merely ‘fond’ to ‘frail’. Dunckerley also dropped a telling line,
and as a result made Freemasonry more open and inclusive.
Here is Dunckerley’s version:
At length he pray’d – and Heav’n reciv’d his pray’r
Pleas’d to behold with pity, and to spare,
Taught him a way the Science to regain,
By arduous study, and laborious pain,
Not as before, by intuition giv’n
To the whole race of man, by bount’ous heav’n;
Now ‘tis forbad the Secret to declare,
Th at all may equally the labor share,
Here is the older version (1758), with the exclusivity clause in the last two lines::
At length he pray’d; and heav’n reciv’d his pray’r
Pleas’d to behold with pity, and to spare,
Taught a way the science to regain;
Th ro’ arduous study, and laborious pain.
But ‘twas forbid the secret to declare,
Th at all might equally the labour share:
And hence it comes the best alone can claim
Th at noblest character, a Mason’s name;
Th e later version, which Dunckerley likely rewrote even if he was not the origi-
nal author, is more explicitly pious, but also signifi cantly more evangelical in its

--- Sayfa 92 ---
Making a Mason
79
assertion that all men willing to undertake the labour may share the secret, and
not just those who are already the ‘best’ of men.16
Another early date for Dunckerley’s involvement in Freemasonry comes
from a letter he wrote in 1792 about his exaltation in the Royal Arch degree at
Portsmouth in 1754.17 Th e Royal Arch degree is a moving target in the eight-
eenth century. In 1751, Freemasons discontented with the ritual practices and
leadership of the Grand Lodge of England established a rival organization, the
Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted Masons of England, according to the Old
Constitutions. Th ese so-called ‘Ancients’ claimed that they practised a purer
form of Freemasonry, according to the ancient practices – hence their adoption
of the name. Ancient Masons claimed the Royal Arch as their own, as a com-
pletion of the Master’s degree.18 Today, Craft , or Symbolic Masonry, consists of
three degrees, the Apprentice, Fellowcraft  and Master. Th e Master was the last
degree to develop, probably in the late seventeenth century or early eighteenth
century in Scotland and England. When the Royal Arch was introduced it was
widely interpreted as a completion or perfection of the Master’s degree, and not
a separate entity. However, throughout the eighteenth century ownership of the
degree was contested, and Dunckerley was one of the key promoters of the Royal
Arch within the Modern Grand Lodge system.
Whatever the ambiguity surrounding the date of Dunckerley’s entry into
Freemasonry, he took to it with alacrity. While still attached to HMS Vanguard
(1755–61), Dunckerley joined two lodges in and around Plymouth, Devon.
At this point in examining Dunckerley’s Masonic career, one is struck with the
conviction that, had the Freemasons set out to devise a naming system designed
specifi cally to confuse cowans (the unitiated); they could scarcely have done a
better job. Early in the eighteenth century lodges were known by where they
met, typically taverns with private rooms which were rented out to clubs and
societies of all stripes. If a lodge changed venues, it changed its name as well.
Names identifying lodges with attributes or occupations became popular aft er
the mid-1760s, and many older lodges subsequently acquired names independ-
ent of their location. Aft er 1717 the newly created Grand Lodge of England
only very gradually began to extend its authority outside London and, as it did,
affi  liating lodges were assigned numbers. In time, the Ancients also adopted the
expedient of lodge numbers. Periodically the Modern Grand Lodge authorized
the publication of updated lists of affi  liated lodges, the so-called ‘Engraved Lists’.
Also from time to time the erasure or lapse of a lodge, and the creation of new
lodges, necessitated a renumbering. Th us a lodge might also change its number
repeatedly. Th e resulting confusion was exacerbated by the Unlawful Societies
Act of 1799, which restricted the authority of the Grand Lodge to create new
lodges. Th e Act also had profound eff ects on the Ancient Grand Lodge and the
Grand Lodge of Scotland. Th e most convenient way around this new problem

--- Sayfa 93 ---
80
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
was for warrants from lapsed lodges to be reissued to new ones. As a result, a
lodge created in, say, 1801 could actually hold a warrant issued in 1754.
By the end of the nineteenth century, lodge nomenclature had become com-
pletely confused, with the offi  cial roster of the United Grand Lodge showing
many lodges as being founded years before they actually were.19 Towards the
end of the nineteenth century, Masonic scholars William James Hughan and
John Lane began to revisit the engraved lists to establish more accurate informa-
tion about the formation of lodges. But they soon found that they needed to
cross-check the information quite widely. Lane, an accountant from Torquay,
became particularly obsessed with this task, corresponding with hundreds of
lodges, and in the process he became known as the Craft ’s preeminent ‘masonic
statistician’.20 Th e resulting work, Masonic Records 1717–1886, outraged many
lodges, since it questioned their seniority in the Masonic roll, but was never-
theless a landmark publication, greatly aiding the study of Freemasonry across
England and the Empire.21 Since then, the Library and Museum of Freemasonry
has assigned permanent serial numbers to individual lodges to facilitate records
linkage. A searchable online version, complete with serial numbers, was intro-
duced in 2003.22
Nonetheless, giving a precise and intelligible description of an eighteenth-cen-
tury lodge remains a challenge. In Dunckerley’s story, which involves dozens of
lodges, we identify lodges as Dunckerley would probably have fi rst known them,
and then provide additional identifi cation in the endnotes. Anything else gets a
bit too fussy. Nor, to the disappointment of some, but probably the relief of many,
we do not visit each and every lodge Dunckerley founded, joined or had dealings
with. Th at would be a book in itself, and dare we suggest, really quite tedious?
To return to Dunckerley in Plymouth, he joined two lodges, No. 67 meet-
ing at the Masons’ Arms Tavern in Plymouth Dock,23 and No. 134 meeting at
the Pope’s Head Tavern in Plymouth.24 In 1757, Dunckerley referred to him-
self as the Master of No. 134, and a 1768 certifi cate from No. 67 names him
as ‘Master for Two Years’, probably dating to about the same time. Dunckerley
identifi es himself as Master of No. 134 in the advertisement for his ‘Th e Light
and Truth of Masonry Explained’.25 Th ough both of these lodges were affi  liated
with the Modern Grand Lodge, there were other options for local Freemasons.
Even a quick survey of lodges meeting in and around Plymouth during Dunck-
erley’s Masonic career shows considerable diversity. Lane’s records, which are
very good but not perfect, reveal two lodges attached to military regiments and
another lodge which met briefl y at the Masons’ Arms Tavern in 1763.26 Th ese
were all warranted by the Ancient Grand Lodge. Th en there were a number of
other Modern lodges, including No. 135 meeting at the Mitre Tavern, No. 232
meeting at the White Hart Tavern in Old Town, Plymouth, Lodge of Sincerity
No. 454 meeting at Th ree Crowns, Lodge of Friendship No. 416 meeting at the

--- Sayfa 94 ---
Making a Mason
81
Bunch of Grapes in Plymouth Dock, Lodge of Contentment No. 269 which
met briefl y at the signifi cantly named General Wolfe in Plymouth Dock, and
fi nally the Lodge of Concord No. 463 meeting at the Old King’s Arms in Plym-
outh Dock.27 Another Plymouth lodge with which Dunckerley may have had
some connection demonstrates the mutability of Masonic allegiances. In 1759,
Lodge No. 237 obtained a warrant. Tradition has Dunckerley assisting in the
organization of the lodge, but his infl uence did not prevent subsequent thoughts
of desertion to the Ancients. Lodge member R. M. Gannon lamented in a 1772
letter to Grand Secretary James Heseltine that half of the members of the Marine
Lodge had applied for an Ancient warrant, eff ectively jumping ship.28
Eighteenth-century Britain was an imperial country and frequently a coun-
try at war. Th ese factors combined to make the Royal Navy both very large and
very active. Because Plymouth was an important naval base it had a signifi cant
transient and very cosmopolitan population of men who brought with them a
variety of Masonic experiences and affi  liations. Masonic historians have argued
that Plymouth, like its companion Portsmouth, served as a fertile Petri dish of
Masonic traditions and rituals, which surely had a profound impact on provin-
cial Freemasons, especially Dunckerley. Perhaps the most potent infl uence was
the newly established rival Grand Lodge, whose members called themselves
‘Ancients’. One of the explanations put forward by the Ancients Grand Lodge
at their founding in 1751 was that the existing Grand Lodge had grown, but in
growing had lost sight of its rich cultural heritage, and that as a result its ‘work-
ings’ or ceremonial rituals were streamlined, dry and devoid of meaning. Th us
in a real sense, the assertion was that Freemasons made or initiated into Modern
lodges were only shadow Masons, deprived of the full knowledge of the secrets
of Masonry. Laurence Dermott (1720–91), the Irish-born Grand Secretary
and chief ideologue of the Ancients off ered an explanation in his catechistical
description of the diff erence between the Ancients and Moderns:
4th [question] Whether a modern mason may, with safety, communicate all his secrets
to an antient mason?
Ans. Yes.
5th Whether an antient mason may, with the like safety, communicate all his
secrets, to a modern mason, without further ceremony?
Ans. No. For as a Science comprehends an Art, (though an art cannot compre-
hend a science) even so antient masonry contains every thing valuable amongst the
moderns, as well as many other things that cannot be revealed without additional
ceremonies.29
Dermott goes on to explain that Modern Masons are ‘unorthodox’, and that their
apostasy was occasioned by the founders of the Grand Lodge in 1717 who abandoned
ancient Masonry for inferior modern innovations. Th e Moderns had an answer to
that sort of impudence and, as it turned out, his name was Th omas Dunckerley.

--- Sayfa 95 ---
82
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Dermott’s were fi ghting words, and many Masonic historians have conjectured
that Th omas Dunckerley was sensitive, if not outright sympathetic to some of Der-
mott’s charges. Some, like George Oliver in the nineteenth century, and Neville
Barker Cryer in the late twentieth, credited Dunckerley with devising, more or
less on the spot, a comprehensive strategy for reinvigorating the moribund Mod-
