ern Grand Lodge. Dunckerley’s plan, they argued, involved the systematic revision
of existing degrees and the introduction of new ones, frequently using rituals and
traditions Dunckerley noted on surreptitious visits to Ancient lodge meetings.30
Th ere was even talk of a spurious set of ‘Dunckerley lectures’ that were alleged to
have been temporarily adopted by the Grand Lodge for universal use.31 An incred-
ible amount of energy has been expended by scholars keen to determine exactly
who or what infl uenced the rituals Dunckerley used in the lodges he founded or
led, and whether he learned or invented the rituals and degrees he seems to have
introduced to England. Th at inquiry is beyond the scope of the current study,
which is preoccupied with what his Masonic career can tell us about Dunckerley,
rather than what a study of him can tell us about the much larger phenomenon
of eighteenth-century Freemasonry. And, honestly, having surveyed the litera-
ture thus far, it seems improbable that those Masonic relics which survive from
Dunckerley’s time can give any satisfactory answers to questions that are essentially
concerned with creation myths – it is rather like trying to reconcile Dunckerley’s
dates. One probably cannot get there from here.
If Th omas Dunckerley was infl uenced by Ancient traditions, it is a chal-
lenge to see it in his earliest Masonic writings. In the autumn of 1757, Th omas
Dunckerley off ered for sale a pamphlet containing two Masonic charges: ‘Th e
Light and Truth of Masonry Explained, being the Substance of a Charge deliv-
ered at Plymouth, in April 1757’ and ‘Th e Moral Part of Masonry Explained’.32
As Róbert Péter has noted, both charges draw heavily on Dunckerley’s Sober
Society lectures for inspiration and, to a lesser degree, for their composition.33
‘Th e Light and Truth’ was widely reprinted, and is frequently cited as proof of
Dunckerley’s native genius for what he calls the ‘Fift h Science’. Th e Masonic
Fift h Science is ‘Geometry’, as commemorated in the ‘G’ oft en enclosed in a
triangle, found hovering over Masonic assemblies or images. Later most Free-
masons would equate immutable principles with God, not geometry, but the
progression is a logical one, and happily did not require redecoration. Dunck-
erley would have understood geometry not as a species of mathematics, but as
the fi xed principles and moral laws Freemasons are supposed to follow. Th ose
immutables are the subject of the lecture. Unfortunately, given its loft y aims,
there is nothing about Dunckerley’s talk that elicits particular admiration. It is,
in many ways, a typical motivational lecture such as you might hear today at
men’s clubs the world over. ‘Aim high’, ‘Be true to yourself ’, ‘Get hold of your
passions’, and the like. However, this is an entirely creditable little talk, espe-

--- Sayfa 96 ---
Making a Mason
83
cially given that Dunckerley had nothing to fall back on in terms of a rhetorical
education. Dunckerley’s oration was probably pitched perfectly to his audience
of mid-level military men and members of the middling classes, who aspired to
politeness and believed in personal responsibility. Th e most interesting aspect of
the charge is that it draws directly on the lectures he wrote earlier in the decade
for a Sober Society audience. His insistence that ‘Indeed we own all Masons as
Brothers, be they Christians, Jews, or Mahometans’ sounds a good deal like his
suggestion that ‘the most Wild and Savage Indians, as well as the learn’d Brach-
man [Brahman] are sure of happiness in the next Life in proportion they have
adher’d to calm Reason’s holy Laws’.34
Dunckerley sneaks up on the Ancients a few pages into the talk, when he
uses no less a personage than one of the patron saints of Freemasonry to tell them
just how misguided they are in becoming schismatics:
Th ese are the Persons of whom Saint John says, Th ey went out from us; but they were
not of us: For if they had been of us, they would no doubt have continued with us:
But they went out, that they might be made manifest that they were not all of us. And
thus it is that those who depart from the Light bring an evil Report to the Craft .35
It is diffi  cult to reconcile this pointed rejection with Dunckerley then going
about, inconspicuously infi ltrating Ancients lodges, but we have already noted
he was willing to do extraordinary things in pursuit of his goals, so who is to
say? Th e fact remains that as time went on, Dunckerley developed a personal
rivalry with Laurence Dermott, which he touched on in his correspondence, but
which manifested itself most profoundly in his determined eff orts to win over
the Royal Arch for the Modern Grand Lodge.36
O Canada!
Despite his best eff orts to stay in port, 1757 found Th omas Dunckerley still in
the Royal Navy and the Seven Years’ War was far from over. Th at September he
shipped out aboard HMS Vanguard, headed for North America. Dunckerley’s
enthusiasm for Freemasonry was unabated and, as we have seen, he seems to have
done some of his most creative thinking while at sea. So it was that in Janu-
ary 1760 when the Vanguard briefl y returned to England, Dunckerley paused
momentarily to bury his mother and then attended to the business that probably
actually brought him to London. He went to the Grand Lodge and obtained a
novel warrant, one that permitted him to constitute a lodge on board the Van-
guard.37 Th is was the fi rst warrant explicitly intended to establish a lodge active
on a ship while it was at sea, rather than one constituted of naval brethren while
they were in port. By the end of his navy days, Dunckerley had convened lodges
on three ships in the Royal Navy, the Vanguard, the Prince, and the Guadal-

--- Sayfa 97 ---
84
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
oupe, using two separate warrants.38 We know very little about these so called
‘sea lodges’, and it is very likely that aside from their fl oating venues there was
nothing particularly remarkable about them. Like many lodges of the time, espe-
cially regimental lodges, their very transience militated against the survival of
records. Even the practical existence of the lodge supposed to have been held on
the Guadaloupe is questionable, since Dunckerley was on board for such a short
time – the end of January to mid-April 1765. What is most fascinating about
these lodges is what the notion of obtaining warrants for them suggests about
Dunckerley. Aside from his obvious enthusiasm for Freemasonry, the sea lodges
are a demonstration of Dunckerley’s inventiveness. If Dermott’s Ancients had a
corner on the market of regimental lodges, Dunckerley was determined to take
the high seas for the Moderns. Since the warrants Dunckerley used for his sea
lodges were personal licences made out in his name, the lodges lasted only as
long as he remained with a particular ship, and subsequently no one else took up
the idea.39 Disappointingly, the notion of sea lodges never seems to have found a
wider audience – but this was far from the last of his Masonic innovations.
When the Vanguard sailed in 1757 it was headed for Canada to fi ght the
French. Th is latest round of hostilities between British forces in the American
colonies and French troops in Nouvelle-France began in 1753 with skirmishes
over possession of the Ohio Valley. As the confl ict escalated, it became part of
the global phenomenon now known as the Seven Years’ War. British victory at
the key French fortress, Louisbourg, in July 1758 opened up the St Lawrence
River, and was followed by the British seizure of Quebec in September 1759. As
gunner for a sizeable British warship, Dunckerley played an important role in
the battle at Quebec, but would not have been a participant on the battlefi eld.
Nor did gunners actually man the cannon, as has been suggested by some eulo-
gists.40 According to Th e Sea-gunner’s vade-mecum:
Th e Duty of a Gunner, in the Royal Navy, is to take charge of the artillery and ammu-
nition on-board; to examine and observe that the former are always kept in order,
and fi tted with tackles and other furniture; and to teach the sailors the exercise of
the cannon.41
Alas, that rules out the sort of personal heroics and marksmanship Dunckerley’s
admirers have ascribed to him. However, he does seem to have done something
rather remarkable of a Masonic nature at Quebec.
Again, lacunae in documentation are maddening. In 1891, Sadler reproduced
a portion of a 1769 letter from John Gawler, a veteran of Quebec who retired to
London in 1767 and acted as unoffi  cial liaison between Freemasons in Canada
and the Grand Lodge.42 In it, Gawler describes events that had taken place a dec-
ade before, involving Dunckerley and his Masonic activities in Quebec.43 Sadler
did not indicate where the letter was, or who had brought it to his attention. In

--- Sayfa 98 ---
Making a Mason
85
the excerpts, the author explains that, in June 1760, Dunckerley presided over
the installation of Colonel Sir Simon Fraser (1729–77) of the 78th Regiment of
Foot, as Provincial Grand Master of Canada, aft er his selection by the assembled
regimental Freemasons in Quebec.44 Th e quote Sadler includes reads:
Bro. Dunckerley, of His Majesty’s ship the Vanguard, who was possessed with a power
from the Grand [Lodge] of England to inspect into the state of the Craft  wheresoever
he might go, honoured them with his approbation of their conduct and installed
Brother Frasier in his high offi  ce.45
Th is, if it happened just so, convincing regimental lodges to create a Modern
Provincial Grand Master, was quite a coup for Bro. Dunckerley.46 By the middle
of the eighteenth century, regimental lodges were a common feature of British
military life. At the Siege of Louisbourg in 1758, nine of the fourteen British
infantry battalions contained regimental lodges.47 A few probably held warrants
from the Modern Grand Lodge. However, many other warrants were issued by
the Grand Lodge of Scotland, the Grand Lodge of Ireland or indirectly through
various Provincial Grand Lodges in the American colonies, especially that of
New England, headquartered in Boston. Th en there was Dunckerley’s nemesis,
the Ancients. Aft er 1751 the Ancient Grand Lodge made steady incursions into
the regimental ‘market’. Th e speculation has long been that this was due to the
Ancients’ willingness to recognize and work the Royal Arch degree, but it seems
clear that there were other intrinsic diff erences between the Ancients and the
Moderns, including social distinctions, nascent national identities and, of course,
politics. For example, long before the end of the War for American Independ-
ence in 1783, there were almost no American lodges that still owed obedience
to the Modern Grand Lodge, which was strongly identifi ed with British offi  cers
and the government they represented. Most American lodges made the switch to
the Ancients, and then in the early days of the Republic, established independ-
ent state Grand Lodges.48
Th e lodges that gathered in the winter of 1759 consisted of eight or nine
regimental lodges, holding warrants from several Grand Lodges.49 Robertson
lists seven: fi ve held Irish warrants, one was warranted by the Ancients, and
another by the Moderns in New England. Th is is the group whose representa-
tives chose a Provincial Grand Master, Lieutenant John Price Guinnett from an
Irish lodge, and, six months later, his successor, Colonel Sir Simon Fraser of the
78th Regiment. Th e story goes that Th omas Dunckerley was in town for Fraser’s
investiture on 24 June 1760. Possessed of his personal warrant of authority from
the Modern Grand Lodge, a sort of roving Provincial Grand Master, Dunckerley
duly installed him with the blessings of the Modern Grand Lodge.
Th is sounds remarkably like yet another of Dunckerley’s stories, subsequently
embellished by his credulous admirers. As we have seen, there is no record at the

--- Sayfa 99 ---
86
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Grand Lodge of England of Dunckerley’s roving commission.50 Dunckerley does
not even seem to have ever mentioned this fi rst commission in his later boasting
of Masonic achievements. Or did he? In 1784, Dunckerley wrote to the Grand
Lodge, pointing out what a zealous and useful Provincial Grand Master he was,
and reminding them he had, ‘collected and remitted large Sums of Money for
the Charity and Hall Funds’.51 Th ey did not really take his meaning, and merely
draft ed a note of hearty thanks. Th en, in 1786, Dunckerley tried again, writing
pointedly this time:
Every Honour that the Society can with propriety confer on me, I am ambitious to
obtain. Surely it is in their Power to give me the Rank of a Past Senior Grand Warden
& give notice of the same in the next printed Acct of the Qua Com [Quarterly Com-
munications] I leave this to the consideration of Bro. Heseltine & your self.52
Th e Grand Lodge duly did just that. Th anks are due to John M. Hamill for
explaining that the position of Past Senior Grand Warden was thus, at Dunck-
erley’s instigation, an entirely new creation. Th is innovation allowed Dunckerley
to maintain his rank and membership in Grand Lodge even aft er the time came
for him to retire from his provincial grand masterships. His continued mem-
bership in the Grand Lodge secured, Dunckerley thanked his friend Grand
Secretary William White, signifi cantly alluding to his twenty-one years of ser-
vice as a Grand Offi  cer. Dunckerley was never good with numbers, but this has
long puzzled his biographers. He was not made a Provincial Grand Master until
1767, which would only make nineteen years. But what if he was in error in the
other direction, and really meant twenty-six? Th at appears to be a real stretch,
until we consider the contents of James Th ompson’s letter box.
Th ompson was a Mason from Scotland who volunteered for Fraser’s High-
landers (78th Regiment), and aft er the war settled in Quebec. For many years
he served there as Grand Secretary for the Provincial Grand Lodge, and was
an active member of St Andrew’s Lodge, No. 2 Provincial, in Quebec. A lovely
biographical sketch of Th ompson can be found in A. J. B. Milborne’s article on
‘Freemasonry at the Siege of Quebec 1759–1760’.53 Fortunately, Th ompson was
a pack-rat when it came to correspondence, and his letter box is now in the library
of the Grand Lodge of Quebec. It contains documents about the very early days
of Freemasonry in Quebec, including the original of Gawler’s reminiscence, and
Th ompson’s own notes about the installation, which was indeed conducted by
Dunckerley.54 Th ompson’s letter book includes a draft  to the Grand Master, in
which he acknowledges Dunckerley’s infl uence. Th e letter details the diffi  culty
the lodges in Quebec faced in trying to maintain regular communications with
London, and especially their frustration at not being allowed to routinely select
their own Provincial Grand Master. Th ompson writes,

--- Sayfa 100 ---
Making a Mason
87
We should not have been this long neglectful of Transmitting our proceedings, but
that we had reason to hope as many worthy brothers, particularly Brother Dunkerly
[sic] of His Majesty’s Ship the Vanguard … promised to recommend our Case to your
Consideration.55
Perhaps Dunckerley did have a writ from the Grand Lodge, or maybe he cal-
culated that personal authority be damned, seizing Canada for the Moderns
would win him credibility back home in London. Either way, Dunckerley saw
an opportunity to wrest Quebec, at least, from the Ancients, and he took it.
Grand Lodge accepted the gift , and Fraser’s name appears in the list of newly
appointed Provincial Grand Masters in John Entick’s 1767 edition of the Book of
Constitutions. Th e other provinces named give us an idea of Grand Lodge impe-
rial strategy at the time: Fraser appears alongside a number of other gentlemen
appointed to similar offi  ces in (using eighteenth-century terminology) Antigua,
Bahama, Gaudaloupe, Georgia and Hamburg.56
Finally, we need to note that roving commissions at the level of Provincial
Grand Master, of the sort Dunckerley was said to have had, were not unheard
of. Beginning in the 1720s, the fi rst Provincial Grand Masters in India had what
was actually a roving commission. Th ey had no staff , no permanent base and
were mostly with the military or East India Company, so were likely to be out of
India a good deal of the time.57 Closer to Dunckerley’s own time, Grand Master
Earl Ferrers granted a roving commission in 1762 to John Bluvitt, commander
of the Admiral Watson, an Indiaman, for East India. He was to inspect and do
Masonic business ‘where no other Provincial is to be found’.58 Perhaps Dunck-
erley was just another exemption that improves the rule with regard to roving
commissions.

--- Sayfa 102 ---
– 89 –
7 PROVINCIAL GRAND MASTER OF ENGLAND
When Th omas Dunckerley died in 1795, several brief obituary notices read,
‘Died: On Th ursday, at Portsmouth, in the 71st year of his age, Th omas Duncker-
ley, Esq., Provincial Grand Master of England’.1 In the decades since Dunckerley
returned from sea, he had indeed become ubiquitous. It undoubtedly seemed that
he actually was the Provincial Grand Master of all of England. He would have
liked that little bit of exaggeration. But in truth, between his 1767 appointment
as Provincial Grand Master (PGM) of Hampshire, until he fi nally relinquished
offi  ce just prior to his death in 1795, Dunckerley at one time or another, presided
over Hampshire, Essex, Dorset, Gloucestershire, Somerset, Hereford, South-
ampton, the City and County of Bristol, and the Isle of Wight.2 At Dunckerley’s
instigation, the Isle of Wight became independent from Hampshire. In 1786 he
successfully petitioned for Bristol to become a Masonic province independent
of Gloucestershire. Sadler observes that the point of this division was to enable
Dunckerley to appoint most of the members of the Royal Gloucester Lodge No.
462 as Provincial Grand Offi  cers. Th ere was no other lodge in the province of
Gloucester at that time. In addition, he wrestled with Freemasons in Wiltshire
over whether they would accept him as Provincial Grand Master – he thought
so, they thought not – with the result that he was designated fi rst as Acting Pro-
vincial Grand Master, and then in one of his rare defeats, apparently fulfi lled the
duties of offi  ce without any title at all. Of the thirty-four Masonic provinces rec-
ognized by the Modern Grand Lodge in 1795, Dunckerley was PGM of eight,
and had a considerable share in creating and presiding over another two.3
Back in London by the winter of 1765–6, Dunckerley had nothing to do
but write letters to putative patrons, and attend lodge meetings. He fell in with
an interesting crowd. In January 1766, Dunckerley joined a Royal Arch meet-
ing at the Turk’s Head Tavern in Gerrard Street. Th rough this chapter, at that
point still termed a lodge, he met many of the most active and infl uential young
Freemasons in the City: James Galloway, James Heseltine, Rowland Berkeley,
Samuel Spencer, Rowland Holt, the Hon. Charles Dillon and the Duke of Beau-
fort.4 About the same time Dunckerley used his personal warrant from HMS
Prince to establish Somerset House Lodge No. 279, and many of his new Royal

--- Sayfa 103 ---
90
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Arch Companions joined the Somerset House Lodge as well. Over the course of
that year Dunckerley’s enthusiasm and fresh ideas impressed his colleagues, and
he assumed a certain stature amongst the other young Masonic reformers, many
of whom became Grand Lodge offi  cers over the next few years. Dunckerley was
immersed in the heady early days of what appears to have been a major reform
eff ort in Modern Freemasonry, and he must have been keen to be more than
an observer. Still, in a society where rank and birth were critical, even further
Masonic achievement might well have been beyond the reach of a man of only
modest social background. Dunckerley busied himself throughout the summer
and autumn of 1766, sending copies of his mother’s Declaration to anyone he
thought might lend a sympathetic ear. Finally, he found his advocate in Sir James
Adolphus Oughton (1720–80).
Perceptive readers will have noted that Dunckerley became PGM of Hamp-
shire in 1767, which was his personal annus mirabilis. Interestingly, Dunckerley’s
appointment as PGM was approved in February 1767, several months before
he was awarded a pension by the King. Th e timing of this appointment has led
several Masonic historians to speculate about the motivations of Oughton,
who Dunckerley credited with being the chief agent behind having Mary
Dunckerley’s Declaration laid before George III.5 If this were a play, the part
of Dunckerley’s guardian angel would surely have been written for Oughton.
He epitomized the sort of man Dunckerley sought to impress. Oughton was a
military offi  cer with a reputation for promoting men of merit but no fortune; he
was an enthusiastic Freemason and, yet again, a bastard son. Although his father
recognized him and undertook his education, the senior Oughton’s early death
left  James without funds. In 1737 he left  school to become a cornet in the 8th
Dragoons, and in the same year he joined the Freemasons. Oughton progressed
steadily in both organizations, eventually becoming Grand Master of the Grand
Lodge of Scotland and commander-in-chief of north Britain.
R. F. Gould, in particular, looked at the way events unfolded in 1767 and
came to the conclusion that Oughton adopted Dunckerley’s cause early on,
with the intention of enabling Dunckerley ‘to devote the whole of his time to
the welfare of the Masonic Institution’.6 Gould’s argument is interesting, but
ultimately not convincing. Bear in mind that Dunckerley scattered his appeals
widely, and while he did send them to Freemasons, a majority of recipients were
not Masonic brethren. A close look at the texts of the letters Dunckerley sent
shows us his talking points, and Freemasonry is not amongst them – the strongly
Masonic component of the accepted narrative is not introduced until the very
last iteration, which was published posthumously. Finally, Oughton was indeed
a dedicated Freemason, but although he was initiated in London and served as
PGM for the Grand Lodge of England in Minorca from 1749 to 1754, he then
became Grand Master of the Grand Lodge of Scotland (1769–71), and had

--- Sayfa 104 ---
Provincial Grand Master of England
91
close ties to both the Grand Lodge of Ireland and the Ancient Grand Lodge.
Oughton even attended the installation of John, 3rd Duke of Atholl as Grand
Master of the Ancients in 1771.7 Th e English Masonic community was in a state
of fl ux, and while Dunckerley eagerly accepted Oughton’s assistance, he would
have considered Sir James to be not the right sort of Mason.
It must have seemed almost miraculous to Dunckerley’s new Masonic col-
leagues when his ‘true’ status as an unrecognized son of royalty was revealed.8
Still, it is unlikely that he planned the story for that particular audience. Recall
Dunckerley had invented his fantastical tale the year before he fell in with his
new London brethren, and the point of the masquerade until then had been to
acquire patronage resulting in paid employment, not a life of perpetual volun-
teerism. Still, his discovery came at a propitious moment in the history of the
Modern Grand Lodge. Previous scholars have suggested that in order to hold up
his end of the reform eff ort, Dunckerley really needed a bit more ballast than he
could command as a retired gunner. Being the son of George II would do quite
nicely, however.9 If that was the case, Dunckerley’s subsequent Masonic career
off ers testimony to the profound unintended eff ects of his little deception.
By the mid-1760s the Modern Grand Lodge was undeniably feeling pressure.
It had not previously been able to exert much infl uence outside the southern
counties of England, and with growing competition from the Ancient Grand
Lodge, it was rapidly losing what little loyalty it had once commanded further
afi eld. If we give any credence to Dermott’s accusations about the debasing of the
ritual, the Moderns were set to lose even more members as individual Masons
and even whole lodges looked for a more spiritually and aesthetically fulfi lling
experience. Dunckerley and his allies were more immediately concerned with
what they perceived as an alarming indiff erence to the situation amongst the
Grand Offi  cers.10 Th e little band of reformers had a great deal of work to do,
and they set out with enthusiasm, even fi nessing a takeover of one of the oldest
lodges in London, No.3, meeting at the Sun and Punchbowl. Th is was a vener-
able lodge, founded in January 172½  , just aft er the creation of the Grand Lodge
itself in 1717, but it had fallen on economic hard times. No. 3 had confusingly
begun life in 172½   as No. 4, but was not one of the Old Four original lodges. It
appears as No. 3 at the 1755 numeration, and in 1814 was renumbered No. 6. It
took the name of the Lodge of Friendship in March 1767.
To greatly simplify the story, early in 1767 the young bucks of the Somerset
House Lodge thought their infl uence would be enhanced if they had a more
senior and thus more prestigious base of operations. Th ey were especially keen
to provide their champion the Duke of Beaufort with a home lodge of impec-
cable antiquity, commensurate with his new title as Grand Master of the Premier
Grand Lodge, an offi  ce he held until 1772. Dunckerley and his brethren there-
fore arranged an amicable though entirely improper warrant swap. Members

--- Sayfa 105 ---
92
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
exchanged lodges, lodges exchanged warrants, and the original group of Masons
that had possessed the warrant for No. 3 was richer by 30 guineas, allegedly paid
to compensate them for the lodge furniture and regalia. Th e young Duke became
Worshipful Master of Lodge No. 3, a suitable position for the Grand Master of
the Grand Lodge.11 In April there was a brief kerfuffl  e at Grand Lodge over the
irregularity of the proceedings, but it was generally excused as an expression of
the high spirits of the youthful Duke of Beaufort and the Hon. Charles Dillon,
who were both in their twenties. Th at hand-slap over, the reformers’ base was
won and their work commenced.
But, within a few years, the reformers’ paths began to diverge. Dunckerley,
for one, decamped. He was born a Londoner, but had spent the better part of the
past thirty years outside the city, and in the company of common folk. His new
royal dignity did not change the heart of the man behind the masquerade, and
he soon found his métier in provincial administration and recruitment, rather
than in the Grand Lodge, where smooth negotiations and high connections of
long standing were prized attributes. By 1770, Dunckerley had made his choice,
encouraged as always by fi nancial realities. When his residence was shift ed to
Hampton Court Palace, he virtually abandoned London and spent most of the
rest of his life riding a circuit in his provinces. He rarely attended Grand Lodge
meetings, though his correspondence routinely made his presence felt. Duncker-
ley was earning the encomium ‘Provincial Grand Master of England’.12
So, what did a Provincial Grand Master do? In Dunckerley’s time, many did
precious little. In the eighteenth century, many Masonic provinces did not have
a PGM, and those that did rarely heard from him. Even through the mid-nine-
teenth century, the Provincial Grand Master was an honorifi c offi  ce, frequently
bestowed on a great provincial nobleman, though without any particular duties
attached. Truth told, outside of London and nearby counties in the south of
England, many offi  cial Masonic provinces were thinly populated with lodges.
Dunckerley must have set a positive example through his assiduous cultivation
of his provinces. His correspondence mentions only a scattering of Provincial
Grand Lodge offi  cials beneath him, and it does not appear that he consistently
established anything like a standing body one could identify as the Provincial
Grand Lodge, as a local counterpart to the Grand Lodge personnel in London.
During the warm months, Dunckerley travelled incessantly, and his correspond-
ence suggests that he made a real eff ort not only to visit provinces and hold
Provincial Grand Lodge extravaganzas, but also to attend individual lodges on a
regular basis. Th e rest of the time, Dunckerley kept up a constant correspondence
with his lodges and colleagues. In one telling letter to Grand Secretary William
White, Dunckerley asserted his control over his provinces, but also described
what he considered his proper duties as Provincial Grand Master. He requested:

--- Sayfa 106 ---
Provincial Grand Master of England
93
I beg your Assistant Clerk may not send any more letters to the Lodges in the Coun-
ties I have the honour to Superintend. I will save him the trouble of writing 90 letters
a year.13
His good example probably did little more than raise expectations – there was
not a groundswell of permanent provincial institution-building for some time.
However, aft er Dunckerley and due to his eff orts and those of a few other activist
Provincial Grand Masters, these offi  cers were increasingly considered an essen-
tial component of provincial Freemasonry. Aft er the Union of 1813, Provincial
Grand Masters were given explicit powers and responsibilities in the Book of Con-
stitutions issued in 1815, and corrected in 1819.14 Still, Aubrey Newman argues
that many provinces were neglected, and the situation changed dramatically
only in the mid-nineteenth century, when provincially based reformers made
a concerted eff ort to secure eff ective, active Provincial Grand Masters who had
suffi  cient social status to command the loyalty and admiration of their lodges.15
Nonetheless, Dunckerley attempted a unilateral reform of the provinces
under his jurisdiction, introducing some semblance of central control for the
fi rst time. His provincial grand masterships coincided with a time of sweeping
institutional reform centred in London and aimed at the inner workings of the
Grand Lodge itself. Th e eff ort was headed by Dunckerley’s friends from Somer-
set House Lodge No. 279 and the Lodge of Friendship No. 3. Th eir main goals
involved bringing nobles and members of the Royal Family into the leadership
of Masonry, while supporting them with a stable corps of well-educated men
capable of doing the actual business of the Grand Lodge. Th e desired result was
a general enhancement of the propriety and dignity of Modern Freemasonry. All
sorts of innovations can be traced to their coming into offi  ce, intended to keep
better records, better regulate the provinces and lodges, make eff ective use of
charity funds, and improve the reputation of the Grand Lodge and the Craft .16
Th is was a Grand Lodge concerned with extending its authority and growing its
membership. While no general scheme of provincial administration seems to
have been planned, and certainly none was enacted, Dunckerley did his part to
enforce such innovations as annual returns and, most importantly, raise money.17
Keeping up appearances is very expensive.
It needs to be understood that as Dunckerley progressed through his prov-
inces, wreaking order as it were, he was the only representative of the Grand
Lodge with whom most Masons had ever come in contact. Th ere was very little
structure or discipline within provincial Freemasonry, nor did most Masons out-
side of Grand Lodge see the need for their imposition. Th ey were not enthralled
with fi lling out forms, and they particularly objected to sending money off
to London. Most of all, they strongly resented interference with the forms of
Masonic ritual to which they were accustomed.18 Dunckerley had his work

--- Sayfa 107 ---
94
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
cut out for him. He had an undeniably cranky disposition – it comes through
quite distinctly in his correspondence. He could occasionally be a bully. He was
prideful, easily off ended and demanding. It is also clear that he could be a loyal
supporter and generous friend. He probably used all those attributes in turn as
he alternately cajoled and threatened his lodges into conformity and obedience.
Make no mistake, Dunckerley insisted on obedience. As he wrote to Grand Sec-
retary William White in 1786, ‘Entre nous – I am not only lov’d, but fear’d.’19As
for Grand Lodge offi  cers back in London, they apparently appreciated Duncker-
ley’s regular remittances, sent out lodge warrants when he requested them, duly
erased the lodges he deemed outside the Pale and otherwise largely stayed clear.20
More than one Masonic historian has noted that Dunckerley was subject to very
little central control. We can read this as Grand Lodge confi dence in Duncker-
ley, and in the early years this was surely the case. Also, aft er 1773 when the Hall
Committee began meeting in earnest, it welcomed every shilling he wrested out
of the reluctant hands of provincial lodges.21 Th e estimate was that the new Free-
masons’ Hall, which was required to be an edifi ce both appropriately grand and
symbolically meaningful, was going to cost £5,000.22 Dunckerley was the only
member of the Hall Committee not based in London, so his provincial fundrais-
ing may well have been key to the success of the endeavour. He seems to have
relished the role of Grand Lodge’s strong arm in the provinces, and routinely
threatened lodges with erasure (oblivion) if they did not pay up for the charities
and hall fund.23
Even Dunckerley’s most ardent nineteenth-century admirers allowed that
he might have been Provincial Grand Master of nearly everything Masonic in
southern England because no one else wanted the job.24 Traditionally, Grand
Lodge offi  cers, including Provincial Grand Masters, were acclaimed, even feted,
but not paid. Th e Grand Secretary of the Modern Grand Lodge drew only £100
a year beginning in 1787 – these offi  ces were clearly considered the voluntary
work of independent gentlemen. Dunckerley appears not to have received any
remuneration, claiming only expenses for postage, warrants and the like. It is
not at all clear that he was compensated for any of his extensive travel. He fre-
quently mentions sums of money in his correspondence with lodges and Grand
Lodge offi  cials, but almost without exception these consist of fees for initiation,
warrants or regalia, or donations for the charity and hall funds. Nothing like
formal provincial accounts survives, and from the remaining evidence it was
unlikely that he was very careful in this regard. Dunckerley rarely refers to his
own expenses. Th us we have to acknowledge that his constant impecunity was
largely perpetuated by a combination of his vanity and his sense of duty. He
was going to be the selfl ess PGM; he was going to be the fi rst in the assembly to
make a donation to the charity fund. An example from August 1793 is typical.
At a Provincial Grand Lodge he held at Chelmsford, Dunckerley ‘recommended

--- Sayfa 108 ---
Provincial Grand Master of England
95
to the consideration of the Brethren, that most Excellent Charity, the ROYAL
CUMBERLAND FREEMASON’S SCHOOL ... he began a subscription by
TWENTY GUINEAS from his own purse’.25
Never mind his wretched, spendthrift  son – the lion’s share of Duncker-
ley’s fi nancial trouble in later life was intimately related to his Masonic duties.
It appears likely that Dunckerley’s inattention or lack of aptitude for keeping
accounts impinged on his relationship with both the Grand Lodge and the
Grand and Royal Chapter of the Royal Arch. Account ledgers for the Modern
Grand Lodge became available too late to consult in preparing the manuscript
for the current study, but other evidence strongly suggests that the casual refer-
ences to sums and draft s in Dunckerley’s Grand Lodge correspondence were not
formalized in regular statements, leading inevitably to misunderstandings.26 A
noteworthy example, and one which made Henry Sadler furious, appears in the
minutes of the Grand Chapter following Dunckerley’s death, when it ‘was judg’d
proper that an enquiry should be made of his Executors for any Accounts relat-
ing to this Order, as he had received from time to time many Sums of Money
... which he had never communicated’.27 Sadler fulminated against Benjamin
Cooper, the Grand Recorder responsible for the entry, but it was a reasonable
request given the looseness of Dunckerley’s record-keeping, and the precarious-
ness of the Grand Chapter’s fi nances in the early 1790s.28
And so it went. Despite an occasional mis-step, Dunckerley clearly thrived
on the work or he would not have kept it up. While it seems unlikely that most
of the Masons in his provinces ‘adored’ him, as Th omas White averred, they
became used to him. Th ey had tastes in common, and he indulged his loyal
lodges. Dunckerley was particularly attuned to the pleasure and status conferred
by special Masonic regalia. In addition to the attention he paid to jewels for
lodge offi  cers, he made special eff orts to establish the privilege for Provincial
Grand Offi  cers to wear aprons lined and edged with red for the Grand Stewards
and blue for other Provincial Grand Offi  cers.29 Dunckerley also liked to put on a
big show when he held a Provincial Grand Lodge, and provincial lodges poured
into town to join him. Th is excerpt from a letter to the Grand Secretary shows
Dunckerley at his most exuberant:
I am to request you will do me the honour to acquaint our Royal Grand Master that
I held a Provincial Grand Lodge (for Dorsetshire) at Weymouth on the 24th of last
June – A Procession was made to and fr om the Church, by a very respectable number
of Brethren in that County, as will appear by the Lists. I also held a Provincial Grand
Lodge at Pool, on the 12th of August, in honour of the Prince of Wales compleat-
ing His twenty-fi rst year. We embark’d in three Sloops, preceded by the Dorsetshire
Band, & din’d by the Castle belonging to Mr Sturt (Member for the County,) where
the fl ag was display’d and a royal salute was made from the Battery, which we return’d
with three times three. In the Evening grand fi re-works were exhibited on our return
to Pool.30

--- Sayfa 109 ---
96
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Th is is a wonderful example of the package of symbols and ideology Dunckerley
off ered provincial Freemasons, and is worth reiterating: marching in regalia, a
little church, more marching in regalia, royal birthdays, marching in regalia with
bands (also in regalia), picnics, castles, loyal members of Parliament, fl ags, gun
batteries, parties on boats and, best of all, fi reworks! Notice that Dunckerley
slipped in the fact he also procured complete membership rosters.
Most of what occupied Dunckerley’s time as PGM was more mundane. He
ordered warrants and copies of the Book of Constitutions for new lodges, advised
their offi  cers on appropriate regalia, badgered them for membership lists and
donations to the charity and hall funds. Generally, lodges complied. Sometimes
they did not, and the result could be protracted and messy. Th e most spectacular
instance of mutual obstinacy is found in the running confl ict between Th omas
Dunckerley and Sarum Lodge No. 47 in Salisbury, Wiltshire.31 Dunckerley was
frequently the fi rst Provincial Grand Master to come into one of his provinces,
and that had its own set of challenges. Th is was not, however, the situation in
Wiltshire, whose fi rst PGM, appointed sometime around 1775, was Sir Th omas
Fowke, an absentee who resided in London.32 Not just any absentee, Sir Th omas
was groom of the bedchamber to Henry, Duke of Cumberland, the Grand Mas-
ter of the Grand Lodge and brother of the King.33 Fowke came from an old and
distinguished Wiltshire family, and apparently set a high standard of both respect-
ability and leaving well enough alone. When the new Masonic regime came in at
Grand Lodge, they deemed it appropriate for a more workman-like PGM to take
over, and Dunckerley was handed the province. Even Sadler acknowledges that in
Wiltshire, Dunckerley’s zeal ‘far exceeded his usual discretion’.34 Dunckerley typi-
cally came into a province rather brusquely. He visited all the lodges, took their
measure, and began clearing house, erasing lodges that were moribund, enforcing
payment for warrants, regularizing initiation fees, extorting charity and hall fund
payments if necessary, and giving instruction on ritual if that needed attention
to bring the lodges into trim.35 Th e record shows that this was frequently a pain-
ful process, especially for provincial brothers who unwittingly found themselves
amongst the detritus to be cleared away. In dealing with these lodges and brethren
Dunckerley exhibited an hauteur rarely seen by his peers.
In Wiltshire, Dunckerley apparently attempted to declare an open season on
Ancient lodges, resulting in an outcry. Lodge No. 200, meeting at the Vine Inn
in Salisbury, was one of his fi rst targets. Dunckerley informed members they had
an illegal warrant, and intimated that they should come over to his side. Th is
lodge was newly warranted, and a bit uncertain of the terrain. Eventually they
did come over to the Moderns, taking a warrant as Modern Apollo Lodge No.
454 in 1783. Before they switched, though, they made a stink, writing to the
Ancient Grand Lodge and complaining about Dunckerley’s aggressive behav-
iour. Th e Ancients Grand Lodge Minutes mention a similar confl ict between

--- Sayfa 110 ---
Provincial Grand Master of England
97
Dunckerley and Ancient Lodge No. 174 in Southampton. Th ere is also a refer-
ence to ‘the false and Malicious insinuations propagated by Mr Dunkerley’ with
regard to Laurence Dermott.36
Dunckerley was not only getting aggressive with the rival Grand Lodge,
though. He was also at odds with Sarum Lodge No. 47. To be fair, problems with
Sarum Lodge pre-dated Dunckerley, and he showed up to reap the harvest –
though he handled the situation miserably. Th is was an old and prosperous lodge
and, having outgrown the tavern, members had recently built their own hall.37
Lodge members were not enthused about Dunckerley’s eff orts to coerce them
to contribute what he thought was an appropriate amount to help pay off  the
loans for building Freemasons’ Hall dedicated in 1776, notwithstanding their
signifi cant recent expenditure closer to home. Sometimes the lack of records
speaks eloquently, and so it is that the lodge minute book for 1777 to 1794 was
‘lost’. Round one ended when the lodge refused to pay and, in February 1777,
Dunckerley had it erased.
Another Wiltshire lodge, the Lodge of True Friendship No. 398 at Devizes,
was also very unhappy. Members resorted to pretending that Dunckerley did
not exist, and refused to answer his letters. Dunckerley was off ended, and com-
plained to Grand Lodge. Th e Lodge of True Friendship was erased as well. Now
this was playing with fi re, and the gentlemen in London knew that. Just as much
as Dunckerley wanted to woo lodges away from the Ancients in Wiltshire, they
in turn were happy to welcome disenchanted Moderns, which Dunckerley was
making in droves.
Sarum Lodge apologized, aft er a fashion, and was reinstated in November
1778, but remained disgruntled. William White, the Grand Secretary of the
Grand Lodge, shot off  a barrage of conciliatory letters to aggrieved parties in
Wiltshire. Th e main problem in Wiltshire seemed to be Dunckerley’s lack of
social standing. If Wiltshire Freemasons had even heard that Dunckerley was a
son of George II, they were not impressed. It could not have helped his image
locally when his own son applied for and received Masonic relief from Sarum
Lodge’s charity fund in 1779. No wonder they wanted a PGM less likely to drain
their coff ers, one way or another.38 White tried to soothe them, suggesting that
Dunckerley was not actually their Provincial Grand Master, he was just a tempo-
rary one until someone more suitable could be found.39 Th is was understandably
unwelcome news to Dunckerley. White invited Wiltshire Freemasons to nomi-
nate an acceptable candidate to become the ‘real’ PGM. Apparently that did not
go well either, and the next letter from White to the Sarum Lodge notifi ed them
that the Junior Grand Warden, James Galloway, would adjudicate the disagree-
ment between the lodge and their ‘superintendent’, Dunckerley. It was already
the spring of 1782, and hostilities had been going on since late 1776.

--- Sayfa 111 ---
98
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Galloway left  Wiltshire in the summer of 1782 hopeful that his earnest nego-
tiations would have their intended eff ect. But, in January 1783, Dunckerley sent
a mournful letter to Grand Lodge, addressed to James Heseltine, complaining
that he had again become invisible. Th e Sarum Lodge had refused to attend his
recent Provincial Grand Lodge, and now would not answer his letters. Duncker-
ley requested that if the lodge tried to circumvent him and communicate directly
with Grand Lodge, it be chastized and its letters returned. Instead, Duncker-
ley was the one who received a reprimand. It was a very gentle one, but there it
was. Heseltine requested, on behalf of the assembled Grand Lodge offi  cers and
members of the Committee of Charity, that Dunckerley voluntarily relinquish
Wiltshire, and off ered the province of Somerset as a consolation.40
To make matters worse, Dunckerley’s daughter, Mary Ann, had married John
Edgar, a member of the recalcitrant Sarum Lodge. He and Dunckerley had a fond
relationship and, when Mary Ann died in February 1783, Dunckerley prom-
ised his bereaved son-in-law that he would live with him forever – in Salisbury.
Dunckerley begged to be permitted to keep Wiltshire, and as PGM, not merely
as a caretaker ‘superintendent’. Th e situation just kept getting worse. Finally, in
September 1783, the Grand Master stepped in, and decreed that there would
be no Provincial Grand Master of Wiltshire, further instructing Dunckerley to
leave Sarum Lodge alone. And that is what happened when things became ugly
in Dunckerley’s domains.
Th e Holy Royal Arch
Most Freemasons are in agreement about the fi rst three degrees of Craft
Masonry: Entered Apprentice, Fellow Craft  and Master Mason. Th ere are slight
variations in how the rituals and lectures for the degrees are handled, but discus-
sions about these three degrees are typically quite harmonious. However, even
Freemasons of the most well-informed and best-intentioned sort frequently dis-
agree vigorously and at length on the origin, meaning and proper rituals for just
about every degree beyond the third, or Master Mason. Th e histories of these
higher degrees, oft en organized into what are termed ‘appendant orders’, are rife
with controversy. ‘Appendant’ orders are those that are not strictly part of Craft
Masonry, i.e. the fi rst three degrees, but generally ones for which a candidate
must already have become a Master Mason. Sometimes there is a further require-
ment that he has already served as the master of a lodge, or has been symbolically
or ‘virtually’ passed through the master’s chair without actually doing the work.
It is important to remember that disagreements about higher degrees cannot be
attributed to the inherent fractiousness of Freemasons – but when invested prac-
titioners look for a single, clearly defi ned answer, the question of origins rapidly
degenerates into confusion. It does not help that much key nineteenth- and early

--- Sayfa 112 ---
Provincial Grand Master of England
99
twentieth-century research is intensely self-referential – rather than consider-
ing non-Masonic sources, Masonic researchers have historically tended to look
within Freemasonry and to quote each other, resulting in Masonic histories that
are isolated from any outside critical literature.
An additional obstacle to harmonious consensus is the fact that the rituals
and degrees within Freemasonry developed from multiple sources over a consid-
erable period of time, taking on distinct forms in diff erent countries, and even
varying between communities. If major established institutions did not keep
accurate records in the early modern era, lodges were even more resistant and
very little remains by way of documentation. Freemasons knew what they were
doing, and it was no one else’s business. Besides, the culture within the lodges
was oral and initiatory. You learned what you needed to know by word of mouth
once you were deemed ready for the knowledge.41
One of the primary elements obscuring historical investigation of Masonic
origins is the likelihood that there was little outright invention involved in the
creation of degrees and rituals in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries.
Whether they were clever or earnest, most ritualists drew on a common pool of
religious and craft  traditions, and especially on their imagery. Early Freemasons
then imbued these shared images and phrases with esoteric, or hidden, mean-
ings; so that they illustrated the particular narrative and lessons the candidate
was supposed to learn as he qualifi ed for and then integrated the knowledge of
each degree. We fi nd deciphering the symbolism and cracking the code of devel-
opment particularly challenging today, in large part because our cultural norms
have changed and symbols that were once readily interpreted now mystify. Th is
is not a new problem, and any careful scan of nineteenth-century Masonic
writing reveals already heated debates about the origin and proper ordering of
degrees, the interpretation of symbols, and the meaning of rituals.
So it is with trepidation that we venture into a discussion of Dunckerley’s life
as a Companion of the Royal Arch. We have already noted that the Holy Royal
Arch was a hot property in eighteenth-century Freemasonry. Of course, it was
not just popular in England. An illustration of the keen competition for seniority
in the degree is demonstrated by the debate about whether Philadelphia (1758)
or Boston (1769) had the fi rst body regularly granting Royal Arch degrees.42
Dunckerley claimed to have beaten both of them, and attests that he was exalted
(the proper term) in the Royal Arch in Portsmouth in 1754.43 Fredericksburg
Lodge No. 4 in Virginia has a minute book recording a 1753 Royal Arch cer-
emony, but Virginia does not fi gure in the great Massachusetts–Pennsylvania
rivalry that dominates nineteenth-century American Masonic literature, and
thus leaves a quieter impression.
Th e fi rst meeting of a Royal Arch body that shows up in the minutes of the
Modern Grand Lodge was held in London on 22 March 1765, at a tavern in

--- Sayfa 113 ---
100
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Covent Garden known as the Wine Vaults, owned by John Inge. Dunckerley
was not then in London, but he visited the group the following January. By then
the chapter had moved to the more elegant Turk’s Head Tavern in Soho. Dunck-
erley quickly became a member and an offi  cer, a path smoothed, if we can trust
his memory, by his previous familiarity with the degree. Others of the coterie
of reformers he ran with joined over the next few months, as did the then cur-
rent Grand Master of the Modern Grand Lodge, Lord Blayney (1720–75). Th us
began the curious, rather bifurcated relationship between the Holy Royal Arch
and the Modern Grand Lodge.
Whatever its origins, the Royal Arch appeared in the British Isles in the mid-
dle of the eighteenth century, where it was worked in lodges affi  liated with the
established Grand Lodges, and also by independent lodges which claimed the
right to meet from ‘Time Immemorial’ rather than from a Grand Lodge war-
rant. ‘Time Immemorial’ is a very fl exible term. While it implies great antiquity,
what it really means is that the lodges were already operating sometime before the
warrants were required – maybe in the mists of time, maybe last year. Th ere are
some constants in the presentation of the degree: Th e rituals of the Royal Arch are
dramatized in the rebuilding of Solomon’s Temple aft er its destruction by Nebu-
chadnezzar, a Babylonian king who reigned from around 605 bce to 562 bce.
And, no matter which group was working the Royal Arch, only Masons who had
already been raised to Master Mason (some required that he had also served as
Master of a lodge), were eligible for exaltation. Th is close connection has led some
to call the Royal Arch the completion of the Master’s Degree. Aft er the establish-
ment of the Ancient Grand Lodge in 1751, the Royal Arch was worked within
Ancient lodges. Because the working was offi  cially excluded from Modern Craft
lodges, it was either worked within with them without permission, or in closely
connected Royal Arch lodges, aft er 1769 called chapters. For a Royal Arch chapter
to exist, whatever Grand Lodge system it identifi ed with, it had to have a ready
supply of Master Masons, hence its routine pairing with Craft  lodges. In 1813,
when the rival Grand Lodges of England successfully negotiated Union, agree-
ment included provision for the Royal Arch thorough this verbal sleight-of-hand:
‘Pure and Ancient Masonry consists of three degrees, and no more, viz, those of
the Entered Apprentice, the Fellowcraft , and the Master Mason, including the
Supreme Order of the Holy Royal Arch.’ Subsequently, the pairing of Royal Arch
Chapter with a particular Craft  Lodge was made obligatory at Union in 1813, and
is part of the structure of the Supreme Grand Chapter of Royal Arch Masons of
England and Wales, as instituted in 1817. Aft er these basic points, writing about
the Royal Arch becomes a bit more chaotic.
Prior to the establishment of the Ancient Grand Lodge in 1751, lodges inde-
pendently chose whether to include the Royal Arch in their workings. However,
the Ancients explicitly endorsed the value of the Royal Arch, and warrants granted

--- Sayfa 114 ---
Provincial Grand Master of England
101
by that Grand Lodge included authority to work the degree. Laurence Dermott
famously wrote in the Ancients’ book of constitution, Ahiman Rezon, that he
believed the Royal Arch to be ‘the Root, Heart, and Marrow of Free-Masonry’.44
Th e Modern Grand Lodge, which had never offi  cially recognized or given sanc-
tion to the Royal Arch, came to terms with its popularity in another fashion. Just
as Dermott’s embrace of the Royal Arch became famous, so too the words of Mod-
ern Grand Secretary Samuel Spencer became infamous. He was reported to have
rejected an Ancient Mason’s application for charity by responding:
Your being an Antient Mason, you are not entitled to any of our Charity. Th e Antient
Masons have a Lodge at the Five Bells in the Strand, &c. Our Society is neither Arch,
Royal-Arch, or Antient, so that you have no right to Partake of our Charity.45
Spencer’s letter was seized upon by the Ancients as proof of the Moderns’ moral
debasement. Despite this categorical rejection, many of the offi  cers of the Mod-
ern Grand Lodge were personally quite enthusiastic about what James Heseltine
termed ‘a private and distinct society’.46 An indication of this unoffi  cial embrace
on the part of Grand Lodge offi  cials is the fact that Grand Master Lord Blayney
was not only exalted in the Royal Arch, but became the fi rst Most Excellent
Grand Master of the Royal Arch of Jerusalem. Th is was the body created when
Lord Blayney, Heseltine, Dunckerley and their Companions at the Turk’s Head
Tavern signed what they styled the Charter of Compact of Grand Chapter.
Th ere is some debate about the date of the signing. Most of the literature cites
22 July 22 1767, though Bernard Jones argues that the engrossed copy of the
Charter of Compact was altered aft er the fact to disguise the fact that Blayney
was simultaneously Grand Master of the Grand Lodge, and head of the Royal
Arch. Jones demonstrates that the proper date is 22 July 1766.47 By this docu-
ment the signatories claimed dominion, transforming their private Royal Arch
lodge into the Grand and Royal Chapter of the Royal Arch of Jerusalem. Th e
Charter enabled them to depute Most Excellent Grand Offi  cers, authorize the
creation of a seal, specify appropriate jewels and other regalia to be used by the
Grand Offi  cers, and lay out the rules determining who qualifi ed as a genuine
Royal Arch Mason, and who was an imposter. Most importantly, by the Charter
of Compact members of the Grand and Royal Chapter arrogated to themselves
the power to ‘constitute, superintend and regulate other Chapters wheresoever
shall be found convenient’.48 In other words, they claimed authority over it all.
Claiming authority and exercising it are distinct operations. It was to Lord
Blayney to claim authority, but Th omas Dunckerley appears to have been keen
to get on with the messy work of enforcement. Th e fi rst Royal Arch chapters
associated with the Grand and Royal Chapter received their constitutions in
January 1769. Amongst them was a chapter in Portsmouth which had at Dunck-
erley’s instigation written to request a constitution.49 He had been associated

--- Sayfa 115 ---
102
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
with working the Royal Arch there in Craft  lodges since at least 1757, and he
must have been pleased fi nally to put those Companions at right with Masonic
authorities.50 Dunckerley was poised to become the most energetic Royal Arch
fi eld operative in the Moderns’ quiver.
Despite the auspicious beginning of the Grand and Royal Chapter of the
Royal Arch of Jerusalem, it actually made only modest progress for the fi rst
several years. Its leadership largely overlapped with that of the Modern Grand
Lodge, and some of the members had additional responsibilities at various pri-
vate lodges and chapters. Even if they were all as unencumbered by employment
as Dunckerley, it would have strained their abilities to pursue this new inter-
est too energetically. However, they were not. For example, James Heseltine (d.
1796), one of the most active reformers and a stalwart Grand Lodge offi  cer, had
an active legal practice and was a proctor at Doctors’ Commons. Even Dunck-
erley was distracted by the civilian world, and still seeking paid employment he
entered into legal studies in 1770. As it turns out, nine chapters were granted
constitutions in 1769, though it is not at all clear that they all met. If we do
not count the constitution sent out for a chapter in Bengal in 1774, there was
only one other constitution granted for an English lodge before 1778. Th en
there was a gradual but steady growth in duly constituted Royal Arch Chap-
ters through the end of the century. Th ough the annual bounty never equalled
the number of constitutions sent out in 1769, there were some years before the
end of the century that came close to that total. Overall, more than 100 Royal
Arch Chapters were constituted between 1776 and 1800 – not a shabby thing
for an entirely new sort of Modern experience. Th e boost in provincial inter-
est – for they were nearly all provincial chapters – seems to have come with the
introduction of Grand Superintendents. Beginning in 1778, Grand Superinten-
dents were assigned to the various Masonic provinces. Th ough their position
was analogous to the Provincial Grand Masters in the Modern Grand Lodge few
were noblemen. Rather, they were senior and respected Masonic offi  cials: John
Allen, James Galloway, William White fi ls and, of course, Th omas Dunckerley.
Th ere is some ambiguity about when the Grand Superintendents actually took
up their offi  ces. Th e offi  cial date in 1778, but it is clear that some like Dunckerley
exercised the offi  ce as early as 1776. Of the thirty-four provinces on the English
mainland, Dunckerley held the grand superintendency of eighteen. Many of
Lord Blayney’s associates and successors in the leadership of the Royal Arch were
also drawn from this more socially modest class, including Th omas Dunckerley,
who tells us he served as the Grand Z of the Grand and Royal Chapter in 1767,
and Most Excellent Z of the Grand Chapter in 1791.51
Dunckerley’s career as a Grand Superintendent was, like his provincial grand
masterships, occasionally bumpy. Complaints sometimes came in that Dunck-
erley exceeded authority and mishandled funds. When this happened the

--- Sayfa 116 ---
Provincial Grand Master of England
103
letters were duly inscribed in the Excellent Grand and Royal Chapter minute
books, now held at the Library and Museum of Freemasonry. He was only rarely
scolded. Dunckerley’s fi rst recorded rebuke came relatively early in his tenure as
Grand Superintendent.52 On 12 December 1777 the Grand Chapter resolved to
send him a polite letter about exalting candidates at Colchester without a dis-
pensation.53 Dunckerley strongly denied it, and asserted that it was Colchester
Lodge No. 51 that had been improperly exalting Royal Arch candidates, as they
never applied for a constitution.54 Th ere were other clashes over the constitution
granted to the Chapter of Harmony at Salisbury in 1780, and a provisional char-
ter given to the First Royal Dragoons also stationed at Salisbury, who had been
exalting candidates gratis on authority from Scotland.55 Th e Grand Chapter
responded gently, by drawing up a regular patent or form for Grand Superin-
tendents to use, and also sent instructions ‘for regulating their Conduct’.56 In his
history of the Royal Arch, Hughan argues that Dunckerley’s ‘zeal may occasion-
ally have been in advance of his discretion’, but concludes that a close look at the
Grand Chapter minute books reveals Dunckerley was tremendously eff ective at
convincing provincial Masons to apply for constitutions. Hughan argues that
without Dunckerley’s eff orts the Grand Chapter would have had relatively little
business to transact.57 A scrutiny of the list of constitutions sent out between
the foundation of the Grand Chapter and Dunckerley’s death bears this out –
excluding the fi rst nine constitutions that were sent all at once, and the few sent
abroad, just over a third of the constituted chapters were in Dunckerley’s Royal
Arch provinces. Arguably, since he held half of the provinces, that fi gure should
have been higher. However, when the number of chapters he constituted is com-
pared to that of any of the other Grand Superintendents, his accomplishment
seems more impressive.
When it came to proselytizing, Dunckerley did not wait for the creation of a
specially designated offi  ce or prescribed forms. We have already seen that Dunck-
erley subscribed to the notion that it was nobler to plunge ahead, assuming
he had whatever legitimacy (or illegitimacy) he might need, and then apolo-
gize later if it came to that. Th is appears to have been his governing principle
when working the Royal Arch in Portsmouth in the 1750s, installing Sir Simon
Fraser in Quebec in 1760, and helping to hijack old No. 3 in London in 1766.
Th ose situations all turned out well, and Freemasonry was stronger for them.
As his Masonic career progressed, Dunckerley marshalled more knowledge and
authority on his side, and his assumptions became more audacious. In general,
Dunckerley appears to have assumed that in his enthusiasm for the Royal Arch,
he could simply fold this new role into his existing brief as Provincial Grand
Master for the Grand Lodge of England. To that end, we see him doubling-up
appearances. For example, in August 1789 he held a Provincial Grand Lodge
for Somerset timed to celebrate the birthday of the Prince of Wales. While he

--- Sayfa 117 ---
104
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
was there in Bridgwater, Dunckerley convened a Grand Chapter of Royal Arch
Masons the following day.58 Following his progress through the minutes of the
Grand Chapter, it is quite clear that Dunckerley cast his net widely, and if he
found Masons wanting to form a chapter, he petitioned the Grand Chapter to
send out a constitution while also requesting that it add that province to his
portfolio. He was not refused.59
An excellent illustration of Dunckerley’s unique brand of Masonic leader-
ship comes in 1769, at the Chapter of Friendship No. 3 in Portsmouth. Th is
was one of the very fi rst chapters to be granted a constitution by the new Grand
and Royal Chapter in London. It was also in Dunckerley’s home territory, and
undoubtedly many of the brothers knew him from his years in and around Ports-
mouth. Personally visiting and inspecting provinces and even lodges was part of
the notion of reform within the Grand Lodge which carried over into the Grand
Chapter, and which suited Dunckerley’s hands-on leadership perfectly. So it was
that he was there for the chapter meeting on 1 September 1769. According to
the minute book Dunckerley delivered the constitution himself, and then he did
something extra.60 Th is portion of the minute book, which was written in code,
notes:
AT A ROYAL ARCH CHAPTER
HELD AT THE GEORGE TAVERN IN PORTSMOUTH
ON FIRST SEPTR. SEVENTEEN HUNDRED AND
 SIXTY NINE= PRESENT THOMAS DUNKERLY ESQ
WILLIAM COOK ‘Z’ SAMUEL PALMER ‘H’ THOMAS SCANVILLE ‘J’
HENRY DEAN PHILIP JOYES AND THOMAS WEBB= THE ‘PRO G.M’.
THOMAS DUNCKERLEY BRO’T THE WARRANT OF THE CHAPTER
AND HAVING LATELY REC’D THE ‘MARK’ HE MADE THE BRE’N
‘MARK MASONS’ AND ‘MARK MASTERS’ AND EACH CHUSE THEIR
‘MARK’ …
ALSO TOLD US OF THIS MANN’R OF WRITING WHICH IS TO BE
USED IN THE DEGREE WICH WE MAY GIVE TO OTHERS SO THEY BE
FC FOR ‘MARK MASONS’ AND MASTER M FOR ‘MARK MASTERS’61
Th is entry is striking. Nothing in the creation of the Grand and Royal Chapter of
the Royal Arch of Jerusalem mentions anything about Mark Masonry. Dunck-
erley seems to be freelancing as, we have already noted he was wont to do. He
appears to have happened onto a couple of appealing degrees, the Mark Mason
and Mark Master, together with a nift y cipher, and passed them on to his deserv-
ing brethren as a lagniappe, a little gift  at the end of a transaction. Th ere is no
indication where Dunckerley acquired these degrees, or how widespread their
working was. Th e loss of records from the eighteenth century, and particularly
the disappearance of lodge or chapter minute books, means that these questions
are probably not answerable. Th is a wonderful example of Dunckerley’s interest

--- Sayfa 118 ---
Provincial Grand Master of England
105
in unusual degrees and orders, and suggests that we should be cautious about
eff usive statements about Dunckerley’s staunch orthodoxy. He was faithful to
the line laid down by the Grand Lodge, and also to that of the Grand and Royal
Chapter, insofar as their offi  cial practices enabled him to promote himself and
his own Masonic agenda. Aft er that, he might stray a bit. It should be noted that
while this is a very early documented making of a Mark Mason, J. M. Hamill has
pointed out an earlier mention of the Mark Mason degree(s) being worked in
England, in 1756 in Newcastle upon Tyne.62
By the end of the eighteenth century, the creation of the Grand and Royal
Chapter of the Royal Arch of Jerusalem gives the appearance of having been a
lark for the brothers in London, where it nevertheless failed to catch on. How-
ever, in the provinces, and especially amongst the Masons in Dunckerley’s orbit,
an abiding fondness for the degree took hold, seeing it through the troubles at
the end of the century when Freemasonry in general, and the anomalously posi-
tioned Royal Arch in particular, came under threat of outlawry as a result of the
Unlawful Societies Act of 1799.
Th omas Dunckerley, Esq., and ‘Pure Ancient Masonry’: A
Reappraisal
When Ron Chudley wrote his 1982 biography of Dunckerley, he chose for his
title, Th omas Dunckerley: A Remarkable Freemason. Chudley must surely be
correct, if we judge Dunckerley’s Masonic career by how much he personally
experienced and accomplished, given his very unlikely beginnings. Belonging
even to one lodge was an expensive thing in the mid-eighteenth century – one of
the primary complaints about the exotic degrees Dunckerley championed at the
end of the century was that Masonic brothers were depleting their already mea-
gre funds chasing the chimerical knowledge off ered by such degrees and orders.63
Dunckerley eventually managed to spend much of his mature life in the very
heart of Freemasonry, passing through dozens of lodges along the way. Alexan-
der Piatiogorsky claims that Dunckerley warranted eighty lodges, and formed
or was Master of at least sixty.64 Th ese numbers may be exaggerated, but give an
idea of the degree to which Dunckerley is identifi ed with Craft  Masonry in the
eighteenth century.
Given his history, it is curious to note that Dunckerley seems not to have
actually enjoyed staying with any one lodge for very long. In Freemasonry, as in
the Royal Navy, he was restless. C. D. Rotch did a little analysis of Dunckerley’s
attendance at the Lodge of Friendship No. 3 between 1767 and 1784.65 Th is,
you will recall, was the old lodge that Dunckerley and his cohort took posses-
sion of in 1767 as a base for future operations. What Rotch reveals is surprising,
fascinating and ultimately borne out in Dunckerley’s other personal lodge

--- Sayfa 119 ---
106
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
affi  liations. Rotch notes that Dunckerley’s attendance was very frequent in the
exciting early days. In 1767 he attended all thirteen meetings and in 1768 he
attended fi ft een out of nineteen. By 1769 his attention was waning; he attended
only six of ten. In 1770, the year he moved out to Hampton Court Palace, about
20 miles from Freemasons’ Hall, his attendance dropped to eight out of thirteen
meetings. Th e next year his attendance was about 50 per cent, and thereaft er he
virtually vanished. Dunckerley went to two meetings in 1772 and one each in
1776 and 1784. One might plausibly explain that he lived out of town, and that
his responsibilities as Provincial Grand Master and Grand Superintendent were
growing. Rotch also off ers a more clear-eyed appraisal of our hero: Dunckerley
bored easily, especially when he was no longer the dominating force in the lodge.
He enjoyed setting things up, and then going on to other challenges. Th e big
Masonic challenge for Dunckerley in the last decades of his life came in the form
of the higher degrees.

--- Sayfa 120 ---
– 107 –
8 APPENDANT ORDERS AND HIGHER
DEGREES
Despite his early and strong connection to Freemasonry, it was not always clear
that Dunckerley was going to devote himself exclusively to the Craft . We have
already seen his enthusiasm for the Sober Society of Portsmouth, and he fl irted
with other clubs as well. Th e eighteenth century was famously a time for clubs,
and Dunckerley may well have been a member of many societies about which we
have no knowledge. Of those we have discovered, surely none is more evocative
than his participation in the Honourable Society of Cymmrodorion, which he
had joined by 1778, when he appears on its membership list.1 Th e Cymmro-
dorion was established in 1751 as a base for Welshmen in London. It was an
initiatory society, fi rmly anchored in ritual, poetry and song. New members were
proposed and balloted, and the initiation fee and annual subscriptions went to
support the Welsh charity school and to underwrite publications in Welsh.2
Th e charity school was established by the Society of Ancient Britons, but the
Cymmrodorion was founded in part to assist in supporting it. Annual subscrip-
tions were instituted in 1777. Members of the Cymmrodorion had be Welsh or
of recent Welsh descent, be able to speak Welsh, and to be willing to sing and
versify during the meeting, at least under the infl uence of suffi  cient alcohol.3
Th e Cymmrodorion met monthly in taverns around London, and was a signifi -
cant magnet for London Welsh. Th e society also became a focal point for Welsh
cultural nationalists back in the homeland because Wales was profoundly rural
and lacked the sort of provincial capital Edinburgh provided for Scotland. At its
inception, the Cymmrodorion was intended to be a Welsh answer to the Royal
Society. Members were supposed to foster Welsh industry, promote Welsh lan-
guage and culture, and sponsor the education of deserving children of indigent
Welsh living in London. Ultimately, it was most notable as a sociable gathering
space for the Welsh colony in London.4
During the fi rst incarnation of the society, which lasted until 1787, member-
ship was fairly heterogeneous, in that it welcomed members of divergent social
and economic backgrounds, though religious Nonconformists were, by design,
thin in the ranks. Th is was the society Dunckerley joined. Published Cymmro-

--- Sayfa 121 ---
108
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
dorion records indicate the nature of the Welsh connection of members – town
or county of origin, or family association. Dunckerley is listed as being of Welsh
descent – which diff erentiates him from members merely married to a Welsh
woman, or who had a Welsh mother or father. Th is suggests that his Welsh iden-
tity extends at least as far back as a grandparent – the most obvious candidate
being his maternal grandmother, Elizabeth (Burgis) Bolnest, whom he mentions
in the accepted narrative, and who played a signifi cant role in his young life.
Th ere was at least one previous Cymmrodorion member named ‘Ynyr Burges’, so
the idea of Burgis being a name found in Wales is not entirely far-fetched.
Dunckerley also appears on the list of subscribers to the published 1775
St David’s Day Sermon, sponsored by the Society of Ancient Britons. While
this does not prove membership in the society, it is a strong corroboration of
Dunckerley’s identifi cation as a Welshman.5 However Dunckerley came by his
Welsh ancestry and language, this is an exciting connection – one which ties
him to the Romantic tradition of Welsh poesy which inspired Iolo Morganwg’s
‘druidic’ fraternalism not much later.6 Dunckerley was already an established
Freemason by the mid-1770s. Nonetheless, the allure of Welsh mythology and
fraternalism might well have had a powerful infl uence over his developing vision
of a sweeping Masonic system, full of what the Cymmrodorions called undeb a
brawdgarwch (unity and fraternity).7
Flirtations aside, Freemasonry remained his true love, and was the cause to
which he devoted his most productive energies. Dunckerley’s greater Masonic
vision must have developed over time and only becomes clear to us by the late
1780s. In its fi nal form, ‘grand’ is the best descriptor. Sweeping in scope, Dunck-
erley’s scheme was suitably ambitious for the purported son of a king, and for
the royal Craft . When we consider his Masonic trajectory, it is not clear how
or when he progressed from earnestness, to opportunism to genuine vision – or
whether all three motivations were there to varying degrees throughout. Still, as
he moved through Masonry, Dunckerley acquired degrees, orders and various
grand masterships until, by the early 1790s, he held in his hand a vast Masonic
empire, stretching across southern England and on to North America – all owing
ultimate allegiance through him to his royal ‘kin’, the titular heads and patrons
of the orders. In this empire of his making and remaking, Dunckerley was Past
Senior Grand Warden of the Grand Lodge, Provincial Grand Master for eight
English counties, Grand Superintendent of the Grand and Royal Chapter of
the Royal Arch of Jerusalem for eighteen English counties, the founder of three
lodges on board ships of the Royal Navy and many more on land, Grand Com-
mander of the Society of Ancient Masons and of the Diluvian Order of Royal
Ark and Mark Mariners, and Grand Master of the Most Noble and Exalted
Religious and Military Order of Masonic Knights Templar of Saint John of Jeru-
salem. Not bad for a porter’s son.

--- Sayfa 122 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
109
Dunckerley Goes on Crusade
Scholars of the development of modern Masonic orders have proven, beyond
any shadow of even the smallest doubt, that there is no historical connec-
tion between the medieval Knights Templar, or Knights Hospitallers, and the
Masonic degrees and orders with similar names that appeared in Britain, Europe
and North America in the eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries. Even in
the eighteenth century, only the most credulous would have asserted otherwise.
However, the Romantic Era began early in England and with it a fascination
for all things knightly. Unlike the Craft  degrees, the Royal Arch and the Mark
degrees, all of which take the Temple in Jerusalem as a setting, the degrees asso-
ciated with the Knights Templar are explicitly Christian, and are set against the
backdrop of that ne plus ultra of medieval romance, the Crusades. And so we
fi nd Th omas Dunckerley’s own, very Romantic, account of the history of the
Masonic Knights Templar in the Freemasons’ Magazine in 1794.8 Th ough it is
a terrifi c story, it has nothing to do with actual history. Dunckerley relates that
the Knights Templar were actually Jewish Freemasons at the time of Zerubba-
bel’s rebuilding of Solomon’s Temple, around 520 bce. About 1,000 years later,
they became Knights of the East and Royal Arch Masons. Th ey were remark-
able for their ‘retired life and simplicity of manners, their sobriety and charity’,
and they called themselves the Essenes. Aft er a few hundred years, during which
they adopted the practice of electing Grand Masters, they became Christians
and Masonic Knights. When the Crusaders arrived, they found ready allies in
these Freemasons, who as Knights of the Eastern Star assisted in the conquest of
Jerusalem, with the aid of their secret signs, which protected them from being
surprised by the enemy. In 1103, Geoff rey of Bouillon entrusted them with care
of the Holy Sepulchre, dubbed them Knights of the Rosae Cruces, and gave
them authority over all Christian pilgrims. In 1118, Baldwin II instituted them
as the Knights Templar of St John of Jerusalem, ‘in which he incorporated seven
Knights Rosae Cruces’. Aft er the conclusion of the Ninth Crusade in the late
thirteenth century, Edward I brought the Knights Templar, together with the
Knights of the East and West, ‘home’ to England, where they lived more or less
contentedly ever aft er. Th ere were also Knights Kadosh, but Dunckerley writes
cryptically that their history may not be divulged.
Th is fantastical tale is marvellous, but it is clearly not history. Rather, it is
advertising. If you do not understand the language, then you are not in the target
market. When he wrote this sketch, Th omas Dunckerley was already the fi rst
‘Grand Master by the Will of God, of the Most Noble and Exalted Religious and
Military Order of Masonic Knights Templar of Saint John of Jerusalem’, and was
preparing his Knights for a modern Crusade – war against the infi del French.9
Th is represents quite an intellectual shift  for Dunckerley, who had always been

--- Sayfa 123 ---
110
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
keen to fi ght the French, but who had previously approached this long-standing
imperial contest from a distinctly secular and practical basis. Beginning in 1749
with his Sober Society essays, Dunckerley’s few personal writings indicate that
he had a fairly conventional enlightened, even rationalist, approach to Chris-
tianity.10 He argued for broad religious toleration, and for assuming personal
responsibility for one’s moral code. Th is fi ts nicely with the values generally
espoused by eighteenth-century Craft  lodges, which required only that initi-
ates believe in a supreme being, and placed an emphasis on helping individual
Masons develop into their own moral agents. As the decades passed, Duncker-
