ley’s personal beliefs, consonant with those of English society in general, became
more socially and religiously conservative, even puritanical.
With the onset of the French Revolution, English society took on a nerv-
ous demeanour, and increasingly sought refuge in political, religious and moral
conservatism. Th e French were perceived as a serious threat, but just as fright-
ening was the threat of rising radical movements within Britain itself, and this
immediate danger jolted many respectable Britons into a more sober frame of
mind with regard to the dangers of Enlightenment liberty and libertinism run
amok.11 In Dunckerley’s mind, fear of the French abroad and radicals at home
was readily translated into an even stronger opposition to the Ancient Grand
Lodge. Th is was not an unreasonable leap, as the potential for radicalism and
Ancient Freemasonry were connected in fact as well as in Dunckerley’s imagina-
tion. Th e Ancients appealed particularly to men who found themselves on the
periphery of accepted society in England – labourers and artisans alienated by
the changes already being wrought by the early Industrial Revolution, soldiers
from the Celtic fringe and Irish immigrants. Th ese were also the men who were
most suspected of radical activism, especially of the sort exemplifi ed in France by
the topsy-turvy rule of the Jacobins. Perhaps in response to these varied stimuli,
Dunckerley turned some of his prodigious Masonic creativity towards overtly
Christian paths, and he fi xed his gaze on what had been up until then the rela-
tively obscure order of the Masonic Knights Templar.
When Dunckerley took ‘refuge in the Christian chivalric degrees’, there
was only a handful of Knights Templar encampments scattered across England,
most working in conjunction with Royal Arch chapters.12 An example can be
found in Dunckerley’s own early experimentation with Templar Masonry in
1778, when he authorized the Royal Arch Chapter of Friendship No. 257 to
introduce a Knights Templar degree into their workings. Th e master, or ‘Z’, of
the chapter, ‘read a letter from Companion Dunckerley, that we might make
Knight Templars [sic] if we wanted and it was resolved to’.13 Th ere was consider-
able variety in the degrees adopted by individual encampments, so once again
Dunckerley’s assumption of authority meant at least an attempt to enforce
uniformity.14 By the end of 1791, Dunckerley commanded a full order, and its

--- Sayfa 124 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
111
degrees are roughly laid out in his historical sketch of the Knights of the Tem-
ple. First come the necessary antecedents, the Craft  degrees and the Mark are
implied in the history of the Temple; then Dunckerley lists the Knights of the
East and Royal Arch Masons (as companion degrees), Knights of the Eastern
Star, Knights Rosae Cruces (in seven points), Knights Templar of St John of
Jerusalem (possibly with nine points), Knights Hospitallers (encompassing the
Order of St Lazarus, Knights of Rhodes, Knights of Malta), Knights of the East
and West (who become Kings of the East and West), Knights of the Temple of
Palestine (identifi ed as the sixth degree), and fi nally the Knights Kadosh, which
Dunckerley calls the seventh degree.15 He added at the end that those Knights
who had served in the Crusades (or who had taken on that persona through
their Masonic initiation) were to wear a gold cross.
Counting degrees within an order can be a tricky thing, as this list dem-
onstrates. In Masonic numerology, seven is a signifi cant number.16 One of
Dunckerley’s earliest Knights Templar encampments is named the Observance
of the Seven Degrees in London. Others that use the number are Eminent of
the Seven Degrees at Bristol, Harmony of the Seven Degrees and Science of the
Seven Degrees, both in Salisbury, and Royal Edward of the Seven Degrees at
Hampton Court Palace. John Yarker, a nineteenth-century Masonic writer fas-
cinated with all things esoteric, agrees with Dunckerley, calling this a system of
seven steps, but it is easy to see room for elaboration into a much more com-
plex progression.17 Indeed, it seems likely that Dunckerley’s close associate in
Cornwall, John Knight, did just that. By 1806, Knight presided over a rite with
twenty-six progressive degrees, which some researchers have tried to attribute
directly to Dunckerley without much success. J. M. Hamill, who reviewed the
so-called ‘Dunckerley Rituals’ at Redruth in Cornwall, concluded that although
these are rituals and degrees with which Dunckerley was probably familiar, he
was certainly not their author.18
Most of the Knights Templar bodies in England would have traced their
roots to founding members who were initiated in Ireland or Scotland, or while
abroad with the military. Th e great wars of the eighteenth century, especially
the Seven Years’ War, moved vast numbers of men across seas and continents,
exposing them to a variety of Masonic and pseudo-Masonic degrees and ritu-
als. It would be surprising had English Masons not forsaken a chaste love of
‘pure ancient Masonry’, as defi ned by the United Grand Lodge at Union in
1813, and picked up a taste for exotic degrees. As a result, through paths we
probably cannot retrace with any accuracy, there were eight known Knights
Templar encampments in England by 1790. Two asserted seniority. One was
the encampment at Bristol (unnumbered), whose Charter of Compact is dated
1780, though that date was probably an early nineteenth-century fabrication.
Bristol claimed time immemorial status.19 Th e other was the Encampment of

--- Sayfa 125 ---
112
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Redemption No. 5 at York.20 Other encampments were London Encampment
of Observance of the Seven Degrees No. 1, Bath Royal Cumberland No. 2, Col-
chester No. 4, Dorchester Durnovarian No. 6 and Biddeford (original spelling)
Trine No. 7.21 Most interestingly, one encampment, Fortitude No. 3, was the
Templar equivalent of a regimental lodge, and was attached to the First Regi-
ment of Dragoon Guards, stationed in Suff olk in 1791.
We do not know where or when Th omas Dunckerley was initiated into the
Knights Templar, though he claimed to have been exalted in the closely related
Royal Arch as early as 1754.22 Surely he was initiated before he authorized the
Royal Arch Chapter of Friendship No. 257 to work the degree in 1778. Th ere
are other scattered references to early initiations. John Knight and Richard
Smith, both active in various Masonic contexts in Redruth, Cornwall, reported
that they were initiated as Knights Templars in 1777 and 1778, respectively.23 In
early 1791 the Knights Templar encampment at Bristol (which took the name
Baldywn sometime thereaft er) concluded Dunckerley would make a fi t leader,
and invited him to be Grand Master. When Dunckerley heard ‘grand’, he thought
‘nationwide’. So he accepted the off er, and parlayed this apparently singular invi-
tation into the creation of the Grand Conclave of the Royal, Exalted, Religious
and Military Order of H.R.D.M., Grand Elected Masonic Knights Templar,
K.D.S.H. of Saint John of Jerusalem, Palestine, Rhodes, etc., with himself as the
fi rst Grand Master.24 Because there was no budget for new orders, the Knights
Templar organization shared both a Grand Master (Dunckerley) and headquar-
ters (Dunckerley’s apartments at Hampton Court Palace) with another newly
established order, Royal Ark Mariners. Under his assiduous cultivation, the
eight original encampments were joined within a year by encampments in Ports-
mouth, Redruth, Hereford and Salisbury. Others soon followed as the number
of Knights Templar encampments increased steadily until Dunckerley’s death
in 1795. As new encampments were added, Dunckerley reassigned numbers, so
that by 1794 encampments in London, York, Bristol and Bath were considered
‘immemorial’, and were left  unnumbered. Th eir previous numbers were reas-
signed to other early encampments, and so on down the list, chronologically.
In 1805, aft er a ten-year hiatus, the order resumed its growth, which continued
through the nineteenth century.
In his enthusiastic, even nostalgic, embrace of the Knights Templar, Dunck-
erley dug deep into powerful memories from his childhood at Somerset House
in the metaphorical shadows of the Temple Church (consecrated in 1185). He
had already reinforced his personal connection with the Knights Templar in
1770 when he chose to enter the Inner Temple to study law. In 1608, James I
granted a charter to the Inner and Middle Temples, giving them possession of
the Temple Church and its grounds in perpetuity. In return, they maintained the
church and churchyards. Men affi  liated with the law societies were oft en buried

--- Sayfa 126 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
113
on Temple grounds, and Dunckerley was no doubt thinking of this when he
included the following request in his 1795 will: ‘First I desire to be decently
buried in the Temple Church near the Knights Templars [sic] if I should die
in London or at Hampton Court.’25 It did not work out that way, but in his
earnest request we can see how profoundly he had been shaped by his Masonic
ambitions. Dunckerley was no longer the retired gunner enthused by what was
essentially administrative reform. Rather, Dunckerley imagined a reinvigorated
Modern Grand Lodge spreading its benevolent aegis over a comprehensive sys-
tem of complementary degrees, with himself somewhere very near the apex of its
hierarchy. In this quest, he was motivated not only by love of the Craft , or even
by his own considerable ambition, but also by an abiding hostility to the Ancient
Grand Lodge, and a persistent fear of all external threats to the peace and sta-
bility of England, especially those coming from Revolutionary France. As we
have seen, in his battle against the burgeoning popularity of the Ancient Grand
Lodge, Dunckerley’s primary weapons were enthusiasm, versatility and, above
all, an acknowledged appetite for organization. He believed that the Knights
Templar were his best weapon against the French.
For those who know something of Dunckerley’s approach with other degrees,
notably the Royal Arch, his mode of operation with regard to the Knights Tem-
plar sounds remarkably familiar. Immediately upon his acceptance of Bristol’s
off er, Dunckerley began sounding out other encampments with the suggestion
that unity would certainly be benefi cial for the order.26 As he wrote in March
1791 to the encampment at York, ‘I suppose there are many more Encampments
in England, which with God’s permission I may have the happiness to revive &
assist.’27 By 1794 this not-so-subtle nudging revived or established twenty-four
encampments under his leadership.28 His enthusiasm for organization bordered
on an obsession, and was perhaps the most immediately useful gift  Dunckerley
bestowed on Freemasonry and its appendant orders – he enforced organization,
unity, uniformity of practice and, best of all for historians, mandated records.
Already in the spring of 1791 he insisted on issuing warrants (or constitutions)
for encampments, even those which had existed from time immemorial. Dunck-
erley took it badly that his home encampment at Bristol neglected to apply for
a warrant and failed to send the requisite fees, though he held out high hopes
for other encampments.29 Dunckerley laid out a schedule of fees to be paid for
initiation, designed appropriate robes for himself as Grand Master, designed and
printed certifi cates, requested lists of members, their ages and occupations and
established regulations for the order.30 An indication of his great hopes for the
prominence of the order can be found in his insistence that Knights Templar
have full uniforms, not merely identifying accessories:

--- Sayfa 127 ---
114
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Th e coat will take 14 buttons – ten in front and four for the hips and skirts with
two very small gilt buttons at the opening of each sleeve and a white Kerseymere
Waistcoat and white French basket buttons – with black breeches ... cock’d Hats and
Cockades are worn with Swords and black velvet Stocks.31
Th ese were to be worn with sashes, rather than aprons, and Dunckerley sent
along fabric swatches and sample buttons, to clarify his intent. In a notable
departure from contemporary Masonic practice, Dunckerley adopted his wife,
Hannah Dunckerley, as the ‘Lady Patroness’ of the order. She obligingly began
signing her Masonic correspondence ‘Sister Dunckerley’.32
Th omas Dunckerley was by this time an old man – several years older than
the seventy to which he admitted. Th us it comes as a bit of a surprise to read the
printed circular he sent to encampments in April 1794, asking all Knights Tem-
plar to ready themselves for battle in defence of the motherland should Britain’s
enemies (France) stage an invasion.33 Th e substance of the circular was printed
in the press.34 He urged them to enlist as a body, under the name Prince Edward’s
Royal Volunteers aft er Prince Edward (1767–1820), Duke of Kent and fourth
son of George III, who was the Grand Patron of the order. Th ey were to be dis-
tinguished in battle by wearing a token, the Knights Templar cross on a black
ribbon fi xed through buttonholes. Dunckerley himself expressed his willingness
to serve alongside them. While some historians have dismissed Dunckerley’s
appeal as high-fl own rhetoric, this seems not to have been the case.35 Dunckerley
was fond of noble-sounding oratory, but his patriotism was genuine, and the
circular was such an unusual step that one is tempted to take him at his word. If
called, he surely would have served.
Th e Strange History of the Royal Ark Mariners
Two hundred years is long enough to bring an event into sharper focus, or alter-
nately, to muddle it completely. From this vantage, it seems clear that the Society
of Antient Masons of the Diluvian Order, or Royal Ark and Mark Mariners, was
established as a maritime counterpart to the Masonic Knights Templar. Aft er
all, the French surely had to come by sea. Actually, they considered an airborne
assault using balloons, but Sophie Blanchard (1778–1819), appointed by Napo-
leon in 1805 to study the plan’s feasibility, apparently recommended against it
on account of prevailing air currents. Th ere is no proper biography of Blanchard
in English; her fascinating life appears only in snippets in works on women pio-
neers and early fl ight.36
Th e present study of Th omas Dunckerley had its roots in an exploration of
the labyrinthine Masonic career of Dr Ebenezer Sibly (1751–99), who around
1788 or 1789 became Dunckerley’s go-to man for things beyond the Pale of
Masonic propriety. Sibly’s connection to Dunckerley is demonstrated by the

--- Sayfa 128 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
115
1789 dedication to Dunckerley of his new and largely plagiarized edition of
Culpeper’s English Physician and Complete Herbal. Sibly’s addition to the classic
work was a section on practical home treatments, much of which is a verbatim
copy of William Buchan’s Domestic Medicine or the Family Physician.37 Ebenezer
Sibly’s devotion to Th omas Dunckerley is one of the more remarkable aspects
of their relationship, and given the key role Sibly plays in both the development
of the Royal Ark Mariners and in laying the groundwork for a possible ladies’
order, a brief history of their association might be helpful. Ebenezer Sibly was
very much a fringe character, occupying an outer orbit in nearly every circle he
joined. Like Dunckerley, Sibly was deeply involved in personal reinvention and,
like Dunckerley, Sibly accumulated Masonic degrees and affi  liations avidly. Both
men probably shared a taste for provincial styles in ritual and pageantry. Th is
makes all sorts of sense, since they spent their formative years as Freemasons
well outside London, and in the heterogeneous Masonic milieus of the west-
ern port towns of Portsmouth, Plymouth and Bristol. Unlike Dunckerley, Sibly
did not discriminate between Ancient and Modern lodges, so sorting through
his Masonic trajectory is particularly daunting. A London boy from a family
of booksellers and shoemakers, Sibly nonetheless began his Masonic career in
Portsmouth, where he was initiated in June 1784 into Lodge No. 79, an Ancient
lodge.38 By December 1784, Sibly was in Bath, where as an ‘Ancient Mason re-
made’ he appears as a visitor to the Modern Lodge of Perfect Friendship No.
230.39 In May 1785, Sibly was admitted as a member.40 However, by then Sibly
was already transferring his operation to Bristol, and in April 1785 he was exalted
in the Bristol Royal Arch Chapter of Charity No. 9, affi  liated with the Lodge of
Hospitality No. 296, a Modern lodge warranted in 1769.41 In June 1785, Sibly
joined the Sea Captains’ Lodge No. 445, a Modern lodge, also in Bristol.42 A
month later, he was initiated into the Bristol Encampment of Knights Templar.
Sibly’s subsequent involvement in a fraudulent lottery scheme was followed
by an ugly exposure in the local newspaper, Felix Farley’s Bristol Journal, which
forced him to wrap up his aff airs in Bristol in 1786.43 He resigned from the Sea
Captains’ Lodge in March of that year. Curiously, the fi rst time we can actually
place Sibly and Dunckerley in the same room is at the Chapter of Charity on 2
June 1786, aft er Sibly had already fallen into disgrace and just before he left  town.44
Th is suggests that from the beginning of their relationship Dunckerley knew Sibly
was a rogue, in for whatever benefi ts might accrue. Th e last mention of Sibly in
the Chapter of Charity minutes comes in December 1787. Moving on to Lon-
don, Sibly helped establish No. 253, an Ancient lodge meeting at the Globe Inn,
becoming its fi rst master in April 1789.45 Given Dunckerley’s outspoken disap-
proval of the Ancients, it seems odd that he would strike up a partnership with this
disreputable Mason – one who readily moved from one Grand Lodge affi  liation to
the other, unless he found him useful, as perhaps he did in Ipswich.

--- Sayfa 129 ---
116
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
It is beyond the scope of this study to discover the origins of the Royal Ark
Mariners’ degree. It is suffi  cient to note that although the symbols and ritual
of the degree draw on that same fund of traditional religious and artisanal
symbolism beloved by Freemasons throughout the eighteenth century, its fi rst
documented appearances in England come in 1790 at Bath in Somerset and
Ipswich in Suff olk.46 Th e Bath manifestation appears relatively straightforward.
Th e Knights Templar Encampment of Antiquity at Bath records that, in 1790,
William Boyce ‘took all the degrees of ye Red Cross, also Royal Ark Mariners’.47
Th is was at about the same time as Th omas Dunckerley’s assumption of the
grand mastership of the Masonic Knights Templar, making a neat connection
between Dunckerley and the Royal Ark Mariners degree. Th e Royal Ark Mari-
ners, with their ritual and symbolism plausibly intended as a naval counterpart
to the Knights Templar, made a logical and attractive addition to Dunckerley’s
quiver. Historian Aubrey Newman has suggested that Dunckerley was break-
ing new ground by experimenting with using both the Knights Templar and the
Royal Ark Mariners to enhance military recruitment at the time of the French
Revolution – he was aft er all an old military man.48 However, appearance of the
Royal Ark Mariners in Ipswich adds another, and far more intriguing, dimen-
sion to Dunckerley’s penchant for ‘adaptive reuse’ of Freemasonry – this time
using excitement about higher degrees as a political vehicle.
In 1790, Sibly would not have been an obvious choice as a campaign man-
ager in Ipswich. He had no experience in politics, and probably did not know
the candidates who hired him, though it is likely that Sir John Hadley D’Oyly
(1754–1818), who had extensive properties near Lymington in Hampshire, was
acquainted with Dunckerley. Both candidates and manager were apparently
unperturbed by these obstacles. So it was that Sibly was engaged to manage
the parliamentary campaign of Sir John Hadley D’Oyly, 6th baronet, and his
brother-in-law, Major George Rochfort, who stood jointly for Ipswich, in Suf-
folk.49 Sibly’s campaign strategy was ingenious and strictly against Masonic rules
as laid out by Anderson in the 1723 Constitutions.50 He established a lodge of
Royal Ark Masons, giving all the appearances of being a trial balloon for the
Royal Ark Mariners, who appear in London just a bit later, and for whom Sibly
played deputy to Dunckerley’s Grand Commander. In Ipswich, though, Sibly
built an Ark, ‘moored’ it at the Bear & Crown Tavern, invited the freemen in,
and they talked of nothing but politics for the next year and a half. Th ere is no
indication that D’Oyly was a Freemason, so it follows that he was unperturbed
by the irregularity.
Th e presentation of Royal Ark Masons in Ipswich was quite diff erent from
the situation in Bath, despite ample evidence connecting the two. Rather than a
degree being worked within an existing local Knights Templar encampment, we
fi nd the rapid development over the course of just a few months of a full- or even

--- Sayfa 130 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
117
over-blown dedicated lodge with perhaps as many as 200 members.51 In Ipswich,
where all sworn freemen had the vote, Sibly aggressively recruited only freemen
for his new lodge, announcing that they were thus sworn to support their breth-
ren in all things, as the by-laws state unambiguously:
Art19th Th at if any member of the sd clubb should refuse to vote for any such person
as the sd committee, or the majority thereof shall nominate as candidates to serve in
Parliament for the Borough of Ipswich, such member or members so refusing, shall
be excluded, as unworthy members thereof.52
Exclusion meant not being able to access the attractive array of cash benefi ts
off ered by the lodge, towards which the initiate had already contributed. Th is
stratagem successfully returned D’Oyly to offi  ce, though he reportedly spent
£20,000 on the seat, a ruinous sum, with much of it funnelled through the lodge
accounts at Royal Ark Mason Bro. John Spooner’s bank. Revealingly, the 21st
article in the ‘Articles of the Samaritan or Ark Mason Club or Society’ specifi es
that £2,000 would be put into the hands ‘of any person whom the Freemen shall
appoint to hold as security for the performance of these articles’.53 Th at person
was Sibly.
Ebenezer Sibly stayed in Ipswich for about a year aft er the parliamentary
election, continuing to build up the lodge with the goal of permanently securing
the borough for D’Oyly’s interest. Aft er successfully managing the municipal
elections in September 1791, Sibly quietly folded his tent and left  town with
the remaining money.54 He spent most of his haul immediately on the purchase
of a medical degree from the University of Aberdeen, and then set up shop in
London for the manufacture of his patented elixir, Dr Sibly’s Reanimating Solar
Tincture.55 Aside from Sibly’s newfound prosperity, the most telling confi rma-
tion of this scam is recorded a year later, when Sibly’s partisans were turned out
of their local offi  ces. William Batley, long-time town clerk, writes, ‘Th e effi  gy of
Father Noah (Sibley) was burnt at the Cornhill and a great many who had been
stiled his dutiful children joined & assisted in the ceremony.’56 Sibly made quite
an impression on the locals.
Despite his rough ways, Sibly provides the critical connection between
Ipswich Royal Ark Mariners and those that appear in London by 1793. More crit-
ically, there is also a solid body of evidence connecting Sibly to Dunckerley, who
was in turn the Provincial Grand Master of everything Masonic in Somerset –
neatly connecting all three early Ark Mariner ‘moorings’. Sibly was an enthusiastic
Mason, an experienced writer and a student of arcana, all of which suited him to
the task of fashioning a ritual for the degree. In terms of documentary evidence,
there are two early Ark Mariner rituals with connections to Sibly in the collec-
tions of the Library and Museum of Freemasonry. Neither is in Sibly’s hand, but
both are described ‘as handed down from Noah to the present Era and carefully

--- Sayfa 131 ---
118
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
transcribed from ancient Records by Ebenezer Sibly D[eputy].G[rand].N[oah].,
1790’.57 Th e symbolism of ark and dove and rainbow specifi ed in the manuscript
rituals also appears in contemporary descriptions of the trappings used by Sibly
in Ipswich.58 Most conclusively, Dunckerley appointed Sibly ‘Deputy Grand
Noah’ to his own Grand Commander of the Society of Ancient Masons of the
Diluvian Order of Royal Ark Mariners in 1793.59
What this digression makes clear is that the Ipswich Royal Ark Mason aff air
was not, as this author has mistakenly argued several times, Sibly’s renegade
money-making scheme. As more information comes to light, that seems a rather
naive hope. Dunckerley was such a busy-body he could not have missed what
was going on in Ipswich. He either arranged the charade himself, or was at least
actively engaged in looking the other way. We must remember that Dunckerley
was the local Masonic authority in East Anglia. Since the death of Duncker-
ley’s friend Rowland Holt in 1786, Suff olk had been without a Provincial Grand
Master.60 In 1790, Th omas Dunckerley was Grand Superintendent of the Royal
Arch in Suff olk and Essex, and Provincial Grand Master in Essex. Within a few
months he became the Grand Master over the Knights Templar Encampment
attached to the First Regiment of Dragoon Guards, then stationed nearby. In
other words, Th omas Dunckerley was certainly in a position to intervene had he
been so inclined. Th is has long puzzled Dunckerley’s admirers, leading Masonic
historian Christopher Haff ner to muse about why Dunckerley apparently did
not reprimand Sibly for his carrying-on in Ipswich, but instead turned around
fairly rapidly and appointed him his deputy.61
What makes the situation even more remarkable is that William Middleton,
one of the defeated parliamentary candidates, was the presumptive Provincial
Grand Master of Suff olk. He had been appointed in 1788, but was not installed
until aft er the 1790 election. One wonders about the delay. Although the local
Masons complained, as they did rather vocally in a post-election pamphlet enti-
tled, A Serious Address to the Members of the House of Commons and Gentlemen
Residing in the Counties of Suff olk, Norfolk and Essex, Dunckerley’s infl uence
probably helped discourage legal action – though both sides engaged lawyers
preemptively.62
Th e inevitable conclusion is that since Dunckerley could have called a halt to
Sibly’s scheme had he wanted, he must have somehow stood to benefi t person-
ally from the situation as it transpired. A clue to what he might have had in mind
can be found in the closing page of A Serious Address, when the author warns:
A person who is a confi dential friend to Ebenezer Sibly, aft er speaking in the highest
terms of the stability of the samaritan club, and the impossibility of overturning it by
any means whatsoever; declared it was a fi xed intention and design to erect a club or
society of the same kind in every corporation throughout the kingdom.63

--- Sayfa 132 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
119
Ipswich was clearly an experiment and, on some level, must have been reckoned
quite successful. Both Dunckerley and Sibly were persistently short of funds,
and their Masonic activities did not pay for themselves. D’Oyly was willing to
pay handsomely for a seat in Parliament. As a nabob, he had cash at his disposal
and he went through it at an astonishing pace.64 If only a fraction of the £20,000
D’Oyly reportedly paid out for this election made its way into Dunckerley’s
and Sibly’s pockets, or into other Masonic coff ers, so much the better. It seems
entirely possible that Dunckerley was intrigued by the notion of securing the
election of candidates loyal to the crown and sympathetic to Freemasonry. If
nothing else, this was a splendid trial run for the new ritual. Th e symbols and
terminology worked, but Sibly apparently had to drop the night-time torchlight
processions and the initiation requirement that involved drinking an alchemical
concoction he called aurum potabilis, a potion laced with edible gold.65 Th at was
just a bit too weird.
Sister Dunckerley
Aft er Ipswich, Dunckerley seems to have required only one further token of
Sibly’s loyalty, and that was the publication of a book advocating precisely the
sort of half-way variety of ladies’ masonry Dunckerley had been quietly experi-
menting with ad hoc for several years. Dunckerley was getting old, he was not in
good health – he had an ambitious programme planned, and the time had come
for bold steps, which were something of a specialty for Ebenezer Sibly.
One of the earliest offi  cial documents of the Grand Lodge in London aft er
its formation in 1717 was James Anderson’s 1723 Constitutions, which gave the
status of women vis-à-vis Freemasonry quite unambiguously:
Th e persons admitted Members of a Lodge must be good and true Men, free-born,
and of mature and discreet Age, no Bondmen, no Women, no immoral or scandalous
Men, but of good Report.66
Th is sentence seems intended to exclude all sorts of troublemakers from the
lodge, especially women. As Freemasonry spread from Scotland and England
to the Continent it developed distinctly national characteristics. Th us, a signal
feature of the diff erences between British and French Freemasons in the eight-
eenth century was their respective attitudes towards the inclusion of women
in the Masonic fellowship: Th e French did include women to an extent, in co-
Masonic and adoptive lodges, and the British did not. But a closer examination
of Th omas Dunckerley’s Masonic career and writings suggests that the distinc-
tion is neither so clear nor as absolute as we have allowed ourselves to believe.67 It
now appears that Dunckerley gave serious consideration to adding the so-called
‘Adoption’ to his overarching scheme of Masonic orders. Such rites for women

--- Sayfa 133 ---
120
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
are termed ‘adoptive’ because female candidates are sponsored or adopted by a
Master Mason, who speaks for their eligibility and typically guides or accompa-
nies them through initiation ceremonies. At least one of Dunckerley’s English
contemporaries also put forward a book advocating the unlimited admission of
women into Freemasonry. Th e book was not well received, though when author
George Smith was expelled from Freemasonry in 1785, it was for forging charity
certifi cates.68
Such a bold proposal might seem out of character for Dunckerley, who is reg-
ularly portrayed as the epitome of Masonic propriety and regularity. However,
we have already noted that in his enthusiastic promotion of the Royal Ark, the
Mark degrees and other appendant orders, Dunckerley was more than willing to
bypass inconvenient aspects of Masonic propriety. He was supremely confi dent
that he could gain at least grudging acceptance once his experiments turned out
to benefi t the Modern Grand Lodge. Aft er all, he was only doing it for them.
Once again, we turn to Sadler for the accepted interpretation of Duncker-
ley’s approach to gender, and he does not disappoint. Sadler presents Dunckerley
as what he wanted him to be – a model for Victorian masculinity. Sadler focuses
on Dunckerley’s naval career, and presents him to us as an upright and correct
naval offi  cer who had seen much of the world and viewed life with good humour
and sympathy.69 He was a man’s man, and in his element in a man’s world. What
Sadler ignores is the importance of women in Dunckerley’s life. Dunckerley’s
actual history demonstrates that he was surrounded and strongly infl uenced by
women, both as a child and in his family life aft er he returned from sea. Duncker-
ley’s domestic life was populated by the women and (mostly female) children of
his extended family, neighbours at both Somerset House and Hampton Court
Palace and also by the wives of his Masonic colleagues. Sister Hannah Dunck-
erley took an active interest in Th omas’s Masonic life, thus it is not surprising
that we fi nd Dunckerley inviting women into Masonic venues, addressing them
in Masonic lectures, and exploring the possibility of introducing a female order.
One of Dunckerley’s most infl uential Masonic publications was a charge
delivered in 1769 to Lodge No. 429 in Marlborough, Wiltshire and it serves as
a good a template for his approach to women in a Masonic context.70 Th e three
great Masonic virtues are brotherly love, truth and relief (or charity). Charity
was the virtue closest to Dunckerley’s heart, and whenever possible his Masonic
events featured both charitable acts and lectures on charity. Th e Marlborough
meeting had both, and is also the earliest public evidence we have of Dunckerley
intentionally inviting women to participate in the Masonic experience, albeit in
a carefully controlled environment. Th e event was not a regular lodge meeting,
but an assembly gathered specifi cally so that Dunckerley could lead the newly
constituted lodge in a formal distribution of charity to twenty-four deserving
poor people, and also relate this specifi c act of charity to the larger eff ects of

--- Sayfa 134 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
121
Masonic charity on the whole of society, of which the ladies in the audience were
the most important part. Dunckerley is particular about that point. Th rough his
lecture he makes striking use of contemporary gender stereotypes, portraying
men as needing the supportive environment of the lodge to achieve and then
improve masculine virtues, while reassuring the ladies, ‘Th e virtues of humanity
are peculiar to your sex.’71 His message is clear: you may have heard bad things
about Freemasons, but do not believe them. Freemasons are the most virtuous
men in society today, and it is because of their participation in the Craft . Of
course, Dunckerley urges the brethren in the audience to practise brotherly love,
truth and relief. But most of the charge consists of Dunckerley’s explanation to
the ladies present of how Masonic virtues are practised and enhanced within the
fraternity.72 It is at this point that what has been described as Dunckerley’s puri-
tanical streak emerges, and he reassures the females that when they send their
husbands and sweethearts off  to lodge, all will be well:
At that peaceable and harmonious meeting he will hear no disputes concerning reli-
gion or politics; no swearing; no obscene, immoral or ludicrous discourse; no other
contention but who can work best, who can agree best.73
Dunckerley asserts that by practising these specifi cially Masonic virtues, Free-
masons become ‘good subjects, good husbands, good masters, and dutiful
children’.74 It is tempting to wonder what Dunckerley was thinking when he
gave this part of the lecture. His own ambiguous relationship with truth aside,
Dunckerley presents these three great principles as the foundations not only of
the lodge, but also of civilized masculinity, and he off ers them as Freemasonry’s
inestimable gift  to women.
Th e most remarkable feature of Dunckerley’s address is that in his conclusion,
he speaks exclusively to the ladies, that ‘fairest part of creation’.75 He welcomes
them, and acknowledges that they would have heard ‘many disrespectful things’
about Freemasonry. Dunckerley affi  rms that the principles of brotherly love,
relief and truth were just as important to the women present as the men. He
declares that the highest ambition of Freemasonry should be to ‘have the sanc-
tion of the fair’, and he concludes: ‘Th e virtues of humanity are peculiar to your
sex; and we fl atter ourselves, the most splendid ball could not aff ord you greater
pleasure, than to see the human heart made happy, and the poor and distrest [sic]
obtain present relief’.76
Aft er the success of his presentation at Marlborough, Dunckerley routinely
went out of his way to include ladies in Masonic events, and the following is just
a sampling of the occasions that were covered in the press. In 1777, Lord Charles
Montagu, Provincial Grand Master of Hampshire, accompanied by Duncker-
ley as the Provincial Grand Master of Essex, jointly presided over a Provincial
Grand Lodge at Southampton. Prior to the day’s Masonic events, the assembled

--- Sayfa 135 ---
122
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
ladies were treated to a breakfast, attended by the Stewards of the Provincial
Grand Lodge. Aft er the repast, the ladies were invited into the lodge room to
see the ‘brethren assembled in ample form’, and they heard speeches by Montagu
and Dunckerley, both of whom took as their theme reconciling ladies to Free-
masonry.77 In 1789, Dunckerley held a Provincial Grand Lodge at Bridgwater
in Somerset to celebrate the birthday of the Prince of Wales. Th is was the sort
of event at which he excelled, and he provided the province with a memorable
Provincial Grand Lodge. One hundred and fi ft y Freemasons, along with a band,
processed in regalia to the church. Th e newspaper account rather incongru-
ously tells us nothing about the preacher except that his mother was an amiable
woman, and a nurse to the Princess Royal. Aft er the amiable woman’s son’s ser-
mon, the assembled body processed again, with music, to the Assize Hall to hear
Dunckerley speak. Aft er all this carefully scripted but exclusively Masonic pag-
eantry, the audience at the Assize Hall was mixed, consisting of both Masons
and non-Masons. Th e subject of the charge is lost, but the newspapers carried
Dunckerley’s message clearly nonetheless:
Brother Dunckerley delivered a charge to the Society, and made an elegant address
to about four hundred Ladies, whom he with strong emphasis repeatedly styled the
most lovely and beautiful part of the creation, without whose charms the life of man
would not be worth possessing.78
Dunckerley also used his authority as Provincial Grand Master to encourage
individual lodges in his jurisdiction similarly to experiment admitting women
to social occasions. In 1779, when the Medina Lodge in Cowes celebrated the
restoration of its former designation, No. 39, Dunckerley was in the chair for
the happy occasion. Th e lodge was feeling buoyant about reclaiming its senior-
ity, and extended its celebration to the ladies by having ‘unanimously agreed
that Gloves be presented to the Wife or Friend of every Member’.79 In 1788 two
lodges gathered to celebrate the constitution of Wiltshire Lodge No. 523 in
Devizes.80 Together with Apollo Lodge No. 454 from neighbouring Salisbury,
the Devizes Freemasons held a ceremony for the constitution of the lodge and
installation of offi  cers, then they processed to church, and aft er the sermon the
entire party retired to the Black Swan Inn, where the lodge room was arrayed for
their meeting. With lodge furniture in place the brethren in their regalia took
their seats, and ‘many Ladies did the Brethren the honour of a visit, and with a
general smile of approbation, added much beauty to the splendor and dignity of
the scene’.81 How better to reassure the women-folk? But Dunckerley had more
in mind than just reassuring the ladies and paying compliments to their beauty.
He was looking for a way to make them more truly sisters.
‘Sister’ was a term Dunckerley frequently used when corresponding with
brother Freemasons. Th us, writing to the Grand Secretary James Heseltine in

--- Sayfa 136 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
123
1783, Dunckerley sent good wishes from Mrs Dunckerley, their daughter and
her husband to ‘Sister Heseltine & little folks’.82 Likewise, writing to Heseltine’s
successor, William White, in 1785 and 1786, Dunckerley repeatedly uses the
term ‘Sister White’ in sending greetings to White’s wife.83 In another letter to
White, Dunckerley refers to his own wife as ‘Sister Dunckerley’.84 Dunckerley’s
use of the term ‘sister’ initially looks like a pleasantry. It may also refl ect an older
Masonic usage: Martin Clare in 1743 referred to the keeper of the tavern where
a lodge met as ‘our Sister Shakespear’, while a 1732 entry in the minutes of the
Medina Lodge No. 35 in London mentions the purchase of gloves ‘for some
brethren and all our sisters’.85 However, in Dunckerley’s case, the usage is more
frequent and insistent. Writing to Th omas West in Bath in January 1785, he
sends aff ectionate greetings to ‘all my sisters and brothers’.86 Writing again to
West a month later, he concludes: ‘My wife desires to unite with me in kind
regards to all our Brothers and Sisters and particularly to your Sister West for
the great care she had the goodness to take of y[ou]r aff ec[tionate] Bro[ther].’87
Dunckerley’s designation of the wives of his Masonic friends as sisters was
more than an amusing civility; he wanted women to play a more active part in
Freemasonry. Among the methods by which Dunckerley sought to make the
Lodge of Friendship No. 3, meeting at the Th atched House Tavern in London,
more prestigious and socially exclusive was to arrange for the Duke of Beaufort
to become Master. Dunckerley was not simply content to secure the duke for the
lodge. According to the minutes of the lodge for 27 May 1767,
Her Grace the Dutchess [sic] of Beaufort having been nominated last meeting as
Lady Patroness of this Lodge, was now unanimously Elected as such. Order’d that the
same be notifi ed to Her Grace, and that she be requested to do the Lodge the Honor
of her Patronage, and that a pair of Gloves be presented to her Grace.88
In addition to the astronomical fi ve guinea cost of the gloves what is remarkable
is the instance of proper lodge procedure being applied to nominate and elect a
woman to an offi  cial position with respect to the lodge. Th e idea of the patron-
ess stayed with Dunckerley, and he used it more audaciously when promoting
the Masonic Knights Templar. Writing to Benjamin Craven about the organiza-
tion of a Knights Templar conclave in Essex in 1791, Dunckerley asserts: ‘Your
Sister D[unckerley] the Lady Patroness of Knights Templars [sic] unites with
me in wishing all happiness to Sister Craven and yourself.’89 What exactly was
Sister Dunckerley, the Lady Patroness, expected to do if the French invaded?
One wonders.
So, as we have seen, over the course of his Masonic career, Dunckerley con-
sistently made more than the usual polite gestures towards reconciling women
to the Masonic enterprise, and even included them in proceedings from time
to time. His eff orts towards inclusion became increasingly bold as he gained

--- Sayfa 137 ---
124
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
confi dence in his Masonic authority. Th is reached its zenith in the 1787 celebra-
tion of the Queen’s birthday at Braintree in Essex, which included a select ladies’
lodge dedicated to Urania, long celebrated by Masons as the muse of astrology
and esoteric knowledge.90 If there were any complaints about this aspect of the
celebration, couched as it was in a display of Hanoverian loyalty, they are not
recorded. Th e lodge seems to have been a creature of the day, but that it existed
at all, in the heart of Dunckerley’s Masonic imperium, is very signifi cant.
Can we establish anything further about the nature of the lodge held by
these Masonic sisters in Braintree? Ròbert Pèter has drawn attention to a book
published in London and Dublin in 1791 called Freemasonry for the Ladies, or
the Grand Secret Discovered, an odd little volume calculated to gauge Masonic
tolerance, or even enthusiasm, for a more comprehensive inclusion of ladies in
English Freemasonry, perhaps even a permanent ladies’ order.91 Th e book notes
the use of the Adoption in European countries and advocates a similar form of
female Freemasonry in Britain, helpfully providing an Adoption ritual for pos-
sible use in England. Th e arguments in the introductory material strongly recall
Dunckerley’s published works, particularly his charge at Marlborough. Th e
introduction to Freemasonry for the Ladies notes that women have occasionally
been introduced into social events by Masonic lodges in England, apparently a
reference to the festivals organized by Dunckerley. Freemasonry for the Ladies
was compiled by someone closely connected to Dunckerley, perhaps the man
himself, or perhaps someone like Sibly who still owed him a favour.
It is frequently diffi  cult to arrive at a satisfactory attribution of authorship
for eighteenth-century books, especially when writers were intent on remaining
anonymous. Th at is certainly the case with this text, though there are suffi  cient
clues to suggest how it came to be published, and to connect it to Dunckerley
and his circle. From its title page, engraved anonymously but depicting not only
the symbols of French Adoptive Masonry, but also of the Royal Ark Mariners
and English Craft  Masonry, to its rather bifurcated text, this is a hybrid. Th e
fi rst chapter, complete with a title page declaring the volume was ‘dedicated by
permission to Her Royal Highness the Duchess of York’, is a rehearsal of why
women are admitted to adoptive orders on the Continent, but not in England.
Th is chapter makes complimentary references to leading Grand Lodge person-
alities and causes: the Duke of York, William Preston, Wellins Calcott, the late
Frederick, Prince of Wales, the Royal Cumberland Free-Mason School and its
Lady Patroness the Duchess of Cumberland. It concludes with an endorsement
of just what we have seen Th omas Dunckerley introducing unoffi  cially, and on
his own authority:
Th at the fair-sex might be occasionally introduced into lodges is a point which does
not merit an elucidation, but that they should not be generally present is equally evi-
dent; and even the Ladies themselves will join in the conclusion.92

--- Sayfa 138 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
125
Th e text goes on to note that there is a time and place for the mingling of the
sexes, and a time for their separation, and that both are desirable. In a marked
contrast to this description of a signifi cant but still very circumscribed admission
of ladies, the remainder of the book gives the appearance of being a fairly faithful
English translation of a full-blown French Adoption ritual, with annotations for
an audience unfamiliar with such a concept. A major study on such Adoption
rituals has recently been published by J. A. M. Snoek, who graciously allowed
the author to consult his manuscript.93 Free-masonry for the Ladies is completed
with a brief selection of traditional English Masonic odes and poems, including,
inevitably, one invoking Urania.
Th is curious work was published by Th omas Wilkinson and William Th isel-
ton, who worked in tandem to publish a number of general-interest and Masonic
works.94 A search of their catalogue leads to Free-masonry: A Word to the Wise!
Being a Vindication of the Science as Patronised by the Grand Lodge of England;
and the Devices of the Craft -y. Published in 1796, fi rst in London and then, in a
slightly diff erent version, in Dublin, this work is a defence of the Modern Grand
Lodge against the Ancients and Grand Lodge of All England at York on the
one hand, and unscrupulous professional Masonic frauds and those who make ‘a
trade of Masonry’ on the other. Th is second book, also published anonymously,
is highly critical of reformers and innovators within the Modern Grand Lodge,
accusing them of frivolously introducing new degrees, thereby seducing good
Craft  Masons into spending time and money they could ill-aff ord on spurious
higher degrees, adopted from unreliable foreign sources.95
Th e author dedicates the Dublin edition to Sir Peter Parker, who was Deputy
Grand Master of the Modern Grand Lodge from 1786 until his death in 1811.
Th e dedication encourages Parker, who was also Supreme Grand Master of the
Grand and Royal Chapter of the Royal Arch of Jerusalem, to pursue a permanent
union of the Grand Lodge and the Royal Arch, and then proceeds to disparage
several other appendant degrees and orders as either impious (Rosy Croix) or
displaying ‘stupidity’.96 Th e author is quite pointed in his comments about the
recent ‘institution of KNIGHTS TEMPLARS, which by a few manæuvres (to
use a military phrase) is attached to Free Masonry’, and also, ‘concerning the
HARODIMS’. He thus neatly skewers both Dunckerley and William Preston,
founder of the Grand Chapter of the Ancient and Venerable Order of Harodim
around 1787. Despite its recent appearance, members claimed it was actually a
revival, not a creation.97
Th e author of Free-Masonry is particularly incensed by men who covertly trade
in Masonic manuscripts, invent rituals and generally subsist on their profi ts from
Freemasonry, reserving special ire for ‘one of the principal imposters’, recently
deceased.98 Th e author identifi es the dead scoundrel as a ‘TRAVELLER’ who
consorted with all sorts of masons, sold Masonic paintings and engravings, dealt

--- Sayfa 139 ---
126
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
in ancient manuscripts, spurious and legitimate, and who provided the manu-
script of Free-masonry for the Ladies. Th ere can be no doubt about the Traveller’s
identity – Cagliostro had just died in the hands of the Inquisition, imprisoned in
the Fortress of San Leo. His death ended an infamous career, much of it based on
Masonic schemes and connections. Th ough his reputation has enjoyed a remark-
able rehabilitation in recent years, this earlier account makes it apparent that, at
the time of his death in 1795, not all Freemasons saw Cagliostro as a martyred
brother. Th us the revelation in Free-Masonry launches us into a rather odd tale,
connecting Dunckerley to some of the most esoteric people and practices in late
eighteenth-century London.99
Unlikely as it seems, Giuseppe Balsamo, better known as Count Alessandro
di Cagliostro, did share some interesting commonalities with Th omas Duncker-
ley. Both men became enthusiastic Freemasons once they were initiated, taking
the fraternity by storm, each in his own way. Likewise, they were both men of
their own invention, though Cagliostro’s imagination was more fevered and the
results more outrageous. Th ey both died in 1795, bringing to a close a period
of particularly fertile Masonic innovation. Th ose points aside, they had very lit-
tle in common with the exception of a shared business transaction. Cagliostro
came to London several times, most importantly in 1776 and 1786, and had
a tremendous impact on what, for lack of a more convenient term, we might
call the city’s esoteric community. His 1786 sojourn came just before a fl urry of
Masonic innovation within and around the Modern Grand Lodge, much of it
presided over by Dunckerley, and within the next fi ve years we see Dunckerley
take command of and organize the Masonic Knights Templar Order in England,
promulgate the Royal Ark Mariner Order, and make plausible moves towards
establishing some sort of ladies’ order.
Was Cagliostro involved? Perhaps. While there is no clear evidence the two
men met, it is not plausible that Dunckerley was neither aware of Cagliostro,
nor of the sort of exotic ritual manuscripts he might have on off er. Cagliostro
had been made a Mason in a French lodge, L’Esperance Lodge of the Strict
Observance No. 369 in London in 1777, and knew Dunckerley’s colleagues
in the Grand Lodge, like Chevalier Ruspini, who hosted him at the Lodge of
Antiquity No. 2 in November 1786. Even more to this point, Cagliostro and his
wife had recently made a stir in Paris with their promotion of the Egyptian Rite
which welcomed women as sisters. Cagliostro was a logical source for someone
interested in purchasing a manuscript Adoption ritual. Th is is where Masonic
brother Ebenezer Sibly reappears in Dunckerley’s narrative, though he proved
so handy in Ipswich one suspects Dunckerley always kept tabs on him. Sibly
was a primary connection between Dunckerley and the esoteric demi-monde in
which Cagliostro travelled. Like Cagliostro, Sibly was a peripatetic traveller who
consorted with irregular Masons, collected arcane manuscripts and lived by his

--- Sayfa 140 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
127
wits – though, unlike Cagliostro, for whom Dunckerley was a dupe, Sibly looked
upon Dunckerley as a patron and promoter. Th is is eloquently demonstrated by
Sibly’s fulsome dedication of his edition of Culpeper’s English Physician, in which
he praises Dunckerley’s goodness and nobility, and solicits his protection.100
Ebenezer Sibly, his brothers Manoah and Job, and their father, Edmund,
were all booksellers and dealers in manuscripts, specializing in the occult and
various heterodox religions.101 Manoah and Job also took shorthand, providing
transcripts of trial proceedings and sermons, and making rapid copies of manu-
scripts before they passed them on to willing buyers. Ebenezer Sibly was deeply
involved in the occult, publishing works on astrology, alchemy and chemistry,
herbal medicine and ceremonial magic.102 He was also a shyster, more than
happy to deal in fraudulent lottery tickets and patent medicines, and we have
seen what happened when he dabbled in Ipswich politics.103 Ebenezer’s brother
Manoah Sibly was an early member of the Swedenborgian Th eosophical Society
and was ordained one of the fi rst Swedenborgian ministers in London. Manoah
thus provided a tangible connection between Ebenezer and the Swedenborgian
enthusiasts Philippe de Loutherbourg, Peter Lambert de Lintot and Charles
Rainsford.104
It is not clear where or how Cagliostro and Sibly crossed paths, but they did,
and one result was that Ebenezer Sibly paid 300 guineas to copy the manuscript,
called the Rotalo, which Cagliostro claimed to have used to hit the jackpot in the
1776 British lottery. Sibly’s clean and illustrated fi nal copy is now in the manu-
script collection of the Wellcome Library.105 Although Joscelyn Godwin touches
on the Rotalo in his work on the Enlightenment origins of theosophy, his expla-
nation of the document as a ‘wheel of correspondences’ does not satisfactorily fi t
the facts of its appearance in England. Sibly purchased an unknown number of
manuscripts from the magus, which given our other evidence almost certainly
included Free-masonry for the Ladies. Th us we have a prime example of the sort
of behaviour the author of Free-masonry decries:
Th at Masons of acknowledged integrity, should for a moment forget themselves, or
set aside their CHARACTERS as Craft smen, is to be lamented, that some of this
description should have become the dupes of men, who for years have made a trade
of Masonry, is a fact, but that they should purchase an ideal property in manuscripts
… is to be regretted.106
Dunckerley, via Sibly, is one of the masons who forgets himself and plays the
fool. Th e anonymous author identifi es the primary transfer of manuscripts hap-
pening at Cagliostro’s death. However, the timing of Sibly’s purchase of the
Rotalo (before 1794, when the illustrated copy was produced) and the 1791
publication of Free-masonry for the Ladies, the manuscript alleged to have been
provided by Cagliostro, suggests that manuscripts changed hands on several

--- Sayfa 141 ---
128
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
occasions between 1786 and 1795. Th e author of Free-masonry gives us a further
hint about the transfer of the Adoption ritual, but because of the irregularity of
Cagliostro’s operations, the details remain obscure:
Th e Free-Masonry for the Ladies, was among the Manuscripts he possessed, and an
imposition of his taking a considerable disproportion of money from two intimate
friends, for an article he traffi  cked in, was the cause, in conjunction with similar
manæuvres, of its Publication.107
Th e ‘imposition’ could refer to any number of Cagliostro’s shady deals, real or
rumoured. In this English context, it might well refer to his London friends and
collaborators, Philippe and Lucy de Loutherbourg, who had joined Cagliostro
and his wife in Switzerland in 1786, and between whom there was a terrifi c
falling-out over alleged debts.108 It is also signifi cant to note a strong hint of
Dunckerley’s involvement, as the author of Free-masonry only uses the term
‘manoeuvre’ in connection with Dunckerley, undoubtedly meant as a dig at his
modest success in both the navy and militia.
Th us, in addition to composing or compiling the Royal Ark Mariner ritual
in 1790, Sibly is a very likely author for the front matter and fi rst chapter of Free-
masonry for the Ladies. He was already in the business of creating rituals, and so
putting the manuscript in English dress was not at all outside his accustomed line
of work.109 Free-masonry for the Ladies has all the hallmarks established in Sibly’s
other, numerous publications: an enticing frontispiece replete with symbols and
a plausible and fl uent introduction followed by the much longer translation
or plagiarized version of someone else’s work, in this case, a French Adoption
ritual. In the ritual itself, in a Siblyesque touch, the traditional Masonic symbol
of Jacob’s Ladder is explained as not an actual or spiritual ladder, but the plant
Valeriana offi  cinalis, that goes by the common name Jacob’s Ladder, and which is
featured in Culpeper’s Herbal, one of Sibly’s most profi table reissues.
Dunckerley was familiar with the complex Masonic systems being created on
the Continent and in the Americas, and by his actions it is clear that he yearned
to establish an English counterpart – a Masonic domain containing a rich pano-
ply of orders under his own control. Did Dunckerley’s Masonic vision encompass
Masonic ladies processing under the banner of Urania, alongside the Knights
Templar, Ark Mariners and Companions of the Royal Arch? Did Dunckerley
dream of himself and Sister Dunckerley as the royal couple presiding over this
new Masonic imperium? Apparently he did. At the time of his death, Dunck-
erley held in his hands the reins of a true Masonic empire which he was on the
verge of drawing together into a unifi ed system. Dunckerley’s most novel and
audacious ambition was to include women in this system, and it appears that, at
least for a day in Braintree, he did indeed add the rituals of the Adoption to the
others he promoted.

--- Sayfa 142 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
129
Loose Ends
Dunckerley’s death in late 1795 prevented him from bringing about the logical
culmination of decades of cultivation and organization. Rather like the Holy
Roman Empire, united only in the person of the emperor or, in this case, a halfl ing
prince, the disparate orders of Dunckerley’s Masonic territories were primarily
held together by their loyalty to the great man himself. He had apparently forged
at least a temporary union between the Ark Mariners, Knights Templar and the
Mark Masons, as suggested by this 1794 notice in the Freemasons’ Magazine:
August. 16. Being the birth-day of his Royal Highness the Duke of York, it was cel-
ebrated with all the honours of Masonry by the Order of Knights Templars resident at
London, united with the society of Ancient Masons of the Diluvian Order, or Royal
Ark and Mark Mariners, assembled at the Surry Tavern in the Strand, by summons
from Th omas Dunckerley, Esq. Grand Master and Grand Commander of those
United Orders.
While one might ask some probing questions about just how ‘united’ those
orders were, it is worth noting that the same phrase is repeated later in the notice,
and in a context that speaks for itself:
Th e United Orders unanimously resolved that they would provide themselves with
arms and accoutrements, in order to defend our country against the enemies of our
happy constitution. Th e day was passed with that harmony and conviviality peculiar
to Masons, and the Grand Chapter closed at eleven in the evening.110
If they were not united in a more than superfi cial way, why would members of
the separate orders meet together in a Grand Chapter to draw up battle plans?
It must have seemed to Dunckerley a tantalizingly short step to garner Grand
Lodge endorsement of these other orders, and forge a coherent system. A similar
system, he knew, was being developed in America based on the higher degrees
introduced a few decades earlier by Etienne Morin, and destined to emerge in
1801 as the Scottish Rite.111
And what do we make of the resolution of the United Orders to acquire
arms? Th is speaks to another of Dunckerley’s desires – to transform Masonry
into a more practical, eff ectual support to the nation and, by extension, to the
House of Hanover. Th e necessity of defending Britain against all comers was
never far from Dunckerley’s attention. Remember that his rationale for join-
ing the South Hampshire Militia in 1777 was for the defence of the country.
Dunckerley was alarmed anew aft er the outbreak of revolution in France, and he
sent a strongly worded letter to the Knights Companion of the Knights Templar
under his jurisdiction. He was by this time a man of advanced age, coming up to
seventy-fi ve, and yet he called on the Knights to hold themselves in readiness to
defend against invasion, and off ered to lead them himself. While he was stead-

--- Sayfa 143 ---
130
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
fast, it is not surprising his empire unravelled when he died. Dunckerley had
done an amazing amount of work cultivating personal connections, but estab-
lished precious little institutional framework to support the linkages between
his orders.
Th e Modern Grand Lodge minutes carried no notice of his passing, but his
request to be buried in the Temple Church tells us he wanted to be remembered
as a Christian knight and a warrior Mason.112 Th e old man had suff ered from
a variety of illnesses for several years. Pain, ascribed to the catch-all diagnosis
‘gout’, had limited his ability to travel and correspond as he was accustomed.
Never easygoing, he became irascible. Recognizing the inevitable, Dunckerley
resigned most of his Masonic offi  ces earlier in that autumn.113 Still, death caught
him uncharacteristically off  guard, and Th omas Dunckerley breathed his last
in Portsmouth, too far away to be buried in the Temple Church. Dunckerley
was instead interred at St Mary’s Church, Kingston. In the eighteenth century,
this was the parish church for Portsea and Portsmouth Common, and was most
likely the church Dunckerley had attended in the 1740s and 1750s when he and
his family lived in and around Portsmouth. Th us it was indeed a homecoming,
but not the one to which he aspired. When St Mary’s Church was rebuilt in the
nineteenth century, all the monuments were moved, leaving the whereabouts of
Dunckerley’s burial unknown.
A Question of Character
It may seem incongruous to bring up Dunckerley’s character immediately aft er
burying him, but both Dunckerley and his biographers frequently allude to his
character, so it is appropriate to comment on it briefl y. As with so many other
aspects of Dunckerley’s life, Henry Sadler has long been our primary interpreter,
and his view is of a uniformly virtuous, genteel, tolerant gentleman. Th e Alexander
Pope epigram he selects for the book sets the tone for the remainder of his analysis:
‘An honest man’s the noblest work of God.’ Sadler clearly saw Dunckerley through
the fi lters of his own era, presenting him as an icon of Masonic probity and zeal, a
man with a gift  for friendship, and a dedicated husband and father.
Sadler wanted to let Dunckerley speak for himself, and so his biography
takes the form of an extended commentary on Dunckerley’s correspondence
contained in the archives at the Grand Lodge of England. However, Sadler did
not always fi nd Dunckerley a cooperative role model, and on at least one occa-
sion Sadler felt constrained to expunge text he found unbecoming the man he
called his hero.114 In a 1786 letter to Grand Secretary William White, Dunck-
erley suggested that the time had come for signifi cant recognition of his service,
and proposed that the Grand Lodge really ought to confer upon him the rank of
Past Senior Grand Warden. He wrote:

--- Sayfa 144 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
131
Every Honour that the Society can with propriety confer on me, I am ambitious to
obtain. Surely it is in their Power to give me the Rank of a Past Senior Grand Warden
& give notice of the same in the next printed Acct of the Qua Com. I leave this to
the consideration of Bro. Heseltine & your self as it might be brought forward next
Friday.115
Th is was accomplished – and though the letter in which Dunckerley requested
the honour is included in Sadler’s collection, the paragraph containing his sug-
gestion is entirely omitted. Sadler fi rst notes the honour by quoting Grand Lodge
minutes of 22 November 1786 which record the unanimous decision to grant
Dunckerley the honorifi c, ‘in grateful Testimony of the high sense the Grand
Lodge entertains of his zealous and indefatigable Exertions for many years’.116
Sadler’s editing has had the eff ect of convincing every succeeding biographer
that Dunckerley was such a manifestly wonderful fellow that his colleagues at
Grand Lodge bestowed this singular honour in a spontaneous outpouring of
aff ection and gratitude. Restoring the text of Dunckerley’s letter puts his recog-
nition in a very diff erent light.
If Dunckerley seems self-serving, he was at least consistent. As early as his
1750 essays for the Sober Society, we can see Dunckerley confronting serious
moral issues, and taking outspoken positions. Interestingly, for someone who
seems to have built much of his public life on a lie, for that is indeed what the
story about George II is, Dunckerley reveals himself in his correspondence as
having been in most ways a moral and humane man, religiously tolerant, chival-
rous to the ladies, loyal to his country, generous to a fault. On the other hand, he
was admittedly prideful, and could be quite prickly with regard to his personal
dignity and reputation. He worked hard for his status and honours, and reacted
very badly when he thought he was not being treated with suffi  cient respect, or
was denied the deference his position merited. Dunckerley had his own explana-
tion for this, telling Grand Secretary William White in 1786,
Entre nous – I am not only lov’d but fear’d – I hope you will not accuse me of Vanity
or Arrogance (as this is a private letter) I wrote the above to shew you that ye prefer-
ments on one hand and discipline on the other has produced the money I now send
and hope to send.117
In this case Dunckerley was referring to the sums he forwarded for the hall and
charity funds, which were considerable and regular. Sadler affi  rms that on this
point Dunckerley led by example, and that lodges under his jurisdiction were ‘by
far the most liberal in England with their contributions to the General Charity
and the Hall Fund’.118
However, Dunckerley might just has well have been referring to other aspects
of enforcing regularity on his frequently recalcitrant brethren. Th e fact is that
although Th omas White’s 1793 panegyric refers to the ‘very great esteem and

--- Sayfa 145 ---
132
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
regard with which he is honoured (we had almost said adored) by several hun-
dred Brethren’, Dunckerley’s own appraisal was probably more on the mark.119
His correspondence with the Grand Lodge is littered with complaints about
slights received from truculent lodges and Masons, unwilling to bend to his
authority and unconvinced of the need to send money to build a hall in London
which most of them would never visit.120 Granted, anyone coming in as Provin-
cial Grand Master at such a time, and under such circumstances, would have
faced similar resistance – but Dunckerley seems to have relished the fi ght, and
he did not hesitate to carry through on his threats to erase lodges that refused to
cooperate.121 In his own words, ‘I shall write and threaten them with annihila-
tion if they do not send to ye Charity & pay the Fees.’122
Th rough his framing of Dunckerley’s correspondence, Sadler presents Dunck-
erley as an upright character model, imbued with that quality most highly prized
by Victorian men, energy. Th is is a quality Dunckerley’s contemporaries would
have recognized in him, though that energetic zeal sometimes overfl owed into
what Major Charles Shirreff , Deputy Provincial Grand Master of Shropshire,
another former military man, disparagingly referred to as ‘offi  cious conduct’.123
Shirreff  was himself a plain-spoken man, full of his own importance, so we
would be wise to consider his complaint in that light. Indeed, J. M. Hamill refers
to Shirreff ’s correspondence with the Grand Lodge as evidence of his forceful
and self-important character.124 However, Shirreff ’s description of how a person
who must have been Dunckerley dealt with common Masons he found insuffi  -
ciently respectful has such a ring of truth that it deserves an extended quotation.
Several lodges had gathered in Shrewsbury on 31 August 1790. Th ey processed
to church, dined and held a lodge meeting. Shirreff  describes what happened
next to Grand Secretary William White:
Everything was conducted to give satisfaction, and would have been completely so
had it not been for the Offi  ciousness of a Brother, whom I was Obliged to call to
order several times; he was a visitor & the most troublesome one I ever had to man-
age, the Brethren thou’t I was too mild with him, but Lenity I think at all times is
best. He is by his own Acct. a very great Mason, now Master of three Lodges, and
S.W. of a fourth Lodge in London, & pretends to have a thorough knowledge of you
& my worthy Bro. Heseltine, & I am since informe’d he disapproves of my Conduct,
& means to relate it to you, if so, you will know the Man, and that is my reason for
not Announcing [sic] his Name in this, in hopes he may think better of it; he talks
much of his power, and if Justice is not done him, he can have you & I turn’d out
of offi  ce; this is just intended for your Information, that should this Genius have in
sight, you may be prepar’d to answer him … but taking him altogether to be not right
in his head, I tho’t it best to act as I did, for he had such an opinion of himself plainly
indicated to me I could not reform him.125

--- Sayfa 146 ---
Appendant Orders and Higher Degrees
133
In his history of Freemasony in Shropshire, Graham proposes that the confl ict
between Shirreff  and Dunckerley had to do with Shirreff ’s persistent use of some
elements of the Ancient workings. It is also worth noting that Shropshire was
not one of Dunckerley’s provinces, and his writ did not run there. It seems that
Major Shirreff  had encountered the likes of Lieutenant Dunckerley before, took
his measure and was not cowed.
In Sadler’s view, not one necessarily shared by many of Dunckerley’s con-
temporaries, Dunckerley had a gift  for friendship, particularly with those from a
higher social class, a trait again ascribed by Sadler to Dunckerley’s years at sea.126
Sadler happily picks up this theme from White and Grierson, who refer in 1793
to Dunckerley having ‘the honour and satisfaction to obtain the commendations
and friendship of the following gallant commanders, under whom he served’.127
Th ey follow with a long list of admirals and captains, though in attempting
to match the list with the captains of Dunckerley’s ships it becomes clear that
Dunckerley could have served directly under only a few of the offi  cers listed. We
have already noted the very real social obstacles this interpretation ignores or
denies. Social barriers were a constant frustration to Dunckerley, and their offi  -
cial banishment from the lodge was likely one of the factors that fi rst attracted
young Dunckerley to Freemasonry. Th us when we read that he was ‘befriended’
by a nobleman or had the ‘pleasure to be known to’ an admiral, it would be a
distortion of contemporary social reality to interpret that as actual camaraderie.
Th ese were men to whom Dunckerley wrote as a supplicant, asking for assis-
tance. By sending a note in reply, the gentleman acknowledged Dunckerley’s
existence, and became ‘known’ to him. Any other interpretation is anachronistic.
Almost without exception, surviving correspondence from Dunckerley to
social or political superiors has a suppliant, apologetic tone. Th is is precisely
where he rehearses and embellishes the melancholic tale of his hidden identity,
and relates the consequent misfortunes he has suff ered. In most of these letters
he eventually gets around to asking for money – which he seems to have suc-
cessfully received from time to time – and patronage or preferment, which he
did not. It is the lack of patronage, a persistent leitmotif in Dunckerley’s letters,
which is perhaps most telling in attempting to parse his relationship with elites.
Dunckerley was undoubtedly perfectly capable of presenting himself well – but
a considerable social barrier remained. None of his ‘friends’ was willing or able
to help him overcome the very real distinction that separated them from the
aspiring porter’s son.

--- Sayfa 148 ---
– 135 –
9 APOTHEOSIS
Revisiting Th omas Dunckerley’s biography proves surprising not only for what
it reveals about Dunckerley’s hidden history, but also for what it suggests about
changes in how late eighteenth- and nineteenth-century Anglo-American
society conceived of what we might mistakenly believe rather static concepts:
personal identity, the stability of social status, what it means to be a hero and,
most relevant to this study, what constitutes a proper biography.1 To most atten-
tive readers, it would seem Dunckerley’s complete life history has been known
since shortly aft er his death in 1795, and was defi nitively restated in 1891 by
Henry Sadler.2 However, the craft  of biography has long been tricky. In Western
history, if a fi gure was signifi cant enough to commission or inspire a biography,
circumstances typically guaranteed that the result would be neither reliable nor
unbiased. Th ink, for example, of Plutarch’s Parallel Lives, or Einhard’s Life of
Charlemagne, both of which deviate considerably from modern standards of
scholarship and objectivity – that simply was not their intent. Dependent on
scientifi c requirements for documentary evidence, scholarly method and at
least a modicum of objectivity, modern academic biographies are a fairly recent
innovation and represent a signifi cant shift  away from the traditional purposes
and methodology of the genre. Th us it is not surprising that we fi nd none of
the eighteenth- or nineteenth-century lives of Dunckerley conforms to today’s
standards of ‘good biography’, not even Sadler’s. Rather, early treatments of
Dunckerley more closely resemble medieval hagiographies, which tell us little
about the life of the saint, but amply demonstrate how to emulate the saintly.
Biography was a dominant literary genre throughout the 1800s, though the
purposes biographies were made to serve varied over the century. Recent research
into the nature of nineteenth-century biography by such scholars as Scott Casper,
Nigel Hamilton and Hermione Lee affi  rms that throughout the century both
writers and their audiences believed widespread reading of the lives of individu-
als worthy of imitation could have a powerful transformative eff ect on society.3
In addition to recording the lives of heroic men and pious women, biographies
tended to be strongly didactic. In the fi rst half of the nineteenth century, authors
focused on inculcating a robust Christianity, largely as an antidote to the irreligios-

--- Sayfa 149 ---
136
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
ity fostered by the Enlightenment and the alleged moral vacuum created by urban
industrialization. Th ough the Evangelical urge persisted, by the second half of the
century biographers increasingly dwelt on personalities and themes conducive to
nation- or empire-building, and the formation of national character.
Th e literary transformation of mere mortals into heroes was confi rmed as a
popular genre relatively early in the century by Th omas Carlyle’s published lec-
tures On Heroes, Hero-Worship and the Heroic in History, in which he depicted
great men as instruments of divine providence, deliberately placed to assist in
the unfolding of history.4 Carlyle wrote about men such as Cromwell, Luther
and Dante, all already broadly recognized as monumental fi gures. Not all sub-
jects were as cooperative, however. Whether by accident or deliberate deception,
the lives of men and women who played important roles in key events or in the
creation of signifi cant modern institutions routinely turned up in the hands of
well-intentioned nineteenth-century biographers without satisfactorily docu-
mented personal histories. Lack of accurate or suffi  ciently colourful information
did not deter writers, as demonstrated by Mason Locke Weems’s adulatory
and occasionally fi ctitious Th e Life of Washington, published within months
of George Washington’s death in December 1799.5 However, if Weems felt
emboldened and even obliged to manufacture the cherry tree anecdote about
Washington, whose personal history was well-documented and memory fresh,
other biographers faced a more vexing dilemma. Many putative heroes, espe-
cially military fi gures, had obscure or ignoble personal histories, which was what
propelled them into the military in the fi rst place. As Jared Sparks, president
of Harvard University and dean of nineteenth-century American biography
observed, there were so few ‘authentic’ sources for lesser luminaries that the
biographer was ‘obliged to resort to his invention for incidents, and to his fancy
for embellishments’.6 A clear example of this tendency is the Scottish-Ameri-
can Revolutionary War hero Arthur St Clair (1736–1818).7 When his papers
as governor of the Northwest Territory were published in 1882, editor Henry
Smith had little to go on with regard to St Clair’s early life. St Clair left  Scotland
in 1757, never returned, and never recorded anything about his origins. Smith
apparently felt pressed to make the only governor of the Northwest Territory
look suitably distinguished. Relying in part on family ‘tradition’, always a suspect
source, Smith wrote a brief and almost entirely fi ctional ‘life’, connecting St Clair
closely to the earls of Rosslyn. Smith described a dissolute father, doting mother
and a distinguished medical education which St Clair allegedly abandoned for a
life of military glory.8 Surely the editor of St Clair’s papers knew about his sub-
ject? Or perhaps not. None of the details he recited turned out to be true. Bereft
of illustrious family ties or a fi ne education, St Clair actually left  Edinburgh just
ahead of the law, having defrauded a distant relative of a substantial sum in order
to fund his military equipage.9 Th us St Clair had good reason for reticence about

--- Sayfa 150 ---
Apotheosis
137
his early years. Yet, Smith’s fabrications are still accepted as fact, and continue
to be enshrined in current scholarship because, as with Dunckerley, it has never
occurred to anyone to question biographical ‘facts’ laid down by nineteenth-
century scholars.10 Curiously, while most modern studies of nineteenth-century
biography acknowledge writers’ habit of omitting unfl attering details, they pay
inadequate attention to the frequency with which authors of that era deliberately
fabricated great swathes about the ‘lives’ they wrote.11 Without further substan-
tial research it is diffi  cult to tell how pervasive this resort to invention was in
