nineteenth-century biography, but individual examples are so easy to come by
that it is diffi  cult to escape the conclusion that it was far more common than
scholars have assumed. Whatever the genre’s relationship to factual evidence,
heroic biography as a form of modern hagiography evolved and became more
pronounced as the century progressed, coming to a climax aft er 1870 when a
confl uence of events turned the attention of both Americans and Britons back
towards the previous century.12
By the last quarter of the nineteenth century it was time to take stock of
the monumental changes that had been wrought and give credit to the heroes
who must surely have been responsible. For the British, the twin triumphs of the
Industrial Revolution and Empire brought hundreds of modest men to the fore.
Some emerged as captains of industry – the heroes of the Age of Steel – James
Watt , John Kay and Henry Cort come to mind, all of whom were immortalized
in James Mason’s 1875 work, Th e Great Triumphs of Great Men.13 Likewise, men
of relatively ordinary birth, such as Cecil Rhodes, the fi ft h son of an undistin-
guished rural vicar, emerged as imperialists of the fi rst order, conquering and
governing vast territories considered inaccessible and even unknowable to Euro-
peans only a few generations before. In some ways, the United States had even
more to consider, as it looked back fi rst to its founding, and then its near escape
from the ruin threatened by the Civil War. Now safely past the Scylla and Cha-
rybdis of unifi cation and reunifi cation, Americans looked on to the promise of
Manifest Destiny, and struggled with the weighty question of national identity.
What did it mean to be an American? And had that meaning changed over the
past century? Surely, it seemed, a closer look at our national heroes could help
answer these questions. Biographers had much to consider, and they realized
time was against them. Th e great personalities of the eighteenth century were
gone, and their intimate family and friends were ageing quickly. Soon both their
memories and whatever documentary evidence they had kept would be lost.
For major fi gures this was a worry, but for their lesser contemporaries it might
mean oblivion.14 Dunckerley clearly belongs to that cohort of lesser comrades
for whom there was little evidence, but considerable enthusiasm.
National honour and identity rested on how scholars in both Britain and the
United States presented their great men and women. Th is was not merely an aca-

--- Sayfa 151 ---
138
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
demic exercise – though it was that as well. Th ough personal biography was well
established early in the century, the fi rst signifi cant eff orts to write national biog-
raphies of the United States coincided with the creation of historical studies as a
‘scientifi c’ academic discipline, complete with university-based faculties such as
that established in 1880 at Johns Hopkins by Herbert Baxter Adams. Th e same
professionalization of history took place simultaneously in continental Europe
and Britain with the creation of research and teaching faculties and institutions.
Additionally, voluntary organizations of professional historians came together
to oversee the task at hand, resulting in the founding of such societies as the
Royal Historical Society in 1868 and the American Historical Association in
1884. Freemasonry was not immune to the urge to associate with the purpose
of turning a critical eye towards the past, and the Quatuor Coronati Lodge No.
2076, the fi rst lodge dedicated to Masonic research, was established in 1884.
Despite claims to scientifi c objectivity, these earnest nineteenth-century
historians were nevertheless caught up in the overriding national and political
concerns of their age. In late nineteenth-century Britain, politics were charged
with a preoccupation to justify and maintain empire. Given the worldwide
nature of the British Empire, the lives of naval men, especially offi  cers, were seen
as particularly useful in defi ning and inculcating the sort of heroism required
to achieve imperial goals. Th is can be seen, for example, in the work of John
Knox Laughton, the founder of the Navy Records Society in 1893, who is widely
regarded as the father of modern naval history. While Laughton laboured inde-
fatigably to introduce new methods of documentary criticism to the study of
fi gures like Horatio Nelson, he did so with the explicit intention of ‘restoring the
luster of Nelson’s reputation’ and establishing Nelson as a tactical and profes-
sional benchmark for the modernization of the Royal Navy during the period up
to the First World War.15 Charles Hamilton notes that one result of this focus
on suitably useful heroes is the National Maritime Museum’s collection of 135
biographies of sailors and offi  cers written between 1830 and 1914, not count-
ing the twenty-eight dedicated to Nelson.16 Sadler’s life of Th omas Dunckerley
is one of them and, not surprisingly, we fi nd some of these same imperial pre-
occupations in Sadler’s treatment of Dunckerley, especially in his lengthy and
entirely hypothetical description of Dunckerley’s service at Quebec during the
Seven Years’ War.17
As both a naval and Masonic hero, Th omas Dunckerley poses a number of
interesting problems for his modern biographer. Th e fi rst complication is that he
was one of those individuals who took advantage of the lack of transparency and
consistency in eighteenth-century record-keeping and consciously chose per-
sonal reinvention. As we have seen, Dunckerley spent most of 1765 and 1766
composing and circulating an elaborate fi ctional account of his early life, the
true nature of which went undetected until the present study.18 Th e romantic

--- Sayfa 152 ---
Apotheosis
139
centrepiece of his tale was that he was an illegitimate son of the late George II.
He was neither royal nor illegitimate, though for the sake of this discussion of
Dunckerley’s apotheosis the story bears revisiting. It is diffi  cult to divine Dunck-
erley’s motivation for this deception from his own surviving writings, which
either do not mention his family at all, or do, but only through the distorted lens
of the accepted narrative. His actual father, Adam Dunckerley, never appears by
name in Th omas’s writing, nor does he ever refer to him as anything other than
his mother’s husband. Dunckerley is only slightly less harsh with his mother,
accusing her of depriving him of status and fi nancial security through ‘false
modesty and delicacy’ with regard to his true father.19 He argues that had she
only confessed to adultery, he would have been raised to the rank and prefer-
ment he deserved. Except of course we know this was not the case. Th e most
likely genesis of the story, as some of Dunckerley’s contemporaries noted, was
his striking resemblance to members of the royal family. Dunckerley seized on
this, and attempted to parlay physical resemblance into a cosy employment aft er
his retirement from the Royal Navy. If that was his goal, he completely failed.
He achieved only a modest royal pension on the recommendation of Sir Edward
Walpole, who nonetheless made it clear to George III that Dunckerley was not
related to the royal family. However, Dunckerley was successful in leveraging his
purported royal blood into an honourable and infl uential career in Freemasonry.
So successful, in fact, that by his death in November 1795, his royal ancestry had
become an integral part of his Masonic persona. Th is is demonstrated by the
remarkable fact that when George III stopped payment of Dunckerley’s pen-
sion in 1783 the Prince of Wales, who became the Grand Master of the Modern
Grand Lodge aft er 1790, stepped in to provide a replacement. Dunckerley then
ensured his reputation by writing his own apology, a detailed and engaging nar-
rative of his life that appeared posthumously in the Freemasons’ Magazine, and
which has stood ever since as the accepted basis for all biographical accounts of
the great Provincial Grand Master.
Th us the genesis of Dunckerley’s apotheosis comes from his own mythologiz-
ing of his birth, supported by the convenient though misleading corroborating
evidence of the grace-and-favour apartments and royal pension. Like many
archetypical heroes, he created for himself a ‘lost’ royal birth, a hidden identity
and a trial or testing he had to undergo before his royal ancestry was revealed. In
1793, Dunckerley also permitted his junior colleagues, Th omas White, Provin-
cial Junior Grand Warden for Essex, and James Grierson, Deputy Grand Master
for Hampshire, to publish an apocryphal tale of his early childhood, which had
been omitted from Dunckerley’s own, earlier, composition.20 Th ey would not
have published their account without Dunckerley’s acquiescence, and it is highly
likely he fed them the entire story. Th is later addition to the accepted narrative
bears the same hallmarks as those portions we know to have been written by

--- Sayfa 153 ---
140
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Dunckerley himself – especially the interleaving of plausible fi ctions with just
enough factual nuggets to assuage the conscience, or convince the reader.
Aft er Dunckerley’s death, his biography sat largely ignored for several decades,
as the Modern Grand Lodge moved on, reinvigorated at least in the provinces by
his eff orts, and engaged in the serious business of union with Dunckerley’s old
nemesis, the Ancient Grand Lodge. Union was achieved in 1813, but this did
not immediately result in Masonic harmony. Th e accompanying reform of the
ritual outraged a strong and vocal contingent led by Robert Th omas Crucefi x
(1797–1850) and his ideologue George Oliver (1782–1867), who argued that
Freemasonry should be more explicitly Christian, more administratively trans-
parent and more socially engaged. Dunckerley, whose writings demonstrated his
personal pilgrimage from what appears to be Christian Deism to a more overtly
Evangelical stance, was reinvented as their totem.21 While it initially seems
surprising that Oliver and his colleagues seized upon such a relatively obscure
hero, their method and theme are entirely consistent with the pattern traced by
Charles Hamilton, who argues in his article on naval hagiography that biogra-
phers of the 1840s set out to prove that their heroes were not just offi  cers and
gentlemen, but exemplars of moral rectitude and Christian piety.22
Th e fi rst indication that Dunckerley might have a new audience appears as
a sketch in the 1842 Freemasons’ Quarterly Review under the heading ‘Th e Late
Brother Th omas Dunckerley’. Th e author, ‘Fidus’, intriguingly acknowledges
that there may have been some criticism of the brother whom White and Gri-
erson described as being held in ‘very great esteem and regard with which he
is honoured (we had almost said adored) by several hundred Brethren’.23 Fidus
writes regarding Dunckerley:
Death aids the historian: aft er years have passed away, doubts and mixed thoughts
alike yield to the sense of justice; and, aft er half a century has closed the tomb of the
departed, the reader is enabled to arrive at the truth, which at the time was obscured
equally by the partial praise of the friend, as by the injustice of the opponent.24
Fidus also notes that one reason Dunckerley held so many high Masonic offi  ces
might well have been a shortage of competent men willing to devote themselves
as much as the enterprise required. It is a thoughtful comment, far removed from
later enthusiasms about Dunckerley’s Masonic greatness. Nonetheless, aside
from these two passages, Fidus, as his pen name implies, is completely faithful
to the accepted narrative, incorporating signifi cant portions of the 1793 and
1796 Freemasons’ Magazine articles into his own account – a testimony to the
strength, charisma and utility of the accepted narrative.
But who was Fidus? Masonic scholars have puzzled over his identity for some
time. Unravelling the mystery leads to a very strange little story about mid-nine-
teenth Masonic shenanigans. Fidus was a regular contributor to the Freemasons’

--- Sayfa 154 ---
Apotheosis
141
Quarterly Review, one of the many nineteenth-century successor publications to
the earlier Freemasons’ Magazine, though with a diff erent and more outspoken
editorial bias. Fidus wrote on a number of subjects, but for the purpose of this
study his most interesting contributions are the sketch of Dunckerley just noted,
and an account of the Old Charges manuscript now known as Grand Lodge
Ms. 1.25 Th e ‘Old Charges’ are a group of manuscripts compiled between the fi f-
teenth and eighteenth centuries which document Masonic legends in circulation
amongst operative (practising) stone masons during those centuries. Although
relatively little work has been done with these manuscripts since the Second
World War, there was a great deal of excitement in the nineteenth and early
twentieth centuries regarding what they might reveal about the origins of Free-
masonry.26 In the article on Grand Lodge Ms. 1, Fidus both introduces himself
and heralds the purchase of this ‘Important Manuscript Roll’, which he identifi es
as having belonged to Th omas Dunckerley. Th ere are a number of really interest-
ing things about this little article, in addition to its mysterious authorship. Th e
fi rst is the stated provenance of the manuscript. Th e article says that in 1839 Ann
Siddall, a granddaughter of Hannah Dunckerley, approached ‘Bro. the Rev. H.
R. Slade’ with what she termed a Masonic relic that had belonged to Th omas
Dunckerley. Fair enough, though Dunckerley’s will does not mention papers of
any sort, let alone Masonic relics. Slade purportedly passed the scroll on to Dr
Robert Th omas Crucefi x for examination, and Crucefi x showed it to his close
colleague George Oliver. Together they determined this was such a special docu-
ment that it needed to belong to the newly established library at the United
Grand Lodge of England (est. 1838), of which Crucefi x had been a major advo-
cate. Th e Grand Lodge Board of General Purposes was convinced, and £25 was
duly forwarded to the elderly Miss Siddall, for whom ‘which sum was a very
acceptable relief ’.27 Fidus ends with: ‘Th e name of Dunckerley, however, must
not be thus passed over; his character, the most important of his age, shall form
a subject for the Annalist.’ Sure enough, a few pages later, in the section of the
journal titled the ‘Annalist’, we fi nd Fidus’s sketch of Dunckerley.
Th us in this little article we are introduced to an interesting triumvirate: Rob-
ert Th omas Crucefi x, George Oliver and Henry Raper Slade (1806–68). Slade
was the least personally prominent of the three, and so it is fi tting that though
he appears frequently, both under his own name, and in masquerade behind his
Masonic nom de plume, Fidus, his role is to play the agent or emissary. He intro-
duces characters and topics, off ers toasts and applauds at appropriate moments.
Oliver and Crucefi x treat him with fond amusement, but Slade is allowed to bear
the brunt of some embarrassing jokes, as when he enthusiastically identifi es the
date on the Dunckerley manuscript as 1183.28 Oliver opines that it is more likely
from the time of Elizabeth – which it was.

--- Sayfa 155 ---
142
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Fidus was an appropriate pen name for Henry Raper Slade in two regards,
either of which would make his identifi cation with the alias very likely. Th e
motto of a prominent branch of the Slade family was ‘Fidus et audax’ (‘Faithful
and bold’), which is an inspiring thought to live by, and one Slade took to heart,
at least in his Masonic career. He was most likely not descended from the proper
branch of the family to lay claim to this motto but, as we have seen in Duncker-
ley’s story, it is so frequently the thought that counts, not the actual bloodline.
In a similar vein, the Library and Museum of Freemasonry has in its collection
an impressive silver candelabrum presented to Slade’s Masonic patron Cruce-
fi x in recognition of his service to the fraternity. Th e coat of arms inscribed on
the candelabrum, long thought to be Crucefi x’s, was investigated in 1977 and
found to belong to the Cruwys family of Devonshire. Crucefi x was not entitled
to bear these or any other arms, but apparently the occasion called for arms, and
he seems to have made up the defi ciency.29 Fidus also makes an excellent pen
name for Henry Slade because of his position vis-à-vis George Oliver, whom he
looked to as an ecclesiastical patron and Masonic mentor. In the Aeneid, Aeneas’s
close companion is Achates, whose fi delity to Aeneas made him a favourite allu-
sion in nineteenth-century literary circles as Fidus Achates. If Oliver had a Fidus
Achates, he was Henry Raper Slade.
Slade was born in 1806 in London, the son of Th omas Raper Slade, who
described himself as a gentleman. Th e younger Slade went to school at Eton briefl y,
and then Stoke Newington in London, and fi nally, as family fi nances declined, he
attended St Bees School in Cumbria. Slade matriculated at Cambridge in 1826,
where he studied law at Caius College, though without taking a degree. Slade later
claimed to have earned an LLD at Cambridge, but that is not confi rmed by uni-
versity records.30 When Henry Raper Slade, Esq. married Louisa Eades in 1829 the
ceremony was performed at the fashionable St George’s, Hanover Square, in Lon-
don.31 He was ordained as a deacon in the Church of England in 1830, and was
named curate at Holcombe, near Bath in Somerset that December. Th e following
year he was ordained as a priest in the Church of England.
Th en something seems to have gone wrong. Slade bounced around quite a
lot, sometimes working as a preacher, sometimes taking students. In 1834 he
applied unsuccessfully for a position as preacher at the Magdalen Hospital then
located in St George’s Fields, London.32 Misfortune followed Slade, and in 1837
he joined other insolvents in the London Gazette.33 His attempt to secure an
elected position at the Hampton Grammar School in Middlesex was dogged by
both his insolvency and his temper.34 In November 1838, Slade was convicted
of swearing at a woman in the street and assaulting her with his cane, apparently
because she did not give way suffi  ciently to Slade and his wife as they all walked
through a narrow gate.35 He was fi ned 20 shillings and all legal costs, in lieu of a
month in prison. He paid. Th ings looked very bad indeed for Slade.

--- Sayfa 156 ---
Apotheosis
143
Th e reverend was not without supporters, however, and Dr Robert Th omas
Crucefi x went out to Hampton to attempt to vindicate Slade’s reputation, and
perhaps save the school election for him. Th e radical press had great fun with
the event, which seems not to have gone well for either Slade or Crucefi x. How-
ever, this does enable us to trace Slade’s association with Oliver and Crucefi x to
sometime before 1838. Th e next Christmas, Slade preached a benefi t sermon at St
James’s Clerkenwell to support Crucefi x’s attempts to establish an asylum for aged
Freemasons.36 About the same time, Slade’s fortunes looked up. In 1841 he was
given the parish of Henley in Shropshire, of which he was rector until 1866.37 He
probably spent little if any time in residence because, by 1846, Slade was living in
Wolverhampton in Staff ordshire, where he served as curate under George Oliver,
who was the incumbent. Most gratifying of all, in 1843 he was ‘awarded’ an hon-
orary doctorate of divinity by Leipsic [sic] University.38 More on the sometimes
ambiguous status of nineteenth-century German degrees shortly.
As his faulty but enthusiastic analysis of the Grand Lodge Ms. 1 suggests,
Slade was an indiff erent scholar, though he published a number of works over
the years. In 1837 he published a volume of sermons, which was severely criti-
cized in the theological press.39 One reviewer noted curtly: ‘When sermons are
committed to the press, we have a right to expect at least some useful informa-
tion.’40 Th is, as well as many of Slade’s other works, was published by Sherwood
& Company, a publisher used extensively by Crucefi x and his rather shadowy
medical partner, Dr Goss. Despite Slade’s lack of great scholarly acumen, he was
the conduit for the discovery of Grand Lodge Ms. 1, a story which deserves a
closer examination.
Th e diffi  culty with the appearance of Grand Lodge Ms. 1 is complicated
– and perhaps illusory. Th ere is no question about the authenticity of the manu-
script, but its provenance now appears very dubious. Slade is alleged to have been
approached with the manuscript by Hannah Dunckerley’s granddaughter Ann
Siddall in 1839. We have already noted that Dunckerley was neither a scholar nor
a collector of books. He undoubtedly did do some business in old manuscripts,
but as demonstrated by Free-masonry for the Ladies and the Royal Ark Mariners
ritual, Dunckerley’s interest was in immediately applicable ceremonies – not
hoary scrolls. Th at noted, the timing of the Grand Lodge Ms. 1 transaction also
looks very suspicious. Ann Siddall did live at Hampton Court Palace, and would
have had no trouble fi nding Slade had she been so inclined. However, Siddall’s
1842 will leaves many bequests to her extended family and friends, mostly of sil-
ver objects, so it is not clear she was in actual need of the money.41 And, while she
still might welcome the £25 paid by the Board of General Purposes, so would
Slade – who was emerging from insolvency, and was not employed just then.
Th e next suspicious aspect to the discovery of Grand Lodge Ms. 1 is that,
although the purchase was made in 1839, the announcement in the Freemasons’

--- Sayfa 157 ---
144
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Quarterly Review was held until 1842 when, in the same issue, Th omas Dunck-
erley made his posthumous debut. Dunckerley and the scroll were brought out
at that point for a reason – Dunckerley ended his Masonic career as an avowed
Christian, and he openly applied his faith to the Craft . George Oliver and Rob-
ert Crucefi x were in the vanguard of a small but vocal group of Freemasons who
were disenchanted with the autocratic tendencies of the Grand Master, Prince
Augustus Frederick, Duke of Sussex. Well-founded allegations about the duke’s
undoubted religious heterodoxy served as an additional irritant.42 For Crucefi x
and Oliver, Dunckerley could be made to represent all that was virtuous and
admirable in the good old days, making him a convenient branch with which
to beat Sussex about the head and shoulders.43 While the venerable manuscript
would have been a wonderful fi nd for the Library and Museum under any cir-
cumstances, its alleged provenance affi  rmed Dunckerley’s new (and growing)
reputation as a Christian oracle within Freemasonry. To make the scroll’s iden-
tifi cation with Dunckerley even more intimate, Fidus tells us that at the end of
the manuscript,
Th e indorsment [sic], from John i. 1., is evidently of a very recent date, and is, together
with the following words – ‘whose sacred and universal law I will endeavour to
observe, so help me God … ’ in the hand-writing of the late Brother Th omas Dunck-
erley.44
Th e recent history of the manuscript is fraught. During the mid-twentieth cen-
tury it disappeared for a time from the Library and Museum of Freemasonry
under suspicious circumstances. When it was recovered, it had been mutilated,
and the endorsement to which Slade referred is now gone. Lacking access to
the actual endorsement, there are still two powerful arguments against Slade’s
attribution. Th e fi rst is that Henry Sadler, who as Grand Librarian had ample
opportunity to examine the intact manuscript, states emphatically that the
handwriting was not that of Th omas Dunckerley.45 Additionally, a very clear
facsimile of the complete Grand Lodge Ms. 1 appears in Volume IV of Quat-
uor Coronatorum Antigrapha, published in 1892. While the handwriting in the
endorsement is large and distinct, like Dunckerley’s, it otherwise bears scant
resemblance to his hand.46
Th e timing of Fidus’s announcement was also signifi cant, coming as it did
in the midst of a titanic struggle between Crucefi x and the Grand Master of
the United Grand Lodge, the Duke of Sussex, which was played out rather
unedifyingly in the pages of the Freemasons’ Quarterly Review.47 Th is confl ict, as
distinct from the one about religion, was ostensibly over Sussex’s opposition to
Crucefi x’s ultimately successful campaign to establish an asylum for worthy but
impoverished Freemasons. Th e Asylum for Worthy, Aged and Decayed Freema-
sons was consecrated in 1850.48 Sussex cautiously preferred to award annuities

--- Sayfa 158 ---
Apotheosis
145
that would permit said aged Freemasons to live at home. Th ere was more to it
than that, though. Crucefi x was another Masonic rogue – a chancer who lived
a shadowy ‘other’ life, of which his Masonic brethren either were ignorant or
tactfully ignored, but which Sussex knew all about.49 Crucefi x was in it for the
money – and for Masonic infl uence.
Like Th omas Dunckerley, Robert Th omas Crucefi x was something of a rein-
vented man and, as with Dunckerley, his standard biographical sketches cannot
be considered accurate. Some of the errors crept in through the enthusiasm
of his dear friend George Oliver, who (along with John Lane) wrote his 1850
obituary, and who could not help but embellish his hero’s life, including making
Crucefi x several years younger than he actually was. Other crimes against the
truth were Crucefi x’s own doing. Th is is not the place for a full rehearsal of the
reinvention – this is Dunckerley’s book, aft er all – but we do need to quickly dip
into the gist of it.
Th ere is only one recent biographical study of Crucefi x, made by Richard
Sandbach in 1989. Tellingly, Sandbach is too gentle with Crucefi x’s reputation,
and it is tempting to see his interpretation of sources that criticize Crucifi x as
the author’s decision to turn a fraternally blind eye to evidence that is really
quite damning.50 Some of the traditional biography is accurate. Crucefi x came
from a family of Huguenot clockmakers who settled in London in the late sev-
enteenth century. He attended the Merchant Taylors’ School from 1801 to
1803.51 Crucefi x indeed trained and qualifi ed as a surgeon, walking the wards
of St Bartholomew’s Hospital with Sir Ludford Harvey rather than, as Oliver
would have it, the ‘celebrated Dr Abernathy’.52 Up to 1813, requirements to take
the examination for admission to the Royal College of Surgeons included an
apprenticeship, usually of seven years, but the actual length of service varied con-
siderably from individual to individual. Crucefi x appears in Harvey’s records for
six months beginning October 1809, and duly became a member of the Royal
College of Surgeons in 1810.
Aft er that, the litany of deception is overwhelming. As a surgeon, protocol
dictated that Crucefi x use the title ‘Mr’ not ‘Dr’. But, by 1836, Crucefi x rou-
tinely referred to himself as Dr Crucefi x MD.53 He additionally claimed to have
earned his medical degree at the University of Geissen, one of a number of Ger-
man universities that earned notoriety in the middle of the nineteenth century
for selling doctoral degrees of all sorts.54 Curiously, Crucefi x neither earned nor
purchased a medical doctorate from Geissen, but he did purchase an LLD (doc-
tor of laws) from that university in 1844, perhaps in response to his painful legal
wrangling with the United Grand Lodge.55 Crucefi x was not alone in his interest
in procuring foreign credentials. At the time he acquired his LLD an educational
journal noted that ‘Five German Universities have a London commercial agent
for the sale of degrees in absentia’, implying a signifi cant English clientele.56 More

--- Sayfa 159 ---
146
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
interesting than the purchased degree is the fact that Crucefi x seems not to have
conducted any sort of regular medical practice – he sold medicine by mail order,
specializing in venereal disease and sexual dysfunction.
When Crucefi x appeared in the court for insolvents in June 1828, he was listed
as a printer of Bouverie-street.57 His address was in a shady neighbourhood off
Fleet Street, known for print shops and less savoury businesses, low rents and fre-
quent bankrupts. Insolvency seems to have run in the Crucefi x family. His brothers
– John Clarke Crucefi x, an ink manufacturer, and James Orme Crucefi x, a painter
and writer – also ended up in ‘Insolvent Debtor’s Court’ in 1826 and 1834, respec-
tively.58 While these insolvencies speak to the perils of small business, they also,
inevitably, tell us something about the individuals involved. It was no wonder that
the Duke of Sussex did not trust Crucefi x with Masonic funds.
Crucefi x did not regularly ‘see’ any actual patients because most of his
patients did not care to be seen – they had embarrassing personal conditions,
and preferred to do their consultation by post. To make his professional arrange-
ments more socially and Masonically convenient, Crucefi x took the prudent
step of procuring a partner, Mr Goss, who did the dirty work for him. Goss &
Co., Surgeons, had established themselves in Bouverie-Street by 1819, when
their advertisement appeared in Th e Times:
Messrs. Goss and Co., Surgeons, members of the Royal College, respectfully announce
that they continue to give their advice, as usual; hours of attendance from morning
until night. Letters communicating cases must contain a remittance for medicine,
which can be immediately forwarded to any part of the world.59
Th e cunning detail is that there was no Mr Goss. In 1814, Robert Th omas Cruce-
fi x married a widow, Jane Score Goss (1789–1869). Her fi rst husband, Th omas
Goss (1781–1813), had been a surgeon and apothecary.60 Aft er his death, he no
longer needed either his wife or his good name, and Crucefi x acquired them both.
Goss & Co. sold medicine through the mail to patients with venereal diseases
and various sexual complaints, who understandably preferred the anonymity of
brown-paper wrappings. Th e fi rm also published ‘non-medical commentaries’
on sexual function, venereal disease, masturbation and procreation – and this
is where Crucefi x’s experience as a printer came in. A typical publication was
Th e Aegis of Life: A Non-medical Commentary on the Indiscretions Arising fr om
Human Frailty, published in 1821.61 Goss & Co. books, illustrated with graphic
engraved plates, were considered pornography in some quarters. Th e medical
establishment was livid.62 Shielded behind ‘Goss & Company, Consulting Sur-
geons’, Crucefi x was unrepentant. He had found the perfect racket, publishing
books that heightened public concern about sexual diseases and dysfunctions,
and conveniently selling ‘cures’ for the very problems described in the infl amma-
tory literature. Curiously, an apparently unrelated man using the name ‘Horace

--- Sayfa 160 ---
Apotheosis
147
Goss’ appeared without explanation aft er Crucefi x’s death and took over the
imprimatur, extending the business into midwifery and some very suspicious
activities involving procuring foundlings.63
Lest we appear too rough with Crucefi x, we should pause briefl y to put his
medical and fraternal careers in perspective. His amalgam of real and fraudulent
medical credentials, suspicious publications, involvement in problematic insur-
ance schemes and extreme Masonic ambition puts him squarely in the mainstream
of a certain type of fraternal fl im-fl am artist who emerged early in the history of
Freemasonry; and, if fraternal warnings on current Masonic websites are to be
believed, continues to be a concern for Freemasons and other fraternal societies.
We have already seen Dr Ebenezer Sibly engage in a similar mix of publications
specifi cally intended to arouse fears of sexual debility and overall malaise in
readers – ills which could be most effi  caciously treated by Dr Sibly’s Solar and
Lunar Tinctures. Sibly was also a Masonic climber, who collected degrees and
orders but, more to the point here, included in his books fulsome dedications
to both Th omas Dunckerley and Freemasons in general, with the obvious aim
of selling the expensive publications to the lucrative Masonic market.64 Cagli-
ostro was another contemporary of Sibly who used fraudulent credentials and
titles to further his Masonic and medical business interests.65 Th e temptation
to take advantage of fraternal connections was apparently too strong for some
brethren to resist, and history is replete with Masonic con artists. William D.
Moore has identifi ed and generously shared a particularly colourful example of
the confi dence man in the career of late nineteenth- and early twentieth-century
American fraternalist Dr Darius Wilson (c. 1839–1927).66 Along with his son,
the Revd James D. Wilson, the elder Wilson peddled degrees in exotic Masonic
orders, including all ninety-six degrees of the Egyptian Masonic Rite of Mem-
phis, an earlier incarnation of which was promoted by Cagliostro. Wilson also
established insurance fraternities which catered to the fi nancial insecurities of
his fraternal brethren. Th e most famous and successful of these ventures was the
Royal Arcanum, which Wilson established in 1877 and which is still in operation
today.67 Wilson likewise dabbled in quack medicine, and was for a time affi  liated
with a Goat Lymph Sanitarium in New York, where he was apparently employed
injecting patients with goat lymph, reputed to cure all manner of disorders.68
One of his most prominent patients was General John Palmer (1842–1905),
former secretary of state for New York and commander-in-chief of the Grand
Army of the Republic, who agreed to pay Wilson privately $200 a month for
injections, and consequently became embroiled in a lawsuit for violating his
exclusive sanitarium contract.69 Like Sibly, the Wilsons’s activities brought them
under unwelcome scrutiny by law enforcement and Masonic authorities. Like
Crucefi x, the Wilsons generally came out relatively unscathed, and carried on
with their questionable activities.

--- Sayfa 161 ---
148
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
To return to Crucefi x, the puzzling bit about his adoption of Dunckerley as
an icon has to do with motivation. If Crucefi x were a quack and quasi-pornog-
rapher who hoped to make a lot of money on Masonic investments, as seems
manifestly to have been case, why was he involved in a campaign to return
Freemasonry to what he apparently perceived as its Christian roots? Oliver’s
enthusiasm, and that of Slade, are much more understandable and on the sur-
face seem legitimate. Both men of the cloth, Oliver was a true believer in the
unbroken line of Masonic descent from Adam, through the patriarchs, on to
the Quatuor Coronati (Christian stonemasons martyred under Diocletian),
right down to Dunckerley himself. Crucefi x’s motivation was likely more com-
plex, though there is no obvious reason to believe that his convictions about the
Christian nature of Freemasonry were any less real than that of Slade and Oliver.
Th ere are many pious sinners. However, we need to place Crucefi x’s opposition
to the Duke of Sussex on this point into a broader context. Crucefi x founded the
Freemasons’ Quarterly Review as a vehicle for his own Masonic agenda, which
was not a simple one. Crucefi x lobbied for more transparency in Grand Lodge
business, and more communication with and emphasis on the provinces. With
some justifi cation, Crucefi x accused the Grand Master of being an overbearing
autocrat who ran the Grand Lodge as a private concern and restricted infl uence
to Londoners and aristocrats. Crucefi x and the duke were particularly at logger-
heads over ‘the little doctor’s’ scheme for building a home for old Freemasons.
Crucefi x was free with his criticisms, and was not as careful with his language
as he should have been. Eventually hostilities between the two Masonic wor-
thies came to a head and, in 1840, Crucefi x was temporarily suspended from the
fraternity. Th roughout the resulting drama, Oliver and Slade remained faithful
advocates, and Crucefi x duly acknowledged their loyalty.70 Th is was no small
thing, as even Sandbach hints that Crucefi x probably hoped to replace Sussex as
Grand Master.71 Th us, Crucefi x’s bruised reputation could only benefi t by a close
identifi cation with Dunckerley, whom he and Oliver present as the Provincial
Grand Master and Christian Mason par excellence.
Following Slade’s strategic reintroduction of Dunckerley, his apotheosis
proceeded apace, and was most immediately entrusted to George Oliver. Oliver
was intent on accumulating all the Masonic legends and facts he could, with the
goal of compiling an unbroken narrative of the Craft , beginning with Adam.
He was keen to demonstrate that Freemasonry was the servant of true religion,
and gradual changes in the religious tone of Dunckerley’s writings fi t Oliver’s
purpose nicely. Unfortunately, Oliver was a leading proponent of what Albert
Gallatin Mackey termed the ‘literary school’ of Masonry. Th us he was a wonder-
ful ideologue, but a terrible historian and a slipshod biographer. According to
Mackey, Oliver’s ‘great error’ was

--- Sayfa 162 ---
Apotheosis
149
a too easy credulity or a too great warmth of imagination, which led him to accept
without hesitation the crude theories of previous writers, and to recognize documents
and legends as unquestionably authentic whose truthfulness subsequent researches
have led most Masonic scholars to doubt or to deny.72
Th is is certainly borne out in Oliver’s treatment of Dunckerley, who features
most prominently in Oliver’s Revelations of a Square, best characterized as a
Masonic romance.73 Speaking through his interlocutor, ‘the Square’, a Masonic
symbol representing Craft  tradition, Oliver embellishes Dunckerley’s Masonic
accomplishments. In the process, he transforms Dunckerley into someone who
would indeed have collected priceless Masonic manuscripts, and used them, too.
Oliver observed that
the Masonic career of Bro. Dunckerley was brilliant as the stately progress of a comet
amidst the permanent orbs of heaven; and he was regarded, according to the testi-
mony of an eminent contemporary, as a great Masonic luminary.74
Th e eminent contemporary was William Preston, who noted it was Duncker-
ley’s ‘indefatigable assiduity’, rather than his great intellect that marked him as a
‘truly Masonic luminary’.75 Preston meant Dunckerley was genuinely dedicated
to the practical and organizational aspects of Freemasonry. In these endeavous,
his discipline as a navy gunner proved an invaluable foundation, as it required
constant vigilance, tactical thinking, meticulous record-keeping (though one
worries a great deal about the numbers) and the ability to communicate eff ec-
tively. Th is, very briefl y, describes the techniques and qualities Dunckerley used
throughout his Masonic career, to his own credit and that of the Masonic broth-
erhood writ large.
Oliver, however, asserted more, portraying Dunckerley as his generation’s
leading sage of Masonic science, and laying out two specifi c claims that though
amply disproved, have never been successfully expunged from the Masonic
record. Th e fi rst is:
His reputation was so great, that every diffi  cult question which elicited more than
one opinion in Grand Lodges, was always referred to him, and his decision was deliv-
ered with such consummate judgment and tact, as to be satisfactory to all parties.76
Oliver’s conclusion was that ‘Bro. Dunckerley was the oracle of the Grand Lodge,
and the accredited interpreter of its Constitutions. His decision, like the law of
the Medes and Persians, was fi nal on all points of both doctrine and discipline,
and against it there was no appeal.’77 Th is was simply not the case. A reading of
the Grand Lodge minutes shows that Dunckerley rarely attended, and no such
questions were ever referred to him for judgeent. Even Sadler, who accepts much
of the Dunckerley mythology, dismisses Oliver, arguing that he wrote a pretty

--- Sayfa 163 ---
150
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
fi ction, but without even consulting Grand Lodge minutes, or any other cor-
roborating documentary evidence.78
Th e second fabrication Oliver introduces is the assertion that Dunckerley
was authorized by the Grand Lodge to construct a new Code of Lectures, by a careful
revision of the existing ritual, and a collation of all the ancient forms, he executed the
task so well, that the Grand Lodge adopted it without alteration, and enjoined its
practice on all the Lodges under its jurisdiction.79
Sadler, consulting the Grand Lodge minute books and archives, again refutes
this – but his refutation comes decades aft er Oliver inserted both the existence
and authorship of such lectures into the record, and in that time the fi ction
became gospel.80 An 1879 edition of Mackey’s Encyclopedia of Freemasonry tells
us that ‘of far more lasting importance to the Craft , was the position occupied by
Dunckerley as an instructor of the Lodges, and a reformer, or at least a remodel-
ler, of the system of lectures’.81 Th e claim is maintained in a 1919 edition:
Th omas Dunckerley subsequently made a new arrangement of the lectures, and
with them the tests. For the eighteen which composed the series of Manningham,
he invented ten, but which were more signifi cant and important in their bearing.82
Th e reference is to the Masonic ‘lectures’ or catechism, instituted by Dr Th omas
Manningham, the very infl uential Deputy Grand Master from 1752 to 1756.
Interestingly, along with Oliver’s assertion that Dunckerley revised the lectures
comes the accusation that he was simultaneously responsible for an evisceration
of the Th ird Degree, transferring the Master’s word from it to the Royal Arch,
in an attempt to wrest the Royal Arch from the Ancients, and wed it fi rmly
to the workings of the Modern Grand Lodge.83 Th ough there is no evidence
for Dunckerley’s refashioning of the degrees, Oliver’s claim had legs, and thus
Mackey observed in 1879 that the Master’s Degree should properly be called
‘Dunckerley’s Degree’, because he had completely refashioned it, even introduc-
ing new symbols, including the circle bounded by parallel lines as a symbol of
Freemasonry’s patrons, the two Saints John.84
R. F. Gould led the late nineteenth-century retreat when it came to the depth
and infl uence of Dunckerley’s Masonic knowledge, writing in 1885 that ‘it must
be freely confessed that the common belief in the profundity of his Masonic
learning is altogether destitute of evidence to support it’.85 Sadler echoed this
sentiment with specifi cs in 1891, arguing that not only is there no archival
evidence indicating that Dunckerley reworked the lectures but that, if he had,
Preston surely would have had something to say about it, and thus his silence is
telling.86 Sadler has some choice words for Oliver as well, saying that in his writ-
ing, ‘truth and fi ction are so cleverly blended as to render it extremely diffi  cult to
distinguish one from the other’.87

--- Sayfa 164 ---
Apotheosis
151
Th is focus on Oliver’s embellishment of Dunckerley’s biography is intended
as a clear example of how an enthusiastic and perhaps ideological, but certainly
misinformed, admirer can easily scatter stumbling blocks in the path of scholars
attempting to do careful research. When, as in this case, the artless biographer
had as his foundation an already imaginary account, the results can be stagger-
ing, and the damage very diffi  cult to undo. Later in the century, Sadler and R.
F. Gould did much to mitigate Oliver’s exaggerations but, even so, pieces of the
Oliver myth remain in popular histories to the present, and use of the Internet
by Masons only magnifi es the errors. Furthermore, Sadler was himself guilty
of harnessing documentary evidence to an ideological purpose. In his study of
naval biographies, Hamilton notes a distinct shift  of focus towards the end of
the nineteenth century, from naval offi  cers as Christian gentlemen to their por-
trayal as heroic and muscular defenders of empire.88 Sadler’s publication follows
suit, portraying Dunckerley as an energetic hero in both the naval and Masonic
spheres, thus presenting the Masonic fraternity as a stalwart cadre supporting
the British Empire throughout the world. Th e telltale clue is Sadler’s decision
to accept the veracity of Dunckerley’s fi ctitious narrative, despite his own clearly
rehearsed and well-founded misgivings.89 Henry Sadler indeed sought the truth
about Dunckerley’s life, but he was convinced that the times required that he
fi nd a truth that reinforced key Victorian and Masonic virtues such as loyalty to
the crown, manliness and defence of empire.90 Had Dunckerley been proven a
liar, he would have lost his claim to heroism, and thus his usefulness to posterity.
With the accepted narrative intact, the unique elements of Dunckerley’s story
seemed to fi t the requirements of Sadler’s age with only a few tweaks and the
right sort of framing. Th is special combination of elements might well have been
what prompted Sadler to compile this particular biography when and as he did,
though there was a personal connection as well. As David Peabody has pointed
out, Sadler had become a merchant mariner at the age of fi ft een, and this may
have further strengthened his sympathy for Dunckerley.91
Th e inescapable truth is that many nineteenth-century biographies are
similarly tainted; or at the very least prudence dictates that we treat them as sus-
pect. Th is is obviously the case for many Masonic fi gures, but Freemasons have
no monopoly on exaggeration. Nor should the blame be fi xed on only one or
two overly eff usive writers – for errors attract errors.92 Already in Dunckerley’s
time baseless assertions made him a master of mathematics at the Royal Naval
Academy at Portsmouth.93 He was not, but the claim added lustre to both his
naval career and his reputation for brilliance. Likewise, as late as 1872, Dunck-
erley received an extracurricular posthumous promotion, when an account
of the Grand Conclave of the Knights Templar referred to ‘Admiral Th omas
Dunckerley’ as the order’s fi rst Grand Master.94 Dunckerley would have been
tremendously fl attered.

--- Sayfa 166 ---
– 153 –
EPILOGUE
Th at was a very diff erent story than the one I intended to write when I began
researching Th omas Dunckerley. I was not going to write about him at all.
He was a distraction, though a welcome one, from another book project. I
was running into dead ends trying to fi gure out what Ebenezer Sibly was up
to Masonically. His name was frequently linked to that of Th omas Dunckerley,
who seemed to be a much better-known and better-researched fellow. Read-
ing about Dunckerley promised to provide insights that would lead to a clearer
understanding of Sibly. But the more I read, the more I became convinced, like
Sir Edward Walpole, that it just was not true – none of it. Most historians are
closet detectives, so tracking down the fi ctions and separating them from the
facts of Dunckerley’s life became an obsession and a pleasure. I was continually
impressed by his inventiveness and audacity. For an English Anglican, he had a
surprising amount of chutzpah.
Th omas Dunckerley was a fascinating man, full of astonishing contradic-
tions. I am convinced he was tremendously talented and dedicated. He devoted
himself to his wife, his king, the Royal Navy (aft er a fashion) and to Freema-
sonry. He just had a problem with the truth. Of course, it was impossible to just
tell one lie and leave it at that. His was a big lie, and it required dozens of ancil-
lary fi bs. He had to keep track of them all, and call them up casually, for the sake
of consistency, for the rest of his life. Additionally, Dunckerley seems to have had
a genuine problem with numbers, as evidenced by his fi nancial diffi  culties, and
that seems to have spilled over into confusion with dates. Th is was so consist-
ently the case that his family and colleagues must not have thought a thing of it
– he probably could not remember their birthdays either. By the end of Dunck-
erley’s life, he undoubtedly believed his own mythology – he would almost have
to have thoroughly assimilated the persona, so that the ‘real’ Th omas, that hus-
band and father who stepped off  the Prince in 1763 to a very uncertain future,
was almost entirely gone. Even with the aura of royalty, his life was much more
diffi  cult than he ever imagined it would be. Who would have thought that a
king’s son could not fi nd someone, anyone, to give him a decent job? But that
was indeed the case.

--- Sayfa 167 ---
154
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
Forget all the nonsense you hear about dark Masonic secrets. Freemasonry is
an inherently moral society; that is real the point, aft er all. Brotherhood, relief,
truth. Telling enormous lies about your family is not part of the ritual. And dedi-
cated Freemason that he was, Dunckerley lived a lie. He got up with it every
day and went to bed with it at night. He probably did not tell his wife, Han-
nah, the truth. How could he? Th at is why he explicitly wrote her out of the
accepted narrative. Mrs Pinkney told him to come alone, and not to bring his
wife. He probably never revealed his secret to anyone, and one wonders whether
that chafed. Did telling that one big lie make him more willing to take the sort
of risks he did with Freemasonry? Trying out new degrees without permission
from headquarters, assuming authority that had not actually been off ered, run-
ning election scams, inviting in the ladies? Or was that all an expression of the
same self-confi dence and restlessness that sent him out of Mr Simpson’s barber
shop in the fi rst place? When Dunckerley found Sibly he recognized the young
Mason would be of use to him, not just because Sibly was a chancer, but because
they both were.
How could he do it? How could he claim his mother was an adulteress, his
father a wife-abuser, and how could he claim that he was himself illegitimate
when his parents were a respectably married, apparently happy couple? It seems
to have been relatively easy, actually. Making up the accepted narrative and
passing it around was by far the easiest bit of his masquerade. He was an elo-
quent speaker, a good storyteller. He knew his audience. Th e odds seemed to be
with him. Th e thing to remember about the eighteenth century is that Th omas
Dunckerley was not the only person reinventing himself. Th ere were many peo-
ple doing it – leaving debts, leaving a bad marriage, leaving a child on the church
steps, changing sexual identity, skipping out on an indenture, assuming unearned
credentials or titles – there were lots of reasons to reinvent oneself.1 Clare Lyons’s
recent study documents people who went even further than Dunckerley, to the
extent of changing gender. Her article begins with two examples of assumed
gender identity in eighteenth-century Philadelphia: the transvestite man Ann
Alweye, and Mary Hamilton, who lived as a man and was convicted in Eng-
land in 1746 of pretending to be another woman’s husband. Compared to this
extreme, what becomes apparent is the genius behind Dunckerley’s scheme. He
could retain his personal identity and stay at home, where he was known and
liked, and at the same time he could become more socially important and mate-
rially comfortable. It seemed almost foolproof.
So, Th omas Dunckerley was not the man I thought he was. Nor was he the
Freemason that 200 years’ worth of his brethren thought he was. Many of my
friends within Freemasonry have been anxious to see how Dunckerley emerges
from this inquiry. In terms of his actual Masonic accomplishments, I think they
will probably be both reassured and a little disappointed. Dunckerley devoted

--- Sayfa 168 ---
Epilogue
155
a tremendous amount of time and energy to building up the Modern Grand
Lodge, and Freemasonry in general. Michel Brodsky hit the nail on the head
when he argued that Dunckerley accomplished two important things in his
Masonic career:
Th omas Dunckerley’s masonic career between 1766 and his death in 1795 contrib-
uted two important modifi cations in the Grand Lodge of England. First he ‘managed’
the Provinces under his authority and contributed to the change in ‘management’ of
the premier Grand Lodge, whilst also actively promoting the growth of the ‘Higher
degrees’, Royal Arch, Knight Templar, Mark, Ark Mariners, etc. … within those very
same lodges which he ruled over as Provincial Grand Master.2
A quarter of a century and a lot of research later, I think that still has to be our
appraisal. No grand, sweeping, permanent changes, but some signifi cant modifi -
cations and enhanced expectations about what a Provincial Grand Master might
accomplish. Oh, and he introduced an expanded menu of degrees and orders,
many of which still enjoy a rather anomalous relationship with the United
Grand Lodge of England.
To understand the charismatic aura that still surrounds Th omas Dunckerley
the man, despite the centuries and despite the lies, we need to turn, a tad sheep-
ishly, to George Oliver, who said that in his Masonic life, ‘Bro. Dunckerley was
brilliant as the stately progress of a comet amidst the permanent orbs of heaven’.3
Comets do not actually have much of an eff ect on us mere mortals, but they
defi nitely catch our attention as they pass through. As for Dunckerley’s appraisal
of himself, if he had it to do over again, at the end of his life, would he have still
sent those letters? I think so. He was a proud man. If the universe had been a fair
place, he would have been a king’s son – and not a bastard or, even worse, a fake
bastard. It was not his fault that we believed him, and turned him into a hero.
Th at is an impossibly diffi  cult role to live up to. No wonder he was cranky.

--- Sayfa 170 ---
– 157 –
ADDENDUM
It is frequently diffi  cult to bring research to a close, even once the text is in the
hands of the editor. Fortunately, all the materials in this little addendum nicely
reinforce the overarching argument of the study – that Th omas Dunckerley
was not the son of George II, as he claimed to be. While Dunckerley’s narrative
omits any mention of siblings, it now appears that he was the sixth of at least
seven children born to Adam and Mary Dunckerley between 1713 and 1726.
An additional sister, Elizabeth, was baptized in 1718 at St Mary-le-Strand. As
children who died before baptism frequently do not show up in parish registers,
even for burial, there may well have been other siblings about whom we will
never know. Baptismal notices of Th omas Dunckerley’s own two children have
been located in the registries of Plymouth churches. Mary Ann was baptized at
Charles Church in 1746, while Th omas Bolnest was baptized at St Andrews in
1747. Finally, two pieces of evidence have appeared in support of William Bray’s
assertion that the widowed Mary Dunckerley turned to mantua-making. Th e
register of duties paid for apprentices’ indentures, held at the National Archives,
reveals that, by 1735, Mary Dunckerley of St James, Westminster, had taken
Marg Muschamp as an apprentice to learn mantua-making. She may also have
been the Mary ‘Dunckley’ of St Botolph, Aldgate, who paid a duty in 1741 for
her apprentice Susannah Short, who was learning the craft  of millinery.

--- Sayfa 172 ---
– 159 –
APPENDIX 1
Although Th omas Dunckerley enjoyed some brief fame in the nineteenth cen-
tury for the alleged brilliance of his Masonic writing, this perception was based
largely on George Oliver’s speculation about works which did not in fact exist.
As for what Dunckerley did write, it is diffi  cult to say what has been lost – cer-
tainly most of the thousands of letters he wrote over the course of his Masonic
career are gone. Some few may be tucked away in forgotten corners, but it now
seems unlikely that there is a cache of vital Dunckerley literature hidden in a Pro-
vincial Masonic archive, waiting to be discovered. Aside from correspondence,
Dunckerley was an enthusiastic promoter of his own Masonic literature, and we
can be reasonably sure that nearly all of what he was most proud of has survived.
Th e most important examples of this are readily available: ‘Th e Light and Truth
of Masonry Explained’ and his 1769 charge to the Freemasons in Marlborough
are both included in Sadler’s biography, which is available online and in facsimile
edition. ‘Th e Moral Part of Masonry Explained’ was originally published as a
companion to ‘Th e Light and Truth’, but had already fallen out of the public eye
during Dunckerley’s lifetime. Copies of this text are rare, but one may be con-
sulted at the Library and Museum of Freemasonry at the United Grand Lodge
of England.1 Likewise, Dunckerley’s unpublished essays for the Sober Society
of Portsmouth are available in photocopy at the Library and Museum.2 Close
readers will notice that Dunckerley drew heavily on these early essays for the
remainder of his career as a public speaker, as well he might – the notebook con-
tains thirteen compositions on many of the most pertinent and persistent issues
of human engagement, and were as relevant for Freemasonry as they had been for
the Sober Society. Sadler and other Masonic publications reprint various songs
and poems Dunckerley composed or adapted for use in Craft  lodges, Masonic
celebrations and for use by the Royal Arch and Knights Templar.3 Th e Freema-
sons’ Magazine is a particularly rich source of such materials, and several volumes
may be consulted online. Th ese little compositions were popular in their day – ‘A
Hymn for a Chapter of Royal Arch Masons’ found its way into John Williams’s
1779 naval log, and travelled with him around the world’s oceans.4 However, as
we have seen with the poem by Fitz George, attribution of authorship for several

--- Sayfa 173 ---
160
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
of Dunckerley’s poems and songs may need to be reconsidered.5 Taken as a body,
Dunckerley’s Masonic writings are interesting as typical examples of their time
and context, and Arthur Waite seems to have been correct in describing them as
merely conventional.6
Dunckerley’s talent for telling a good story in prose comes through most
clearly in the letters and papers he wrote to promote his campaign for recogni-
tion as an illegitimate son of George II. Again, Dunckerley’s fi nal version of this
accepted narrative can be found in Sadler, and does not need to be reproduced
here.7 Th e earliest iteration of the story, however, has not appeared in print, and
may be of interest to some readers. What follows is two documents. Th e fi rst is
the letter Th omas Dunckerley wrote to Major Edward Walpole in June 1766.
Th is may be taken as typical of the letters Dunckerley sent to a whole host of
gentlemen he thought might be in a position to carry his story to George III. Th e
second document is headed ‘Mary Dunckerley’s Declaration to Mrs Pinkney,
January 2d: 1760’. Both are in the Clinton Papers at the Clements Library at the
University of Michigan, and were thoughtfully reviewed by Arlene Shy in 1997.8
Shy came to the obvious conclusion – the one that Dunckerley had intended –
that Mary Bolnest had been seduced by George II, and Th omas Dunckerley was
the result of that mésalliance. Now, however, we all know better.
Both documents appear with the kind permission of the William L. Cle-
ments Library, University of Michigan.
Document 1
Th omas Dunckerley to Major Edward Walpole
June 9th: 1766
Sir,
According to your request, is inclos’d a copy of my Mother’s Declaration,
fi ve days before she departed this life: as it may not be improper to give some
account, in what manner it came to my knowledge; permit me to acquaint you,
that in January 1760, soon aft er my return from the Expedition against Quebec
I reciev’d an account of my Mother’s death; and having obtained permission to
be absent from the Vanguard for fi ve days, I came to London and attended her
funeral. Among the few that I invited to the Ceremony, was Mrs Pinkney, who
had been many years a Neighbor to my Mother in Somerset House; and on our
return from ye burial, she desired I would call on her the next morning, hav-
ing something of consequence to tell me. I waited on her the next day: and the
inclosed [sic] is a copy of what I took in writing as she related it to me.
She also told me, that my Grandmother Bolnest, Mrs Cannon a Midwife,
and her Self, were present at my birth on the 23d day of October 1724: that my

--- Sayfa 174 ---
Appendix 1
161
Mother then said the Prince of Wales was my Father; but enjoin’d them to the
strictest secrecy. Mrs Pinkney likewise inform’d me that my Mother was a Physi-
cian’s Daughter, and lived with the honourable Mrs Walpole, when the Prince of
Wales debauched her; that Mrs Walpole discovered what had happened, and had
her Married to Mr Dunckerley, who was then attending the Duke of Devonshire,
on a visit to Sir Robert Walpole at Houghton in Norfolk, and that the Cer-
emony was perform’d by Doctor Bland.
I must confess this Information gave me great surprise, and much uneasiness:
but as I was obliged to return immediately to my duty on board the Vanguard,
I made it known to no person at that time but Captain Swanton, who said that
those who did not know we would look on it to be nothing more than a Gossip’s
tale. We were then bound a second time to Quebec, and Captn Swanton was of
opinion, that if on our return I could be introduced to the King, he would cer-
tainly recolect [sic] the aff air: But on our return the King was dead. I have since
that time mention’d it to several to whom I have the honour to be known, par-
ticularly Lord Chesterfi eld; Lord William Gordon; Sir Edward Walpole; Th os
Missing Esqr of Stubbington in Hampshire; Coll Maxwell; and several Offi  cers
at Gibraltar, and Minorca: and to Captain Digby; Captain Rutheven; and Cap-
tain Meadows of the Navy: who are all of opinion I should endeavor to get it
made known to the Royal Family.
I believe the virtues of humanity are predominant in the hearts of the King
and his Royal Brothers: but I am unacquainted at Court, and know not how to
gain access to the Royal Family, or their belief of an Account that has no living
Evidence, but my self (that I know of) to support it. – Mrs Pinkney being also
dead.
Aft er having been an Offi  cer in the Navy twenty years, I am superannuated
with a pension of forty four pounds per annum, which will barely procure Sub-
sistance [sic] for my self and family: and it is my misfortune to have a Daughter,
whose leg was cut off , occasioned by a fall.
When I refl ect on the late King being my Father, which I sincerely believe;
when I think on the hardships I have known; my present obscurity, and narrow
Circumstances; I cannot help condemning that false Modesty and Delicacy of
my grandmother, and Mother, that concealed me from the knowledge of my
Royal Father:
I am well convinced of your Friendship, and submit the above to your Con-
sideration: requesting your Opinion and assistance. I am your much obliged
humble Servant,
Th os Dunckerley

Document 2
Mary Dunckerley’s Declaration to Mrs Pinkney,
January 2nd:1760

Somerset House, January 2nd:1760
Mary Dunckerley being dangerously ill with the Gout in her stomach,
and believing it will be her Death, is desirous (at the request of her friend Mrs
Pinkney) that this her deathbed Declaration, may be made known to her son,
and to none but him.
At the latter end of November 1723, Mr Dunckerley went to Chatsworth in
Derbyshire, on some business for the Duke of Devonshire; and did not return
till the May following. At Christmas I went to see Mrs Mekin at Lady Ranelagh’s:
Mr Lumley happened to come there and paid me the greatest respect, and said
that I stood in my own light, or I might be the happiest Woman in England. I
knew his Meaning, but made no reply; and went back to Somerset house the next
day……A fortnight aft er, I had an Invitation to Lady Ranelagh’s and her Coach
was sent for me; I was surprised to fi nd Mr Lumley there again. He handed me
from the Coach to the parlour, where I found the Prince of Wales, who I had too
well known before my unhappy marriage. At his request, for I could deny him
nothing, I staid severel [sic] days, during which time he made me fi ve Visits; and
on Candlemass day I went home. Soon aft er I found my self Sick and Breeding,
and was resolved to make an end of my life. I was taken very ill.
Lady Stanley came to see, but I durst not let her know my disorder….. Mrs
Mekin came, and I told her the consequence of what had happened. Th e next day
she came to me again, and brought me Bank Bills for Fift y Pounds, in a cover from
Mr Lumley, acquainting me, it was the Command of his Royal Highness; Th e said
Lady Ranelagh was coming to see me; and in less than an hour her Ladyship came.
Th ey advised me to go in the Country, and said a House was taken for me at Rich-
mond, but I refused it; desiring they would not let the Prince or Mr Lumley know
that I was with child, and I never found they did. Doctor Mead attended me: he
ordered me to be bled, and in two days I could sit up. Mr Dunckerley came from
Chatsworth in May, and seem’d not displeas’d to fi nd me with Child. I disdained
to deceive him, and told him what had happened: he commended my Conduct
with so much Joy that I could not help despising his Meaness [sic]; and his barba-
rous behavior to me in the last month of my Time, is what I always resented; when
he threw a Cat in my face, and swore he would mark the Bastard.
Our Separation follow’d aft er my Delivery, and he kept the Secret on his
Account, for he had two Places, and several considerable Advantages at the price
of my unhappiness. My son might have been known to his Royal Father; but
my dear Mother reclaimed me from so criminal a passion; and dread of public
shame prevented my making it public.

APPENDIX 2
Another document that is key to understanding how the various versions of the
Dunckerley narrative fi t together is the little sketch collected by William Bray.
Bray was a student of human nature, and his fascination with our foibles is amply
illustrated in the rough draft  of an essay he intended to write about Th omas
Dunckerley.1 Th e similarity between this sketch and fi ve related contributions
to the Gentleman’s Magazine in the months following Dunckerley’s death sug-
gest Bray was the anonymous author of some or all of those pieces as well.2 What
appears below with the kind permission of the Surrey History Centre, is a direct
transcription. Th e erratic spelling and punctuation are faithful to the original.
Document
Dunckerley, Mr having some resemblance to ye royal family pretended to be a
son of ye late k/G2 & by that means obtained a pension of £400 a yr & apart-
ments in Hampton Crt. His history is this; His grandmother’s name was Bonus,
she was nurse to Sr. Ed. Walpole as soon as born & continued to 6 years. In this
time she recommended one of her daus. To be a chambermaid at Houghton, an
uncommonly plain & homely girl. Th e D. of Devonshire was on a visit to Sr.
Rob. at xmas when a servant of his, Adam Dunkerley took a liking to the girl
& married her. Th e D. appointed him porter at Somerset House. Mr D. was
their son. Sr. Edw. Always bore great regard to Mrs Bonus, got her an apart-
ment in Somerset House & allowed her £20 a year. Ld. Plymouth whom she
had also nursed, allowed her £20 more & Adam D dying, got an apartment for
his widow in Somerset House. Th e old woman oft en came to Sr Edwards house
& was always kindly reced. Her dau: became a mantua maker & was employed
for Sr Edws children & Mrs Clements, as they have many rects to bills to testify.
Th e old lady said she loved roast beef, but had no convenience to dress it, nor
coud she dispose of so large a joint, wished she might send on a Mond. Morn. to
Sr Eds house (then at Chelsea)—accordingly her grandson, Mr D., then a little
boy, brought a basket & clean napkins every Mond. Morng for a long time. Benj.
Brown then cook to Sr E has verifi ed this. Th e boy was aft erwards put apprentice
to a barber, but ran away & got on board of Sr J Norris’s ship when going with a

--- Sayfa 177 ---
164
Th omas Dunckerley and English Freemasonry
fl eet into ye Baltic. His mo. Came in great distress to Sr E., the masr threatened
to prosecute, & then she’d be ruined. Sr E gave the msr 10 guineas to silence
him, & went to Sr J Norris, told him a very clever boy was got on board his ship,
& desired him to take care of him [this?] he promised & performed. He was
taught what was necessary in ye sea service, in wch he made gr profi ciency, & was
aft erward recommended by Sr E to Ld Anson as ye fi ttest person in ye world to
be a masr of the [mathematical?] school at Portsmouth. Here he behaved so well
that Ld. A thanked Sr E for sending him. He made himself a masr of gunnery &
[illeg.] went to the siege of Quebec in the last war & gained much credit.
Aft er his return & ye death of his grandmother & mother, he imagined he
coud gain credit to the story of his supposed father, & hinted to Sr E that he
had heard such a thing.-------Sir E. treated his story with ye utmost contempt, &
expressed himself strongly to him about ye attempts. However he got it carried
to ye king, & brought ye circumstances of ye apartment in Somerset House as
a proof, insisting that his mor, tho’ poor, was a gentlewoman & appealed to Sr.
Edw. W. for the truth. Th e k. sent Mr Worsely to Sr. Edw. Who told him that Mr
D. had behaved so well, that he really believed he deserved some pension, but
told him the rest of his history. Th e k. gave him £100 a year & sent word to Sr
Edw. Th at it was for his recommendation. However he aft erwards found means
to interest some persons about ye k in his favor, & he got £300 a year more &
apartments at Hampton Crt.
Sr. E was so off ended at his eff rontery in appealing to him for ye truth of a
story, wch Sr. E so well knew to be false, that he ordered his servt never to let him
in, & did not see him for 3 or 4 years, but at last a new servt who did not know
him, one day let him in; Dr Butler, now bishop of Oxford, was with Sr. Edw, who
entered into the story with Mr D, but he denied that he knew any thing of what
is above stated, prior to the recommendation to Anson, but owned Sr Ed had
told him all of it, when he fi rst mentd the story.
Sr E had a long letter on the subject in his possession written by D. to a gent
who gave it [to] Sr Ed, in which the latter pt of which he contradicts which he
had asserted at the beginning.
Sr. Ed. Walpole
19 Apr. 1777

23. Warranted in 1735, this lodge met at a number of taverns in Plymouth Dock, now
known as Devonport. It was erased in 1777.
24. Th is lodge was warranted in 1748, and met in several taverns in Plymouth. Temporarily
erased in 1780, it was reinstated in 1781 and renamed the Prince George Lodge No. 86.
It was permanently erased in 1828.
25. Chudley, Th omas Dunckerley, p. 10; London Chronicle (13 September 1757); [travel-
ling] certifi cate from Lodge No. 67 at Plymouth Dock to Dunckerley, February 1768,
LMF, not yet catalogued.
26. Th e lodge at Masons’ Arms was No. 50, and lapsed soon aft er it was warranted in 1763.
Th e regimental lodges were in the First Regiment East Devon Militia, No. 216, war-
ranted in 1781, and in the Twenty-third Regiment of Foot (Royal Welsh Fusiliers), No.
252, erased in 1822.
27. No. 135 was warranted in 1748 as No. 200. It was erased in 1758. Lodge of Concord No.
232 was warranted in 1758. From 1769 to 1803 it was a Master’s Lodge, held to confer
the Th ird Degree. In 1777 it was named the Lodge of Unity. It lapsed sometime during
or aft er 1802. Lodge of Sincerity No. 454 was warranted in 1769 and was still meeting
in 1869. Lodge of Friendship No. 416 was warranted in 1771 and was still meeting in
1871. From 1771 to 1808 it also functioned as a Master’s Lodge. Lodge of Contentment
No. 269 was erased in 1786. No. 463 was warranted in 1784, and lapsed around 1800.
